# Behind the Mystery of Ether

© Dr. ing. Josef Daneš, 1985

**ZA TAJEMSTVÍM ÉTERU**
**(Behind the Mystery of Ether)**

Dr. ing. Josef Daneš

Reviewed by doc. dr. ing. Miroslav Joachim, ing. Zdeněk Prosek.
Responsible editor: Vlasta Hušková.
Cover and graphic design by Miroslav Hruška.
Artistic editor: Jaroslava Hrachovcová.
Technical editor: Pavla Rodná.
Published by Nakladatelství dopravy a spojů (Transport and Communications Publishing House) as their 5,930th publication.
First edition. Prague 1985. 192 pages of text, 24 pages of appendices, 42 illustrations.
Printed by Moravské tiskařské závody, n. p., Olomouc.
AA 15.69, VA 16.23. Print run: 8,000 copies.
OS-31-050-85-05-38. Price: Kčs 19,—.

**ALL NAMES IN THIS BOOK ARE REAL, ALL EVENTS ARE TRUE. ITS SOURCES ARE AUTHENTIC DOCUMENTS, CORRESPONDENCE, AND PERSONAL MEMORIES OF PARTICIPANTS.**

---

The concept of ether (not as a chemical substance, but in the sense of superseded physical theories) designated a hypothetical elastic fluid without mass, filling space, capable of penetrating even solid bodies and enabling the propagation of electricity, light, and heat, but above all the propagation of electromagnetic waves.

»It is a medium that not only flows around all material particles, but penetrates them more intimately than water penetrates the pores of a sponge,« writes Joseph Roussel in his book »Le premier livre de l'amateur de T.S.F.« (Libraire Vuibert, Paris, 1922).

Frederic A. Collins dedicates the first ten pages of his book »Wireless Telegraphy« (McGraw, New York, 1905) to the then-current conceptions of ether, particularly in light of the works of Huygens, Faraday, Maxwell, and Hertz.

Albert Einstein put the final period on the physical concept of ether. Yet the concept of ether as a poetic metamorphosis persists...

---

**IN PLACE OF A PREFACE**

name: "2026-09-15-za-tajemstvim-eteru-chunk-002"
description: "Translation of chunk 2 from 'Za tajemstvím éteru' by Dr. ing. Josef Daneš, 1985. Introduction to the Prague setting and radio pioneers. Source: https://ok5nw.nagano.cz/e-publikace/za_tajemstvim_eteru.pdf. Language: Czech. Author: Dr. ing. Josef Daneš."
---

# In Place of a Preface

The golden spires of the Mostecká Tower glisten. Native Praguers and visitors from distant lands find themselves enchanted again and again by the unique views from the ancient Charles Bridge. The double stone gate, bent in the middle and adorned with three battlements, leads to Malá Strana, into the aristocratic and diplomatic quarter, into the quarter of palaces and houses with red-tiled roofs.

The first street on the left after the bridge is Lázeňská Street. At its end stands the former convent building of the Order of the Knights of Malta, once part of the fortified complex between Čertovka, Velkopřevorské Square, and Maltese Square. Adjacent to it is house number 6 on the corner of Lázeňská and Saská Streets. A yellow inscription on a marble plaque recalls that there were once baths and a hotel here. In 1893, the author of travelogues from Paris to Jerusalem and Memoirs from the Beyond, François René de Chateaubriand, stopped here for a week. In 1698, Peter the Great lived here.

I enter the passageway. A white-haired old woman descends one of the staircases. I ask her if she remembers where engineer Štěpánek lived after the First World War.

»I've lived here since '35,« she replies, »but I don't know that name.«

»He lived on the second floor and had windows facing the Maltese Garden.«

»Then it could be in this wing,« the old woman points. »Ask there, but older people don't live here anymore.«

And indeed, I found no one who remembered when engineer Štěpánek lived here with his wife Klementína...

Štěpánek was born in 1891. He was the editor of the magazine Nová epocha (New Epoch), which wrote about all manner of things. About astronomical telescopes, about cuneiform script, about ostrich farms in South Africa, about the principle of relativity, about treasures in the tombs of pharaohs, and about how much a punch weighs. Engineer Štěpánek also wrote about radiotelegraphy, and from 1922 he began publishing the magazine Radioamatér as a supplement to Nová epocha, which also included instructions for practical experiments. It was the first magazine of its kind in our country and, after England and America, the third in the world.

With proofs, engineer Štěpánek went to the Haas printing house in Anenský dvůr, where a young graphic artist named Soukup eagerly read his articles. Eventually, he left the printing house, began working at Nová epocha, and became an enthusiastic collaborator in Štěpánek's experiments...

The Štěpáneks' apartment thus served simultaneously as an editorial office and laboratory. It was spacious, simply but functionally furnished. One of the most remarkable objects was a funnel-shaped loudspeaker from which Morse code signals emanated.

On May 17, 1923, someone knocked firmly on the Štěpáneks' door. Mrs. Štěpánková, who had long been unable to shake off oppressive feelings, turned pale. She glanced at her husband and Mr. Soukup, but both continued working calmly, as if they hadn't even noticed the knocking. Two gentlemen entered the apartment.

»Police,« one of them showed his service badge with a practiced motion.

»I am engineer Hněvkovský from the Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs,« the other introduced himself.

They presented a search warrant and got to work. The uninvited guests paid no attention to papers. Only police agent Rousal flipped through the correspondence, briefly examined some documents, and set them aside again. The attention of both gentlemen was drawn to the apparatuses...

Perhaps the reader won't be disappointed that we haven't entered a den of crime, but rather the modest workplace of one of the pioneers who passionately devoted themselves to uncovering the secrets of waves propagating through the »ether.«

From their stories and fates, of which we will approach the most interesting, there wafts the romance of discovery, the power of humanity's age-old desire for knowledge, overcoming even incredible obstacles—superstitions, prejudices, and intrigues. We will simultaneously peek behind the scenes of events connected with radio and its application, which in their time excited the world.

We could also recount many directly adventurous stories from the most recent times, connected with building radiocommunication systems linking continents and enabling penetration into the cosmos, with searching for signals arriving from other planets. However, that already exceeds the scope of our little book. Besides, we have focused especially on the past decades also because archives are often stingy with them, and there are few direct witnesses among us anymore.

It remains only to add that amateur radio, whose first steps in our country we will follow in particular detail, was never and especially today is not merely a noble pastime. There is perhaps no other human leisure activity so closely connected with the effort to place technical progress in the service of humanity as amateur radio. Today it is therefore considered an essential complement to the functioning of radiocommunication systems, and the boundary between amateur and professional in this field is increasingly blurring.

We would therefore wish that this publication contributes at least modestly not only to knowledge of history, but also to awakening interest especially among young people in this promising field, so that, like the heroes of our little book, they might dedicate their lives to it.


---

# AUTHOR

## EXCITING SPARKS


---


---
name: "2026-09-15-za-tajemstvim-eteru-chunk-004"
description: "Translation of chunk 4 from 'Za tajemstvím éteru' by Dr. ing. Josef Daneš, 1985. Early radio history in Czech lands, 1908 Prague exhibition, Popov's invention. Source: https://ok5nw.nagano.cz/e-publikace/za_tajemstvim_eteru.pdf. Language: Czech. Author: Dr. ing. Josef Daneš."
---

Today it is hard for us to believe that those interested in the secrets of wireless signal transmission often had to hide their experiments from the law and were even suspected of charlatanism. This was connected to the fact that the first knowledge from this field spread only very slowly.

We would find small reports in the magazines Vynálezy a pokroky (Inventions and Progress), Domácí dílna (Home Workshop), and Epocha. Ottův kapesní slovník naučný (Otto's Pocket Encyclopedic Dictionary) from 1908 defines it thus:

»Wireless telegraphy is the electrical transmission of dispatches without the transmitting and receiving stations being connected by wire. In practice, electromagnetic wireless telegraphy has found application, which, using electric waves developed by the discharge of a Ruhmkorff inductor, transmits dispatches through the air to the receiving station... Wireless telegraphy, used in the navy, is not limited only to communicating dispatches to ships or the shore, but is also used for controlling torpedo navigation, igniting mines, etc.«

In 1916, the book »Základy telegrafie bez drátu« (Fundamentals of Wireless Telegraphy) by Prof. J. M. Kadlec was published, in which the theory of the electromagnetic field is interestingly explained.

In our country, practical experiments were begun by Prof. Dr. Domalíp in the laboratory of the Czech Technical University in Prague in the last years of the 19th century. The public first became acquainted with radiotelegraphy at the commercial and industrial exhibition in Prague in 1908. The station was demonstrated by the Imperial-Royal Postal Directorate in Prague. It was loaned by the firm Siemens & Halske, A. G. in Vienna. The apparatuses were placed in a wooden pavilion near Marold's panorama of the Battle of Lipany. The Prague Illustrated Courier published a photograph of the station on August 4, 1908, and writes:

»We present the interior of the telegraph station for wireless connection between Prague and Karlovy Vary. If you have weak nerves, the deafening crackle of electrical discharges and the dazzling glow of powerful sparks will soon drive you away. In the middle of the pavilion is a so-called table containing the receiving circuit and appropriate apparatuses, telegraph apparatus, telephone receivers, etc. Behind it are placed the apparatuses of the transmitting circuit, a copper silver-plated hollow tube, spirally wound upward, which has a spark gap at the top, i.e., two zinc rings where electricity discharges in large, lightning-like sparks, accompanied by the aforementioned crackling. Beside it are placed 6 tall Leyden jars and below an inductorium which produces current with a high voltage of 60,000 Volts. Behind the building is placed a mast, called an antenna, with wires branching in all directions. The lower wires serve to secure the antenna, the upper ones, leading from the top, serve to capture electrical waves and there are 12–14 of these wires. The Prague antenna, 26 m high, is made of a metal mixture called magnelium, the Karlovy Vary station has a steel antenna, 30 m high. The wires leading from the antenna terminate in insulators, which are then tied by rope to various surrounding objects. Telegraphing is done exactly as on ordinary telegraph apparatuses. The official places his hand on the key and taps out dispatches. With each press of the key, a spark flies through the spark gap and a crack is heard. Simultaneously in Karlovy Vary, the receiving telegraph apparatus captures the dispatch in dashes and dots on paper. The message is thus tapped out in telegraphic alphabet and the listening official translates this ticking into ordinary speech of us mortals with surprising speed and ease.«

In the crowd of younger and older curious onlookers besieging the radio station is also a tall, thin, nine-year-old boy with a tousled mop of hair and sharp eyes. Pravoslav Motyčka. The fact that a telegram is transmitted without a wire fascinated him. He wanted to get to the bottom of it. Nechvátal's »Telegrafie a telefonie v železniční dopravě« (Telegraphy and Telephony in Railway Transport) later clarified some things for him. He devoured technical magazines and books.

Vynálezy a pokroky published a year before the exhibition, in 1907, a series by Prof. Dr. Jaroslav Jeništa titled »Telegrafie bez drátu« (Wireless Telegraphy). Prof. Jeništa informs about hydrotelegraphy, about electromagnetic and electrostatic induction, about Branly's coherer from 1890, and writes:

»Only in 1884 did the first experiments with telegraphy using electric waves begin, and in 1895 the first description of complete equipment for recording electric waves was finally published. This equipment was intended exclusively only for registering atmospheric discharges, by which electric waves are always also created, and was produced by Russian professor Popov. At that time, he constructed the first recording station for wireless telegraphy.«

On page 196, a description of the station is printed, and on the following page, Jeništa's article continues:

»Popov, professor at the military academy in Kronstadt, submitted a report on his invention in April 1895 and states there that he obtained with the described equipment an apparatus that marks each individual electric wave with a short ringing, whereas if waves are created continuously one after another, the bell rings quite regularly. But Popov went even further: he did not only capture waves created by atmospheric discharges, but created waves himself using a Hertz oscillator and captured them at a distance of 1 km. When he then used a more powerful oscillator and an even more sensitive coherer, he could detect signals sent from one station to another even at a distance of 5 km.«

In 1909, a description of a home station for spark telegraphy with an inductor and coherer, whose resistance drops from 800 to 5 ohms after the impact of electromagnetic waves, was published in the magazine Vynálezy a pokroky.

The Prague–Karlovy Vary connection at the 1908 exhibition was not the only public demonstration of that kind. Smaller in scope and range, but also interesting, was the public demonstration of spark telegraphy in Městeck Králové in 1912.

A half-meter-long antenna ending in a metal disk was connected to the inductor. The receiver activated an electric bell. Expert teacher Vonka lectured in the town hall hall; the apparatus was borrowed from the physics cabinet of the burgher school.

After the lecture came the most interesting part: listeners came to the apparatus, pressed the key, and the bell faithfully reproduced signals transmitted over a distance of 8 m. The most effective was when the transmitter or receiver was alternately taken out into the hallway and electromagnetic waves were allowed to pass through the wall.

The lecture had an official aftermath.

The next day, an official from the district court came and began investigating:

»Was this a prearranged sleight of hand? Didn't you have another person in reserve who was activating the electric bell?«

He could not be convinced by any verbal explanation that the audience had not been intentionally deceived. Teacher Vonka had no choice but to put the apparatuses into operation again, this time under official supervision, and demonstrate wireless signal transmission from one end of the cabinet to the other. Only then was the case closed and the files filed away.

## THE CATASTROPHE OF THE CENTURY


---


---
name: "2026-09-15-za-tajemstvim-eteru-chunk-006"
description: "Translation of chunk 6 from 'Za tajemstvím éteru' by Dr. ing. Josef Daneš, 1985. The Titanic disaster and radio's role in rescue. Source: https://ok5nw.nagano.cz/e-publikace/za_tajemstvim_eteru.pdf. Language: Czech. Author: Dr. ing. Josef Daneš."
---

Mr. Boušek became the hero of the day. People crowded into his shop on Celetná Street and at his branch on Na Příkopě Street.

»May I have a look?«

»Did Mr. Peterson survive?«

»He only went to see. He didn't sail to America,« Mr. Boušek explained, goodness knows for how manyth time.

Mr. Peterson, a pipe manufacturer, had visited Prague a few months earlier as a member of an official delegation from the City of London. He met with his customer, Mr. Boušek, a wholesale dealer in smoking supplies. From Belfast he then sent a postcard: »Gruss aus dem grossten Schiff der Welt. Charles Peterson.« On the postcard was depicted the 280 m long, seven-deck, gigantic for that time and not yet completely finished ship colossus Titanic.

The front and back of this postcard, both the image and text, were printed by the Prague Illustrated Courier on April 19, 1912. The entire print run was snatched up within hours. Completely. And the demand for it was such that the postcard from Mr. Peterson had to be printed again on April 23.

Titanic sailed from Southampton on April 10, 1912. Her voyage was to become the sensation of the year: a victory for the White Star Line in the battle for the Blue Riband. The ship's radio station was operated by two telegraphists, Phillips, not yet twenty-six years old, and his assistant Bride. They have their hands full. Besides normal operations, they must still handle several thousand telegrams with wishes for a happy voyage.

On the fourth day at 6 o'clock in the morning, the station stopped working. Phillips and Bride test the individual circuits.

The spark transmitter was not complicated. A robust telegraph key had, instead of a knob, a cylinder 4 to 5 cm high with a round insulating support for the fingers. It was equipped with a device for quenching sparks, because the keyed current reached an intensity of several tens of amperes. The source of primary current was sometimes an accumulator battery, but mostly a dynamo. With each interruption and closure of the primary current, a high voltage was induced in the secondary winding. The source of high-frequency oscillations were sparks jumping across the spark gap. The transmitter's output depended on their length and frequency of jumping. The frequency of sparks depended on the interrupter inserted in the primary circuit. The interrupter was vibratory, rotary, electrolytic, or mercury. It was required that the spark frequency be adjustable, and there were corresponding Q-code operational abbreviations:

- QSW increase spark frequency
- QSX decrease spark frequency

Tuning was done with oscillating circuits; tuning was called »syntonization.«

The detector in the receiver was a coherer. Calzecchi's coherer was a tube filled with metal filings, with a lead on each side. The filings offered high resistance to the flow of direct current, which dropped after irradiation with electromagnetic waves, and the coherer switched on the signaling device circuit or the electromagnet of a Morse writing apparatus. The conductive state persisted and it was necessary to shake the filings.

Branly's coherer had a piston with which it was possible to regulate the compression of the filings. Lodge replaced the piston with an adjusting screw. Kadlec writes in his book that the principle of the coherer is not clear and no one has yet succeeded in providing a serious theoretical explanation of its operation.

But Phillips and Bride didn't worry their heads about that. They found the fault in a broken cable from the dynamo in the engine room, and by noon they were transmitting again.

Meanwhile, the officers on the bridge watched the thermometer with concern. A sudden drop in temperature on the night of April 13–14 signaled the proximity of icebergs. They knew about the nighttime conversation between Captain Smith and the company president, Sir Bruce Ismay. Shortly after midnight, Phillips brought Captain Smith a radiogram from the ship Tourine. It warned of an iceberg spotted at 46° north latitude. With the radiogram in hand, the captain knocked on Ismay's cabin door:

»We should reduce speed by at least half.«

»And arrive in New York ten hours later?« countered Sir Bruce Ismay.

»I guarantee the passengers' safety. And on this ship, I am the master,« said Captain Smith.

»Yes, but only here on this ship,« the company president concluded the conversation.

What future awaits the captain if he loses the Blue Riband?

At seven o'clock in the evening on April 14, the Titanic's radio station received another warning. This time from the ship Baltic. Titanic rushes forward at full speed. In the salon, preparations are underway for a banquet to conclude the voyage.

**QTH 41 16 N ORTH 50 14 WEST**

The radio telegraphist of the steamship Californian, Mr. Morrison, is preparing for sleep. But first he puts on his headphones and listens to the hiss of sparks. In the mixture of signals, a strong, apparently nearby station stands out. Titanic. Mr. Morrison reaches for the key: »We have stopped our engines and are waiting here until morning. Our captain is afraid of icebergs.«

Phillips is corresponding with station VAZ, Cape Race, for which he still has several telegrams. He has no time for unnecessary chatter.

**QSO VAZ QTC**

**R 73 OM** - Mr. Morrison signs off. He shuts down the station and goes to lie down.

A few minutes after half past ten, the gong strikes. A man with binoculars sounds the alarm. On the bridge, they throw the ship's telegraph: Full steam astern. The ship rushes by inertia toward a dark mass with several dim lights that grows with every moment. Only a few dozen meters before the iceberg does it turn and pass by. On the bridge, they breathed a sigh of relief.

Only a few passengers noticed the slight rocking of the ship. In the lower cabins, they heard a rough sound. The captain, who was present at the banquet in the salon, hurried to the bridge.

»That was a close call,« they greeted him on the bridge.

The telephone rang. »The ship is torn open from the bow to the level of the bridge,« the engine room reported.

**CQD CQD C Q D ........ SOS SOS S O S ...........**

Less than a quarter hour after the intermezzo with the Californian, Titanic calls for help. Phillips alternates the distress signal CQD with the newly introduced SOS. Bride, who was supposed to be on duty from midnight, is already in the radio cabin. Mr. Morrison sleeps.

Lifeboats are being prepared. Women and children first.

First? There won't be enough even for them of those few lifeboats available on the Titanic. Women boarding the lifeboats will never see their husbands again; children will never meet their fathers again, and mostly not even their mothers.

Sailors with weapons in hand try to maintain order and prevent panic. They don't succeed. Many women suffered broken arms, legs, internal injuries in the crush. Some lifeboats overturned during lowering, and women and children disappeared in the waves. Sailors fire rockets. At one o'clock in the morning on April 14–15, the bow has sunk several meters. The deck is tilted. Men with life jackets jump into the icy water. The jackets keep them on the surface and prolong their dying. Water floods the cabins.

The third officer of the Californian sees the Titanic's lights through binoculars, sees rockets, but doesn't know it's the Titanic and has no idea what's happening there. At the radio station, they record the call signs of ships responding to the distress call: MKC Olympic, NBC Baltic, NQD Connecticut (at that time, four-letter call signs for ship stations had not yet been introduced).

On April 15 at a quarter to two, the nearest was Carpathia, a ship of the competing Cunard Line—68 miles; Mount Temple 70 miles, Birma 100 miles, Frankfurt 140 miles, Parisien and Virginia 150 miles, Baltic 300 miles, Olympic 560 miles (and Californian 22 miles). The disaster occurred 300 miles from Newfoundland. The coastal station VAZ at Cape Race hears and follows the entire correspondence. But why would it alarm the coastal fleet when so many ships are going to help?

Bride recognizes the Californian's lights with the naked eye.

»Can't they see our rockets?«

»Fishing boats use such rockets to summon their boats,« Phillips replies, knocking the pistol from Bride's hand and sitting down at the key again.

**SOS SOS SOS MKC MKC MKC . . .**

He reports to Olympic that they are going down. Both radio telegraphists already have their legs in water; papers, books, and various objects are floating in the cabin. People climb onto the railing, onto the roofs, onto any elevated spot. At 2:20, the pressure of water crushed the engine room doors. Water flooded the dynamos. When the lights began to flicker, Phillips managed to send one last dispatch: Give my love to my mother.

The radio station went silent; Titanic was plunged into darkness. Phillips and Bride wade onto the deck. They hear the screaming of people, maddened by despair and terror.

Carpathia arrived at four in the morning. It found 11 lifeboats and one pinnace. The rescued, with minor exceptions, were incapable of movement. Loading the 685 rescued took five hours. The ship's doctor of Carpathia, Dr. Larousse, operated on 18 patients, mostly amputations of arms and legs. Eight rescued died; several went mad. At the bottom of the boat from which they pulled Bride lay the body of a young man with fair hair and blue eyes. Suffocated. It was Phillips.

Bride was placed in the ship's infirmary with a fever. And when the radio telegraphist of Carpathia collapsed from overwork and from the sight around him, Bride takes his place and serves until Carpathia reaches New York. While working, he overhears the ship Mackay-Bennett calling the coastal station in Halifax:

**VAA VAA VAA D E MMB MMB MMB** - Reporting that it had recovered 306 dead.

Captain Smith had a chance to save himself. He didn't take it. He was last seen swimming toward the sinking ship. First Officer Lightolder was saved, Second Officer Max Dittmar-Pittmann, a handful of crew members, and—Sir Bruce Ismay.

Mr. Leary, a correspondent for the New York Times, happened to be spending his vacation on the Carpathia; he captured and preserved all the information. Important documentary materials also include the transcripts of investigative commissions in New York and London, British Parliament files, and the volume Shipping Casualties.

The newspaper Hlas národa (Voice of the Nation) reported on the Titanic disaster on Tuesday, April 16, 1912. The information from the Reuters agency is dated from the previous day. Hlas národa placed it only at the end of the daily news. It is very inaccurate. It claims that all passengers were rescued and that Titanic is slowly sailing toward Halifax. The following day, April 17, Hlas národa brings a report from Boston quoting spark telegrams from the ships Olympic and Carpathia. It states that over 1200 people perished and describes desperate scenes outside the White Star Line office in London.

In the following days, news about the Titanic filled entire pages of newspapers. Individual shipping companies considered it their duty to inform the world public where their ships were at the time of the disaster and what they were doing, or why they couldn't come to help.

Titanic disappeared from the Prague press only at the beginning of May. Her voyage, which was to be the event of the century, became the catastrophe of the century. However, it also became the decisive impulse for convening a conference on safety of life at sea, which met in London the very next year. The radio rescue service, despite all the self-sacrifice of individuals, was not yet up to the task. Those who survived, however, owe their lives solely and exclusively to radio telegraphy.


---

## AUSTRIA-HUNGARY


---


---
name: "2026-09-15-za-tajemstvim-eteru-chunk-008"
description: "Translation of chunk 8 from 'Za tajemstvím éteru' by Dr. ing. Josef Daneš, 1985. Early radio development in Austria-Hungary, military stations, Prof. Nušl's receiver at Ondřejov observatory. Source: https://ok5nw.nagano.cz/e-publikace/za_tajemstvim_eteru.pdf. Language: Czech. Author: Dr. ing. Josef Daneš."
---

In Austria-Hungary, the navy began with radio in 1902, the army in 1903. The first transmitters were spark transmitters. After 1910, experiments with Poulsen arc transmitters on wavelengths 850 to 3700 m were conducted between Korneuburg, Vienna, and Innsbruck. Arc stations established in 1912–1914 in Laareberg, Trient, Sarajevo, and in the Ministry of War building in Vienna had an output of 4 kW; Trebinja 6 kW; and Przemyśl 15 kW.

In 1909, the warships ORZ/Radetzky, OUH/Habsburg, OFD/Franz Ferdinand, and 17 others had radio stations. Coastal stations: Castelnuovo LRC, Pola LRP, and Sebenico LRS. The official list of coastal and ship stations and their call signs, published in Bern in 1909, has 112 pages; from 1918, 365 pages. Austrian station call signs were already changed: Castelnuovo OHC, Pola OHP, Sebenico OHB, and Triest OHT was added with wavelengths 600 m and 1800 m; OHT also 300 m.

Radio fell under the competence of the Imperial-Royal Ministry of Trade from January 7, 1910. The ministry established an inspectorate for spark telegraphy in Trieste in 1912, and on January 31, 1913, issued a decree stating:

»It has again been determined that unlicensed stations for wireless telegraphy are being established. Measures must be taken to have these facilities removed from operation within three days, or immediately.«

On the basis of the decree, the Imperial-Royal Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs for Bohemia issued a circular decree on February 28 to »An sämtliche Telegraphen und Telephon Liniensektionen« to report within eight days whether any unlicensed private facility for wireless telegraphy existed in their district. Signed by Dr. O. Kučera. All reports were negative except for Teplice, from which came a report on March 13, 1913, that teacher Emil Rochelt and Wenzel Werner in Světec were interested in experimental physics and had set up an experimental apparatus. This information was clarified on December 11, 1913, by the Imperial-Royal Telegraph Line Section in Ústí nad Labem, which determined that Rochelt had a complete transmitting and receiving station, an antenna stretched on a mast 12.5 m high, and an inductor with an output of 5 kW with a spark gap of 0.55 m.

It was more powerful than the Titanic's station and ten times more powerful than the transmitter of the later Czechoslovak ship Morava, which—as one of the last in the world—still used spark telegraphy in 1932.

The Imperial-Royal Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs asked the District Office in Duchcov to have Rochelt monitored by the gendarmerie, and ordered its office in Ústí to try to persuade Rochelt to liquidate the station. Which, according to a report dated January 14, 1914, succeeded.

Applying to the Imperial-Royal Ministry of Trade for a concession for a receiving or especially transmitting station was not very hopeful. Sub-official Ludwig Bernhardt of the Imperial-Royal Regional Court in Cheb submitted a request on May 24, 1913, to have an older Morse recording apparatus sold to him. He justified his request by saying that in his free time he was engaged in building a transmitting and receiving station for wireless telegraphy. But he himself had neither the possibility to build nor to purchase a Morse recording apparatus. The Imperial-Royal Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs forwarded the request to the Imperial-Royal Ministry of Trade, which rejected it on June 12, 1913.

Professor František Nušl of the Czech Technical University in Prague applied on July 18, 1913, for permission for a receiving station at the Ondřejov observatory to receive time signals from Paris and Norddeich, so he could precisely measure the geographic longitude of the observatory and Pecný hill. He intended to conduct these measurements during the holidays. He would combine the pleasant with the useful. But the holidays passed, and only on September 9 did consent arrive from Vienna with these conditions:

1. Only reception for scientific purposes is permitted.
2. The state administration may inspect the station at any time by its own organs or by organs of the military administration.
3. The permission, granted for a period of 2 months, may be revoked at any time.

On September 11, 1913, Prof. Nušl confirmed that he accepted these conditions and announced that the station would also be operated by Dr. Boh. Mašek, professor of physics at the Imperial-Royal secondary school in Žižkov, and the owner of the observatory, Josef Frič. The Imperial-Royal Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs had all three sign an oath of loyalty to the Emperor on a form intended for state and public employees upon entering service, and construction of the station could begin.

The receiver was a product of the Parisian firm Ducretet-Rogel. The antenna consisted of two parallel copper wires 92 m long, stretched 8 to 10 m above ground. One end of the antenna was fixed to a wooden shed in which the receiver was placed, the other to the ridge of the roof of the residential building. After two months, Prof. Nušl announced that the station had been abolished. This, of course, did not mean any dismantling. It was merely disconnected from the antenna and stored.

At the observatory, they recognized the value of precise time. Therefore, on December 11, 1913, Nušl submitted a request for a permanent concession for the purpose of controlling the observatory's clocks.

The Directorate of Posts could already rely on the previous consent of the Ministry of Trade. However, it did not dare to issue a concession without time limitation and granted permission from case to case. In the state archive is stored a letter dated February 20, 1914, in which Prof. Nušl announces to the illustrious Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs that the station was established on Sunday, February 15, 1914, and after receiving time signals from Paris and Norddeich, it was again abolished, and requests permission to establish the same station on the days March 1, March 15, March 29, April 12, and April 26, 1914.


---


[Non-text page — the original contains only a decorative graphic; no recoverable text.]


---


In our country, Ing. Karel Ort is considered the first radio amateur.
He was born on 11 February 1889 in Koštice near Budyň nad Ohří. He
studied at the Realschulen in Louny and in Ječná Street in Prague. As
a fourth-year student he heard a lecture by Prof. Zenger on Thompson's
electromagnet. The lecture, combined with a practical demonstration,
had a decisive influence on Ort's life. His interest was captured by
electric doorbells and home telephones. After his school-leaving exams
he studied at the technical universities in Prague, Berlin, Eberswalde
and Karlsruhe. As a student he wrote an article for Vynálezy a pokroky
(Inventions and Progress) on Marconi's experiments between Poldhu
(England) and Glace Bay (USA), and together with J. Rieger he published
a series on wireless telephony. The two friends attempted a radiotelephone
connection at that time, and with the help of Poulsen's electric arc
they achieved good intelligibility over a distance of half a kilometre.

In 1912, when he described Goldschmidt's dynamo in his article "A New
Advance in Wireless Telegraphy" in Vynálezy a pokroky no. 14, Ort was
already an engineer and worked for the Berlin firm Lorenz, which had
built this machine for the Eberswalde transmitting station. Ing. Ort
dealt with the problem of frequency multiplication and the results of
experiments between Eilwese near Hanover and Tuckerton, and between
Nauen and Sayville, in his article "Machine generation of HF currents
in radiotelegraphy", published in Vynálezy a pokroky in 1914. For his
holidays, and later for his vacations, he always travelled home, to
Koštice. There he had a receiving station with which he listened to
ship and other radiotelegraphic traffic. On the eve of the outbreak of
the World War, Ort worked feverishly on building a station. In June
1914 he travelled with Prof. Šimek to Vienna to discuss a permit for
constructing a transmitter.

On 26 July, with the help of Ing. Rieger and Prof. Prašitz from
Berlin, Ort set about building an antenna. When they were finished,
drumming sounded through the village. The village constable was
proclaiming mobilisation. The transmitter never went into operation,
but the antenna stood by the Koštice mill until 1935. Ing. Ort left
for Sweden in 1915, and from there for America. He died on 1 February
1920.

His body was embalmed and transported to Europe. He rests in the
family tomb by the wall of the cemetery on the Koštice-Louny road.

Koštice was under surveillance for the entire First World War. Every
month the telegraph inspector Held, and from December 1916 Vošahlík,
came there and reported to Mladá Boleslav that the radio station was
not in operation.

Another closely watched station was at Ondřejov. The monthly reports
of the telegraph inspectors Bohatý and Křečan were collected in Mladá
Boleslav, from which regular, stereotypically repeating reports went
out:

     An die k. k. Post-und Telegraphendirektion Prag.
     P. O. Z. 3464/PP/M III-1914 berichtet, dass die Radiostation in
     Běronitz nicht mehr besteht.        Roháček.
[To the Imperial-Royal Post and Telegraph Directorate, Prague.
P. O. Z. 3464/PP/M III-1914 reports that the radio station in Běronitz
no longer exists. Roháček.]

Attached to each of these in the original is the report of the
telegraph inspectors from Koštice and Ondřejov, likewise written in
German. The last report, of 30 October 1918, is already written in
Czech and ends with a polite enquiry as to whether these reports
should continue to be submitted.

No mention of the said radio station at Běronice has been preserved
in the state archive. The retired district schoolmaster Josef Svoboda,
however, remembers that Vincenc Libus owned a ring kiln brickworks at
Běronice. His eldest daughter Věra married Ing. Schneider, a professor
at the Vienna Technical University, who set up a receiving station in
the brickworks and stretched an antenna between the chimneys. The
station was shut down at the beginning of the First World War. Libus
died on 30 June 1919 and his family scattered.

There were two radio stations in Brno under Austro-Hungary. The first,
located on the third floor of the machine hall of the Czech Technical
University, was authorised by the Imperial-Royal Ministry of Trade by
decree no. 8547/P of 19 April 1913. The primary winding of the
inductor, through which a current of 15 A flowed at 32 V, was keyed.
A voltage of 5000 V was induced in the secondary winding. The antenna
was 25 m long. The station could work on a wavelength of 250 m. The
Telefunken receiver had an electrolytic detector. There are no records
of this station's operation. The second Brno radio station, with a
24.5 m mast visible from far away, was located in a wooden hut on
Žlutý kopec (Yellow Hill). It served military purposes. It maintained
contact with Vienna and with St. Pölten. It was commanded by First
Lieutenant Jan Pleský. After the [1918] revolution it ceased
corresponding and devoted itself to listening.

There was a military station at Letná in Prague, another in Plzeň and
in Moravská Ostrava, but only the Brno station was operational.

We would go astray if we tried to form a picture of the army solely
from The Good Soldier Švejk. Radiotelegraphy served very well here.
Not only in communications, but also in the intelligence service
(which was then called the Evidenzbureau). It had a system of fixed
and mobile listening stations that carefully monitored the enemy's
correspondence, and from its frequency, structure, audibility and the
results of goniometric bearings drew conclusions about the movements
and concentrations of enemy troops. Nor could it be misled by the
deceptive signals the enemy transmitted. Captain Heřman Pokorný
successfully broke Russian ciphers for the headquarters of the 4th
Austro-Hungarian Army.

Colonel Hauf and other officers who built up the Czechoslovak
intelligence service after the revolution were interested in the
Austro-Hungarian materials: intercepted and decrypted enemy
radiotelegrams and the records evaluating the military situation on
the basis of such telegrams. Under the Treaty of Saint-Germain,
Czechoslovakia had a legal claim to the release of these documents.
The section chief of the military liquidation office in Vienna,
however, could only inform the Czechoslovak legation that such
documents of the Eastern Staff of the Evidenzbureau had been destroyed.

The listening group Radiogruppe 7 was commanded by First Lieutenant
Ing. Josef Voborník. The end of the war caught him in northern Italy.
Radiogruppe 7, which had three receivers with it, received orders to
move from Trient (Trento) to the Tyrol, to St. Johann. Trient, though
it then belonged to Austria-Hungary, remained with its little towers,
palaces, wells and churches a typically Italian town. Ing. Voborník
and his men passed without great interest by the monument to Dante,
by the luxurious Imperiál Hotel Trento, and settled into a train. The
journey led up the valley of the Adige towards Bolzano and Brixen.
The destination was a small town south of Salzburg, a popular resort
with magnificent alpine scenery.

It was late autumn. Nature revelled in a palette of vivid colours.
The soldiers in the packed train, however, did not delight in its
beauty. They stood on the platforms, on the steps, and whoever did
not get inside sat on the roof. Thick cigarette smoke hung in the
carriages and excitement prevailed. Sankt Johann? Not a chance. When
the train ran along a high cliff, the men of Radiogruppe 7 opened the
window and threw all three crates out. After them flew the rest of the
junk that is of no more use to a soldier who has decided to end the
war and go home.


---


AFTER THE WAR


---


The Czechoslovak Republic needed contact with the world, above all
with Paris. The postal lines across defeated Germany and victorious
France were in an operationally unsatisfactory state. A direct link
with Paris (or even London) was not even conceivable. It would not be
possible for perhaps a year, until the Mainz-Paris line is completed,
as the French reply to the enquiry of the Czechoslovak authorities
(28 December 1919) attests:

     "La liaison directe Prague-Paris ne pourra être réalisée que lorsque
les nouveaux fils Mayence-Paris en voie de réalisation seront achevés.
                                       Commandement en chef
                                       des armées alliées
                                       état major général DCCRA
                                       service télégraphique."
[The direct Prague-Paris connection can only be established once the
new Mainz-Paris wires, now under construction, are completed.
— General Headquarters of the Allied Armies, General Staff DCCRA,
Telegraph Service.]

The building of radio stations was taken up by the army.

The Petřín radio station was established in the cellar space beneath
the lookout tower. Its operators were a group of soldiers headed by
Lieutenant Ing. Bohumil Konečný, who before the war had worked as a
designer at the Czech Technical University (ČVUT). The station's
creator was the ČVUT professor Ing. Ludvík Šimek. Prof. Nušl helped
as well. Prof. Šimek made available a 65 kW motor-generator and
high-voltage capacitors. The coils and variometers were made in the
ČVUT workshops. Ing. Konečný procured a spark gap from Austrian field
stations, whose wrecks were found in Prague and Plzeň. Křižík lent a
220/1500 V transformer. The keying relay they obtained from the
discontinued tram line across Charles Bridge.

The nine-wire antenna was fastened to the chimney of the tower.
Ing. Konečný found an EKH Berlin receiver at the airfield in Cheb.
It was a crystal detector set with a two-valve amplifier. On 19
November 1918 they completed the radio station, and Prof. Šimek put
it into operation. The station chose the call sign PRG (Prague). It
established its first connection on 29 November with FQ, St. Pölten,
the following day with WAR (Warsaw), and received its first dispatch
from it. It was a transit telegram for Bern. From 10 December regular
sessions ran with FL (the Eiffel Tower), and from Christmas Eve also
with Coltano Radio. In December 1918 PRG sent 62 telegrams (5,263
words in total) and received 37 (2,865 words). The telegraphists
serving here were Lieutenant Jan Hacker, Warrant Officer Šafránek,
Sergeants Ant. Slavík and Jan Velík, Lance-Corporals Leopold Severin,
Frant. Hájek, Jindřich Šíp, Frant. Douda, Rudolf Biják, Oldřich
Žákavec, and Private Vlastimil Velík. The sappers Ferdinand Adler and
František Šilhán acted as machinists. From 5 November 1918 to
9 February 1919 the telegraph fitter Josef Šebánek also served here.
He ran a direct telephone line to the meteorological institute at
Karlovo. On 23 January 1919 at 16 hours 45 minutes, Petřín transmitted
the first consolidated Czechoslovak meteorological telegram. The line
was built from three-millimetre iron wire on type-B insulators.

A second direct telephone line, 2 km long, ran to the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs. That was indeed the Petřín radio station's main
customer. Not only did it send and receive telegrams, and its cipher
department (Dr. Fritz, Dr. Machatý, Dr. Štěpánek) approved telegrams
sent through the Ministry — the Ministry itself needed information on
the international situation, and especially on events in the Soviet
Union. In the post-revolutionary period there were not yet accredited
correspondents there, there were no diplomatic relations, no press
came from there, and the only source of information was the
intercepted bulletins and correspondence of Soviet radiotelegraphic
transmitting stations.

In Slovakia, the Italian army had during the war its own radio
network: GHG Bratislava, GHP Košice, GVA Nitra and GZD Lučenec. On
their departure they donated all the equipment to our army (handed
over by Lieutenant Tenento Marini). In March 1919 First Lieutenant
Konečný was sent to build the Košice radio station, and remained there
for some time as its commander. Petřín was taken over by First
Lieutenant Ing. Voborný. In March 1919 a new transmitter was put into
operation at Petřín: a 10 kW high-frequency alternator, supplied by
the French firm SRF. The station was moved from the cellar of the
lookout tower into a brick building, and on twenty-one-metre masts a
nine-wire antenna 200 m long was stretched. A valve transmitter was
purchased and put into operation in November 1919, referred to in the
official files of the time also as the "tube" transmitter (valve — in
German, Rohre). It worked on a wavelength of 1800 m, the alternator on
a wavelength of 10,000 m, and Šimek's original spark transmitter on
its wavelength of 4100 m.

The army had, in 1919, a reliable network of stations: POS Brno,
1200 m; PSA (also MOS) Moravská Ostrava, 1200 m; PBI Bratislava,
1200 m; PKS Košice, 3800 m; PUR Užhorod, 1200 m, and more were added.
The Bratislava station was set up in the Apollo factory on the bank of
the Danube, with an antenna suspended between two factory chimneys,
70 m and 55 m high. It corresponded with Prague, Košice, Padua and
Warsaw. Night after night, however, shots were fired at it from the
Hungarian side.

In April 1919 the technical department of the Ministry of National
Defence ordered that only PRG might correspond with foreign countries.
The military stations also worked for the ČTK news agency and
intercepted foreign agency material for it. Brno began this activity
on 18 November 1919, Prague two months earlier. From 1 December 1919
Petřín began broadcasting press reports of the Presidium of the
Council of Ministers in French. The reception of news for ČTK was in
Prague separated from Petřín and moved to the barracks at Vršovice.

A testimony to Petřín's success is the report of Capt. S. W. Bryant
of the Naval Communication Service in Washington of 2 April 1921, that
the press reports Petřín transmits at 1200 GMT are received in
Annapolis on a frame or outdoor antenna with a receiver using detector
and two AF stages. A listening report also arrived from the American
station Otter Cliff.

As the state administration was being built, the Prague postal and
telegraph directorate became the nucleus of the emerging ministry.

One of the most serious tasks the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs
set itself was to take radiotelegraphy away from the soldiers and
seize it. On 8 February it therefore called on the Ministry of
National Defence to hand over the Petřín station. The army did not
want to give up its prerogative, and after several urgent reminders
the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs turned to the Council of
Ministers with a request for a fundamental decision on whose
competence spark telegraphy fell under. It was not simple. The
services of Petřín, especially with regard to the preparation of the
peace treaty, were warmly appreciated in the highest circles; on
2 April the President of the Republic visited the station.

The year 1919 was full of revolutionary ferment. On 21 March the
republic of councils was proclaimed in Hungary.

Military radiotelegraphists with headphones on their ears carefully
recorded the events of the Hungarian revolution on half-sheet forms
headed RADIOGRAM. On 30 April Oldřich Pospíšil was on duty at the Brno
military station. At 21:00 he went off duty and Veselý took over. At
21:20 he tuned in station HB on a wavelength of 4300 m (call sign
derived from Hungaria Budapest). The RADIOGRAM sheet began to fill
with neat handwriting: "CQ DE HB — to all the workers' parties of the
world. Today we have sent the following note to the Czechoslovak,
Yugoslav and Romanian governments: Mister President. In the name of
the revolutionary government of the Hungarian councils I have the
honour to declare to You that we unconditionally accept the
territorial national claims which You have put forward ... On the
other hand we demand the immediate cessation of hostilities, non-
interference in internal affairs, as we too shall refrain from
interference in Your affairs, free transit traffic, the conclusion of
trade agreements. Thereby You have achieved everything You wished
for; You need no longer shed a single drop of blood or sacrifice the
life of a single soldier ... Kun, People's Commissar for Foreign
Affairs. We would like to point out that from this moment the
maintenance of the present state of war is justified by nothing."
(The radiogram, preserved in the Military Historical Archive in
Prague, is written in German.)

The government of the Hungarian republic of councils turned to the
workers' parties of Europe. In this spirit it sent a May Day greeting
to the council republic in Munich, intercepted by the Brno military
station at 12 hours 17 minutes: "RV DE HB — BPEST NR 2009 M 120 2340
M — government of councils Munich — to the proletariat of Bavaria:
This day of demonstrations of proletarian solidarity finds us in a
fight for life and death ..."

At 13:00 Lance-Corporal Kratochvíl took over the watch. He
interrupted listening to Budapest and carried out the scheduled
session with Moravská Ostrava:

1305 MOS DE POS - QRM - QRK?
1307 MOS DE POS - nothing here for you - QRK SK

The situation in Hungary on 3 May is characterised by a radiotelegram
from Budapest to Moscow, addressed to the press agency ROSTA and
intercepted in Brno:

     "MSK DE HB NR 3564 W 73 BPEST 3 5 28 - ROSTA Moskau - the
revolutionary government of councils issues this mobilisation order:
For the rescue of the proletarian revolution we decree a general
mobilisation of the proletariat. Every proletarian trained for
military service must report immediately for front-line duty. Every
worker without military training will either be assigned to training
detachments or be obliged to carry out fortification work. We hereby
proclaim Budapest operational territory as of today, and we call on
the proletariat to fulfil its duty to the utmost."
(These telegrams quoted here are likewise written in German in the
original.)

The Czechoslovak authorities ordered mobilisation and proclaimed
martial law in Slovakia. The Presidium of the Council of Ministers
decided (ref. no. 26274/10 of 11 December 1919) "to postpone
consideration of the question of competence until a later time, when
conditions become normal."

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs goes on the offensive. By a
letter of 20 March 1920 it asks the Presidium of the Council of
Ministers for an expedited decision on the dispute of competence. The
Ministry of National Defence found itself on the defensive. Captain
Kuník prepared a nine-page report for Prime Minister Vlastimil
Tusar, in which he argued that the Ministry of National Defence had
qualified personnel and all the necessary equipment. The army must
have telegraphists in peace and in war, and the training of a
radiotelegraphist is possible only in real operation. The army demands
authority over all radiotelegraphic traffic in the state, and at
minimum over military traffic.

When, for example, the professor at the grammar school in Truhlářská
Street in Prague, František Hrubý, asked the Ministry of Posts and
Telegraphs for a receiver permit (a crystal detector set with a tuned
circuit), the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs rejected the request,
saying it could be granted only after an inter-ministerial conference
on competence. It also rejected the request of the watchmaker W. Pohl
of Trutnov. The technical students Florián Budina and Karel Český
submitted their application for a receiver permit to the Ministry of
National Defence, and on 26 March 1920, under ref. no. 1V/328351/11,
received a favourable ruling. The Astrophysical Observatory at Stará
Ďala also submitted an application to the Ministry of National
Defence. Not only did it permit the station, it sent Captain Kuník,
Lieutenant Slavík and Staff Sergeant Heinz, who built the station and
put it into operation.

In the middle of 1920 the disputes over competence reached their
peak. Mutual accusations flew regarding professional competence. And
the postal service, lacking qualified personnel? It emerged that in
the district of the Prague directorate alone, 37 postal workers had
completed radiotelegraphic training in the Austro-Hungarian army.

It seems that the decisive factor, or perhaps the feather on the
scales, was the position of the Ministry of Finance, which gave no
weight to Captain Kuník's argument that a soldier with his pay comes
cheaper as a radiotelegraphist than a postal official. It considered
the postal sector more economical than the military sector and
supported the position of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, and
so even in our country radiotelegraphy was divided between the
civilian sector and the military sector. And at the meeting of 5
February the soldiers even undertook to train postal radiotelegraphists
and allow them to gain practice at the military stations.

In October 1919 the Italian legation submitted to His Excellency the
Minister of Posts and Telegraphs a proposal from Senator Marconi and
his company: that it would deliver, at its own expense, to the
Czechoslovak government all the radiotelegraphic apparatus for
communication with the great stations of Europe and America, would
supply personnel for running them in, and would guarantee the regular
operation of the stations. The company would receive a share of the
fees over a number of years to be determined.

The position of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs was unequivocal:
radiotelegraphy must remain in the hands of the state.


---


August is ending and September 1920 begins. Among the curiosities that
arrived on editorial desks is a quarter-sheet of coarse cyclostyled paper:
»No. 52112-V1I-20. NOTICE.
Radiotelegraph stations in the Republic of Czechoslovakia must hold a
concession granted by the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, and this
applies even to mere receiving stations. The Ministry of Posts and
Telegraphs therefore calls upon all institutions and individuals who
operate radiotelegraph stations to apply without delay to the said
ministry for a concession, enclosing a description of the station
together with a diagram of its connections.
Prague, 2 September 1920.«
This announcement was preceded by a decree of the Ministry of Posts and
Telegraphs of the same session number, dated 30 August 1920, marked on
the ministerial reference sheet »on official initiative«, addressed to the
postal and telegraph directorates. The notice dispatched to the daily
press is quoted there verbatim, and at its close it states:
»The directorate concerned is therefore instructed to determine
without delay whether there are any unconcessioned radiotelegraph
stations within its district«
»with the request that the political authorities likewise search out
spark stations of radiotelegraphy and report any unconcessioned ones
to the state telegraph administration«. ..
It was a reprisal of the action undertaken at the end of January 1913
by the Imperial-Royal Ministry of Commerce.
           THE AUTHORITIES CRACK DOWN


---


A commotion broke out at the radio station of the telegraph regiment in
Mladá Boleslav.
»Show me that,« the commander takes the record from the telegraphist's
hand. Nothing sensible can be made of it. If there are any letters in
the intercepted »text«, they make no sense. The telegraphists lie in
wait. The moment the hiss of sparks is heard, the frame antenna is
rotated and the bearing found is entered on the map. The same is
repeated at another position. Then the soldiers set out for the place
where the two lines intersect.
They entered house No. 177 in Rašínova street. Professor Vopička was
just then working a spark transmitter mounted on a sewing machine, at
which sat Mr Navrátil, a works foreman from Škoda, pedalling. Electricity
had not yet been installed there. Mr Navrátil, a lodger at the Vopičkas',
drove with the sewing machine a dynamo that fed the transmitter.
The head of the District Political Administration summons the offender.
Václav Vopička, professor at the Mladá Boleslav reálka (secondary school),
is one of the town's known and respected figures. He was born in 1890
in Prague. After his school-leaving examination he studied at the
Academy of Fine Arts. For a long time he could not decide between
painting and engineering. When his father died, he lacked the means to
continue his studies and went to teach drawing, physics and mathematics.
His interest in engineering remained, and so came the affair of the
mysterious signals, which ended with the soldiers escorting the
professor to headquarters.
»Really now, professor, one simply doesn't do that. Experiments like
these. Give it up,« the district captain says, dismissing him.
The soldiers are satisfied. They are not petty.
For some time Professor Vopička devotes himself once more chiefly to
art. He paints the Bohemian Paradise, the Mladoboleslav region, the
Giant Mountains, the Sázava valley. He exhibits his paintings, but
refuses as a matter of principle to sell them. He keeps some, gives
others away. Official records of this incident have not survived.
Perhaps no report was ever submitted »upstairs«. But Professor Vopička's
daughter, Mrs Medová, remembers it well. It was one of the dramatic
experiences of her early childhood...


---


In the valley on the Čerčany – Sázava line, by the river, which here
flows in a calm broad current, stands a small, lonely station. At the
beginning of October it lies already in shadow at four in the
afternoon. Autumn here is charming. Behind the river, grown dark and
lined with shrubs and trees, a slope rises. Green, yellowish, reddish,
orange. A traveller from Vlkov is greeted by the sign SAMECHOV, fixed
to a brown annex as tall as the station building. Up from the station
a road climbs, bordered by massive stone posts. Here it meets a footpath
that vanishes into the forest. At this spot, on 7 October 1920 at four
in the afternoon, stood two men in grey-green uniforms. Leather belts,
carbines with bayonets fixed. They were looking toward the rear tract
of the station building.
They adjusted the carbines on their straps and set off.
Five days later, on 12 October 1920 at 16 hours 30 minutes, the
telephone rang at the Ministry of the Interior, Department N. The
call came from the Presidium of the Provincial Political Administration
in Prague. Department N really did bear that designation. It handled
matters of state security and was headed by staff councillor Karel
Rotter.
The officer on duty took the message and drew up a record. The message
was given number 619. It was immediately passed on to the Office of
the President of the Republic, the Ministry of National Defence, the
Ministry of Railways and the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. In the
meantime it had already been delivered to the Presidium of the Ministry
of the Interior.
The Office of the President of the Republic asked the Interior Ministry
on 14 October for more detailed information. There the file was marked
in coloured pencil with the large inscription »A t  o n c e«. A letter
to the Presidium of the Provincial Political Administration was drafted
immediately; Mrs Pavlíčková wrote it out fair that very day, Mr Brož
checked it, and Mr Navrátil dispatched it.
On 16 October 1920 the Provincial Gendarmerie Command in Bohemia
prepared under ref. no. 12475 a report signed by the provincial
gendarme commander Colonel Skorkovský and approved on 18 October by
the General Commander of the Gendarmerie of the Czechoslovak Republic
(signature illegible). It reached the Presidium of the Ministry of
the Interior on 19 October 1920 and was registered there under ref.
no. 22332. The report states:
»While on patrol on 7 October 1920, chief sergeant Josef Poskočil
with sergeant Jaroslav Horký found, at the turning of the road leading
from the village of Samechov to Samechov station, on the hill called
»Na záhonech«, a 4-metre-high wooden pole, at whose upper end a zinc
wire was fastened by a glass insulator; this wire, on the slope of the
said hill, was spread out into a larger network which served for the
reception of radiotelegraph messages and which was strung to the
station building in Samechov.
»Further investigation established that above the road in front of the
station building these wires joined again into just two, which led
into the office of the station building, where they were attached to
a receiving apparatus for the interception of radio telegrams, namely
to the wall beside the telegraph line. This apparatus consisted of a
small board 21 cm long, 9 cm wide and 2 1/2 cm thick, on which a brass
wire in the form of an open hook was fastened, which rested on the
wave chokes and was fixed to a small plate, from which a lead wire
ran to two coils located under the apparatus. The device was very
primitive, so that persons moving about the station were aware of
nothing of the kind, and it was the property of the stationmaster
František Vlasatý.
»For the examination and any interception of a telegram, expert
sergeant Václav Kvapil of the 48th Infantry Regiment was provided;
on 13 October 1920 he indeed intercepted on the said radio apparatus
a portion of a radio telegram, which was attached to the notification
sent to the District Court in Benešov, and he established that the
apparatus was capable of receiving messages from greater distances,
so that messages from Berlin and other European cities could be
intercepted.
»Questioned regarding the establishment and the unauthorised possession,
or rather use, of wireless telegraphy, Vlasatý states that he had
set it up in the spring of 1919, namely as a hobby and for receiving
meteorological reports.
»Since Vlasatý held no authorisation for keeping this station, and
during the interception of telegrams committed theft by using
electrical energy from the state line, the above-named equipment —
receiving apparatus, 1 antenna, 2 cross-wound self-induction coils,
1 detector, 1 buzzer, 1 telephone earpiece, lead and connecting
wires, receiving protocol and wave chokes — was on 13 October 1920,
with the participation of the traffic adjutant of the railway
administration in Čerčany, Ladislav Weissel, and the expert sergeant
of the 48th Infantry Regiment Václav Kvapil, confiscated by sergeant
Jaroslav Horký and sent as corpus delicti to the District Court in
Benešov.
»During a house search at the said man's home for the purpose of
finding any earlier radio telegrams, nothing was found except the
receiving protocol, since the said man entered the incoming messages
on a piece of paper which he later burned. Notification in this
matter was made to the District Court in Benešov.«
On 3 December 1920 the Presidium of the Provincial Political
Administration in Prague informed the Ministry of the Interior that
František Vlasatý takes no part in public life, made no political
appearances, and had no contacts with persons of unlawful opinions.
He used the radiotelegraph for receiving meteorological reports from
Nauen and from Berlin. Investigations are continuing. Criminal
proceedings were halted on 22 October 1920. The Presidium of the
Ministry of the Interior therefore turned to the Ministry of Posts
and Telegraphs with the question of how to proceed. That ministry
replied on 8 December 1920 under ref. no. 66977/VII-20 that spark
telegraphy, which is nothing but a part of telegraphy in general,
is subject to the penal provisions contained in the decree of the
Imperial-Royal Court Chancellery of 28 January 1847, no. 2581:
whoever sets up a telegraph without permission is punished by the
destruction of all telegraph installations, which is carried out
eventually at his own expense. And the Ministry of Posts and
Telegraphs reminds that it is preparing a telegraph law which will
apply also to radiotelegraphy, and that discovered unconcessioned
stations will not be destroyed, but confiscated for the benefit of
the state.
The telegram intercepted by sergeant Kvapil has been preserved and
is stored together with the rest of the files in the State Archive.
It is written in two columns:


---


91300                  000186
    110898                 410000
      164510                590001
      809166                6100093
      51951 SD              610110095
      00001                 20510
      1008231               2398001190118
      1 3 9130              0200 0166
      1108

Sergeant Kvapil was content with the mere finding that Vlasatý's
apparatus was indeed capable of receiving radio signals. He did not
try to determine what the intercepted numerals meant, nor to identify
the station that had sent them.
Nauen in 1920 really did broadcast meteorological reports, namely
at 9 o'clock and at 19:40 GMT, on wavelengths of 3100 m and 12 600 m,
under the call sign POZ. Königs Wusterhausen (LP) transmitted
altitude observations on the 3200 m wave. Petřín (PRG) transmitted
weather telegrams at 9:30 and at 20:30 GMT. The messages were composed
in five-digit numerical code; only the observation station was given
as a two-digit group of numerals, e.g. 01 Prague, 02 Tábor, 03 České
Budějovice, and so on. In some French transmissions the station was
given by two letters instead. It does not appear that Kvapil
intercepted a weather telegram or a military telegram (those were
composed in five-digit, sometimes four-digit, rarely three-digit
groups). Nor has it been verified that the reception was flawless,
and that the text — which, it seems, Kvapil wrote in columns rather
than in rows — was received without errors. The old inhabitants of
Samechov say that Vlasatý was a likeable and kindly man. He served
at that little station of his in Samechov until his retirement to
Čerčany, where he died in 1952...


---


"Look to the Fialkas' garden," someone advised the Blansko gendarmes toward the end of 1920. "Fialka is building himself a telegraph there."

"A telegraph?" wondered Staff Sergeant Kabelka. He had more worries and more important ones. What was some telegraph... But then he recalled the recent decree about spark stations. And he didn't want to discourage his informant either. The man had already been useful to him several times.

"Mikl, they'll take a look at it when they're passing by," he turned to the sergeant.

A few days later, Sergeant Alois Mikl reported to him:

"Fialka has three insulators on an ash tree in his garden, and on each one a bronze wire, stretched between the trees across the shed roof into the front garden. There he has a telegraph pole with four insulators, and next to it a garden arbor."

The locksmith Adolf Fialka lived at house number 419. He worked at the K. R. Ježek machine works, where he was in charge of electric lighting. In the military he had served with the telegraphists. The gendarmes found that during the great strike at the Ježek works, Fialka had clipped the wires from the pole so as not to come under suspicion.

On December 12 a people's assembly was held in Blansko, at which it was decided to expropriate the large estate. The district political administration in Boskovice declared martial law. Gendarme reinforcements were being drawn into Blansko from the surrounding villages. In this situation, Fialka came to the conclusion that he should remove the antenna.

The gendarme station decided to act on January 6, 1921. Staff Sergeant Kabelka and Sergeant Mikl took along the municipal guard Alois Stloukal and burst into Fialka's home. They caught him in the act: he was working — as the gendarme report states — on assembling an induction coil. During the house search, the following items were seized and entered into the official record:

" 1 telephone receiver
  1 coherer
  1 lightning conductor (Erdschluss)
  1 induction coil partially wound with thin wire
  1 induction coil without wire
  15 m of insulated wire
  1 inductor"

They were stored at the gendarme station. "The telegraph pole and the disconnected antenna were left for the time being in Fialka's garden," Staff Sergeant Kabelka writes to the district political administration in Boskovice, and closes his report: "Fialka allegedly did not know that a permit was required for such devices, since he wanted to educate himself by means of them. The local engineer Václav Melichar examined the items found at Fialka's and states that Fialka, since these items are not serviceable, could not have used this station for receiving electric waves. It is noted that Fialka would still have needed a long time with the items he had made before he could receive electric waves."

Fialka later reportedly worked as an electrician at a hospital in Brno. He was the first, or at least one of the first, radio license holders in Blansko, successfully built amateur receivers, and his traces vanish in Olomouc.

THE PREFIX OK ARISES


---


The first experiments with wireless telegraphy took place at the end of the 19th and the beginning of the 20th century.

In Great Britain, the first license for an amateur transmitting station was granted in 1905. In 1913, applicants were taking compulsory examinations. In 1910, the first list of amateur transmitting stations in the USA was published in the Wireless Blue Book. There were 90 of them. They operated on various wavelengths from 32 to 950 m, and their powers ranged from several tens of watts up to several kilowatts. Stations, whether amateur or professional, operated on arbitrary wavelengths and used arbitrary call signs, assigned by no one. Even the manner of operation depended on the whims of the telegraphists. All of this hampered not only international but also domestic cooperation. That is why the first international conference, which sought to bring order to wireless communication, met in Berlin on August 4, 1906. It is noteworthy that the term "radio" was used for the first time precisely at this conference. In 1912, the Second International Conference on Radiotelegraphy met in London. In the USA, the Alexander Radio Act enters into force, which permits amateur transmission on any wavelength shorter than 200 m. In the opinion of experts, these wavelengths are unusable for wireless communication. So let the amateurs play around on them... In 1914, the first Call Book with four hundred addresses of amateur transmitters was published in the USA. The 1920 list of amateur transmitters in Great Britain contained two hundred addresses.

In 1920, amateur reception and transmission are permitted in Iceland, Great Britain, the USA, Canada, Australia, and from 1921 in France.

In Czechoslovakia, several receiving stations existed: in the physics institutes of the universities, at the observatories in Ondřejov and Stará Ďala, at the Realschule grammar school and the girls' lyceum in Chrudim, and at the municipal school in Ústí nad Orlicí. The validity of previously issued permits lapsed by decree of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, but the stations kept operating. And there certainly would have been quiet for some time longer, had it not been for... two letters.

One was written on September 12, 1920, by Václav Němec, proxy holder of Živnobanka in Karlovy Vary, residing at Haus Teplmünde, third floor.

The second letter is dated September 18 of the same year and comes from the editorial office of the Prager Tagblatt newspaper. Němec is engaged in experiments with wireless telegraphy and requests a permit for a receiver and a transmitter with a range of 500 m. Prager Tagblatt ordered from the firm Lorenz, Berlin, an apparatus for the reception of press dispatches. The Office for Foreign Trade issued an import permit, and the instrument weighing 66 kg is already on its way.

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs should now issue permits to both applicants. First, however, it will request the opinion of the Ministry of National Defense and the Ministry of the Interior. The soldiers have no objections and merely wish to be informed. The Presidium of the Ministry of the Interior writes on October 3, 1920, under ref. no. 20473:

"The Ministry of the Interior declares itself against the granting of permits for the establishment of private radiotelegraph stations, even if they were merely receiving stations, since under the present unsettled social and political conditions there is a fear that such stations would be misused in a manner endangering state security.
 For the Minister: Kubát."

What now? The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs convenes an inter-ministerial meeting for December 7, 1920, at 10 o'clock, in what was then Karlova, now Holečkova Street. Mr. Němec and the Prager Tagblatt editorial office are sent decrees stating that their requests cannot be granted for the time being.

How will it turn out? Who will get the first license? Němec, or Prager Tagblatt? Neither the one nor the other. There is yet another interested party. Someone for whom — as the Interior Ministry's Kubát puts it — the unsettled political situation will not matter. Neither the shots fired in the streets of Prague, nor the general strike. And ministerial councillor Dr. Otto Kučera studies the files and prepares the draft of the first license. This operator of a radiotelegraph installation (which, incidentally, has long since been functioning) does not even reside in Czechoslovakia. It smells of faraway places and the exotic. It is a ship. The first Czechoslovak ocean vessel, the LEGIE.

It has a radio station of Japanese manufacture. On September 4, 1920, the Bureau International de l'Union Télégraphique requests the technical data and call sign of this ship's station.

Dr. Kučera first of all establishes that the radio officers' documents are not in order. Josef Fuchs holds a certificate issued to him by the Austro-Hungarian government. The second radio telegraphist, František Douda, has nothing at all. The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs summons them both, nostrifies Fuchs's Austro-Hungarian document, examines Douda, and issues him a brand-new one — the first Czechoslovak radio telegraphist's diploma.

By telegram of November 2, 1920, it communicates to Switzerland the technical data of the Legie's radio station and requests that the International Telegraph Union assign a call sign:

"Announce establishment station de bord sur navire tchécoslovaque Legie appartenant a Banque Légionnaires. Portée normale 300 milles nautiques, système étincelles musicales, longueur d'onde 800 m et 600 m, service PG heures d'ouverture, unit taxe 40 centimes. Prière nous donner télégraphiquement indicatif d'appel pour cette station et de nous envoyer 10 exemplaires nomenclature radiotélégraphique et annexes."

The reply arrived on November 3, 1920:

"Réservons à Tchécoslovaquie indicatifs OKA jusqu'à OKZ."

This telegram was confirmed by a letter of the following day, November 4. It is therefore clear that the OK prefix came into being in the Swiss offices of the International Telegraph Union on the afternoon of November 2, 1920, or on the morning of November 3, 1920. It was issued from the letter groups OGA–OMZ, which before the First World War and after it belonged, together with the groups HAA–HFZ and UOA–UZZ, to Austria-Hungary.

Are the initials of Dr. Kučera's name really encoded in the Czechoslovak prefix OK — he who was the negotiating partner of the Bureau International de l'Union Télégraphique and enjoyed great respect in professional circles? In the State Archive, where all the documents cited here are deposited, we find no further record. The first radio station to use the OK prefix will thus be the ship Legie.

Dr. Kučera drafted the license document, the first that the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs ever issued. It received file number 66164/VII and was signed by director-general Dr. Fatka. The Ministry's dispatch office sent it to the Czechoslovak Central Economic Commission in Vladivostok, branch Prague V., Mikulášská Street. The radio station of the ship Legie was assigned the call sign OKA. The Legie bore it until it was established by international treaties that ship stations would use four-letter call signs. License 66164/VII remained in force until 1933, when the ship Legie was sold to the Greek firm Charlambos Pateras.


---


THE BIRTH OF POSTAL RADIOTELEGRAPHY

Radiotelegraphy had strategic importance for contact with foreign countries, because it made possible communication links independent of transit through the surrounding states — states not exactly well disposed toward Czechoslovakia. For domestic contact, the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs assumed that radiotelegraphy would lead to savings on expensive telegraph lines.

On January 7, 1921, Dr. František Raus of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs was entrusted with the search for a suitable site for the construction of a large radiotelegraph station. The conditions were sufficient soil conductivity, flat terrain, proximity to a power plant, and good rail connections with Prague. Dr. Raus (with engineer Zoubek) chose Poděbrady. Čelákovice and Český Brod were also under consideration. As early as March 18, 1920, the town council of Jičín had offered the Ministry of Posts the construction of a transmitting station on the basalt hill of Velíš, 430 m high. These towns considered it an honor to host a radio station. And when the postal administration bought from the Poděbrady estate a plot of land covering 125 korec at a price of 2,000 Kč per korec, the mayor of Český Brod, Macháček, sent an indignant letter directly to the Minister of Posts, demanding that land in Český Brod be used for this purpose instead. On August 11, 1921, the ministerial council approved the construction of the Poděbrady station and authorized the sum of 12 million Kč for the purchase of equipment.

A concept was drawn up according to which the republic was to be divided into telegraph districts, at whose centers radio stations with a range of 350–500 km would be placed.
