These would serve to relay telegrams of the postal, press, stock-exchange, weather, and official-circular kinds. The cost of building a station for overseas contact was estimated at 100 million Kč. It was therefore decided to build a center for correspondence within a range of about 3,000 km. (Only the use of long waves was contemplated, of course.)

In Moravská Street no. 9 in Prague-Vinohrady (not far from the shop in Budečská Street so well known to amateurs) stands the main post office of Královské Vinohrady. Its director, appointed in 1915, was Karel Paspa, a native of Libčany near Hradec Králové. He moved in interesting circles. Among his acquaintances were the author of books on social conduct and later castle master of ceremonies Dr. Jiří Guth-Jarkovský, the travelers Holub and Enrico Stanko Vráz, and the painter Vácha. And it is largely thanks to him that the first Czechoslovak postal radio station was established at the post office in Moravská Street. On April 1, 1921, the receiving station was put into operation; on January 12, 1922, the transmitting station. It was no longer a spark station, nor an arc station. It was a tube (valve) station with a power of 250 W. From December 1923 to February 1924 it delivered 10,158 telegrams with a total of 175,861 words.

Every radio listener knows how waves of 1,000 to 2,000 m suffer from atmospheric disturbances.

The records, written in ink or ordinary pencil, deposited in the Postal Museum in Prague, are still perfectly legible after more than half a century and offer a glimpse into the behind-the-scenes workings and the problems of the postal radiotelegraph service of that time:


---


Oct 6, 18:00–20:00 OKB complains of heavy atmospherics.
Oct 7, 16:15 WAR did not come up.
Oct 8, 08:15, 09:30, 10:00 OKB is not answering.
      11:15 OHW: Auf Wiederhören morgen. [“Until we hear each other tomorrow.”]
Oct 11, 19:30 OKB sends: Here heavy QRM, QRN. Can you shift to 1600 m, where there is less QRN? There is interference on 1300 m.
      In response: I changed the wave to 1600 m and ask OKB how it hears us on this wave. OKB replies: It is better, you may send. Then sent at normal speed. Then OKB: This is impossible work. Here heavy QRN, we cannot hear you at all, please send every word twice. About 50 words sent, every word twice. Then OKB: Impossible work, absolutely impossible, the atmospherics drown out everything. ČTK notified, to send by wire. Then OKB sent a dispatch of 82 words once. Taken here flawlessly. Weber.
Oct 12, 22:15 POZ had given no Kursfunk from 20:00 to 22:15. Waited in vain. Only at 22:15 was the station closed. Pavlík.
Oct 15, 18:00 WAR did not answer my call. Telegram no. 317 I sent blind. Weber.
Oct 16, 08:00 WAR transmits without prior announcement on the 2500 m wave.
      12:00 WAR came up on the 1500 m wave without prior announcement.

(The call signs then in use meant: OKB Brno, WAR Warsaw, OHW Vienna, POZ Nauen.)

The Vinohrady postal radio station gradually became involved in air transport as well. From the State Meteorological Institute it received telephone reports on the weather situation and broadcast them at 10:00 and 12:00 on the 1400 m wave to Strasbourg, at 10:10 and 11:10 to Cologne, at 10:50 and 13:00 on 2500 m to Warsaw, and at 6:40, 8:50 and 11:00 on the 2500 m wave to Vienna. The times were chosen so that the information would always reach the aircraft before its departure for Prague. For aircraft taking off from Prague, Vinohrady took the weather from Vienna at 8:00 and 14:00, from Strasbourg at 10:00 and 12:12, from Warsaw at 10:50 and 13:00, and relayed it by telephone to the Kbely airfield. Radio communication in civil air transport did not exist yet even in 1923; a proposal for a radiotelegraph service in military “air transport” was drawn up by the Ministry of National Defense in May 1923. For a pilot it was a great convenience to receive weather information from the route and from the destination airfield — the so-called “fiška” — at least before takeoff.

Meanwhile, construction of the building in Poděbrady had advanced so far that by the end of 1923 the installation of the machinery could begin. There was a five-kilowatt tube station as well as an alternator station. Schedules were maintained with Paris, London, Beirut, Rome, Bucharest, Warsaw, Bern, Strasbourg, and Cologne. Transmission took place on the 6000 m wave; the other stations worked on waves between 1200 m and 3000 m. Košice had a power of 100 W, the other stations only 10 W. Liberec (then OKL) operated only during trade fairs, OKZ Mariánské Lázně only in season.

When the receiving station at the Vinohrady post office ceased to be adequate, a new one was built, away from the city bustle, at the Břevnov monastery on Bílá Hora. From January 1924 a radiotelegraph exchange was built up at the post office in Jindřišská Street. Kbely, with its two 1 kW transmitters, still had a special mission: broadcasting, and prospectively, the aviation service.

Radiotelegraphy in the service of the post office is proving itself. In November 1925, radio carried 27,835 words on the Prague–Moravská Ostrava route, 26,107 words on Prague–Košice, 30,152 on Prague–Brno, 70,983 on Prague–London, and 105,561 on Prague–Paris. In December 1935 the post office carried by radio 24,290 words on the Prague–Belgrade route, 64,251 on Prague–London, 98,752 on Prague–Paris, and 2,740 on Prague–Moscow.

Construction of the Poděbrady transmitter continues; the construction administration ends its work, and on December 15, 1925, it hands the station over to Telegraph Construction Office No. 3 in Prague. The source of RF energy is two high-frequency alternators with an output of 50 kW, which rotate at a peripheral speed of 170 m/s and produce a frequency of about 30 kHz, i.e. roughly 10,000 m. Keying was done by short-circuiting the alternator in the gap. By means of a diplexer both alternators could operate in parallel. Difficulties arose with Paris on the long wave, so at the end of 1925 a tripler was installed — a transformer which distorted the supplied current so that it contained harmonics. The third harmonic was selected by the tuning circuit, i.e. 3270 m to 3400 m. This operation was conducted under the call sign OKT. With Paris it then went well, but Belgrade in turn complained that its schedules with Vienna, Berlin, and Sofia were being interfered with.

The French would not take telegrams until they had flawless reception. Poděbrady had recurring trouble with bad perforation, the keying relay failed, and the voltage from the Kolín power plant fluctuated.

The post office kept transmitting and receiving on long waves.

Mr. Read of the General Post Office sends engineer Strnad at the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, on November 20, 1925, a proposal to begin short-wave experiments between London and Prague using transmitters then being set up at Leafield. Our post office, however, is not yet working on short waves and declines the proposal for the time being.

The first short-wave experiments took place in February 1927 in the laboratory of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs; from May these experiments continued at the Postal Technical Testing Institute in Žižkov, whose construction had begun in 1926.

The first experimental short-wave transmission by our post office is dated 1929, from the Poděbrady station with two 20 kW transmitters, on the Prague–London schedule.

Among the oldest Czechoslovak postal radiotelegraphists belong Ladislav Náprstek, Rudolf Emmer, Jindřich Weber, Rudolf Pošík, Prokop Ryvola, Vlastimil Velík, Emil Řehůřek, Jaroslav Mourek, Oldřich Žákavec, and Alois Špinka.


---


AMATEUR STATIONS?


---


Ministerial Councillor Dr. Kučera took the floor.

     "Gentlemen.
We have invited you to this conference so that we may deliberate on how the state administration should proceed in the question of the so-called amateurs — that is, persons who build and operate their own radio stations, for whatever purpose, from components they have made themselves or obtained elsewhere."

      At the conference table sat Ministerial Councillor Procházka from Department XII, Chief Building Councillor Eng. Strnad from Department XIX, and ministerial secretary Matouš from Dept. XII. Department XI, headed by Dr. Otto Kučera, was further represented by ministerial draftsman Dr. Burda and ministerial secretary Vaclík.

      "I welcome the representative of the Ministry of Industry, Commerce and Trades, ministerial secretary Dr. David, and the ministerial deputy secretaries Fuxa and engineer Šíma. I further welcome ministerial secretary Dr. Herites from the Ministry of Finance. I regret that the Ministry of the Interior did not appear, although it was duly and in good time invited. Nevertheless, we shall inform the Interior of our proceedings and send them the minutes."

      After this introduction, Dr. Kučera briefly recapitulated the development of the problem since the inter-ministerial conference of 7 December 1920. At the end of 1921 a controversy arose between the Ministry of National Defence (MNO) and the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs (MPT), the latter accusing the Ministry of National Defence of continuing to issue licences for receiving stations (I242-Pp-XI mob-21). In its letter to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs of 5 November 1921, file no. 437304/tel. 21, the MNO firmly rejected this charge, maintaining that it had licensed private radio stations only until such time as the question was decided by the ministerial council.

      In 1921 the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs refused applications for receiving-station permits from the municipal girls' lyceum in Chrudim, the people's agricultural school in Rabštynská Lhota, and Emanuel Kostelecký, head teacher in Postoloprty.

      In 1922 a similar application received a negative ruling from the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs from, among others, the municipal office in Tábor, the business academy in Jablonec, Karel Anděl in Olivětín near Broumov, PhMr. Adolf Krause in Mikulášovice, and other applicants.

      Magistrate Krause addressed his application for a receiving-station permit "to the Illustrious District Political Administration in Šluknov" on 18 May 1922, justifying it by his need for precise time for his astronomical work. He was a lover of nature, an excellent huntsman and an outstanding amateur astronomer. Mikulášovice is perhaps the longest village in the state — to walk it from one end to the other at a brisk pace takes more than an hour. Yet finding the house where Adolf Krause lived and worked is easy: a rose-painted villa near the church, with a little round tower and a perfect astronomical dome of the kind we know from observatories. The building now houses a semi-finished goods shop.

      Adolf Krause would not rest content with the refusal.

      "An astronomer needs precise time. The time signal is a vital necessity for me," he wrote to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. The ministry passed the Krause case to the State Observatory and the Mathematical Institute of Charles University for their opinion. Both institutions recommended granting the application. The correspondence dragged on for several months, however.

      "Submit a new application and support it with a detailed description and plans of the receiving station you intend to build," the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs finally wrote back to Krause.

      Dr. Kučera (he had recently returned from a study trip through Germany, Holland, England and France) did not dwell at length on these and similar cases. Instead, all the more emphatically, he stressed the danger that the existence of amateur receiving stations posed to the security of the state. Receiving stations, he said, could allegedly be used for espionage or other anti-state purposes, and at the same time one could not overlook that the secrecy of telegraph traffic was endangered by these amateur radio stations. And he concluded with the rhetorical question:

      "Are these stations to be permitted, and under what conditions? Or are they to be banned outright?"

      "And under what conditions, then?" asked Dr. Herites.

      "Given the present state of affairs, many amateur stations have already been set up, and many firms are preparing to manufacture components on a large scale," declared Eng. Strnad. "Unless resolute measures are taken very soon, such factories will invest considerable capital in the new field and will thereby confront the state administration with a fait accompli that will be hard to change."

      In the discussion, however, the view of the Ministry of Posts prevailed. Espionage, the security of the state — this was what every state official feared. Who would take it upon himself to be indifferent to such questions? And in the matter of telegraph secrecy the council of the Ministry of Posts was certainly a greater authority than a ministerial secretary from Finance, or from Industry, Commerce and Trades. And so the conference at the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, held on 2 December 1922, ended with this resolution:

      "Those present agreed unanimously:
      1. The establishment and operation of amateur radio stations shall be entirely prohibited.
         Receiving stations for radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony shall be licensed by the Ministry of Posts to private persons only on condition that they are purchased only from a specific manufacturer and retailer empowered by the state and under state control (at present Radioslavie).
      2. The MPT shall agree with the customs department of the Ministry of Finance on measures to counter the smuggling of radiotelegraphic equipment and its components."

      The resolution, together with the minutes of the proceedings, was sent to all participants in the conference, to the Ministry of the Interior and to the Ministry of National Defence.

      The state held radio as its monopoly. The amateurs would literally wrest it from its grasp. They were taking possession of radio technology. Out of passion. To exercise and sharpen their abilities, their technical talent, the skill of their hands; to have a noble pastime for the rare moments of free time; to be able to exchange thoughts, ideas, knowledge with one another. In the 1st issue of the 2nd volume of Nová epocha, Štěpánek's serial "How I easily build myself an inexpensive radiotelegraph station" begins publication. Habersberger (a superb populariser of radio from the coherer and ticker all the way to the superheterodyne, though he devoted himself only to medium and long waves and remained faithful to them his whole life — he never even listened on short waves and took no interest in transmitting) opens the serial "A home radiotelegraphic station" in the 3rd issue of the 8th volume of Domácí dílna.

      Another important collaborator of Eng. Štěpánek, Jan Bísek, an engineer graduated in France, signs his articles with the abbreviation -ek. They betray an experienced technician at home in everything that can be picked up on any wavelength. It was said of him that during the First World War he ran a secret receiving station on the Sázava, which he used to supply dispatches to the Mafia. [Translator's note: "Mafie" was the code name of the Czech domestic resistance network during WWI.]

      Štěpánek's first technical articles and instructions from 1922 and 1923 lack the thoroughness of Habersberger's work, but they are written instructively, engagingly, and in a style that awakens in the reader the desire to try it himself. Eng. Štěpánek goes a step further still: he is not satisfied merely with publishing, but helps his readers with components as well.

      The first systematic information about short waves and about amateur transmission, however, was published thanks to Pravoslav Motyčka, the inspirer and founder of Czechoslovak amateur transmitting. He wrote in Radioamatér under various signatures: -čka, P. M., Piem. As the first, and for a time the only one, he devoted systematic attention to amateur transmitting and to short waves. In the 4th issue of the 1st volume of Radioamatér his important contribution appears: "American amateurs will again transmit across the Atlantic Ocean."

      And what did the amateur who built himself a one-tube or two-tube set, or even a "four-tube high-frequency amplifier," after Habersberger's or Štěpánek's designs, actually hear? An article tells us — "Grains of practice for beginner colleagues" in the 7th issue of the 1st volume of Radioamatér (1923):

      "That arc transmitters can also produce a flawless, ringing tone we have the opportunity to hear when we follow station OXE (Lyngby near Copenhagen)... Of undamped transmitters working on the system of controlled sparks, let us mention Carnarvon MUU. It has a constant wave of a character hard to describe; we recognise it by a weak parasitic wave lying close to the main wave. Most often we can hear the pleasant tone of the Eiffel Tower (2600 m), by which it gives various hourly time signals as well as meteorological and other reports of general importance. The second station most often audible during the day is Moscow MSK, damped. It has a lower tone than the Eiffel Tower and monotonous transmission. Shortly before one o'clock at noon we can hear the penetrating voice of the damped Nauen POZ (time signal). Somewhat higher, a conspicuously ringing tone, has SAJ (Karlsborg). Now and then we may catch Budapest HB, or Pula IQD, rarely Poldhu MPD, or Coltano ICD..."

      With radiotelephony — broadcasting — the first timid experiments were under way: Kbely occasionally on wavelengths of 1500 to 2500 m, Poděbrady between 10 and 12 and between 15 and 17 o'clock on a wavelength of 3100 m. At the end of 1922 and the beginning of 1923 the Hloubětín factory Elektra conducted radiotelephony experiments. It announced itself with the words:

      "Transmitting station of the Elektra factory in Hloubětín, six kilometres from Prague."

      Transmitting was impossible without an antenna, and it did not escape the keen eye of the gendarmes in Dolní Počernice. And so there landed on Dr. Kučera's desk a report of the Provincial Gendarmerie Command for Bohemia, Prague Division No. 1, station Dolní Počernice, file no. 489 of 17 March 1922, stating that "the Elektra electric lamp joint-stock company in Hloubětín has in recent days built a radiotelegraphic station at its factory. This station is allegedly intended, as the undersigned gendarme learned, solely for testing electric lamps and testing electric current. Notice is hereby given." (Signature illegible.)

      On 30 September 1922, at the initiative of Eng. Štěpánek and Eng. Bísek, a consultative meeting assembled in Elektra's sales office in Karlín (opposite the theatre), which resolved to submit an application for a radio club permit.

      On 21 November 1922, under the editorship of Karel Malva, the magazine Radio-nálída [Radio Guide] began to appear, a supplement to the magazine Práce a vynálezy [Work and Inventions]. It carried descriptions of simple receivers, for instance "A regenerative receiver for wavelengths from 100 to 16,000 m." It discussed components, antennas, radiotelegraphic meteorological reporting in France, and so on. It did not last long, however.


---


"Ref. No. 23447 S 11.
The Provincial Political Administration in Prague hereby notifies, by its decree of 8 November 1922, Ref. No. 2-A 6712 ai 22, file ref. 403632, that the Ministry of the Interior, by its decree of 4 November 1922, Ref. No. 83282/6-22, has refused to take cognizance of the notification by Eng. Frant. Štěpánek of Prague III, Lázeňská Street 6, and his comrades, of the intended formation of an association called the Czechoslovak Radioclub, with its seat in Prague, because provisions 2 and 3 of the submitted statutes, under which the association intends to organize a broadcasting service, establish transmitting stations, experimental workshops, and courses and schools for the training of certified radio telegraphists, as well as to transmit radio-telegraphic or radio-telephonic messages, are contrary to the legal order, since they intrude upon the telegraph monopoly belonging to the state under the decree of the Court Chancellery of 25 January 1847 and under law No. XXXI of 1888. You are hereby informed thereof.
For the President of Police:      Illegible signature."

Eng. Štěpánek gazed for a while through his strong, dark-rimmed spectacles (few in Prague wore such at that time) at the police decree. The black brush under his nose bristled. Then he took up a pen, dipped it in the inkwell, and wrote.

Readers of the Radioamatér found, in issue 4 of volume 1, under the heading "What Has Come to Pass," the verbatim text of the police decree, together with a juicy commentary filling a whole page.

"The Court Chancellery's shelf of 1847 is no doubt thickly coated with almost century-old dust," Štěpánek writes there. "It will need a bit of airing out, for it reeks of moldy backwardness."

On 11 December 1922 a lecture entitled "The Miracles of Radiotelegraphy and Radiotelephony" was held at the Žofín. Two thousand interested persons came. Eng. Štěpánek was prepared to demonstrate an amateur receiver, but at the last moment the police issued a prohibition. The partisans of the monopoly were on the alert. The last December issue of the Radioamatér commented on the situation at the close of 1922 as follows:

"The monopoly would endanger, above all, the true amateurs — that is, those who build receiving stations with their own hands. Here the ministry is defending the interest of a privileged company, which is to organize the broadcasting service in our country."

Work on the draft of a law on radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony proceeded feverishly. The Ministry of Supply raised no objections to the draft. The senate of the Czech Technical University recommended the most liberal possible regulations for receiving stations. The regulations for transmitting stations of individuals were to be such that public operations would not be disturbed by them and that they could not be misused to the detriment of the state. The Ministry of National Defense, too, raised no objection to a lenient procedure. As for amateur transmitting stations, the MND concurred with the recommendation of the Czech Technical University and further stressed the requirement of a high standard in the content of broadcasts and in the manner of operation. The Ministry of the Interior reconciled itself only reluctantly to private receiving stations, and categorically rejected amateur broadcasting. The Ministry of Justice sent a fair copy that helped the draft to perfection of expression and to legal correctness. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs demanded that the broadcasting of news abroad be permitted only with its consent, but in the course of further negotiations it abandoned this requirement.

Air out the Court shelf of 1847? That it reeks of moldy backwardness? You are mistaken, Engineer Štěpánek. For had you studied that Court shelf, or had you at least pondered more closely the cases of Vlasatý and Fialka, you would have known that the worst thing this heavily dust-covered Imperial-Royal Court shelf can inflict upon the operator of an unauthorized telegraph is the official destruction of the confiscated apparatus, which may, if need be, be carried out at the expense of the unauthorized operator. Work is already under way on airing out the Court shelf, and you will soon feel the consequences yourself, on your own skin.

            MOTYČKA'S EXPERIMENTS


---


"We have received orders to inspect all rooftops discreetly, to see whether there are any antennas anywhere. You have one of those suspicious wires here too. Take it down, so you don't get into trouble," says to Motyčka the telephone fitter from the post office, who comes to the Lucerna to repair the telephones.

"That would not be worth it," Motyčka imagines the possible consequences.
He has in the Lucerna a small, handy room, a former storage closet, which Mr. Havel placed at his disposal. It was here that he carried out his first experiments with spark telegraphy. In place of an induction coil he used a high-voltage transformer from an ozonizer, which delivered 11,000 V on the secondary. He made the condenser from discarded photographic negative plates and tinfoil from the tinsmith Wohlrab's in Jilská Street. Brass hammers from dismantled electric doorbells served as the spark gap, and he wound the coil from copper wire 5 mm in diameter, bought at Rotta's on Malé náměstí. Next to Rotta's was the firm of Deckert & Homolka; there he bought a telegraph key.

The receiver coil, wound on a wooden cylinder with square cheeks, was tuned by three sliders. He bought the galena crystal at Frič's natural-history shop on Vladislavova Street, and made the detector from a Christmas-tree candle socket. The earphone came from an old telephone. When, at a clearance sale at Maniny, he procured a high-impedance earphone, a vario-coupler, and even a rotary converter, the receiving station came to life. He hid the whole apparatus in an antique chest.

The aforementioned antenna consisted of two copper wires, suspended on a small mast at the very top of the structure above the palace elevator shaft.

A small light bulb in the antenna indicated transmission. He never established a connection, however. The Nušl brothers and Eng. Bísek did make some experiments on the Vltava, between Professor Šimek's boat and Střelecký Island — Eng. Bísek even circulated a collection of Austrian silver coins and made a Wien spark gap — but Motyčka's monster simply made too great a racket to be allowed to run in the Lucerna. And as if by spite, in the neighboring building on Štěpánská Street the Czech News Agency (ČTK) had settled, and had put up a receiving antenna some 80–100 m from Motyčka's.

Take down the antenna? Just now it was doubly inconvenient for Motyčka. He no longer receives on a crystal, or even on a coherer. The battery manufacturer, Eng. Lorenc, brought him from Germany a tube. A real, genuine Telefunken EVN 171 tube with a disc anode and a grid in the form of a flat spiral. He goes on using the coil with its three sliders; one slider now controls the regeneration. And what an experience it was when the regeneration suddenly caught and Motyčka hears not a few but a whole series of telegraph stations, and among them now and then some phonie tests as well.

Motyčka knows what is happening in the world. The long waves no longer satisfy him. He wants to go below 200 meters, to where the amateurs work. He obtains a vario-coupler, and among the taps he finds the one with the smallest number of turns... He knows that in England American amateur stations have been received, and preparations are under way for experiments to bridge the ocean by short waves, with amateur means...

"It really would not be worth it," and reluctantly he climbs to the roof.

The antenna is down; Motyčka coils up the wire.

■ • • • ■


---


A raw, damp December night of 1923. The cobblestones of Vodičkova Street gleam dully in the light of the street lamps. A fiacre that brought guests to the Lucerna bar drives away. The windows are dark; only here and there a subdued light penetrates through a curtain.

Inside the bar there is life. The ladies in long gowns, the gentlemen in dinner jackets, champagne in ice buckets. The guests dance and amuse themselves. No one suspects that at this very moment, against the dark sky above the roof of the palace, an even darker figure has appeared. In a moment a second one emerges, and yet another. Three men look around. They move quickly and surely. To a four-meter pole at the highest point of the Lucerna they fasten a wire. Within a few minutes they vanish among the terraces. They reappear briefly again only toward morning, when the last guests have already left the Lucerna bar. They take down the wire and disappear without a trace into the darkness.

This night scene, noticed by no one, was repeated several more times in December 1923 and January 1924.

The wire led into Motyčka's little room.

Motyčka was not the only one who slept so little in the winter of 1922/1923 and in the spring of 1923. Lieutenant Tranier, radio officer of the French cruiser Jules Michelet, sits day and night at his instruments. The cruiser calls at Fremantle on 5 July 1922. After a brief stop it continues to Melbourne, Sydney, Auckland, Wellington, Nouméa. On 14 February 1923 it sails via Santa Cruz to Yokohama. It has been under way since 12 October, when it put out from Brest together with the cruiser Victor Hugo. Their voyage ends on 11 July 1923 in Toulon.

Lieutenant Tranier is charged with a special mission. He is tracking the range of the French long-wave radio-telegraph stations. In the Pacific Ocean reception is good, in New Zealand excellent, in Japan excellent so long as atmospheric disturbances do not set in, in Shanghai weak and irregular.

The conclusion is in complete accord with the prevailing general opinions of the time: "The energy must be increased, to assure good communication with the Far East and with Australia." And this when all the long-wave telegraph transmitters are already working with powers of tens and hundreds of kW.

And what is Motyčka up to at night in the Lucerna? Why is he not at home in bed like a proper citizen, or out enjoying himself like a proper bon vivant?

Warships follow French telegraphy in Oceania, in the Far East, and off the African coast. The Zi-Ka-Wei observatory near Shanghai receives, on a loop antenna, Nauen, Bordeaux, St. Assise, Hannover, Leafield, Rome, Coltano, and Warsaw, and keeps long-term watch on how the deviations in their bearings fluctuate. Long waves and great powers carry radiotelegrams to the other end of the world. Having trouble? We shall increase the energy. Further tens and hundreds of kilowatts. And gigantic antennas over areas of several square kilometers.

Waves a few hundred meters long are good only for shorter distances. When on 13 January 1923 a telegraphist at the Awarua station in New Zealand caught the signals of station FFS (Saintes Maries de la Mer) on the 600 m wave, it was so rare and utterly exceptional an event that it was worth the director of the Awarua station reporting it to France by a special letter.

In Great Britain they take the 1000 m wave away from the amateurs. Let the amateurs amuse themselves on waves that are good for nothing: around 200 m and shorter.

The professionals can revel in power. They calculate their optimum antenna and build it wherever they please. And a long wave truly needs a long antenna.

The amateur's apparatus is modest. It huddles in some corner where the wife tolerates it more or less. (Nasty, ugly junk. It only gets in the way.) The wife scolds about it, but at the same time transfers to it a share of her tenderness, and if it happens that the amateur somehow loses his station, the wife has tears in her eyes. The antenna? The amateur first looks where there is a bit of space, and only then thinks about what could be stretched there.

The professional is academically trained. The post-First-World-War professional knows that only very long waves are suitable for long-distance communication, can compute the required power and design a suitable antenna. He can explain precisely why long-distance communication cannot be carried on short waves.

The amateur does not know this; he has merely read something. So he transmits only on those waves on which he is permitted to transmit, or on which the other party transmits. He tries, and he experiments.

Thus there come the attempts to bridge the Atlantic Ocean with amateur means. And on amateur waves.

The first series of tests in the winter of 1920/1921 was unsuccessful. No American station was received in Europe.

For the second test the ARRL sent P. F. Godley, 2ZE, to Europe, who settled in Ardrossan in Scotland. On a suitable site he put up a hut and stretched a Beverage antenna. On a cold, rainy, and windy night of 8 December 1921 he succeeded, on the 270 m wave, in receiving the American station 1AAW — the first and last spark amateur station ever heard in Europe. Its operator, however, was a "pirate," unlicensed, and was never found. Several times during his stay in Scotland he received the American station 1BCG and further stations (all tube-operated — thirty in all), whose authenticity was verified. Station 1BCG transmitted on a wave of about 235 m, the other stations around 200 m. In the third test, 1922/1923, 315 American stations were received in Europe, and 2 British and 1 French station in the USA.

Why, then, should two-way contact not be possible?

And that was achieved in the 4th series of transatlantic tests, in November 1923.

The first contact between America and Europe on short waves did not take place between official, military, or any professional stations, but between amateur stations.

Motyčka followed them on his receiver in the Lucerna. He informed the readers of the Radioamatér about them, including a detailed history of the French station F8AB of Léon Deloy, the first European station to establish contact across the ocean.

Let us quote what he wrote in the article "Ten Years Ago" in Československý Radiosvět, the Watch of Czechoslovak Amateur Transmitters, volume 1934, issue 1, page 1:

"Deloy announced that he would transmit on 24 November, i.e. 26 November by European time. At the appointed hour Schnell sat in the ARRL's main station with his friends, at a receiver thrown wide open and tuned by a wavemeter to the 110 m wave that Deloy had announced. They caught Deloy in America the instant he sent the first dot into the ether. The signals from France, transmitted with unrectified 25-cycle current, could be heard all over the house in which the League's offices were; a good beginning. The next day Schnell had his own transmitter ready as well, and cabled Deloy to attempt a contact on 27 November. After a long, shrill calling, there finally came the signal GA — go ahead."

That was the sign for Schnell to start his transmitter, in which he had four fifty-watt tubes, two and two in push-pull. You can imagine the trembling as Schnell switched over to receive, and how he held his breath until he heard Deloy's reply. It came. Hurrah. The Atlantic Ocean was bridged — the first RRR in both directions. The record that hundreds of amateurs had striven for had fallen to a Frenchman.

We surely concur with the content of the message which Deloy took down as the reply of his American friend: "I am receiving you on two tubes 7 meters from the earphones. It is miraculous. Remember our talks of five years ago (Schnell had been an American soldier — note by P. M.). Our finest dreams have at last come true — I shall never forget this moment. Congratulations. Mon vieux, tonight we are writing a historic page of history..."

Yes, they were writing a historic page, for this amateur contact across the ocean meant that a new epoch was beginning in radio-telegraph technology, which until then had worked only with long waves."

After Schnell, 1MO, contact was established by Reinartz, 1XAM (later W9UZ and W3RB). This contact on 110 m lasted several hours. The second European station to establish contact with the USA was G2KF. The contact with Schnell (1MO, later W1XW) took place on 8 December 1923. On 16 December 1923 the French station F8BF (M. P. Louis) worked with 1MO on the 100 m wave. The press of the whole world devoted attention to the amateur tests at that time. It made a stunning impression that, to bridge the distances between continents, amateurs needed an absurdly small amount of energy compared with the energy of professional stations — and, for an antenna, a bit of wire over a cottage.

Of the first transatlantic contact on short waves, Sedláč of OK1SE writes in the magazine Praha-Moskva 2/1967, p. 28: "Thus was born one modest record, at whose cradle stood enthusiasts who asked neither money nor fame for their discovery, though with it they helped science one small step forward." Professor of naval hydrography M. Mesny says in the French scientific journal L'Onde électrique (1924, p. 25): "Waves from 100 to 300 m have yielded marvelous results in the hands of amateurs. The whole world knows the transatlantic tests of the last three years..."

Right in 1924 the professionals begin to profit from the amateurs' successes. The Eiffel Tower begins research into the propagation of short waves. Marconi did not even wait for the two-way amateur America-Europe contact. The result of the preceding transatlantic tests, in which American stations were heard in Europe and, conversely, European ones in America, was enough for him to have a ten-kilowatt transmitter on the 100 m wave installed at Poldhu that same year 1923, to load the best short-wave receiver aboard his yacht Electra, and to put to sea to carry out experiments on the 100 m wave.

During the tests Motyčka heard the European amateur stations corresponding with America. He did not catch the Americans. Is that perhaps a failure?

No.

Above all, for the first time he listened properly into amateur traffic on the short waves. And he was not alone on the continent in not hearing the overseas stations. Comments appeared in the press to the effect that overseas stations can be received only on the Atlantic coast; inland it is out of the question.

Is that true? Were the American stations not drowned out by strong European signals?

Motyčka was cautious in his listening. He did not want anything to be audible outside. Might the fault lie precisely in his reducing the receiver's sensitivity too far?

The fourth transatlantic tests of 1923/1924 came to an end. With them ended the night acrobatics of Motyčka and his friends Kořínek and Třešňák on the roof of the Lucerna. Motyčka is preparing for further activity and for further transatlantic tests in the winter of 1924/1925.

Is Motyčka the first in Czechoslovakia to have received amateur stations? According to the documents we have had the opportunity to see and study, it can be said that he is.

And the soldiers?

The military station at Hradec Králové allegedly caught an amateur station's transmission in November 1923. So at least claimed Captain Rudolf Vlasák, chief of communications of the 4th Infantry Division. He cited neither the call sign, nor the wavelength, nor the content of the correspondence the soldiers were supposed to have intercepted, but he invited the head of the telegraph construction office, Eng. Schneider, and representatives of the District Political Administration in Hradec Králové. All three crisscrossed the whole town and its surroundings, looking to see where there was an antenna.

On 3 January 1924, the manufacturer Petroff, Eng. Nejedlý of Kuklen, director of the electric utilities Eng. Ervín Nápravník, and the director of the Lido-Bio František Kulička were interrogated at the District Political Administration. Petroff and Nejedlý ceased to be suspects. They knew nothing at all of the Morse alphabet and held licenses for receiving stations. Eng. Nápravník had indeed for some time been occupied with the idea of using radio for communication between power stations. He had filed an application for a permit, but had never had, and has not, a transmitter.

A radio was found at the Kuličkas'. It was a Standard receiver, installed by Radiojournal. The application for a permit was filed by Lido-Bio director Kulička after the installation, once he had satisfied himself that it really played. To the postal administration, however, the receiver was unlicensed, and the MPT recommended filing a criminal complaint. The District Court in Hradec Králové opened criminal proceedings, and on 8 March 1924 it dropped them.

No transmitter was found, and the telegraph construction office recommended to the institutions concerned that in future such signals be monitored with loop antennas.


---


THE CRITICAL YEAR

The clouds begin to gather over Eng. Štěpánek. From the District Political Administration in Slaný the following report reached Prague:

"No. 268 pres.                    Slaný, 26 February 1923.
Tuchlovice, wireless
telephone station.
                                      To the Presidium of the Provincial
                                      Political Administration in Prague.

On 24 February 1923 the commander of the gendarmerie post at Tuchlovice, Senior Sergeant Karel Šnajdr, with the assistance of Eng. František Žižka, senior building councillor of the postal and telegraph directorate in Prague, detailed for this purpose by the presidium of the police directorate in Prague, established that in house No. 273 in Tuchlovice the baker František Vobořil and the confectioner and miner Antonín Svatoň had set up a wireless station in order to listen to a concert from Paris. The persons named have hitherto been of good conduct and have not engaged in political activity in any way. Vobořil was until recently a member of the Sokol; Svatoň's father is a Modráček man.

The persons named built their station according to the instructions of Eng. Štěpánek, Prague III, Lázeňská 6.

There is a suspicion that a further 6–8 such wireless stations are to be found in the Slaný and Kladno districts; from the correspondence searched at Vobořil's and Svatoň's it would appear that such stations also exist in Rozdělov, Štěpánov, Unhošt, Bratronice, Libušín, and Smečno.

I further report that in Tuchlovice it was already known, on 18 February 1923 at 0.13 hours, of the death of Mr. Finance Minister Dr. Rašín, although no telegraphic or telephonic report of this event had been delivered through the postal and telegraph office at Tuchlovice.

                                        Ministerial Councillor:
                                        Vysoký, senior official."


---


The events of 1923 took a dramatic course. On 26 January, Radioslavia applied for permission to use the transmitting station at Kbely and for authorization to set up a receiving station with an aerial antenna at the Sanssouci cinema (later the Kapitol, now a student canteen in Opletalova Street).

On 26 February the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs informed the presidium of the Ministry of the Interior that the establishment of private, unlicensed radiotelegraph stations was being handled by "a certain firm in Malá Strana," and asked whether the gendarmerie — or in Prague the police guard — could be called upon to assist in searches of private homes during the investigation.

In its spare time between radiotelegraph traffic, the Kbely station experimentally broadcast concerts. Radioslavia picked these up and presented them to the public at a trade fair. On 13 March 1923 Radioslavia wrote to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs:

"Since the operation of radio broadcasting requires very intensive activity and some experience in journalism, our company has joined forces for this purpose with the Association of Czechoslovak Journalists, with which it intends to create a special company that will deal exclusively with radio broadcasting."

On 19 March, Prague OKO began regular radiotelegraph service with Paris U FR. The Kbely radio station took part in the first experiments with radiotelephony in aviation on the 900 m wavelength. These experiments were conducted by La Compagnie Franco-Roumaine de Navigation on the Strasbourg–Prague route with the aircraft Berlíne SPAD 46, station D C 4.

On 23 March 1923 the National Assembly passed the Telegraph Act, no. 60 Coll. of Laws and Decrees, which subsumed radiotelegraph and radiotelephone under the concept of the telegraph and designated it as the exclusive right of the state. For establishing or operating a telegraph without a permit, harsh imprisonment from 6 months to 1 year was threatened; for unauthorized interception of a telegram or telephone call, imprisonment from 8 days to 3 months.

"This law will certainly interest all radio amateurs, chiefly on account of its unusual severity, by which it forbids everything," commented Štěpánek in issue 13 of volume I of Radioamatér.

On 28 March the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs convened a poll of interested parties in the great assembly hall, in which ing. Štěpánek took part on behalf of the radio amateurs. The discussion came out in favor of completely freeing reception and rejected the monopoly on manufacture and sale.

The following day, ministers, senior state officials, and members of the Prague diplomatic corps gathered at the Sacré Coeur convent in Smíchov. The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs put on a radio concert for them. The postal radio station at Vinohrady did the broadcasting, and the technical commentary was delivered by ing. Strnad.

Motyčka sounded things out at the Ministry of Posts.

"We have to rework those statutes. With that broadcasting business, we'd never get through," he says to Štěpánek and Bísek.

"We'll appeal. We'll protest. We must convince government circles that it is necessary to go with progress," Štěpánek storms at meetings of friends and in the pages of Radioamatér. They submit a new application with revised radio club statutes.

Another party interested in broadcasting appeared: the Czechoslovak News Agency (ČTK). By a letter of 28 March it informed the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs that the existing method of distributing ČTK news to subscribers was very laborious. ČTK must telephone each of its subscribers individually. The press agencies in Vienna and Budapest use mass connection of all subscribers by wire telephone. In Prague such a thing simply cannot be arranged. Germany serves its press-news subscribers by radiotelephone. Could the postal administration place at ČTK's disposal a radiotelephone station that would make it possible to notify ČTK subscribers of news immediately, as soon as it arrives from the radiotelegraph station at Vinohrady? This letter was preceded by an innocent inquiry of 22 March 1923 from the press department of the presidium of the council of ministers to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, asking what the state of the radio news question was, and what had so far been undertaken in the matter. The inquiry was signed on behalf of the prime minister by F. Ebel, but its inspiration was none other than the director of the Czechoslovak News Agency, Kalman.

The police directorate sent its disposition of the second application for a radio club permit:

"By decree of 9 April 1923, file no. 23983/6/23, the Ministry of the Interior stated that it cannot take cognizance even of the new notification of the intended formation of the association Radioklub československý with its seat in Prague, because Article II of the statutes of this association, according to which the association intends to organize radio amateurs, as well as the provision of Article III point 5, according to which the association intends to procure news of interest to radio amateurs, intrudes upon the radiotelegraph monopoly belonging to the state under § 1 of the Act of 23 March 1923, no. 60 Coll. of Laws and Decrees, and is therefore unlawful."

"In darkest Africa and on Kamchatka they surely have their radio clubs by now, but here it won't work," Štěpánek comments in issue 14 of volume 1 of Radioamatér.

The ministry of the interior's referent at the Ústav šlechtičen (Institute of Noblewomen) palace fobs off the deputation of the radio club's preparatory committee:

"I act only according to the letter of the law. It is personally a matter of indifference to me whether some radio club or an association of shuffleboard players exists." (Radioamatér, 14/1, p. 55.)

On 15 May 1923 the Educational Union put on a public radio concert at the boys' school in Vladislavova Street. The broadcast was supplemented with commentary by ministry councilors dr. Kučera and ing. Strnad. Three days later, the Kbely station began regular program broadcasting — a year behind London, which had been the first in Europe to start. Germany was still experimenting at that time; Austria was building.


---


—


---


On 17 May 1923 a house search was carried out in the editorial offices of Nová Epocha. How it actually came about is explained by record no. 234 of the presidium of the District Political Administration in Slaný of 12 March 1923:

"Senior sergeant Šnajdr searched on 25 February 1923 for wireless stations in Slaný and in Prague, and ascertained that ing. Štěpánek, at Prague III, Lázeňská Street no. 6, likewise possessed a similar wireless telephone station. Senior sergeant Šnajdr reported the case to ministry councilor Janoušek of the Postal and Telegraph Directorate, and the latter arranged for this station to be confiscated as well.
Ministry councilor: Vysoký v. r."

Ing. Štěpánek estimated the damage caused at 4,000 Kč. What was confiscated:

2 frame antennas
1 RF amplifier of domestic manufacture
1 AF amplifier
1 coupling transformer
2 honeycomb coils
2 flat coils
1 RF transformer
4 pairs of headphones
1 Telegrafia condenser
5 Elektra tubes
1 Philips tube
4 Huth tubes

"Since it was not possible to confiscate the editor's brain, Radioamatér will continue to appear...," Štěpánek wrote after the search.

The following month, June 1923, was however a month of triumph for Štěpánek. Radioamatér, which until then had appeared as a supplement to Nová Epocha, met with such success and there was such interest in it that it could be published independently. On its cover it bore the proud and justified subtitle: "The first magazine for the popularization of radiography and radio broadcasting."

Dr. Kučera resolved to launch a general attack against ing. Štěpánek, in whom he saw the most dangerous opponent of the monopolistic efforts. Under file no. 40930-XI/1923 he sent a letter to the Ministry of the Interior on 7 July, signed by Minister Tučný. Dr. Kučera began by pointing to the inter-ministerial agreement that amateur stations would not be licensed and that all attempts to frustrate this principle would be firmly rejected, and continued:

"Now, however, we have found that someone here is stirring up and organizing a movement intended to work toward the reversal of this principle and toward the assertion of freedom of amateur radio manufacture and amateur telegraph operation. The instigator and mouthpiece of this movement is the magazine Radioamatér, published by the editorial office of ing. František Štěpánek, at Smíchov, V lesíčku no. 2. In the double issue 1–2 of the second volume of this magazine, of June 1923 (see especially the article on p. 23 and following, 'The Situation Here'), readers are urged to submit demonstrative applications through this magazine to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, requesting that amateur radio stations be licensed to them.

"In this way mass pressure by a whole crowd of applicants is to be exerted upon the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. The said goal — namely to reverse the course which the government has taken in the matter of amateur radio, for justified reasons of state security — the magazine Radioamatér also seeks to achieve by means of various derisive invectives and mocking asides with which it interlaces its pages in entirely inappropriate places. (See the beginning of the article 'A Simple Amateur Receiver' on p. 118 of the cited double issue.) The magazine publishes advertising prospectuses offering various radiotelegraph apparatus for sale by persons (ing. Fr. Štěpánek) who lack any authorization to do so under § 5 of the Telegraph Act of 23 March 1923, no. 60 Coll. of Laws and Decrees.

"We permit ourselves to draw attention to this activity of ing. František Štěpánek, who also, by public lectures, encourages the amateur radio movement still unauthorized in our country, and we request that consideration be given to whether attempts to reverse the course of government policy in the amateur radio question — in this manner and especially by such mass demonstrations — should not be brought within proper limits, and that from the very beginning.

"Kindly inform us of the measures you take in this matter.
Minister: Tučný"

In the attacked article "The Situation Here" it says:

"...we urge our radio amateurs to submit provisional station applications immediately. Applications are to be addressed to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs and affixed with a 2 Kč stamp... Perhaps our experts, once they learn the number of interested parties, will let themselves be persuaded more readily..."

The article "A Simple Amateur Receiver" was written by Motyčka. In it he describes his one-tube set with a cylindrical coil, tuned by three sliders. The tube can be switched off and a crystal detector connected to the coil — a reserve for the event of a dead battery or a burned-out tube.

By 1923 radio was no longer the hobby of just a few individuals. It was coming to the forefront of public interest. Several thousand people heard the broadcasts of the Kbely station at the trade fair. Those who heard it at the Sanssouci cinema — and who learned of it in this way — could be counted in the hundreds. And the number of people who would like to have this miracle at home kept growing. The Kbely station was already broadcasting daily. And yet it was forbidden to build, buy, or sell a radio.

Motyčka ironized over this grotesque situation in the introduction to his article with these words:

"Since the Telegraph Act, by some oversight, forgot to forbid the describing of an amateur radio station, I sketch below a schematic of a simple tube receiver."

That, then, was one of those derisive invectives and mocking asides with which, in the opinion of dr. Kučera, Radioamatér interlaced its pages in entirely inappropriate places.

And what about the sale of "various radiotelegraph apparatus"? On the last cover page of Radioamatér, under the heading "Nová Epocha Laboratory," a "Price List of Radio Supplies" was printed. Ing. Štěpánek offered Elektra audion tubes for 75 Kč, tube sockets at 1.50 Kč each, double telephone earpieces 100 to 180 Kč, apparatus terminals 2 Kč, small ebonite knobs for 6 Kč, large ones for 12 Kč, rheostats for the filament circuit for 10 Kč, etc. It was a service only for subscribers of Nová Epocha and Radioamatér, as was expressly noted.

From the fact that the letter of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs to the Interior was signed by the minister himself, it was evident what weight dr. Kučera attached to it. The arguments were so telling that a result had to come: the magazine Radioamatér would be stopped, or at the very least forced to confine itself to printing theoretical information and articles and to cease its agitation of radio amateurs.

Director Kalman was not idle either. On 11 June 1923 he asked the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs for permission to establish ČTK's own transmitting radiotelephone station at Štěpánská 53b and at Mariánská Street no. 5 (now Opletalova).

On 19 June a meeting was held at the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs at which it was agreed to set up, on trial, receiving stations at the Banking Office of the Ministry of Finance, at the Živnostenská Bank, the Prague Credit Bank, the Czech Bank, and the German Agrarian and Industrial Bank — at ČTK's expense. Director Kalman refined his demands in letter no. 73/742 of 25 July 1923, and at the same time attempted to attack Radioslavia's operations through the press department of the presidium of the council of ministers. That department sent the Ministry of Commerce a letter on 21 June 1923 under file no. 1407/23, marked "urgent." In the letter, signed on behalf of the prime minister by F. Ebel, it says:

"The presidium of the council of ministers (press department) communicates that it is in principle against the granting of a telegraph agency concession to any party whatsoever, including the company Radioslavia."

The sharply defined position of the press department of the presidium of the council of ministers, which could not be ignored, was the first crack in the monopolistic conception. The second, still more serious crack was the letter of the Ministry of the Interior of 12 July 1923 to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs:

"The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs communicated to this ministry by letter of 7 July 1923 that the magazine Radioamatér is stirring up and organizing a movement that will strive for freedom of amateur radio manufacture and amateur telegraph operation, thereby working against the intentions of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs as well as the other interested ministries — including the Ministry of the Interior — which proceed from the conviction that the interest of state security does not permit complete freedom in the development of this sporting branch.

"The Ministry of the Interior insists, as it already communicated by letter of 29 December 1922, no. 25537/pres., that the fundamental prohibition on the establishment and operation of amateur radiotelegraph stations not be broken; nevertheless it has no legal means by which it could act against the appeal of Radioamatér no. 1–2 of 1923 on p. 25, which says that radio amateurs should immediately submit provisional station applications to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. The submission of such applications is not, after all, punishable, since the Telegraph Act of 23 March 1923, no. 60 Coll. of Laws and Decrees, not only does not declare such activity unlawful, but under certain assumptions even admits the favorable disposition of such applications. Nor can this appeal be subsumed under the facts of the police offenses of § 11 of the regulation of 20 April 1854, no. 96 of the Imperial Code.

"Agitation for a purpose that is permissible under the law cannot be prevented, provided the manner in which the agitation is carried out (in this case the submission of applications) does not contravene valid laws. In this case the manner does not contravene valid laws.

"Accordingly the ironic remark at the beginning of the article 'A Simple Amateur Receiver' on p. 18 of the cited double issue also appears unobjectionable from the standpoint of the press law, since the manner in which the idea is expressed violates no provision of the criminal code.

"It is another matter, however, that the magazine publishes advertising prospectuses offering various radiotelegraph apparatus for sale by persons (ing. Fr. Štěpánek) who lack the authorization required under § 5 of the Telegraph Act, no. 60 Coll. of Laws and Decrees. In this case action can be taken against anyone who, without state permission, manufactures or sells such apparatus. The Ministry of the Interior requests that a more thorough expert basis for a criminal complaint be communicated to it, since for lack of expert personnel it has no possibility of constructing a criminal complaint on its own from the mere list of radiotelegraph supplies contained on the last cover page of the said magazine."

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs requested an opinion from the Ministry of Commerce, from which came the reply on 5 October that Nová Epocha had no permit for the import of radiotelegraph supplies, still less for their manufacture, sale, or possession.

On 11 October 1923 the Ministry of the Interior instructed the presidium of the Provincial Political Administration in Prague to initiate criminal proceedings against ing. František Štěpánek.

At the decisive moment, then, it did not prove possible to silence Radioamatér. The August–September 1923 double issue carried an open letter to the Minister of Posts and Telegraphs and to the deputies of the National Assembly. On the cover is the emphatic inscription:

"Down with the un-republican and undemocratic monopoly."

"We ask you, Mr. Minister," wrote ing. Štěpánek on behalf of the preparatory committee of the Radioklub československý together with F. Prokopec, "in the interest of the nation's economic advancement, to enable less affluent radio amateurs to share in the growth of radio as well, by allowing them, in the implementing regulation, to establish receiving stations according to officially approved designs..."

Štěpánek, in fact, originally had no inkling that he would become a pioneer, propagator, and fighter for amateur radio. His ideal was to publish a popular magazine with a broad educational range. He had been a reader of the French magazine Je sais tout and of the pre-war Epocha since 1909. In 1921 he took up the tradition of Epocha and began publishing Nová Epocha at his own expense, a fortnightly for the popularization of the natural and technical sciences. Štěpánek was led to radio by the article "How I shall build myself a receiving radio station" in the February 1920 issue of Je sais tout. It awakened Štěpánek's interest. Ing. Štěpánek claimed that it was he who introduced the term "radioamatér" into the Czech language. He is probably right.

It was clear that amateur radio could develop only if a component base was available. Even resistors and condensers made at home were interesting as experiments — they had their shortcomings, however. With the avalanche-like spread of interest in radio, it could be assumed that enterprises supplying radio amateurs would have good prospects of prosperity.

Ing. Bísek of Elektra came to the Štěpáneks', ing. Lorenc (battery manufacture), Žáček (later head of the firm Radio-Žáček), director Ety, Gesmacher, ing. Brodský of the firm Krešl, which represented Telefunken in Czechoslovakia, and Vik and Neškudla of Galvanie in Jablonné, and Štefiiček (later representative of Marconi, Burndept, and other firms).

A third serious crack appeared in the monopoly conception: the onslaught of manufacturers and industrialists who foresaw a boom and had no intention of leaving the field free to Radioslavia.

The Chamber of Deputies had already twice returned the draft of the act on the manufacture and possession of radiotelegraph and radiotelephone apparatus for reworking. Things were getting rough.

"The intended monopoly would jeopardize and suffocate the entire development of amateur radio," writes ing. Štěpánek in an open letter "To the Deputies of All Political Parties" in no. 3/4, vol. II of Radioamatér (August/September 1923).

No final settlement of the legal conditions of radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony came by the end of 1923. The waiting stretched past the New Year. Some, however, need not have waited. On 5 September 1923 the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs issued the first concession for a receiving station. Its holder was JUDr. Josef Lachout of Řevnice, an advisor of the Chamber of Commerce and Trades. A further five prominent personages were granted concessions on 1 October 1923. The Standart-brand receivers were purchased from Radiojournal, which also installed them. The receiver cost 5,000 Kč, the antenna 400–500 Kč, and installation cost about 300 Kč.

All of this was paid also by the owner of a chemical factory in Nové Město nad Metují, O. A. Břich. Radiojournal's workers installed the apparatus for him, put up the antenna, and when the customer was satisfied and decided to keep the set, they asked the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs to issue the concession. The Ministry of the Interior, however, did not permit Břich to establish a receiving station. Radiojournal thus could do nothing else but send its people to Nové Město and dismantle the whole installation at Břich's, of which it reported to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs on 29 September 1923.

Břich filed an appeal on 27 October against the position of the Ministry of the Interior. He stated that he had been a member of the committee of the Czechoslovak committee in Moscow, that he was a member of state-founding organizations, treasurer of the district educational corps, and a member of the automobile club.

Deputy Trnobranský wrote on 11 October 1923 to his brother minister Tučný, on the official stationery of the Club of Czechoslovak Socialists in the National Assembly, Chamber of Deputies, this letter:

"Honored Mr. Minister,

the manufacturer O. A. Břich of Nové Město nad Metují applied on 29 August 1923 for a concession to establish and operate a receiving radio station. His application was rejected on 25 September 1923 without reasons given.

"Since this concerns a reliable citizen who is active in the state-founding party of the Czechoslovak Socialists and who intends to use the station chiefly for research purposes, I most respectfully request that his application be examined anew and the decision, if need be, changed. Should it not be possible to grant him the concession, I politely request that the reason be communicated for my information.


---


With an expression of complete respect
                                      J. Trnobranský,
                                      deputy."

On the basis of this intervention, the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs asked the Interior to re-examine the matter.

On 24 November the ministry replied that it had re-examined the application of O. A. Břich and still insists on its rejecting position. And it probably took negotiations at a high level between the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs and the Ministry of the Interior before the Interior issued, on 21 January 1924, the decree that after the new examination it no longer had objections to the concession for a receiving station being granted to O. A. Břich.

By the end of 1923, 36 Standart receivers had been sold in Bohemia, 11 in Moravia. The construction of amateur receivers was, however, unconditionally forbidden, and any attempt in that direction threatened hard prison.

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs considered a radio amateur to be only someone who built and operated his own radio station himself, from components he had made himself or obtained elsewhere. In 1922 the Ministry added to this definition the proverbial rider "for whatever purpose." In 1924 two criteria were officially established for the concept of the radio amateur:

1. The receiving station is not used for profit.
2. The radio amateur builds the station:
   a) entirely himself;
   b) from components that can be regarded as radiotelegraph apparatus, or partly also from components that are not radiotelegraph apparatus.

The others — who (in the words of the draft government regulation) "subscribe to reception by the postal and telegraph administration" — are not considered radio amateurs (since that automatically means having the station built by Radiojournal).

The question arises: Who is a radio amateur? A question that has not been properly answered to this day.

For even those who bought a finished set and looked after nothing but charging the battery and replacing the plate tube called themselves amateurs. Their stations, after all, were not official, not professional. They were therefore amateur. And they sent their applications to the radio club.

Motyčka's ideal was not only reception, but transmission as well. Experimentation. Radio must be promoted. When a person succeeds at something, picks up something interesting, solves some problem — what good is it if he has no one to talk it over with? The radio club. An environment where amateurs will gather, where one advises another, where friendship, born of a common interest, will reign.

THE FOUNDING OF THE RADIO CLUB


---


The second rejection of the Radio Club's statutes, already revised, annoyed Motyčka. Once, while he was absorbed in the statutes in a sour mood, Karel Lamač caught him there. A twenty-six-year-old film actor and director, sociable, cheerful, enterprising.

»I would like to speak with Dr. Baštýř.«

»With Baštýř?« Motyčka wondered.

Lamač was half pharmacist. He had studied pharmacology, and his parents waited in vain for when this madness with film would pass, so that he would return to the university again. He sent offers to several film companies, but nobody wanted him. He therefore decided that he would film on his own and bought himself a camera. He shot his first footage on the front during the World War and sold it to the wartime newsreel in Vienna. He shot, developed, copied, edited, cut it himself.

He then worked for a small fee as an actor. When he had saved up 6,000 crowns, he wrote the screenplay »Gilly's First Time in Prague«. He acted and directed it himself. The whole story took place outdoors, because there was no studio.

Then he made several smaller works with Anny Ondráková and Ota Heller, with alternating success, but in the end they were again without funds. Lamač played minor roles in films. Anny colored postcards, sold them for 2 crowns each in shops, and Ota worked as a projectionist at the Lucerna. That is where he met Motyčka.

Lamač was working on a new screenplay. »White Paradise« was taking shape. Soon they were at the end of their money. Karel, Anny and Ota set out on a begging pilgrimage. They gradually visited fifteen Prague financial magnates.

»You know how hard we tried. We did what we could. We even played some scenes for them. And nobody helped us. Except Dr. Baštýř.«

Anny Ondráková acted by day, and at night she painted designs for costumes and sets and sewed. For the shooting she brought a carpet she had secretly taken from home while her father was on duty and her mother was in town.

»And of all things a piece of carbon from the arc lamp had to fall onto that carpet. That was a disaster... That Dr. Baštýř knows plenty of influential people. He will be interested in radio. He has an antenna on his house.«

Dr. Baštýř did not yet have a concession. Only in February 1924 did the intelligence division of the Prague police directorate examine his political reliability. He was not, however, a black-listened [illegal] listener either. The Radioslavia company supplied him a receiver, strung an antenna with a downlead, led into Baštýř's bedroom, protruding on insulators over the street, and notified the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs afterward that Dr. Baštýř had provided a room in his house for testing apparatus in the center of the city in the evening hours.

His house was on Královodvorská street, on the side where the Kotva department store stands today.


---


---
name: chunk_0032.en
description: Translation of chunk 32 from Za tajemstvim eteru (Behind the Mystery of Ether) - Czech book about radio history. Source: https://ok5nw.nagano.cz/e-publikace/za_tajemstvim_eteru.pdf. Language: Czech. Author: Jaroslav J. Motyčka.
---

The maid in a white apron led Motyčka into the reception hall, which was over two stories high. Above was a balcony from which one entered guest rooms. In the hall, at an enormous table, sat Anny Ondráčková, Lamač, and Heller. The host, a sturdy sexagenarian, was not exactly handsome. But his receptive eyes and lively interest made a good impression on Motyčka.

Dr. Baštýř listened attentively to Motyčka's account. He had an expensive apparatus, but he heard nothing other than Kbely. He was a dentist. He graduated in 1887 and began practicing in 1894. He was involved in public life. He became a city councilor, was a member of the health commission, the board of the Electric Enterprises, and a member of almost all charitable organizations in Prague. At his own expense, he founded free dental care for the Prague garrison. He was a co-founder of the Jedlička Institute and a member of the presidium of the Prague Book Society. During the war, he established a military reserve hospital in the Straka Academy, where he built a dental clinic at his own expense. After the revolution, leading figures of Prague society were among his patients.

The idea that he could listen to the Eiffel Tower, Königswusterhausen, Eberswalde, and London excited him. Motyčka draws, writes, explains.

Within a few days, Dr. Baštýř had a new receiver built by his own hand. Motyčka inspected it, corrected it, helped put it into operation. Shortly thereafter, Motyčka was invited to Baštýř's again. The English and French ambassadors arrived. They listened to an opera broadcast from Paris. Motyčka was in reserve, in case something happened to the apparatus.

Dr. Baštýř assembles a preparatory committee for the Radio Club of Czechoslovakia from university professors, industrialists, and other prominent figures.

The double issue of Radioamatér 8-9, Volume II (February 1924) carried, under the prominent headline "Radio Amateurs!", an appeal which states:

"We are approaching a new, third, and hopefully last attempt to establish the Radio Club of Czechoslovakia...

For the preparatory committee of the Radio Club of Czechoslovakia:
Prof. Dr. J. Pantoflíček              Eng. Ant. Zikmund,
Head of the Geodetic Institute        Prague,
College of Technology in Prague.

Applications are accepted by J. Gesmacher, factory manager, Královské Vinohrady,
Řipská 15."


---


Ing. Štěpánek no longer figured as the main organizer of the Radioklub. He had exposed himself too much; to the authorities — and especially the Ministry of Posts — he had become unpleasant because of his radical approach, and at the time this appeal was published, proceedings were being prepared against him. They came to pass on 16 February 1924. "Právo lidu" (The People's Right) remarked on the margins of this trial:

"At the provincial criminal court, property offenses, various public acts of violence, etc., are ordinarily dealt with. The case of Ing. Štěpánek was a rarity in that chamber. It concerned the unauthorized construction of a radiotelegraphic station."

Ing. Štěpánek was sentenced to six weeks of hard imprisonment, aggravated by one fast day, because without a permit from the Ministry of Commerce he had stored and sold radio components. He was acquitted of the charge that he had unauthorizedly built and operated a radiotelegraphic station.

Štěpánek's conviction was an act of persecution of a progressive technician and publicist, a fighter for the freedom of amateur experimentation. He was to be silenced.

It did nothing to diminish his honor. But the power of law had taken effect, and it became an obstacle everywhere a clean criminal record was required. So Štěpánek, at the third attempt to found a radioklub, stepped into the background — at least before the authorities.

From Galvania, on 20 December 1922, a radiotelephonic connection between Jablonné and Prague was carried out — the first radiotelephonic connection in Czechoslovakia ever. The transmitter was designed by Prof. Šimek, and the experiment was organized by Ing. Bísek. A report went to the press saying the broadcast had taken place from the Electrotechnical Institute of the Czech Technical University in Prague. The Prague transmitter, however, was located at Elektra; that institution had no permit for such experiments. Therefore Ing. Bísek claimed the broadcast had been from the technical university. A university is academic ground, and the police cannot enter it.

On 20 December 1923 the National Assembly passed a law regulating the manufacture, sale, and possession of radiotelegraphic and radiotelephonic equipment, as well as its import from abroad. § 1, para. 1 of this law stipulated that radiotelegraphic and radiotelephonic equipment could be manufactured and possessed only on the basis of a special permit. This permit was required not only for activities carried on under the trade-licensing law, but in all other cases as well. From §§ 3–5 it followed that land and water transport, schools, and the army were governed by special legal norms. Which cases, then, were other than commercial? The bureaucrats of the day really imagined that an amateur, before starting to build a crystal set or any other device, would first apply to the Ministry of Commerce for a permit, demonstrate his competence to it, and that the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs would then decide on his application in cooperation with it.

On 10 February 1924 the MPT launched its final assault:

"To the State Prosecutor's Office
in Hradec Králové.

The Presidium of the Provincial Political Administration in Prague has filed a criminal complaint against the firm Galvania in Jablonné nad Orlicí for violations of the telegraph law and under the law on the manufacture, possession, and import of radiotelegraphic equipment.

It is suspected that the entire secret radio-amateur movement in Czechoslovakia — which is, and will continue to be, ruthlessly prosecuted by the state — was nourished precisely by Galvania's illegal production. The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs requests that the case of Galvania, whose name has reportedly been changed to Radiofon, be investigated with all severity.

Deputy of the Director-General of Posts and Telegraphs:
Breicha."


---


[Illegible OCR artifact — no translatable content in the source chunk.]


---


The year 1924 brought the resolution of all the main problems.

On the matter of ČTK versus Radiojournal, Dr. Kučera noted in the reference-sheet vote: "ČTK also had in view the establishment of broadcasting alongside the Radio-Journal company. Director Kalmus strove in every way to gain an advantage within that organization, so that the MPT would allocate him certain wavelengths and he could immediately procure the corresponding types of receiving stations. Radioslavia, or rather Radiojournal, which at that time had received a license from the Ministry of the Interior for broadcasting concerts, lectures, and also stock-exchange and press reports, strove for the same thing.

"Since the two competitors could not agree, both demanding the same wavelengths, and since the manufacture of radio equipment was prohibited, the matter reached a dead end. '...But since, in the spirit of the government decree being prepared, we shall probably be obliged to withdraw fundamental consent to the establishment of transmitting stations for both ČTK and Radioslavia, the question of the division of wavelengths — which would in any case cause no small difficulties — will become moot.'"

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs issued the relevant decrees to the Czechoslovak Press Bureau only on 23 June, but that memo was one of the last nails in the coffin of the monopoly. On 2 April 1924 the founding general meeting of the Czechoslovak Radioklub took place.

People often strain, fight, sacrifice whatever they have. In the end they reach their goal. They have achieved it. And they discover it is not as they imagined. Yes, they won, they have what they wanted — but it is something else. It suffices to look at the composition of the board of the Czechoslovak Radioklub. Prof. Ing. Šimek was elected chairman; First Vice-Chairman JUDr. Emil Miříčka; Second Vice-Chairman Colonel Ing. Josef Dočkal; First Secretary Dr. Josef Linhart, Director-General of the Czechoslovak Red Cross; Second Secretary Ing. Jaroslav Hajíček, assistant at ČVUT (Czech Technical University); Treasurer Ing. Ant. Zikmund. Members of the board became MUDr. Alfred Baštýř, Prof. Dr. Fr. Nušl, Prof. Dr. Jar. Pantoflíček, Prof. Dr. Karel Rón, Prof. Dr. Žáček, and Ing. František Štěpánek.

Of the fighters for the radioklub, only Ing. Štěpánek made it onto the board, and that as a member without office. Motyčka, Ing. Bísek, and MUC Šimandl became alternates, as did Gesmacher and Prokopec. The army placed on the board, besides Colonel Dočkal, one of its leading experts on radiotelegraphy, Staff Captain Dr. František Kuník. As auditor of accounts he will have access to everything, and the office will not burden him much either, since the general meeting elected further auditors: Rudolf Habersberger, official of the Zemská Banka; J. Radimský, official of the Prague Credit Bank; and Václav Havel.

The board set to work.
