It intervened with the Presidium of the Government on the matter of the government decree on radiotelegraphy; it began advising members, collecting and processing their applications for receiving-station licenses. At every board meeting new members were admitted. On 5 May 1924 there were 186 of them. Those were not all the applicants. A number could not be admitted because they failed to supply declarations from three guarantors. It was decided to verify their reliability and consider the application later.

Radio became a fashionable affair of the "upper ten thousand." Among the founders of the "First Czechoslovak Radioklub, seated in Brno" — according to the report in the journal Rádiotelegrafie a telefonie — belonged prominent figures of public life: Ministers Dolanský and Srba, professors of the Brno university and technical college, the great industrialist Dr. Ing. Kovařík (head of the Prostějov Wikovka works), figures from the ranks of industrialists, and finally the radio amateurs. The First Czechoslovak Radioklub, section for Slovakia and Subcarpathian Rus, met in Bratislava at the YMCA on Valy. It had more than 70 books and subscribed to 38 foreign periodicals. Its board included YMCA director Henry Barden, railways director Ing. Jindřich Beneš, Director-General Egon Bondy, Mayor of Bratislava Dr. Ľudovít Okálik, Provincial military commander General Spiré, and others.

The government decree determining the conditions for the establishment, maintenance, and operation of telegraphs was issued on 17 April 1924. By decree of 14 May the MPT set the fees for operating a receiving station and the procedure for submitting license applications. The complicated procedure connected with it was illustrated by an official file in the matter of Prince Schwarzenberg — namely JUDr. Adolf Schwarzenberg, great landowner, residing in Prague IV, Hradčanské nám. No. 186, registered in Libějice u Netolic:


---


"Prague, 28 July 1924.
Presidium of the Provincial Political Administration in Prague.
No. 22758.
Dr. Rudolf Schwarzenberg applies for the establishment
of a private receiving station in Prague IV.
                                       To the Presidium of the Ministry of the Interior
                                       in Prague.
With reference to the decree of 10 July 1924, No. 12631, I hereby notify you, on the basis of the report
of the Police Directorate in Prague and the District Political Administration in Prachatice,
that there is no objection to Dr. Adolf Schwarzenberg, born 18 July 1880, either in the political
or in the moral respect. No objections are therefore raised
- to granting the request of Dr. A. Schwarzenberg for permission to establish
 and operate a radio-electric receiving station.
                          For the President:
                                     (Illegible signature.)"
     Would anyone today, buying a radio receiving set in a shop in the form of a "transistor radio," give a thought to what problems there used to be?
     Shortly after the constituent general meeting, Professor Pantoflíček appeared in Motyčka's booth in the Lucerna. Troubles began. Correspondence
with interested parties and with the authorities, a great deal of writing and errands to run. The committee even
imagined that all applications for a receiver concession would be submitted
only through the radio club and with its recommendation. Surely none of those prominent gentlemen who had let themselves be elected to the committee would do that. And for that,
Motyčka would be the right man. They named him honorary secretary, which in the Czech
of that day meant that he would do the work for nothing.
     Motyčka was pleased by the personal visit of the university professor. He was pleased
too by the trust the committee placed in him. And he did not disappoint. He toiled. He did that work
at the technical university under Prof. Šimek. He wrote in his diary:
     "I am on duty from 8 to a quarter to 2 and in that time I do not stop once. There is a pile
of inquiries and membership applications to deal with. In the afternoon, after a quick
lunch, back at the cinema. After the last evening screening I am again with my
beloved apparatus in the glass roof."
     He sacrificed even the earning possibilities that the morning
work in the AB studios offered him, and those were not bad at all.
     Sometime in March 1924 he got mail from the USA. It was from Špička, a former
employee of the Lucerna, who had emigrated there. He described the sea voyage and his
first impressions of the overseas country. A postcard was enclosed with the letter. For
the brunette, from Miss Jelínková.
     In Vodičková Street next to the Lucerna stood an old house "U Pavlánských,"
which had two courtyards. Into the second one opened the windows of the cinema's projection booth,
the window of the preparation room, and a balcony. From there one could see the front facade
of the courtyard apartment building, on whose first floor was located the fashion salon
of Miss Jelínková, and through the open windows the girls who worked there.
The young men of the Lucerna occasionally glanced over there; the brunette they caught sight of only rarely. In the courtyard Mánes had an exhibition hall. Motyčka lowered himself from a window of the Lucerna
staircase onto its roof and by way of it reached the window of the tailoring
salon. He was looking for the brunette, who had meanwhile slipped away to the back into the workshop.
     The girls observed him with interest. He held up a note: "Please,
could you call her over?" Her friends were willing:
     "Pavla, Pavla, you've got a postcard here!"
     She took the note, looked at Motyčka, and smiled.
     Motyčka would gladly have asked what her full name was, and what message she would send
to Špička. He would have said something else too, but at that moment he did not know exactly what.
His whole being took in her gaze, her brown eyes, her dark hair, her noble
face, her beautiful figure.
     He had never been interested in the fashion salon before. Now he looked
in there every day. Pavla sat somewhere in the back. What was her name, he wondered? He ought to ask
the girls. But no. He would not ask. He was not going to come on to her like that.
     He spent the last July days of 1924 at the summer residence of his chief
at Žďárek. On the first of August the season at the Lucerna began again, and on 31 July
he returned to Prague by train.
     His first glance went to the fashion salon. Both windows were open,
otherwise it was empty and deserted. It was a beautiful, sunny day. Motyčka took
a small mirror and flashed sun signals. Presently Pavla appeared and looked up
with a scolding air. When she saw Pravoslav, the stern expression vanished.
     About a week later, at 11 o'clock in the evening after the screening, Motyčka locked
the projection booth and let himself down. Noiselessly he approached the window of the fashion
salon, pinned up a letter with a pin, and climbed back up. He was so shaken
that he did not sleep a wink that night. The next day he was in the projection
booth already at half past seven in the morning. In front of the balcony door there was an iron
hatch, through which one could get by ladder down to the foyer. It was meant as an escape route
in case of fire. Motyčka slid the hatch open. This time Pavla had her sewing
machine by the window. Pravoslav observed through the crack that she now and then looked up.
His agitation reached its height. He had never corresponded with girls
and now he was displeased with himself. That invitation to a meeting should have looked
quite different.
     He stepped out onto the balcony. Pavla smiled and nodded. A few seconds later he stood
down in Vodičková Street. When she came, she offered him her hand:
     "Erbenová. Pleased to meet you."
     In 28 October Street they boarded the open summer trailer of the number seventeen
and rode to Stromovka. They returned at noon. In the evening Motyčka
wrote in his diary: "Everything I did today I did more joyfully, and many times
I looked downward."
     In the days that followed they saw each other only rarely. Miss Jelínková had returned
from her holiday. She was strict and did not allow the girls any suitors.
When Pavla sometimes stayed in the workshop until the end of the screening, Pravoslav
escorted her. She lived in Divišova Street (now Italská) with an aunt who was
even harder on suitors than Miss Jelínková. Motyčka left the hatch
permanently slid open and through the crack brought the fashion salon into his field of view.
     On Tuesday, 19 August 1924, Pavla sat in the projection booth and watched
the film. After the screening they went together to Vinohrady, toward Divišova Street.
When they came near the forbidden zone, he summoned his courage: "Wouldn't you
give me a little kiss for goodbye?" and made something like an awkward attempt
- at an embrace. They always laughed when this episode came back to their minds.
But that first kiss sealed a faithful love that outlasted death itself.


---


THE DIRECTOR'S TROUBLES


---


The sundial on the tower of the church of St. Lawrence in Náchod has
the interval between eleven and twelve stretched out. The afternoon
numerals are crowded together. Perhaps it is so that the townsmen who strolled
here before noon could follow the time exactly. So that their wives
need not be cross with them.
     The holidays were over, the school year had begun. The calendars showed 4 September
1924.
     An elegantly dressed gentleman, of sturdy build, with black hair and a black
moustache, glanced at the dark, onion-shaped domes on the wooden stand.
He strolled at a leisurely pace across the square. When he reached the old town hall,
he cast a sidelong look at the stone pillory with the remnants of the chains to which
condemned men had once been fettered. It was plain to see that he was thinking of something very unpleasant.
     He took a few more steps and stopped. He pulled a newspaper out of his pocket. He did not read
it. He looked only at one article he did not need to search for.
     He finished his Virginia cigarette and set out toward Dobrošov. As he was leaving
the square, he met a group of students, who greeted him politely.
     He often walked to Dobrošov, especially in clear weather. From there he wanted
to see Sněžka. He was a tourist, a lover of nature. Once a year he went
fishing. Near Domažlice, at the home of his wife's parents. He looked forward
all year to that stream with its clear, crystal water. His wife went fishing
with him. Not long ago they had moved to Náchod.
     At the conference of the teaching staff of the state secondary school
on 30 October of last year, the keeper of the physics collection, Professor
Štíbr, had laid on the table a catalogue of teaching aids. He turned to chapter IX,
"Induction Electricity." On offer there were induction coils with a core, a medical
inductor, Geissler tubes, a dynamo-electric machine, Bell's telephone
and wireless telegraphy. Professor Štíbr pointed to a picture showing
two boards of polished hardwood. On one was mounted
an inductor, a spark gap, a battery cell, a telegraph key, and a vertical metal
rod with a horizontal arm. On the other, the same vertical rod with a horizontal
arm, a coherer, a battery cell, and a bell.
     "Operates at a distance of 5 to 10 m," the catalogue guaranteed.
     "Radio telegraphy will have an ever greater and greater significance. We must
explain to the students and at least demonstrate the basic principle. I propose
that we purchase the wireless telegraphy set for our collection," said
Professor Štíbr.
     There were opinions for and against in the staff. Some professors would rather
have seen something else bought, from their own field. The headmaster, Jindřich Spal, himself
a mathematician and physicist, stood behind Professor Štíbr's proposal. The school
ordered the set from the firm FALCO of Mariánské Lázně.
     Both the transmitter and the receiver worked flawlessly. Word of the successful
experiments began to spread around the town. Someone reported it all to the authorities, and an investigation was opened against Headmaster Spal. The case was conducted at the District Court in Náchod under file number N t IV 195/24. Neither Headmaster Spal nor the teaching staff
could understand how it was possible. After all, they had ordered for the school, according to
the catalogue of teaching aids, one set from a firm that offers and supplies them. What
was objectionable in that? The set was kept in the cabinet, it was used for
nothing but teaching in the physics lesson. If it were something objectionable, could the firm FALCO manufacture and sell such aids at all? Would they be allowed
to be offered in the first place? Why was there not even a mention in the catalogue?
How could such a toy be compared with the apparatus actually used in radio
telegraphy?
     Such and similar questions whirled in Headmaster Spal's head. What a disgrace,
what a scandal, to be dragged through the courts. He, a school headmaster. Why? What
had he done to anyone? Because he strove to have his students as well prepared as possible?
Because he wanted to provide them with the best he could procure for the school? He,
Jindřich Spal, an honorable man of character, who had always prided himself on
his honesty, who lived only for his work and for his family?
     "Are those instruments capable of carrying a message over any distance at all, however tiny?" they asked the headmaster at the interrogation.
     "That they are."
     "So it is a wireless telegraph?"
     "As a wireless telegraph we bought the set for the school. As a teaching aid, of course. Surely you understand that we do not intend
to operate a telegraph service with it?"
     The pages of the Collection of Laws and Ordinances rustled. Volume 1923, section 27.
Law No. 60.


---


§ I-

1) To establish, maintain, and operate telegraphs within the territory of the Czechoslovak
   Republic is the exclusive right of the state.
2) Exceptions are determined by this law (§ 3, par. 3).

1) A telegraph within the meaning of this law is understood to be an electric telegraph or
   telephone, both wire and wireless (radiotelegraph), as well as electric
   signaling devices.
2) In this law the word radiotelegraph also means radiotelephone.

§ 3.

1) The state may lend
      a concession for the exercise of the right designated in § 1 of this law. The conditions shall be determined by a government decree.
2) . . . radiotelegraphs on ships . . .
3) Wire telegraphs inside buildings or on contiguous
      plots of land belonging to the same owner require no concession, provided their continuity is not broken
      by another person's land, by public waters, or by another person's private waters,
      nor encumbered by a right of way.
         "The school for which you are responsible has established and maintains a wireless telegraph.
No concession has been lent to it for that. Nor did you apply for one."
         "We conducted the experiments partly in the cabinet room, partly in the classroom. The range
of the instrument is so small that outside the building its signals could not
be picked up."
         "The law says nothing about the range of a radiotelegraph. A wire telegraph
you may set up in the school. From one classroom to another, or indeed from the cellar to
the attic. But not, however, a wireless telegraph."
         "That is not a wireless telegraph. That is a model," the headmaster thought to himself.
         The red rowan berries by the wayside, the deep forests, the views into the valley of the Metuje
and toward the Orlice Mountains pleased Headmaster Spal. He pulled out the newspaper again.
         Decision of the President of the Republic of 2 September 1924.
1. It is ordered that no criminal proceedings for the offenses named in § 18 of the law of 23 March
1923, Coll. of Laws and Ordinances, on telegraphs shall be brought against Czechoslovak citizens . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
 . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . and if criminal proceedings have already been brought,
they shall be discontinued, provided that within fourteen days of the date of the proclamation
of this decision the persons concerned apply additionally for the concession mentioned in § 3 . . . . . . .


---


Jindřich Spal knew § 18 of the Telegraph Act by heart: "Whoever establishes
or operates telegraph apparatus without the permission of the Ministry
of Posts and Telegraphs, with the exceptions stated in § 3, par. 3 and in
§ 7 to § 9, shall be punished for a misdemeanor by imprisonment from
6 months to 1 year." As he was returning to the town and descending
through Amerika toward the brewery, he already knew what he would do.
     He discussed the matter once more in the professors' council, and on
19 September 1924 a request went from Náchod to the Ministry of Posts
and Telegraphs from the grammar school directorate, asking for retroactive
permission to purchase the wireless telegraph model and for the
halting of criminal proceedings against Director Spal on the basis of
the President of the Republic's decision on amnesty. Round stamp,
Jindřich Spal, director.
     Meanwhile the physics collections were enriched with further exhibits,
which someone had donated to the grammar school: a decommissioned
amateur single-tube receiver and a crystal detector.
     The wheels of the judicial machinery turn slowly and inexorably,
like the cogwheels of a clockwork. One does not even notice their
movement. Yet the hands advance, ad vance... The press wrote of the
amnesty proclaimed by the President of the Republic as an amnesty
for amateurs. Meaning black (unlicensed) amateurs, of course.
That was also the position of the postal ministry. It declared that the
amnesty did not apply to Director Spal. This was, after all, not a matter
of an unlicensed listener.
     Spal's claim that this was not a wireless telegraph in the true sense
of the word, but rather a model of a wireless telegraph for teaching
purposes, had to be verified, its correctness or incorrectness proven.
On 20 October 1924 a technical official of the Posts and Telegraphs
Directorate in Pardubice came to Náchod. He carefully inspected the
grammar school's physics collections. The postal directorate then sent
the following report to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs:
     "The transmitting radio station consists of a spark gap, which can
be brought into operation by an induction coil and a Rumkorff coil,
old components of the institute's physics cabinet. Since the said coil
is damaged, no precise judgment can be made about the transmission,
power and range of the station; however, since the spark gap resembles
a toy rather than anything else and there is no antenna, the station's
effectiveness does not reach beyond the building.
     It is noted that an oscillating circuit of a receiving station can also
be connected to the spark gap.
     The receiving station consists of a variometer coil, a rotary capacitor,
a socket for the triode, a heating resistor and connecting terminals,
mounted in a small cabinet.
     The institute does not yet have triodes, a battery, an antenna, or
receivers, only a crystal detector. Once the receiving station is fully
equipped, it will be capable of receiving time signals and Morse marks
from large telegraph transmitting stations.
                                              President:
                                                         Jindřich,"


---


It reached the MPT on 30 October 1924 and was assigned file number
67.334-VI-24.
     The proceedings against Director Spal were, in the end, halted after
all. Already at the stage of preliminary investigation. Could it be that
the amnesty of the President of the Republic applied only to amateurs?
Nothing of the sort is stated there. The spark gap resembles a toy rather
than anything else, there is no antenna. The receiving station is not
equipped for reception. These are models.
     Director Spal died on 14 December 1937. I spoke with his wife
in Náchod at the end of 1973. She told me many interesting things
about her life, about her husband, about Náchod and the Náchod region.
She remembered the incident with the transmitting station at the grammar
school only very vaguely. It did not intrude disruptively into the
Spal family in any way, and it ended without consequences.

THE FIRST CONNECTION


---


A light carriage drives along the Zbraslav road. It is Saturday.
8 November 1924, morning. The driver sits on the box, flicks his whip,
listens to the rhythmic clatter of hooves and thinks about his own
affairs. From behind, a truck approaches at a leisurely pace. And just
as it wants to overtake, a motorcycle comes from the opposite side.
The truck can no longer brake, it is alongside the carriage. The
motorcycle zigzags across the road from side to side, the driver with
bulging eyes has his hands full, does not know what to grab, the
passenger is shouting at him. The truck accelerates, the engine roars,
the horn blares, the horse rears up, the truck pushes against the
carriage, the horse panics, and a motorcycle is balancing in front of it.
     In the last fraction of a second the truck finally pulls back into
lane, and by some miracle the motorcycle threads through the gap
between it and the edge of the road. In the trailer sits its owner
Dejl, driving (if that word can be used at all) Motyčka. The motorcycle
is a one-liter four-cylinder ACE. They drive to the Cukrák [sugar
refinery], turn around there, and head back to Prague.
     Motyčka had a thermal ammeter in the antenna feed at his
transmitter. It seemed to him that it was deflecting too little, and
it occurred to him to see what would happen if he connected it into
the oscillating circuit. The needle slammed against the stop, and that
was the end of the ammeter. Dejl got him a new one, and Motyčka in
return helped him with a receiver. Dejl was so enthusiastic that he
promised Motyčka he would teach him to ride a motorcycle in exchange.
     One more little episode at the crossing gates at Malá Chuchle,
where Motyčka, instead of easing off the throttle, opened it up, and
then could not brake in time. But in the end they arrived safely at
Smíchov, where Dejl took over the machine with a sigh: "This will
cost me a year earlier at the general overhaul." At home Šimandl
awaits:
     "Štěpánek wants us to try a connection between the Lucerna and
Malá Strana."
     "To his place in Lázeňská street?"
     "He wants to hear it. I will answer with a feedback receiver."
     That was an idea. Motyčka had already retired his spark transmitter,
with which he had never managed to make a single connection, and
which made a racket besides. He built himself a tube transmitter.
From the "U Nováků" distillery a 120 V direct-current line led to
the Lucerna. Motyčka powered the transmitter from it, and later
bought batteries as well. He listened on a three-tube receiver — RF,
detector, AF. On 14 October 1924 he completed the assembly of the
second AF stage. At Easter Šimandl went home for the holidays and
arranged with Motyčka that he would try to catch him. Motyčka spent
the entire holiday at the key. He transmitted on a 160 m wave. The
little lamp in the antenna glowed. He could not wait for Šimandl's
return. But disappointment came. He did not catch even a dot.
Motyčka listened to French, English and Dutch amateurs, corresponded
with them and gained experience from them. On 13 March he received
a letter from Deloy.
     Mr. Havel gave him at the Lucerna a small room previously used
as a film storeroom. Motyčka heard that his application for a broadcasting
receiver concession would be granted. So he strung an antenna, which
he led from the roof through a window onto the staircase. There he
bored a hole through the wall and inserted a feed-through insulator,
of the kind commonly available at the Radiojournal shop on Národní
třída. He placed the receiver on a table, the transmitter between
the double roof of the cabin. He brought in power, got hold of an
electric heater, and the "radio cabinet" was complete. The apparatus
was ready, the experiment could begin. He decided that he would
transmit under the call sign OK1. He assigned Šimandl the call sign
OK2. The experiment took place between 14.05 and 14.45 hours with
excellent results. The first Czechoslovak connection on short waves
was accomplished, and also the first Czechoslovak amateur short-wave
connection. It is documented by an entry in Motyčka's diary with the
note: "I was in an elevated mood until evening."
     This experiment was repeated on Sunday 9 November 1924 at noon
on a 150 m wave. About the OK1 call sign Motyčka noted again on
13 November: "I chose this call sign because the letters OK internationally
denote Czechoslovak nationality, and with the attached numeral one
I wanted to distinguish myself from professional stations. Czechoslovak
postal stations, after all, have yet another, third letter after the
letters OK."
     The OK1 to OK2 connection gave Motyčka courage. Night after
night he called European stations. In vain. He did not give up. He
stretches out a counterpoise. On Sunday 30 November 1924 at night he
tuned to a shorter wave, on which his antenna resonated better. At
0.45 hours he called CQ DE OK1 somewhere between 137 and 140 m.
Into the antenna go 300 mA. At 01 hours he called the invitation again
on 170 m. Here the antenna current was 400 mA. He heard 1A1D DE
G2SH, 5UL DE 8UU and CQ DE 8DU. He led the counterpoise from the
door into the corridor, and from there through a window into the
courtyard.
0145: CQ DE 0CA
         ?? DE 0CA
        0CA DE OK1
        OK1 DE 0CA - QRK? QRA? QRS K
      0CA gave a report of R8 and said that he received on two tubes.
OK1 was not yet a seasoned telegraphist and was too agitated to
concentrate. 0CA gave him something in Dutch, which Motyčka did not
quite catch and had noted down only in fragments. It was clear that
0CA had his station in Rotterdam. The exchange of a report and the
statement of the station's location are generally considered essential
parts of an amateur connection. This night had historic significance:
the first Czechoslovak connection with a foreign country on short waves
was accomplished. Not merely an amateur one, but the first of any kind.
      The close of the year 1924, so significant for the history of
Czechoslovak radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony, brought yet one more
sensational event: reception of transatlantic short-wave stations in
Prague. Only recently even the technical press had considered such a
thing impossible. It was 2 December 1924, Motyčka was receiving, and
the results of the experiments were published in Radioamatér.


---


IN BRNO


---


Úvoz is one of the long streets of Brno. It starts at Konečného
square, near the Czech technical university; along the western foot
of Špilberk it slopes down toward Sladová.
    One winter day in 1923 two young men were pulling a sled along
the snow-covered Úvoz. The load on the sled was carefully wrapped
in a shaggy blanket. The sled glided over the snow, the young gentlemen
ran alongside it. And right at the spot where one rides down a rather
steep little hill, a policeman crossed their path. The young men wanted
to avoid him. They went in an arc around him. Suddenly, however,
the sled overturned and the load spilled out into the snow. The young
men froze. The policeman fixed his gaze on them.
    Vladimír Lhotský and Zdeněk Petr set the sled upright and gathered
up the load. The young men looked respectable, and plenty of people
cart things around on sleds. The policeman saw nothing suspicious
in it and continued on his beat.
    "What about the tubes?"
    Nothing happened to the tubes. Fortunately. But sulfuric acid
did spill from the accumulators, from the heating battery and from
the small cells of which the anode battery is composed.
      Petr and Lhotský built together a four-tube receiver following
Štěpánek's instructions "Four-tube high-frequency amplifier," which was
printed in installments in issues 4 through 10 of Volume I of Radioamatér.
The name "high-frequency amplifier" was not precise. In reality it was
nothing other than a resistance-coupled amplifier with controlled
capacitive positive feedback between the last, or in some cases the
second, stage and the first. At the amplifier's input was a tunable
LC circuit made of a rotary capacitor and a loop antenna, or a coil
with a sliding contact when listening on an outdoor antenna. An
interesting feature of this receiver's construction was the so-called
"resistance bridge" — a small ebonite plate on which all the resistors
were inked in indelible pencil; on this plate were also mounted
capacitors made of tinfoil and mica. In the photograph of the inside
of Petr's receiver, the connections from the "resistance bridge" to
the individual tubes are clearly visible.
      (We deliberately use the word "tube" [lampa], not "electron
tube" [elektronka]. Motyčka had already pointed out in issue 3 of
Volume I of Radioamatér that the Washington Radiotelephone Conference
of April 1922 recommended the term "electron tube," but this proposal
— apart from military terminology — never caught on in our country.
People wrote and said "tube" until 1938, when the term "electronka"
was introduced.)
      They carted the station back and forth. One Sunday they received
at the Petrs', the next at the Lhotskýs'. The first thing Zdeněk Petr
caught on the loop antenna was a Sunday concert from Paris. Konigswusterhausen
and several telegraph stations could also be heard.
      Lhotský was twenty years old, Petr seventeen. When the first
broadcasting stations appeared on the medium waves, they tried transmitting
as well. A simple oscillator, absorption modulation with a microphone
in the antenna. They did not announce themselves, nor did they make
connections. For that, they had a virtuoso in front of the microphone,
who played the violin. An acquaintance of Vladimír Lhotský's, at that
time a student at the Brno technical university, picked up the broadcast
somewhere near Vyškov. The transmitter was located at the Lhotskýs',
in Brno at Na Nivkách.
      The year 1924 brought an improvement to the original Štěpánek,
or rather Duroquier, four-tuber — the resistance-coupled amplifier
with a tuned circuit at the input:
      "On a loop antenna one can receive the time signals from Nauen
and from Paris quite strongly," Štěpánek wrote. "Nauen can be heard
with headphones lying on a table through three rooms. Konigswusterhausen
on 4000 m, both telegraphy and telephony on 2700 m, on a loudspeaker.
Likewise Eberswalde, and the evening Paris concert and the forecast
at 23.10 can be caught clearly. Radiojournal comes through on the
loudspeaker. If we connect further capacitors in parallel with the
input circuit capacitor, we can log the slow telegraphy of the
transatlantic stations."
      Even with this improvement, however, the receiver could not
hold its own in the situation that took shape over the course of 1924:
broadcasting stations multiplied. People wanted to hear the spoken
word and music; those interested in telegraphy could be counted
on the fingers of one hand. It may be supposed that Štěpánek's
enthusiastic apologetics were motivated to a considerable degree by
commercial interests. The "resistance bridge," inked in pencil on
ebonite, with its tinfoil-and-mica capacitors, was a profitable
commercial article.
     In issue 12 of Volume II of Radioamatér, Štěpánek published the
description and schematic of the "All-concert Four-tuber." He translated
the name All-concert into Czech, but then came back to it again.
The Allconcert had a tuned radio-frequency amplifier stage, a detector
stage with feedback, and one or two audio-frequency amplifier stages.
From the laboratory of Nová Epocha, from the corner house of Lázeňská
and Saská streets on Malá Strana in Prague, the Allconcert set out
on a triumphal march through the republic. The era of the Allconcert
lasted several years.


---


BROADCASTING AND BUREAUCRATS


---


In 1925 the Slovak Section of the radioclub resided in new premises
in Dlhá Street no. 33. Amateurs gathered there daily. Once a week
a public radio concert was held. Even the large concert hall could not hold
all the listeners. On Sunday, January 11, 1925, at one such concert
Moscow was captured for the first time. Its program was reproduced from
6 to 7 p.m.
      In Bohemia, broadcasting was moving to medium waves. On January 28, 1925,
Strašnice began transmitting on a 550 m wave with a five-hundred-watt transmitter; Brno had done so in September 1924.
 The radiotelegraph transmitter OKB at Komárov was keyed from the tower of the Provincial
House on Žerotínovo Square. The receiving station was located here too. Besides
the keying line, the telegraphist had a direct telephone line at his disposal for
arranging matters with the transmitter operator. In the second tower, Radiojournal set up its
 Brno studio. Every day at 6 p.m., after the end of operations, the
 telegraphist would switch both lines over to the studio, and in the morning he took them back. During the day the
 Komárov station transmitted as OKB postal telegrams; in the evening, as "Radio-
journal Brno," it broadcast news, music, and lectures.
      The economic situation of Radiojournal looked bad. Míla Tučková
announced and recited, but above all she took care of the bookkeeping. And sometimes she did not even
know how to scrape together the fees. The company's share capital was
melting away. There were still few listeners, the fees brought in little, and what
was earned by services to listeners — lending, assembling, and repairing
 receivers — was not enough to cover the budget deficit. Baťa wanted to help.
However, in such a way that he would take over Radiojournal. Neither the Ministry
of Posts and Telegraphs nor the Presidium of the Government agreed to that. In March 1925
the government resolved to rehabilitate Radiojournal. It entered the company
with 51% of the capital. A Board of Directors was established, composed of representatives of the Ministry
of Posts and Telegraphs, the Press Department of the Cabinet
Office, and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Radiojournal's workers began to
gain solid ground under their feet. Fees were lowered, and the construction
of new transmitting stations was prepared.
      On January 26, 1925, at the Ministry of the Interior, Dr. Sołotka met with Dr. Ku-
čera and Staff Captain Dr. Kuník. They noted that England had over a million
broadcasting listeners, Germany three-quarters of a million, Austria over
100,000, and Czechoslovakia only 1,500. It is true that there were not enough cheap
receivers, that there were few transmitting stations, and that fees were therefore high, but
all three agreed that the main brake and obstacle to progress was the cumbersome
permit procedure and especially the obtuse and clumsy investigation of political
reliability, which caused delays, excluded many applicants, and deterred even more.
Moreover, for every new subscriber the following stereotypical report had to be sent to the Ministry
of National Defense:
"Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs in Brno.
No. 16557/VIII.                                    Brno, February 25, 1925
Subject: František Sedlák of Lažánky near Blansko,
       concession for a receiving radio station.
                                                      To the Ministry of National Defense
                                                     (Telegraph Department)
                                                     in Prague.
We have granted, on behalf of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, to Mr. František
Sedlák of Lažánky no. 23 near Blansko, born in Rudice, resident
of Lažánky near Blansko, district Boskovice, a concession to establish, maintain,
and operate one fixed and permanent radiotelephone receiving station,
four-tube, of his own amateur design according to the approved wiring
formula, for listening to radio broadcasting for his own entertainment and instruction. The station will be
located at Lažánky near Blansko, number 23.
                                                  For the President.
                                                  Illegible signature,
                                                  Vice-President.
                                      Round stamp:
                                      Directorate of Posts and Telegraphs
                                      in Brno."
     When this letter arrived at the Telegraph Department of the Ministry of National Defense, it received a
registration stamp and the stamp FILE RETURN! and was routed to the Second Department of the General
Staff. While the newly minted owner of the radiotelephone receiving station
listened with his family to music and agricultural broadcasts, intelligence
officers carefully entered his data into the card file of persons suspected of
interest in electromagnetic waves. When the file finally returned to the Telegraph
Department, it received the big blue stamp AD ACTA and was deposited on the heap of
other, identically worded and equally useless papers.
      It was already clear to the General Staff that this was good for nothing; it was clear
to Staff Captain Kuník and to Dr. Kučera, and finally the Ministry
of the Interior understood it too. But it took until November 27, 1925, before the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs could officially announce that it would no longer issue
lists of concessionaires of receiving stations nor submit reports to the
Ministry of National Defense. The permit formalities were simplified.
On April 16, 1925, Government Regulation No. 82 of the Collection
of Laws and Regulations came into force. Concessions for receiving stations would be granted by postal offices.
The removal of bureaucratic obstacles had a beneficial effect: at the end of
1925 there were already 14,542 radio listeners.
      Radio? Yes, radio. Not "rozesílání," not "broadcasting," not "radiofonie." In 1925 the term "rozhlas" (broadcasting) took hold. Editor J. D. Richard
coined this word and used it for the first time on May 21, 1924, in Národní listy.
In his article we read: "Broadcasting means in Czech roughly the same as wide
scattering, sowing. The station truly broadcasts — 'rozhlásí' — received news or music
to everyone, and therefore we are justified in proposing for its function the name 'rozhlas.'
We hope that 'rozhlas' will find understanding in the circles of owners of receiving
stations as well as among all who care about the purity and expressiveness of our language..."
      Like mushrooms after rain, shops with radio components
and radio sets grew and multiplied. The joint-stock company Orbis bought Radioam-
atér from Eng. Štěpánek and publishes it in large format and lavish design. Ště-
pánek directed it, together with the editorial board, and contributed to almost every
issue. The Czechoslovak Radioclub began publishing its own magazine, "Česko-
slovenská radiorevue." The editor-in-chief was Dr. Baštýř; Šimandl was the responsible
manager.
      Daily, except Saturday and Sunday, from 5 to 7 p.m., the radioclub secretariat operated in the Lucerna,
on the 5th floor, with the secretary's office, a library,
and a laboratory. Every Friday at 8 p.m. a members' meeting was held. Technical
lectures alternated with debate evenings. The Radioclub moved into new
premises on Slovanský Island. Lectures took place in the Geodetic
Institute; social and debate evenings were held at the Měšťanská beseda in Vladislavova
Street.
      Excursion after excursion made its way to Petřín. In the Military Historical
Archive in Prague a file is preserved containing several
dozens of applications for permission to visit the Petřín military radio station
in 1925. Among those interested were, for example: The Bakers' Choir of the Union of
Food Workers in Prague, the directorate of the municipal girls' school in Prague VII
Bubny, Vinařská Street (headmistress Emilie Kozáková), the Society of Workers in Iron
Constructions in Prague
and its Surroundings Mutually Supporting One Another (secretary Jindřich Kožíšek, Českomoravská no. 807), "Domovi-
na," an association for the intellectual, moral, and physical development of youth, Prague VII,
no. 1140, the Supporting and Employment-Providing Society of Machine Locksmiths
"Strojnická beseda" in Prague (secretary Fr. Tamchyna, Prague VIII,
Drahobejlova 957), etc.
      Two applicants were refused. The Youth Education Department of the Communist
Party in Žižkov (chairman Fr. Kolář, Přibyslavská 4, secretary Budin-
ský), which wanted to arrange an excursion for children, and the Czechoslovak Radioclub. The former was
refused on the grounds that children were not allowed access for security reasons.
The radioclub's excursion was to be arranged on the occasion of the nationwide
meeting on Sunday, March 22. However, the application was sent too late, and the clerk
of the Telegraph Department of the Ministry of National Defense did not receive it on his desk until March 23.
      Further local clubs were founded, not only in the Prague suburbs
but also in towns outside Prague. It was therefore decided to abandon the system
of branches of the Prague club and to found a union of clubs under the name Czechoslovak
Radiosvaz (Radio Union).
      The amended implementing government regulation to the Telegraph Act
contained in § 9, letter f), an important provision that had not been in the previous version:
"Exceptionally, a concession for a transmitting station may also be granted to other persons, provided they are of legal age, reliable, and of good repute, provided they prove that they intend to establish and operate the transmitting
station solely for scientific purposes, provided further that the Ministry of the Interior and of National Defense expresses consent, and provided they
submit — at their own expense — to a special examination before the postal and telegraph administration authorities, in knowledge of radio technology, in the handling of radiotelegraphic and radio-
telephone apparatus, and in the regulations on radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony.
The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs will issue more detailed provisions on this examination."
The first small step toward drafting regulations for examinations of operators of amateur
transmitting stations was taken by the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs on August 14,
1925. The legal prerequisites for permitting amateur transmission began to be created.
Implementation, however, was still nowhere in sight. The strict ban
still stood.
            CSAA2


---


Motyčka had station cards printed for himself and a large stamp made,
 OK1, which he stamped onto these cards. He sent the first ones on December 9, 1924, namely
 to stations G5RZ and G5TZ. On December 18 he bought a gold wristwatch as a gift
for Pavla, and on Saturday, December 20, 1924, he wrote in his diary: "This
evening after ten o'clock I carried the material for a new
T-antenna up onto the terrace of the Lucerna. I obtained the
wire by unwinding a discarded choke coil, which
I had gotten from the stage of the Lucerna cabaret. I scraped off the burnt insulation
and measured the required length according to earlier experience and experiments. That same
night I stretched the antenna between the building in Vodičková Street and Štěpánská
Street. I had coiled up the old telephone line earlier in the day. Above the highest
point of the Lucerna (toward Štěpánská) I erected a wooden pole, and by midnight,
after adventurous climbing over the roofs of the Melantrich (that was the way one had to go
from the Lucerna terrace to Vodičková) and the Bergr buildings, I finally connected the wire
through a stairway window to the terminal of a feed-through insulator into my radio cabin.
The new antenna pulled better than the old one, and the reception was also stronger. This was partly due to
its larger dimensions, but mainly to the more favorable height, since the top conductor was
above the level of the metal roofs of the Melantrich, the telephone building at the Bergr buildings, the Lucerna,
Vodičková Street, and the metal roof of the Moravian Agrarian Bank.
      I have earned a good deal of money lately with evening demonstrations
of cinema equipment and occasional screenings for the film exchange, which paid
100 to 150 Kč per morning. I could therefore afford myself the luxury of an anode
battery made of small pocket cells with a total voltage of 400 V. I earned money for radio
components through my contributions to the magazine Radioamatér.
I was able to pay off the plot of land I had bought in Bráník, and I still had
enough left over for a gift for Pavla. I am so busy morning, noon, evening, and night
that my mother scolds me and worries about my health. So far I do not feel
at all tired. I am glad when I always have something to do."
      On May 30, 1925, at 10:50, he started up his new transmitter: TEST DE OK1
The anode voltage was 330 V, the anode current was 50 mA. At 11:45 he established
contact:
      ÍOK S2 - R OK OM QSA TOO R9 OM TWO LAMPS DR OM VY
GLD TO R OK U CALL ME SOME SUNDAY MORNING ON 80 M
ANSWERED BUT NO REPLY - VY SRI OM BUT HAVE SCHEDULE
WITH G2XY AND MUST LISTEN FOR HIM NW - WILL BE
TOO TOMORROW MORNING AT 500 GMT EXCUSE OM VY
SRI - OK1 BS2
      There is one small mysterious detail in this contact of Motyčka's with the Belgian
station BS2 (A. L. Stainier, Louvain): ANSWERED BUT NO REPLY.
"I answered, but I never received an answer back." It had nothing whatever to do
with the fact that Motyčka was to call him some Sunday morning. To whom
then had station BS2 answered, and from whom did it never again wait for an answer?
It apologized that it now had a scheduled contact with G2XY and had to listen for him.
Motyčka listened in on that contact: 1125 WAS CALLED BY
CZECHOSLOVAKIA CSAA2 ES OK1 AND HAD TO ANSWER...
Now the Belgian BS2 was in turn apologizing to the Englishman G2XY for being delayed.
He had been called by Czechoslovakia CSAA2 and OK1 and had to respond. Now it was all
clear: another Czechoslovak amateur transmitting station had appeared on the scene,
CSAA2. And Pravoslav did not even realize it at that moment. His article
"When QSL cards were still sent in envelopes" bears witness:
     "...There was not the slightest trace to be heard of any other
Czechoslovak station working. Neither its own signals could be heard, nor the calls of
foreign stations that would indicate that anyone else in Czechoslovakia was working
on short waves. Therefore one's breath stopped when one evening, relatively early,
at the end of January or the beginning of February 1925, lonely call signs broke into the 80 m
band, for at that hour Europe was not yet in Prague in
full strength and bustle. Under the pencil the call signs appeared: CQ CQ CQ DE
CSAA2 CSAA2 - EXPERIMENTAL TRANSMISSION FROM ELEVEN
O'CLOCK CENTRAL EUROPEAN TIME - CSAA2. In the first moment the writer
of these lines (Motyčka) was so startled that he did not even think of reaching
for the telegraph key. What if it were a trap? Who was this mysterious station?
Even cautious inquiries at the radioclub yielded no result. We were
afraid of one another........ Above the call sign, captured on a winter evening, there remained a silent
question mark. Only after a long time, at the close of summer 1925, was
CSAA2 caught again, and that in answer to a general CQ call."
     Motyčka did not record the first listening to station CSAA2 in his station log.
He does not mention the information from station BS2 in the quoted
article either. Either, then, he paid no proper attention to this incident and did not
register the existence of station CSAA2, or, with the passage of years when he wrote
the memoir, he had simply forgotten it.


---


■ • • • tm
     The French magazine La TSF moderne carried on page 41 of its January
issue of volume 6 (1925) a report by the operator of the French
station F8AE about a listening report from Prague, which also contained meteorological
data and an assessment of overall conditions. F8AE received several listening
reports. He writes: "un de ces correspondants habite Prague, à 1 500 km de
Paris" ("one of these correspondents lives in Prague, 1,500 km from Paris"). This was not the only French station that received
reports from Prague at the beginning of 1925. A photograph of the sender also arrived — a young student
with glasses and thick, slicked-back hair, sitting at a table on which
stands an allconcert receiver. A bookcase fills the wall behind the table. No
short-wave receiver is on the table. And one more thing: this young man is none other
than the operator of station CSAA2, which had so excited Pravoslav Motyčka.
     On July 17, 1925, Motyčka said his definitive goodbye to film work, liquidated
all his wires and the radio cabinet in the Lucerna, and joined ETA. He dedicated
his life to radio engineering. By September he was already transmitting from ETA.


---


Technical magazines took notice of the signals on short waves. The transmissions
of station OK1 were registered by Wireless World of May 6, by La TSF moderne
in its July issue, by Wireless World of September 9, of November 18, of November
25, and of December 16, 1925. This last one, Wireless World of December 16,
also carried a report about the captured Czechoslovak amateur
station CSAA2, from three correspondents.
      CSOK1 worked with stations FABC, S3UB, G5SI, and again BS2.
To all of them he gave his full Prague address during the contact. NAME MOTYČ-
KA? FABC asks him to repeat. This morning I read your letter. I will
write back to you at once. I have a loosely coupled Hartley, I am working on a wave around
40 m, he tells BS2. And what time is it in Prague?... I will send you a letter in a
sealed envelope, Mr. Motyčka, promises S3UB. G5SI tells about his
cage-shaped antenna, tuned by a series capacitor.
     The night of June 10 to 11, 1925. Three ten in the morning.
     OK1CS U1CMX - GM OM QRN FIRST ES QSA R5 QSS QRA
BORDEN 291 CHERRY FALL RIVER MASS -
     - R - VY GLD TO QSO OM - QTC?
     - R - WILL DO THAT OM - NW CUAGN 73S
     FB
     The first contact of a Czechoslovak amateur transmitting station across the Atlantic
Ocean. The first Czechoslovakia - USA contact ever!
     R received. VY GLD TO QSO OM — I am very pleased with this contact,
Old Man. QTC? Do you have any more messages for us?
     Motyčka did.
     This contact was also captured in England, and news of it was immediately delivered
to the magazine Wireless World. It published it in bold type on June 15,
1925, on page 77:
     "From Czechoslovakia to Massachusetts.
     Mr. J. Borden (1CMX) of Fall River, Mass., last month achieved
contact with OK1 in Prague. The Czechoslovak station, working on 42 m,
sent greetings to the American Radio Relay League and its president,
Mr. Hiram Percy Maxim."
     - R - WILL DO THAT OM - received - I will pass it on, OM -
     How is it that Wireless World writes that the contact was achieved last month?
There is only one explanation: the information reached the editorial office after the
issue had gone to press and was intended for the first July issue. In view of its topicality
and importance it was still inserted belatedly into the June issue,
but in its original wording.
     The thanks from the ARRL and its president arrived first by radiotelegraph
via Denmark and then also in writing. The letter was framed and
for a long time adorned the room of the Radioclub.

■— • • • wm


---


In 1925 much was written and said about amateur transmission. The Radio
Club ought to get involved as well. In its name the chairman, Emil
Heindl, a section chief at the Ministry for Supply of the People, submitted an application for permission.
The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs passed it on for comment to the Ministry of
National Defense. The head of the Telegraph Department, Staff Captain Dr. Kuník,
knew Heindl. He knew that he directed Department I of the ministry, which handled
ciphers, coal, timber, petroleum, grain, electric power, and gas. He knew he was a
correct and pleasant gentleman, a radio enthusiast, but technically
not much to speak of, and he did not know Morse code. Kuník knew what it was about, but his workload encompassed the entire broad field of military communications, in which
there was as yet no place for short waves. Construction of the radio network
and of the listening service continued (in some papers we even find the term "de-listening"
service). Work was proceeding on methods of radio contact with aircraft and balloons
(a systematic proposal had been prepared by the Ministry of National Defense as early as 1923), but everything was happening
on long and medium waves. The army did not yet have short waves.
As a specialist, it was clear to him that such a thing could have great significance.
He knew, however, of the negative stance of the Interior Ministry and the cautious position of the Intelligence
Department of the General Staff. He therefore drafted (October 21, 1925) the reply:
      "The Ministry of National Defense has no objections; it points out, however, that after this request is granted, new requests will certainly follow... It will be necessary to establish a monitoring
and listening service, and not only on the technical side, but also for the control of the transmitted
messages. Moreover, it may happen that, besides the stations for which a concession is granted,
stations built without permission will emerge, and these may
perhaps be the most dangerous of all."
      The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs preferred to reject the radioclub's
application. It was, however, not really possible to refuse the application of the State Industrial
School at Smíchov, because the technical purpose was indisputable.
By decree No. 332 of November 3, 1925, the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs assigned it the
call sign CA1 and stipulated that the transmitting antenna must not be higher than 30 m,
the power must not exceed 20 W,
and the wavelength must not be longer than 150 m. The officials of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs recalled
how they had once granted Professor Nušl a concession for a receiver at the Ondřejov
observatory, and they ordered the operator of station CA1, Professor Milinovský,
to announce every individual transmission to the ministry in advance. Rejected
were the applications submitted on January 11, 1926, by the West Bohemian Radioclub in Plzeň,
on January 31 by Hugo Nietsch of Krnov, and on March 18 by the East Bohemian Radioclub
in Hradec Králové. The latter wanted to build a one-tube 3–5 W transmitter
on a 150 m wave with absorption modulation, following the brochure Wie baue ich mir eine
Sendestation (Audionverlag Wien), for announcing members' meetings,
club news, and for holding lectures and entertainment and cultural
productions. Further rejected applicants were the official of the Czechoslovak Radiosvaz
Eng. Dr. Fr. Švadlena, the plumber Rudolf Fousek of Ústí nad Labem, the administrator
of the state meteorological station at Třebíč Richard Šplíchal, and the referent
for radiotelegraphy at the patent office in Prague, Eng. Rudolf Sieber.
The Ministry of National Defense, however, stated in all of these cases
that it had no objections and merely required that the applicants submit to a professional
examination.


---


THE OUTCRY OF DON POWER


---


At Ondřejov they had long since forgotten about the telegraph supervisors Bohatý
and Křečan. Years packed with events were passing; memories of the war came
alive only in the stories of old soldiers. So they were considerably surprised
at the observatory when, out of the blue, the Ondřejov post office asked them whether they had a transmitting
station.
     "An underground transmitting station," the postal official insisted. "Type or
mark RP 95."
     "Nonsense. What would we do with it? Experiments with underground
antennas are indeed being conducted, but not here."
     At the post office they carefully re-examined the correspondence card with the address:
"RP 95 (underground transmitting station), Ondřejov near Prague." The sender,
A. V. Bendler, Žižkov, Jagelonská 13, had written:
     "Dear Sirs,
     As you wished, I hereby inform you that on the 27th of this month at 18:20
I captured your transmission. Although I am about 1 km from Strašnice, I heard you very well between
the transmissions of Prague. I hope next time to tune Prague out completely. Congratulations
on your success. If you are interested, let me know
when you will transmit again; I will also try the crystal. I listened to you on a three-tube
set.
     The broadcast was very distinct.
      With all respect and radio greetings,
                                        A. V. Behdler."
     The Ondřejov post office stamped the card, marked it
"Addressee unknown," and returned it to Prague, where it reached the department of
undeliverable mail. There it was held and passed to the Ministry of Posts and
Telegraphs. However much Bendler fumed, he never received an answer.
     Almost simultaneously with Bendler's correspondence card there arrived
at the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs a report from the Ministry of the Interior that on November 26,
1926, around 7 p.m., a station had been captured identifying itself as "Underground
transmitting station RP 95 on a 680 m wave. Experimental transmission. Antenna
power 25 kW." Music from records and a reading from the newspapers followed the announcement.
     This strange station had already been operating for a whole year. On December 18, 1925,
listeners could hear this program:
     10:00 announcement
     10:10 The Peasant Cavalier
     10:17 To Our Friends
     10:20 Habanera
     10:30 In the Shady Arbor
     10:35 Pagliacci
     10:40 Cinguantaine
     10:50 reading — Kasch: The Automaton
     11:00 Poem
     11:05 Waltz Vanité
     11:07 I miss my Miss
            Pasadena
            Vačkář: Awakening
     In the first days of January 1926 it included less music and more
reading, and on a 44 m wave it relayed the Prague broadcast.
     On January 7, 1926, it broadcast the reading "The Outcry of Don Power" from 10 to 11 a.m.
At the end of March it could be heard on waves of 32 and 35 m.
     It worked mostly around noon and toward evening. Its programming included
lectures and feuilletons from Národní politika; it commented on events
with brief sarcastic, even biting remarks. Sometimes it identified itself:
     "Hello, Prague, Czechoslovakia 1RS."
Other times:
     "Underground station RP 95, Ondřejov near Prague."
The identification was repeated in German and in French.
     The short-wave transmissions escaped the wider public and the authorities; a listening
report was sent by the Belgian station BS2. Its operator, Monsieur Stanier
in Louvain, listened on December 13, 1925, at 17:30 GMT, on a wave of
43.5 m. He captured a foxtrot and music with singing. The transmission at 21:15 of the same
day was followed by another Belgian station, BQ7, at Anderlecht-Brussels, 50 Avenue de
Brouckère. This transmission — it was only modulation tests — took place
under the call sign CS1RS.
      When the station then appeared with regular programs on medium
waves, the laboratory of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs began to take an interest in it.
      The evening Czech Word of June 14, 1926, and Venkov of June 15, reported:
"An unknown station announces that parliament has been dissolved and there are
bloody battles in Prague."
      An order arrived at Kutná Hora, No. 1175/conf./IV Dept. of June 1, 1926,
that the direction-finding station of the 1st Telegraph Battalion "immediately begin the search
for an unknown transmitting station, which according to its announcement is at Ondřejov,
but it is not excluded that it will be nearer to Prague. It transmits on a wavelength
of 670 m; it has been heard between 12 and 2 p.m., in the evening, and also at night."
      The search was carried on in Prague as well. One direction finder was placed in the building of the Ministry of National Defense
in the ground floor at no. 20, the other in the Vršovice barracks. The duty was performed by staff sergeants
daily from June 17 to July 6, 1926, always from 5 to 11 p.m. and
longer. The search on the 670 m wave, however, brought no result. On short
waves the military listening stations could not receive anything
anyway, because they had nothing to receive with. In 1926 they received on waves from 300 m to
10,000 m: German military and police stations such as 7IB, LAU, LAN,
7DS, 7TC, 7ZS, the circuits fuchi, polchi, chigr, the German railway network
DZ5, ZBI, RU5, RM3, RM4, SW5, etc.; everything from Hungary including
broadcasting; and Soviet stations RAI on 7500 m, RDW on 4400 m, RET
on 7600 m, FAZ 3400 m, RAG 1900 m, even Orenburg RAM
on 5700 m. About the amateurs they knew nothing.
      Eng. Singer of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs had no goniometer. He did, however, own a field-strength
meter and a receiver with a frame antenna. It showed that the secret station might be
somewhere in the direction Řepy–Smíchov–Vršovice–Michle. Eng. Singer needed
one more bearing line. He tried it from the Žižkov post office. In vain — in a reinforced-concrete
building, direction finding is poor. He could not set up out on the street. He
studied a map of Prague. Some park? In the end Eng. Singer, Eng. Gruber,
and their assistants camped out on the Pankrác cemetery. As a test they
took a bearing on the Strašnice transmitter. In order. And when it sounded again: "Hello Prague,
Czechoslovakia, 1RS," they turned the frame antenna and plotted the found
direction. The two bearing lines intersected.
     "For good measure, let's try it once more from Záběhlice."
     The intersection became a triangle. The engineers set up their instruments at the castle
of the Ministry of Posts. The triangle became a quadrilateral. Its area
was larger than that of the triangle.
     "What now?"
     "Director Gesmacher is said to have been boasting at the trade fair that he has a transmitter
ready, just waiting to set it going."


---


h   • • • mm
     Motyčka used to go home for lunch. On December 3, 1926, he sat,
as usual, in the tram. St. Nicholas Day was coming, then Christmas; a rush at work, thoughts chasing through his head. On Bělehradská Street they
met a column of passenger automobiles. On two of the cars they noticed plates marked
N-Íl. Probably some government delegation.
     When he returned to the ETA factory after lunch, he found that it had been occupied by the police.
They let him inside. They let nobody out. It is a five-story building
with a hexagonal tower, behind the Vršovice railway station, at the corner of Kloboučnická
Street and what was later Bartoškova Street.
     The detectives divided up by floor. Police agent Tuček
ran up to the third. He stopped at a door bearing the sign LABORATORY. From inside
he heard a voice. Tuček listened at the door. He recognized a feuilleton from today's Národní
politika. He drew his pistol, released the safety, and burst in.
     In the laboratory there is a table, on it a receiver, next to the receiver a loudspeaker.
At the table sits a man of about thirty with dark, slicked-back hair,
a thin face and narrow, tight-set lips. On his wide-open eyes
he has spectacles. In one hand he holds a newspaper. With the other hand he hastily pulls banana plugs from the sockets. Tuček aims his pistol:
     "Sir, you have been transmitting. Immediately push those banana plugs back in the way they were."
     The man with spectacles and thick black hair is bewildered. He throws down the newspaper,
but obediently pushes the banana plugs back into the sockets. Eng.
Singer enters the door.
     "What do the gentlemen wish?" the raided man recovers himself.
     Eng. Singer grasped the situation in a flash. He leaned toward the loudspeaker:
     "The transmitting station has been discovered."
     "You may turn that off," he turns to the man in spectacles. The man does not know that the sentence
"The transmitting station has been discovered" has just been recorded in the laboratory of the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. And that this sentence will serve as evidence. Eng. Singer and Eng.
Gruber secure the instruments in the laboratory. In the director's office they find a
gramophone.
     "This is what you used for the broadcasts. And we recognize your voice too,"
they told Director Gesmacher.


---


mm • • • m
    A table covered to the floor with green cloth, upon it a cross with two
candles at its sides. On the table, a bundle of documents. The Senate of the Provincial Criminal
Court in Prague, under the presidency of Chief Councillor Nedvěd, puts the full stop
to the activities of the secret transmitting station 1RS, or rather RP95.
      The accused, thirty-seven-year-old J. Soukup, had worked at ETA since October 1,
1925, when he left Eng. Štěpánek and became a designer. He was employed
on prototypes of receiving stations. He defends himself by saying he had to conduct scientific
experiments, which could not be carried out without transmission. He tells the court that Czechoslovakia
is one of the few states that do not permit experiments with transmission. He points
to France, where a number of amateur transmitting stations are in operation. He takes everything
upon himself. He declares that neither Director Gesmacher nor anyone else knew of his experiments.
      On May 4, 1927, the court sentences Soukup to three months of hard
imprisonment, suspended for 2 years. Director Gesmacher is acquitted for lack
of evidence.
      The secret radio station made it into the pages of the daily press twice:
first when it was tracked down, second at the trial. The newspapers reacted variously.
      "Mr. Eng. Jan Soukup of Prague, presently in Chrudim, asks us to state
that he is not identical with the J. Soukup, designer of the ETA factory,
living in Nusle," wrote Národní politika on December 7, 1926, and commented:
"It is a mystery what the perpetrators wanted to achieve by this... Was it the naive contraption
of hot heads, or the deed of men who had not mentally outgrown their boyhood years?"
      "The very commission that carried out the seizure acknowledged the technical wit
of the designer, who for a long time knew how to deceive not only his surroundings
but also the guardians of the law," wrote Národní osvobození on December 14, 1926, and
continued: "Czechoslovak radio engineering is the youngest branch of Czechoslovak industry.
It is the most rewarding field of work, and it is therefore necessary to devote to it the greatest
care on all sides. The sanctity of the laws must not be disturbed. But on the other
hand one must leniently weigh the situation of a man who stands before the dilemma: obey the law and make no progress — or seek new sources of
progress and violate the strictness of the established laws."
      "Promote radio, perfect it, invent — and they'll jail you for it,"
the verdict was ironized by Právo lidu on May 5, 1927. "We somehow have a mandate
to remain on the last rung of progress, for what is an ordinary matter elsewhere is, with us, a matter
of paragraphs and prison."
      "Experiments or malice?" asked České
slovo on December 9, 1926, in the subtitle to the article "On the unmasking of the secret transmitting station in Prague."
      In the article "Hellóó, here 1RS" the same paper wrote on May 5, 1927: "First of
all, this is the first court case in which an accused sits on the defendants' bench
accused of secret transmission. Second, it concerns a secret transmitting station which the
laboratory of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, with the participation of the entire radio-amateur
public, hunted for a long time. And third, the radio amateurs themselves are using this trial
to air once again the question of transmitting stations
in principle. The radio amateurs take it very hard that transmitting stations are not
permitted here, whereas everywhere else in the world, they say, things are quite different."
     The police liquidated the "underground radio station RP95," but the transmitter
CS1RS they never did get. It was housed in a hut atop a tower with a twenty-five-meter
mast and an antenna at its top. Climbing this mast
was an acrobatic feat even for a man as skillful as Soukup.
The anode voltage of the final stage, 2,000 V, was supplied by a dynamo driven
by a motor. When the belt snapped (it happened twice on September 18, 1925:
at 10:30 p.m. and 11:35 p.m.), Soukup carefully recorded even such an event
in his diary. The transmitter was driven by a small exciter built into
the cabinet of an all-concert receiver. The police confiscated this exciter, and everyone marveled
that so small a transmitter had been so well heard.


---


Fifty years later I put to Mr. Soukup the question to which the daily
press had once tried in vain to find an answer: Why?
      It was in Prague, at Zelený pruh, in a villa on which three large egg-shaped
antenna insulators are plainly visible, running an antenna off somewhere into the garden,
a small quad antenna, a spiral antenna, and a parabolic reflector for very
short waves. From the street side there stands by the villa a monster welded from tubing,
not unlike an oil derrick, reaching up to the attic-room window.
Soukup has his laboratory here, in which until recently — up to his 76th year
(when he applied for a pension) — he repaired television sets and radios. That tower is
an ingenious elevator by which the instruments are carried up. The door from the corridor
into the laboratory is the gateway where the first and last
two decades of the twentieth century meet. On the walls are pinned station cards
from the years 1925 and 1926, photographs of station CS1RS, and period pictures. On the shelves
rest bulbous electron tubes with cylindrical anodes and shiny,
chromed bases, measuring instruments with old-fashioned knobs and micro-
scales, alongside a modern swept generator, an oscilloscope, and special instruments
for work on VHF. On the table lie unfinished printed circuit boards
with semiconductors and integrated circuits.
      Here is the control unit for a rotatable beam antenna. Soukup receives several
television programs, of which the most distant (though only by chance) were
Milan, Cardiff, and Barcelona. I stand face to face with the man from whose hands came
the first radio-amateur components ever available in Czechoslovakia.
      "I'm not really an amateur. I am a professional. The first professional
in Czechoslovakia. Štěpánek began supplying people with components, and I
manufactured them for him. That was my bread and butter."
     "Was it those articles, the ones you got hold of at the print shop, that led you to radio?"
      "Well, but mainly the first phonetic experiments from Eilvese and from the Eiffel Tower
in 1922."
      "And what about Petřín?"
      "Telegraphy never interested me, and I never learned Morse code.
I only wrote down dots and dashes and then deciphered them. The few contacts
I made were by voice."
      Soukup was a gifted and recognized designer. His five-tube
receiver with two neutralized high-frequency stages, the crystal receivers "David" and "Goliath,"
a seven-tube superheterodyne,
various amplifiers, a powerful loudspeaker, an electromagnetic pickup — reputedly the first
of its kind in Europe (he still has it) — amazed visitors to exhibitions and trade fairs
at the end of the twenties and the beginning of the thirties. "Unquestionably
our most advanced designer," Československý radiosvět assessed Soukup.
      He shared a technical interest with the transmitting amateurs. The substance of his
transmission experiments, however, was entirely different from an amateur's. He had the idea
of providing listeners with a program that would interest them. He kept
careful records of his broadcasts. He commented on daily events and newspaper readings
with peppery remarks. He denies that he ever transmitted alarming or inflammatory
information.
      "A newspaper canard. The editors wanted the papers to sell,
so they spiced up the reports about that little transmitter. If I had really broadcast anything
like that, it would certainly have come up at the trial."
      Broadcasting was at that time the object of sharp criticism from Dr. Baštýř
and the people under his influence. There was even talk of listeners returning
their licenses. ETA
manufactured radio receivers and could hardly want any such thing.
Director Gesmacher welcomed anything capable of stimulating the interest of
listeners, not to mention that he himself was an enthusiast of radio experiments.
And both of them were sure that no one would betray them.
      Station CS1RS (Radio Soukup), or rather RP95, was thus an action of its own
kind and cannot be counted among the transmitting amateurs.
      After leaving ETA, Soukup worked until 1929 for the Žáček company.
With Fechtner he published the monthly Radioslužba, which stood out not only for its attractive
and technically interesting content, but above all for its color graphic design,
reminiscent of certain American and Japanese magazines, and such as even after
so many years the average European radio-amateur magazines
have not attained. Within a year he fell out with Fechtner and stopped working
on Radioslužba. It rapidly declined and soon perished. In 1929 he established himself
in Vodičková Street, but by then he had missed the right moment. He did not survive
the predatory competition of the capital-strong firms. In 1930 he definitively settled in his
present home.
            GOODBYE ON THE AIR! (NA SHLEDANOU V ÉTERU!)


---


A group of sixteen- and seventeen-year-old lads tumbles down the staircase
of the house at no. 22 in Železná Street. They come out the doors into Havelská Street. Some
walk toward Old Town Square, others turn down Rytířská Street toward Můstek.
Classes have ended at the Bergmann Business School.
      "So long, guys."
      "Hi, Antenna."
      He was small of stature, quiet and modest. He had already come
to school with that nickname.
      "Why do they call you that?"
      "I have a radio at home. A crystal set with a frame antenna. I listen to
Kbely and Strašnice. I've even caught Paris."
      Lojzík Weirauch was the oldest in the class. He was 22. Among adults
an age difference of five or six years means nothing. But whether someone
is sixteen or twenty-two — that is already a difference. Lojzík was more serious. Even the teachers
looked at him a little differently. The class teacher Wertheimer, tall, with a goatee and a stiff collar, kept
a special eye on him. Weirauch was studying in Prague
for the second time already. In 1919 he had attended the goldsmiths' school. In Městec Králové he
had sung first tenor in the Hlahol choir and had acted with the local amateur society Klicpera.
He was also a member of the committee of the Sports Club, where he recorded
the course of matches and all the notable events. He played tennis.
      "Kbely, Paris — that must be interesting."
      "Come to our place. Come and have a look."
      "Gladly, Fando."
      Lojzík gazed at the Estates Theatre. In his mind he was still at school.
He hears the clatter of typewriters. A blond bob bends over one of them.
      "I can't today. I have no time."
      They said goodbye, and Weirauch set off.
      He did not long put off the visit to Fanda Richtr. Shortly afterward the two
set out together for the Lucerna, to a lecture with a radio demonstration.
      When he came home to Městec Králové for the weekend, he could not stand still.
He swerved right around the big advertising clock-face, along the fence,
and at the crossroads turned left. He walked a few dozen paces and stopped
before wide green gates. His friend the carpenter Plodr lived here.
The smell of wood and shavings hung about the place. He already had experience. With Eng. Ta-
chovský they had also built a radio and listened. On June 27, 1925, school ended.
Lojzík brought home a report card with honors.
      The decision was made. He submitted an application for a concession. On September 12, 1925, Alois
Weirauch was granted — the fifth in Městec — the concession certificate for an all-
concert three-tube receiving station and a prismatic free antenna
with six rays of 10 m length.
      Weirauch did not start with a crystal set. His stepfather bought him
an allconcert kit according to Eng. Štěpánek's instructions. The little house with the ornamental
gable, where the Weirauchs live, stands on the corner of the square. When
The Bartered Bride was broadcast, a whole crowd of people gathered in the open space by
the Weirauch family's clockmaking shop.
      In the evening Lojzík climbed into bed and wrapped himself in the quilts. The radio beside
him, he puts the headphones to his ears and hunts for stations. But now and then a hiss and crackle sounded in the headphones, drowning out not only the foreign stations but Prague as well.
      "What can it be, Láďa?" Plodr asks. "Eng. Tachovský doesn't have anything
like that."
      "How does it behave when you disconnect the antenna? Does it still crackle?"
      "No. It stops. But then it stops playing too."
      "That means it's coming from outside. But from where?"
      Near the Weirauchs' house stood a large transformer. Could that be it? They pulled
the allconcert out of its cabinet and observed it.
      "Those bare wires are evidently picking up interference. Perhaps it would help if
they were insulated."
      "Take the whole station apart and wire it again with insulated wire. Do you know
what a job that would be? And maybe it wouldn't work the same afterward..."
      No. They would not take the station apart. They brought sleeving, measured the length
of each connection, and cut it. Then each cut piece was carefully
slit lengthwise and slipped on, connection by connection. Fiddly work, but in the end
successful.
      Reception clear and clean... Glory. Weirauch was satisfied. After all, it had
not been for nothing. He is already looking forward to putting the headphones on in the evening.
      But no such luck. After a moment of clean reception the hiss and
crackle returned. The sleeving had not helped.
      It could not go on like this. Alois Weirauch had to get to the bottom of it.
He subscribed to the magazines Radioamatér and Čs. Radiorevue. He bought books.
He plunged into the study of physics, electrical engineering, and radio technology. He read
instructions as well as theoretical articles. He set about building a complicated reflex
receiver with several feedback loops. The tube performed more than
one function. Yet his first amateur-built set was succeeding. With all the greater
zest he threw himself into further study and further experiments. In the first issue of volume IV
of Radioamatér there appeared an article by Zdeněk Petr: Adapting the allconcert
for short waves. Petr wrote: "Amateur stations can already be received in our country
around 11 p.m., and in daytime on shorter waves." He listed a number of stations he
had managed to capture in Brno and proposed introducing a system of receiving
stations with assigned call signs and the possibility of sending station
cards. Lojzík had an allconcert. Zdeněk Petr's article decided the whole
life of Alois Weirauch.
      When it was finished, there came over him such a feeling of impatience as he
had never known. Carefully he turned the knobs. He searched for Pittsburgh. He drew in
the regeneration. Suddenly he heard an irregularly interrupted tone. He took
pencil and paper. He wrote down dashes and dots. In old magazines and calendars
