he looked up the Morse alphabet and began to decipher. From what he had read so far,
he knew that the captured text was being sent by some amateur station. But it made
no sense. The next day he sat — as every day — with a loupe at his eye at the clockmaker's
workbench. He was already looking forward to the evening. And everything was going
more or less his way.
      The jazz band of the Savoy hotel had stopped attracting him. Pittsburgh too. His interest
was entirely captivated by telegraphy. And he had his first success: out of the mixture of dots and dashes
he deciphered an address: R. Hoffmann, Mulhouse, Haut-Rhin. He switched off the receiver
and laid down the headphones. On a Městec Králové picture postcard he wrote in German:
"Dear Sir, tonight I captured the signals of your station. I heard
you well and I am glad of it."
      Fourteen days later the postman brought him a card bearing two stamps of the République
Française, 40 and 50 centimes. By the postmark it had been posted at the railway station post office
in Mulhouse, Haut-Rhin, on October 14, 1926, at 12:15. On the card was
printed, prominently, the call sign F8MUL, a description of the station — Hartley 120 W,
1700 V, 46 mA — and many other details that Weirauch did not understand.
Only three sentences, added in pen, were clear: Besten Dank für Ihre Karte.
Kennen Sie CSUN und CSYD tschechoslowakische Amateure? Hoffe
Sie auch einmal im Äther zu finden. ("Best thanks for your card. Do you know CSUN and CSYD, Czechoslovak amateurs? Hope to find you in the ether one day too.")
      Lojzík turns the card over, reads it again and again, and does not know what to make of it. He knows only
that the little card he holds in his hand conceals a mystery he must
decipher, one that will open a new world to him. The window of the parlor looks out on a triangular
courtyard overgrown with grass. Behind the wall a red roof peeps out.
There lives Břetislav Kimminich, perhaps the only person in Městec who
knows English. He translates some of the words — "your," "here," "strength,"
"receiver," "remark" — but the rest remains incomprehensible.
       On November 2, Weirauch captured the Austrian station ÖKE and
obtained the address: Erich Kohout, Sauerbrunn, Berggasse 200, Burgenland.
Another Městec Králové postcard. This time he was cleverer. He asked
for an explanation of the abbreviations QRK, QSL, QSC, QRB, QRH, CTPS, 1NPT, DX.
      Erich Kohout answered promptly. He explained in detail all
the secrets of station cards and amateur contacts. He was not, however, satisfied
with the report from Městec Králové. It lacked the details an amateur
needs. He sent him his own station card and underlined the abbreviation QRK three
times. With the letter he enclosed the Austrian magazine Radioamateur
from October 1926 with instructions for
a one-tube fifty-watt transmitter for waves from 20 to 60 m.
     "What do you think of it, Láďa?"
     Plodr did not know Morse code. He did not practice telegraphy. He was
a radio enthusiast, but an experiment with a transmitter would interest him.
     "In August, Radiorevue printed Pešek's instructions for a transmitter," Plodr leafs
through the magazines. "It's according to QST."
     They compare both schematics.
     "I'll make the cabinet for you. I'll turn the coil former out of hardwood
and boil it in paraffin. The variable capacitor, the rheostat, and the rest you'll have
to get hold of yourself."
     "We'll do it according to Pešek," Lojzík opines.
     The post brought more cards. ÖJI from Salzburg and ÖJZ from Julius Zwiring
of Vienna.
     Weirauch penetrates into the mysteries of amateur contacts. He already knows that it would not be enough
to write dots and dashes; he is learning to write letters. Something escapes him, but he takes in
a great deal. He looks up the stations he has heard on a map. He pays attention to information
about receivers and about the transmitters the stations work with. After a little
listening he has an overview of the weather in Europe and learns many interesting
things. He stands on the threshold of a new world. Will he manage to break into it?
     Československá Radiorevue printed Pešek's transmitter instructions with certain
misgivings. What would the censor say? Would there not be entanglements with the authorities? Nothing
happened. The article in three installments passed smoothly. Next, then, came the article
"Antennas and amateur transmission." The author? CSUN. In the introduction, printed in
no. 10/1926, the author accuses the Czechoslovak Radiosvaz of not pressing energetically enough
for the freeing of amateur transmission, and writes: "Since many Czechoslovak
amateurs yearn for transmission, we resolve to treat amateur
transmission systematically in Radiorevue... Everything we describe here flows from the practice
of the amateurs ÖAA, ÖHL, ÖWA, and the other Ö amateurs, who wrote up their experiences
in the excellent Viennese magazine Radiowelt, and from the insights of the Abyssinian
amateurs CSYD and CSUN."
     The initiated smile at the delicate irony.

m   • • • wmm


---


Weirauch and Plodr check all the connections once more. They connect
the antenna and the counterpoise. Everything is ready.
    "Go ahead, try it."
A second's hesitation. Both watch the little bulb intently as it lights up
with antenna current. Alois Weirauch lays his fingers on the telegraph key.
A light press.
    Crack.
    There was a flash in the transmitter, a bang sounded, and the lights went out at the Plodrs' house. Láďa
replaced the fuse, and Lojzík slipped out through the wide green gates with the little box
under his arm.
       At home he discovered a punctured capacitor. It had not withstood the 220 V from the mains with which the anode of the transmitting tube was fed (without rectification). The capacitor had
to be made from stanniol foil inserted between used photographic
glass plates. It held — but the bulb in the antenna circuit did not light. Why not, he wondered?
Weirauch was certain he had failed to observe one small thing: the grid resistor.
The prescribed value was between 5 and 10 kiloohms. Such a resistor he could not
get hold of. Surely it did not matter so much, when receivers used grid
resistors in the megaohm range?
       Every evening brought new, thrilling experiences. Weirauch already knew the
prefixes. He knew that B was Belgium, Ø D Denmark, N Holland, K Germany,
W Hungary, U the United States of America. He switched off the receiver and set down
the headphones. And as he happened to glance at them, an idea flashed
in his head. The winding has a resistance of 4,000 ohms. He had had every intention of leaving
everything and going to sleep. But now he could not resist. He found little coils from old discarded
headphones and assembled from them a resistor of 8 kiloohms. He connected the antenna and the
counterpoise, switched on the filament and the anode voltage. He hesitated a second, then pressed
the key and tuned. The bulb in the antenna lit up. The transmitter worked.
       Weirauch was tempted to make a call. Would anyone hear him? He put the
headphones on again. But he heard no one. And he could have used a few hours of sleep.
       During the day he invented a call sign for himself. As a Czechoslovak he must have
CS (that is the amateur prefix). The first letters he had ever learned were r and v.
And in the evening the call CQ CQ CQ DE
CSRV CSRV CSRV K spread from Městec Králové into space.
       Around Christmas there was no time for it. The New Year 1927 was welcomed by the theatrical
and singing society with a colorful and cheerful program.
       Three Sundays passed. Weirauch's sensitive fingers hold the telegraph key.
The bulb in the antenna lead lights up and fades in the rhythm of the Morse
characters. CQ CQ CQ DE CSRV CSRV CSRV — to all, to all, to all,
here am I, your new and unknown friend, will you hear me? Where are you,
CSUN and CSYD? It is already past midnight. Weirauch slowly turned the tuning
knob of the receiver.
      At that he flinched. Someone is calling him. Who could it be? CSUN or
CSYD? Lojzík's heart pounds, his breath stops. In a moment he will know.
The pencil follows the signals.
       CS1RV CS1RV CS1RV DE G6BR G6BR G6BR K
      England. Why, that is from the other end of Europe, and actually already across the sea.
While the Englishman repeats his call sign a second and a third time,
Weirauch grows gloomy. What is that numeral one? Surely those signals are not meant
for him? And so he rejoiced... At least he would listen in and find out whom
G6BR was actually going to work.
      CS1RV CS1RV CS1RV DE G6BR G6BR G6BR K
      "That's strange," Weirauch thinks. "But perhaps it is for me after all.
Maybe he copied my call sign wrongly."
      He switches on the transmitter. His hand is nowhere near as steady as when
he opens the bezel into which the ruby bearing is fitted. Trembling
fingers convulsively grip the head of the railway telegraph key. He taps out
a greeting. He informs the English amateur how he is hearing him. What tone
he has, what the strength and readability of his signals are. And that this is his first
contact.
      He switches to receive. CS1RV DE G6BR - R GM DR OM GLD TO
FIRST QSO QRK R5 QRN... good day, old man, everything received,
I am delighted with our first contact, your signals are excellently readable,
even though I have atmospheric interference here... Weirauch is beside himself. So it really was
      "I nearly fell off my chair," he recounted fifty years later, every smallest
detail of that unforgettable January evening of 1927.
It was on a 44.8 m wave — today we would say on a frequency of 6,696.3 kHz.
      Lojzík attempted no further contacts that night. He was living through a strange
mixture of feelings that permeated his whole being. He felt as if he were
breaking stones or splitting stove-wood. Yet at the same time he experienced an ecstasy, as if,
after a long and exhausting jolting ride, forcing his way through thickets and climbing,
he had reached the summit of a mountain and been intoxicated by the magnificent view over a wide
and far-reaching land. He felt he had just lived through something great, something significant,
something like a bird's first flight or a child's first steps. He would gladly have
woken his mother and his sister to tell them about it, to share his joy
with them.
      He switches off the instruments and grounds the antenna.
      The morning is wiser than the evening. Something occurs to Lojzík. He hurriedly leafs
through the first issue of Radiosvět. He had actually already glimpsed it, but in his excitement
had forgotten: CS1 is Bohemia, CS2 Moravia, CS3 Silesia, CS4 Slovakia.
And there was something else there: Motyčka's report about the WAC diploma — Worked All
Continents — for contacts with all parts of the world. Which of the Czechoslovak
amateurs will be first? Motyčka asks; Weirauch takes a red pencil
and emphatically underlines this question in Radiosvět.
      Before long the postman brought a card from G6BR, sent via the radioclub.
Weirauch takes it eagerly in his hands. But what is this now? The card was not
addressed to station CS1RV, but to station EC1RV. The date of the contact and everything
else, however, matched. The operator of station G6BR, G. H. Ramsden, was an
experienced ham. He had worked Australia, North America, and India. He knew
that at midnight of January 31 to February 1, 1927, the new arrangement of
amateur prefixes would come into force, containing the designation of the continent:
A Asia, E Europe, N North America, O Oceania, etc. So he had written the card
according to the new system. Weirauch learned this properly only from Motyčka's article
"Amateur nationality identifying prefixes" in the 2nd issue of Československý
radiosvět. In that issue we can also read a short report
about the contact of EC1RV with G6BR, and about the second contact of this station, namely with station
GC6NX, Glasgow, on a 44.8 m wave.
     Weirauch was proud of those few lines by which he was ranked
among those who were written about, who meant something. The April issue brought
Weirauch's article "A high-performance receiver for short waves" with construction
details and photographs. With this debut Alois Weirauch joined
Motyčka and the whole author collective of Československý radio-
svět.
     On May 22 he logged EURP, the station of the famous radio laboratory
of Nizhny Novgorod; on July 2, Eng. Pešek, EC1KX; on July 12, ECAA2;
and on August 13, 1927, the great day arrived. The station clock, checked
precisely by time signals, shows 18:42; the receiver knob was on the 290 mark.
Alois Weirauch pricks up his ears. He quickly tunes in, grabs the indelible
pencil he always keeps ready by the station log, and writes: EF8MUL.
F8MUL. That was Hoffmann of Mulhouse, after all.
     The French used a different telegraphic sending style than the other states.
Weirauch called in the French manner:
8MUL 8MUL 8MUL EF EC 1RV 1RV 1RV K
The answer came back at once: 1RV EC EF 8MUL - R GE UR SIGS QSB
AC STDI - HW? - ER E QRA MULHOUSE - 1RV EC EF MUL K.
Good evening, your signals are good, faultlessly readable, tone bad,
typical of transmitters with an anode fed by alternating current, but the signal
stable — the station here is Mulhouse — how do you hear me? Switching to
receive. Go ahead.
     Some Q-code abbreviations had a different meaning in 1927 than in
1980. QSB then meant: The tone of your transmitter is bad. Or
QRL: I am having trouble receiving; send twenty times . . . — . so I can adjust
my receiver.
     OM Hoffmann, so today we meet in the ether at last.
     ES FR EU T MICH OB — I am delighted, old pal, taps out EF8MUL.
Alois Weirauch was living through a triumph. One unanswered
question still remained: Do you know CSUN and CSYD?
     Weirauch did not like unresolved problems. He already knew a good deal, of course.
For example, that both of them lived in Moravia, not far from each other. That they
had worked not only Europe but North Africa as well, and that at the very beginning
of their activity a comic incident had befallen them. CSYD had called CQ for about 4 minutes.
Then he began combing the waves from 47 m downward. On the 37 m wave he caught
a poorly readable signal, barely making it through the interference, broken: CSY
CSYD YD D E ... U N ...
     "So you, little rascal, want to tease me?" he says to himself, thinking of the
address CSUN. (In 1984 he would probably have used a more pungent expression than in
1926.)
     ?UN ?UN ?UN DE CSYD CSYD CSYD - GE OM - STOP
THE SILLY JOKES AND ROUGH BEHAVIOR - YOU WON'T CATCH ME WITH
A LITTLE CARD - HW? - K
     The interference on the 37 m wave suddenly ceased, the signal strengthened, and CSYD
receives: CSYD DE NGUN - GE OM - QRK R7 SRI NO UNDER-
STAND - NIL - PSE RPT + K — CSYD, the Dutch station
NGUN is calling you, good evening, your signals are strong and well readable, unfortunately
I do not understand, nothing at all, please repeat, switching over.
     The astonished operator of station CSYD appreciated at that moment that NGUN
did not know Czech. It took him a great deal of work to explain everything, because station
NGUN wanted to know what it was all about. And he breathed freely only when the Dutchman telegraphed back:
R RR NOW ALL OK HI HI HI, which meant that everything was clear to him
and that he was heartily laughing.
     The contact with EC2YD did not come off for Weirauch until September 5, 1927, at 19:05,
on the 32 m wave. On September 12 at 17:35, EC2UN appeared. He tells
Weirauch about his contacts with North and South America. He draws
30 W, reduces to 10, and wants to know how it will sound in Městec Králové.
Weirauch did not answer.
     "I have interference here, I cannot hear, repeat, send everything twice,"
EC2UN requests. In the station log of Alois Weirauch there is a note at that
point:
     "(If only it were so. Hi. My tube howled.)"

Water roars
     in the lap of cloven rocks
     the wind whistles
     as if laughing at it all
     the rapids carry the canoe away. . .


---


A deep canyon. Spruces, pines, rock formations of bizarre shapes, bare or overgrown with lichen and moss. Shrubs and meter-high grass. Steep slopes, and the sky with tufts of whitish little clouds is mirrored in the water. Stones jut from the river: light ones, dark ones, the water leaps over them, swirls around them, spurts up, splashes, forms crests of white foam and hurries on between the rocky cliffs. Here one can breathe the scent of forest, water, flowers and a campfire. Here a man becomes a traveler, a backwoodsman, a sailor, a climber, an explorer and an adventurer. He returns tanned and leaves a piece of his heart behind.

Two students from the Žižkov secondary school are making their way down a winding little path. They are carrying a tent they borrowed at school. They will camp on the Napísky sandbanks. And in Luka pod Medníkem they have some higher interests. They come here often and remain faithful to the Sázava even after their school-leaving exams and after military service. Once, a copper wire fastened to a rope and stretched across the river catches Max's attention.

As a six-year-old boy he had been given an electric battery-powered motor. That was his favorite toy. The next was an electric bell. When he learned to read, he got hold of anything and everything about electrical engineering. He was fascinated by the magazine Nová epocha with a picture of a field radio station with a frame antenna, displayed in the shop window of Jerman's bookshop in Žižkov. In opticians' shop windows there used to be Leyden jars, electric anemometers, Geissler tubes, and at Očenášek's in Vodičkova street a complete set for wireless telegraphy including a coherer and a key serving as a telegraph key. He took the instructions "How to build your own little home power plant" quite literally. The tire was removed from the rear wheel of a bicycle, pedaling drove a dynamo, and an accumulator was charged. From it wires led to a table where he mounted screw-in terminals and set up a distribution of current. He visited engineer Štěpánek and built himself an "allconcert." Now he was looking at that wire, curious where it led. He came to a tent in which stood, on a table, a four-tube receiver, an "amplion" loudspeaker by Radiolavox, a large accumulator, batteries, three pairs of headphones, a milliampere meter, a shortwave receiver with tuning knobs on long spindles, and a transmitter. At the instruments, a young man with glasses.

"I'm Bollard," the newcomer introduced himself. "I'm interested in radio. I've read Duroquier, I built an allconcert. I did experiments with a frame antenna. It was wound with 2 mm wire on a 1.5 x 1.5 m square."

When the young man in the tent heard about the frame antenna wound from 2 mm wire, he smiled. "Schaferling," he said with a slight bow. "I study at the technical university. Don't you go to the radio club?"

"I know some of the gentlemen from the radio club. I bought tubes and some components from engineer Štěpánek. When I built that allconcert, it didn't make a sound. I carted it over to the Štěpáneks. Mr. Šimandl was there at the time. He looked over the whole apparatus and said it was in order. He switched it on and it played beautifully. The tubes were glowing. I was afraid of burning them out, so I kept them barely warm — that's why it didn't want to play. Then I hauled that allconcert all the way back from Malá Strana to Žižkov. But I was happy." Bollard looked at the instruments in the tent.

"I brought everything over by canoe. This is a transmitter for waves from 30 to 200 m, a Hartley single-oscillator with grid modulation. I key the anode circuit," Schaferling pointed to a small case 30 x 25 x 15 cm. "I've tried several antennas: 11 m, 33 m, 55 m, counterweights and grounding, but I've had the best experience with a vertical antenna. I started out as CSAA2. Now I have the call sign ECAA2."

Bollard liked the transmitter. He had always been fond of simple but effective constructions. ECAA2 seemed to read his thoughts:

"From here I've established contact with various European stations. The farthest is near Tromsø, a distance of about 2400 km. I maintain regular daily sessions with ECOK1 and EC1KX. We track the influence of daylight and weather on the propagation of short waves."

He handed Bollard a graph with two curves. One applied to a 45 m wave, the other to 136 m. On the X axis, time in hours; on the Y axis, received signal strength R1 to R9.

Bollard studied the graph with interest. No less did the carefully ruled notebook interest him, in which ECAA2 recorded not only his own connections but everything he picked up on short waves in his tent. He also logged the state of the weather. Back then, in 1927, the influence of the ionosphere on the propagation of short and medium waves was already known, but it was assumed that the troposphere also influenced propagation, and amateurs tried to contribute to clarifying this problem. It was a generation raised on listening to professional radiotelegraph operations. The official term "station" did not sound foreign to it. The fashion of the thirties and later years was for a radio listener to own a set that differed as little as possible in appearance from a sideboard, a chair, and other pieces of furniture. The ambition of the amateurs of the twenties, and of transmitting amateurs of the first half of the thirties, was to have a radio station and a laboratory. The term "radio station" also corresponded to the efforts toward refined operation of professional quality. The word "laboratory" appears often in the discussions and magazine articles of the time. Amateur laboratories had no ostentatious equipment. Yet they deserved the name, because the amateurs kept careful records of their experiments and observations, conducted purposeful and systematic experiments, and tried to get to the bottom of things.

The tent on the Sázava at Luka pod Medníkem was a laboratory too. Bollard received a new impulse. After returning to Prague he built a single-oscillator with coils made of copper tubes on glass rods. He tuned the antenna with a light bulb. And now, what call sign? Maximilián Bollard — EC1MB? No. That might be conspicuous. He'll take 1MC. No one will figure that out. And even after several decades had passed, he still recalled how that call sign, which he had just invented, came back to him out of his headphones.


---


The calm weekend atmosphere of Saturday, May 21, 1927, is stirred by the news that on Friday, May 20, at 12:50 Central European Time, Captain Lindbergh took off for a flight from New York to Europe. Nothing like it had yet been accomplished by anyone, and the tragic fate of the French airmen Nungesser and Coli, who had attempted the Atlantic crossing from Europe to America, was still very much alive in memory. Prague radio is just broadcasting Gollwell's evening with Karel Hruška, followed by the comedy "Robert and Marianna" with A. Sedláčková. Brno has a program of music and recitations on the theme of "A May Fairy Tale."

At 22:00 both stations simultaneously broadcast the ČTK news bulletins. Lindbergh had crossed over Newfoundland during the night. He had been sighted by the ship Empress of Scotland. The weather over the ocean was nothing to speak of. Brno signs off right after the news; Strašnice plays reproduced music until 22:45 and then ends.

In Paris at that time, crowds in the thousands are streaming toward the airfield, which police and army try in vain to hold back. All the lights on the field and on the hangars are lit. On Mont Valérien, the most powerful French searchlight, with a range of 300 km — switched on only in exceptional cases — has been activated. At 22:15 the airplane appears. After 33.5 hours of flight, Charles Lindbergh is seized by fear for the first time when he sees those two hundred thousand people at the airfield. He circles and lands with the last of his strength. When they pull him from the plane, they give him a caffeine injection and a refreshing drink. Jubilation, roaring, ovations thunder across the airfield. The roof of one building cannot bear it; it collapses and sweeps down everyone who is on it.

None of this is known in Czechoslovakia. The radio stations have signed off, and only tension and an unanswered question mark remain. Something like news bulletins every hour, or half hour, and even all night long — that was not known then. The next day is Sunday. Prague opens at 10:00 with agricultural programming. Brno begins half an hour earlier and has on its program a lecture, "Dangers to Man from Contact with Animals and Their Products." Since it is Sunday, the next news will not be broadcast until evening. No information will make it into the Sunday papers before the deadline. The telephone lines to Paris look just like the roads to Le Bourget airfield.

When Weirauch had listened to the end of the Strašnice program, he switches on his own station. At 23:05 he calls CQ DE EC1RV. At 23:12 he picks up F8CP, which calls him back a few minutes later:

1RVEC EF8CP - BSR OM - PSE HRD AND QSP THIS MSG — LINDBERGH EST ARRIVE AU BOURGET A 22R30 OK?

Alois Weirauch thereby becomes — if not the first, then one of the first — Czechoslovaks to learn so quickly of the successful completion of the first flight across the Atlantic Ocean.

TO THE NORTH POLE


---


In 1928 I was living in Újezd u Boskovic. It is a village situated high on a hill. There was no road leading there yet at the time, only field paths. There was no electricity either. On a single tube you could hear plenty of stations on headphones. Rome and Budapest broadcast Morse code courses. Rome in the evening, Budapest always on Wednesdays at half past two in the afternoon. I made a point of getting home from school in time so as not to miss that course. Telegraphy always attracted me more than telephony. Mainly because telegraph dispatches could be recorded more easily. I had coils that went up to 20,000 m. The long waves were crowded with stations back then. Reception was excellent: day and night always the same, reliable, without fading. Only in summer were there troubles with atmospheric disturbances.

On April 15 I learned that the airship Italia had taken off from Milan during the night on a flight to the North Pole, and at 8:10 it had flown over Ljubljana. The commander of the airship was General Umberto Nobile; a Czech, Dr. Běhounek, was also on board. Meteorological and magnetic observations were being made, along with measurements of atmospheric electricity and the intensity of radio signals. The takeoff had been kept strictly secret until the last moment.

At 9:50, squad leader Josef Janeček took over duty at the military listening station in Brno, conscientiously recording the intercepted correspondence. Petřín had previously had the direction finder E 276 sent from Trnava. Duty at the radio station was directed personally by staff captain engineer Konečný.

At 10:58 the Italia informed the Vienna airfield OHW: "Arriveremo Vienna alle 11 GMT." Prague OKL and Brno OKB called the Italia: QRV K. The airship's first contact with Brno took place at 12:28: OKB V ITALIA HR QRU DONNEZ BULLETIN K. ITALIA DE OKB ICI BULLETIN OBS 11 GMT 452 55646 and further number groups containing weather reports. Finally, in open speech: OBSERVATION AERODROME BRNO DE 1130 GMT COUVERT PLUIE HAUTEUR DES NUAGES 600/1000 METRES VISIBILITE 8 KM CALME — NOUS VOUS ECOUTONS BIEN DE 0840 HEURES GREENWICH A 900 METRES — PRIERE DONNEZ VOS POSITIONS ET DIRECTION. At 12:46 Brno urges:

ITALIA V OKB K.

The airship answers two minutes later:

OKB V ITALIA - QRK - NOTRE DIRECTION FOR STOLPA VVV NOTRE DIRECTION EST WIEN DIREKT STOLP + QRU SK

At 12:50 the Brno airfield asks: ITALIA DE OKB PASSEZ VOUS BRNO? The Italia does not respond. Brno calls it several times. It is not until 14:40 that it sounds: AEQ V ITALIA K ÖNNEN WIR DEN WEG WIEN BRÜNN BRESLAU STOLP NEHMEN ODER MÜSSEN WIR ÖSTLICHER FAHREN - NOBILE.

The military stations Petřín, with the traditional call sign PRG, and Brno, Královo Pole, PBR, constantly take bearings and exchange their findings. Brno radio is in telephone contact with the airfield, interrupting the concert every little while to inform its listeners.

The Italia flew over Vienna and headed toward Brno. From the map I could see that the route to Breslau leads over Újezd u Boskovic. I followed Brno radio and the OKB airfield, which was working on a 900 m wave. When our people couldn't get me to come to lunch, there was trouble. As soon as it was possible, I was sitting at the radio again. We had no loudspeaker. At 15:27 OKB sends a greeting telegram: ITALIA DE BRNO - 15/4 - AERONAVE ITALIA - BRNO CITTA DELLO SPIELBERG E COLONIA ITALIANA INVIENO FERDIV1 SALUTI AUGURALT - ZANIBONI - EVIVA L'ITALIA - ZDAR DRU BĚHOUNKOVI RÁDIO BRNO - PREGO DAVE SIETE ADESSO? K

Now Father had had enough. He took away my headphones and locked them up. "You don't seriously think an airship like that is going to fly right over Újezd?"

I disconnected the anode battery and the accumulator and grounded the antenna. Then the boys from our gang came: Tonda Odehnal (he was called "Baráčků"), Miloš, Frantík Deml. We ran down the hill, crossed the Svitava river at Mladkov, and around five in the afternoon we were on the meadows between Chrudichromy and Svitávka.

There we saw her. She emerged over Újezd and flew along the railway line toward Letovice and further north. A hundred-meter grey-blue colossus with three engines and a command cabin at the front. The name ITALIA was clearly visible. She seemed to be flying slowly, but in a short while she had vanished.

In the evening she got into a gale and storm over Kladsko. Lightning was flashing all around. The airship was filled with hydrogen. She had to keep low to avoid the storm clouds, but not too low, so as not to run into mountains. Visibility was miserable. In that maneuvering the Italia lost her bearings, and at 21:15 she asked the Prague airfield for a bearing. Brno PBR measured 360°, Prague PRG 124°. When these figures were plotted on the map, the nonsense came out that the Italia was 10 km south of Brno. No one on board took it seriously. At 23:42 a message was intercepted that the Italia was on her way to Stolp and would be over Opole in half an hour. Then she switched on her shortwave transmitter and corresponded with IDO (Roma, S. Paolo). PBR had tried to take bearings before that, but found no minimum. The Italia could be heard with equal strength around the full 360° circle. The only measurement the Italia had asked of us ended ingloriously. Nevertheless, the following telegram came from Stolp: "Royal military attaché, Italian embassy, Prague. Please convey warm thanks to the aerological and radiotelegraph service for the valuable assistance given me. General Nobile."

The military attaché, lieutenant colonel Mario Pennaroli, passed it to the Ministry of National Defense in letter no. 121 of April 17, 1928.

The Italia reached the North Pole on May 24, 1928, twenty minutes after midnight. Two hours later she set out on the return trip to her base, Kings Bay. They had a strong headwind and it was foggy. All day they saw nothing beneath them but pack ice and ice drift. They should have been at Kings Bay already, but the wind was carrying them off, so they didn't really know where they were. The bearings from the base ship Città di Milano, anchored at Kings Bay, were inaccurate. Thus they flew a day and a night.

On the 25th of May they still had the same picture below: fog, ice, snow, pack ice and ice drift. At half past ten the airship began to descend. The ground rushed toward them, the ice floes grew and took on menacing shapes. There was a bang, a terrible crash and the crackling of crushed wood and metal. The command cabin tore off and shattered. Lightened and now uncontrollable, the airship rose, flew away with several crew members, and vanished without a trace.

On the ice in the polar wasteland seven men remained, among them General Nobile, the radiotelegraph operator Biaggi, and Dr. Běhounek. A slow dying awaited them — death by hunger, cold, and exhaustion.

General Nobile was injured and unable to move. The others set about searching the wreckage scattered all around. They collected a little food, found a tent, a sextant, a mercury horizon and a chronometer, so they were able to determine their geographic position. Biaggi discovered a shortwave receiver and transmitter, an accumulator and an anode battery. The transmitter was a single-tube set, in a three-point circuit, with an input of about 25 W. Biaggi received press reports from IDO (the naval war station at San Paolo near Rome, known to amateurs from the long wave of 11,000 m), but he could not raise either 1GJ (Città di Milano, which also belonged to the Italian navy) or anyone else. On the 32 m wave he transmitted a distress message: SOS ITALIA NOBILE QTH FOYN CIRCA 80 38 NORTH 26 50 EAST.

A week passed, and a second one began. The elation that the radio worked had evaporated. The castaways were losing hope. The accumulators and the anode battery were weakening. The specter of polar death evoked feelings of desolation.

When Biaggi received the news from IDO on June 6, he rejoiced: "They've heard us!" Città di Milano? Rome? Kings Bay? Some other official station? No. An amateur.

His name was Schmidt and he lived in Arkhangelsk. On June 3, 1928, at 19:30, he caught fragments of signals, which he recorded thus: SOS ITALIA NOBILE FRANCESCO SOS TERRA TENGO EHH. From the incompletely received name of the island Foyn and the word "circa," he pieced together that the castaways were near Franz Josef Land. Schmidt had no transmitter. He had to wait until the post office opened the next morning. He sent a telegram to the Obščestvo druzej radio (Society of Friends of Radio), the forerunner of DOSAAF. From there the telegram went to the Committee for the Rescue of the Italia Castaways, which forwarded it to the Council of People's Commissars, and that body to the Italian government. That same day it was on the desk of the undersecretary of the navy, Siriani. The castaways learned of it from IDO, and joy and dread overcame them. Joy that they had been heard, and dread because Franz Josef Land is quite far from the site of the disaster. General Nobile immediately recognized his mistake and crossed out the words Foyn and circa. The ice was drifting about 4 miles a day. At last contact was established with IGJ. On the Città di Milano they did not want to believe it and asked for Biaggi's service number as verification. They received the answer: 80 30 N 28 E GIUSEPPE BIAGGI 86891. A rescue operation got under way, unique in history, which culminated on June 13, 1928, when the Soviet icebreaker Krasin arrived and took the castaways aboard.

When, in 1928 and 1929, ship stations worked with amateurs, they generally used their normal call signs, under which they corresponded in maritime service on the ship bands. In January 1929 the station OKAA2 (ex-ECAA2) had contact with WSQ, the steamship Lydonia, which had sailed from Florida, and with OGRA, the Danish ship Olaf near Gibraltar. On February 10 at 6:58, XEURCZ sounded — a Soviet icebreaker in the Baltic Sea. It probably did not take OKAA2's name down accurately. In the diary there is written something that looks like Tranvor or Trouvor. Aboard was the first radio officer of the Krasin, Jean G. Eckstein, who sent through OKAA2 a cordial greeting to Dr. Běhounek. Eckstein's transmitter on the Krasin was a two-hundred-fifty-watt Hartley. He had a 35 m antenna, a counterweight, and worked under the call sign XEUKS (when transmitting from home, that is, EUKS). His later call sign was U3AG.


---


THE FIRST WOMAN


---


The last time I was in Telč, it was a mild, pleasant evening. The square nearly empty, just a few small groups — evidently tourists who have dined at the Black Eagle and are taking a walk before bed. In contrast to the streets beyond the gates and the town moats, the square is lit only by the dim light of angular, period lanterns, which lend a special charm to the discreet corners of the arcades along the square and to the enchanting gables of the snug burgher houses. Some windows are lit; in others the bluish reflection of picture tubes flickers. This ancient town, surrounded by ponds and walls, must be dear to anyone who values the history of Czechoslovak radiotelegraphy and amateur radio. On the square with its Renaissance, Baroque and Empire houses with oriels, arcaded passages and artistic portals, there lived at no. 67 Láďa Vydra, CSYD, later CS2YD, EC2YD, OK2YD and OK2AG, and at no. 39 Zdeněk Neumann, CSUN, later CS2UN, EC2UN, OK2UN and finally OK2AC. They had been friends since early youth and were the first to acquire radio in Telč. They made their first radio contact on June 21, 1926, at 15:00, and soon their signals were known around the world, as witnessed by the F8MUL card to Weirauch from October 1926 and their report on their activity for 1926, published in Čs. radiosvět, issue 1 of 1927. They stand in the close wake of our first pioneers, and the level of their articles on technical and operating topics in Čs. radiosvět in 1927 attests that they had been following the field for a long time.

From the Neumanns' kitchen, a narrow staircase leads up to a little attic room with a window onto the garden. As late as September 1973 the original table still stood here, at which Zdeněk worked. On it he had a receiver — a regenerative Reinartz with two RF stages — an absorption and heterodyne wavemeter, a telegraph key and the station log. Above the table, on two massive, sturdy beams, a board was mounted carrying the transmitter and the antenna coupling element. Zdeněk was fond of demonstrating to visitors, by means of a "seismograph" in the form of two glasses of water, how the transmitter was secured against vibration and how important that was. The transmitter was a TPTG with a single TC 0.4/10 tube. Under the table stood a car battery as the source of filament voltage. The anode was fed from a 2 x 500 V eliminator. Its 50-kilohm bleeder resistor was permanently immersed in an oil bath.

Near the station, not far from the crossroads from which roads fan out to Stará Říše and Nová Říše, stood the power plant with two diesel generator sets, which supplied current only in the evening. During the day only on special occasions. Otherwise Telč was without power. The manager of the plant, Mr. Heřman, kept a little electrical shop there, where amateurs went to buy wire, insulators, batteries, screws and whatever was needed. The pleasant, quiet-spoken Mrs. Heřmanová died on October 15, 1928, at the age of 42. The Heřmans had two daughters: Anička and Jarča. When their mother died, Anička was twenty; Jarmila was a year younger. Zdeněk was 23, Láďa 24. They became friends. The Heřman sisters were cheerful and pleasant. Anička the more energetic, Jarča the persistent and tenacious, sometimes even stubborn. She had known the Morse code for a long time already. She had learned it in the girls' troop, and she could also signal with a flag. The young people gathered in the gym, scared each other in the secret passages beneath the church, played tennis, held academies and musical soirées, and rode in flower-decked farm wagons to Čaňkov, to Studená and elsewhere.

It vexed Jarmilka that Zdeněk was not much for social life. Whenever he had free time, he liked best to spend it in his little attic room. One day, when he noticed an interest in radio in Jarmilka, he offered: "Jarča, we'll work together."

A few small mementos remain: several sheets written by the hand of Dr. Zdeněk Neumann for Jarmilka. They contain brief technical characterizations of the frame antenna, the Poulsen arc, the properties of triodes, and operating abbreviations. Others are written in Jarmila Heřmanová's own hand. They are notes on high-frequency electrical engineering and on measurements, and records of training texts from practicing the Morse alphabet. Two little station logbooks have also survived, containing records from the period when Jarmilka transmitted illegally under the call sign OK2AJ, and from the time when she already held a license with the call sign OK2YL, along with several letters from Australia and from America, responses to the contacts she had made. Jarmila Heřmanová is the first woman ever to work at the key of a transmitting station in Czechoslovakia. She made contacts domestic, European and overseas.

Student loves are delicate and fragile. Sometimes little is needed — a small misunderstanding, a jolt from outside — the magic dissolves and there is no return. In the heart remain memories that both warm and ache, and that do not fade even after years. Jarča cried hard when they parted. The call sign OK2YL fell silent. Zdeněk was unhappy. For a long time he went to the Heřmans' and brought Jarmilka flowers. By then he already had a license and the call sign OK2AC, but he rarely reached for the key. He sought consolation in his work. The illusion vanished, and hard fate swept them both into the whirl of life. And to each of them fate behaved like a brute.


---


THEY'RE AFTER THEM!


---


The regime of the Hungarian regent Horthy regarded the revision of the Treaty of Trianon and the return of the "severed territories," above all Slovakia, as one of the chief goals of Hungarian policy, toward which it was necessary to work by all possible peaceful means and, at a suitable moment, by military ones as well. In Hungary, mechanical little maps of Greater Hungary were sold on which — at the press of a button — the territories of Slovakia, Transylvania and others were shown as its parts. There was constant tension on the Slovak-Hungarian border, and incidents occurred. Hungarian intelligence worked intensively against Czechoslovakia and — especially after Hitler's rise to power — developed close cooperation with German intelligence. It is natural that the Second Department of the General Staff of the Czechoslovak army also had to watch what was happening in Hungary. Private Slabihoudek, lance corporal Houška and other radiotelegraph operators monitored, in Lučenec — and from April 1928 in Prešov — Hungarian military and police transmitting stations, the Danube flotilla, Hungarian radio, and from 1928 also amateur stations. The Ministry of National Defense ordered them to eavesdrop on the radio Morse courses on Wednesday afternoons as well. What if some cryptogram were stitched in there?

Hungarian amateur stations used, from February 1, 1927 to the end of 1928, four-letter call signs with the prefix EW. In 1929 they switched to HAF with a number. From intercepted Hungarian amateur correspondence, the intelligence department of the General Staff learned also of the existence of Czechoslovak amateur transmitting stations, whose call signs the military training radio stations marked in red in their reports. From the content of the correspondence they tried to determine addresses. In Lučenec they caught a contact between ECAA2 and EWAA and noted that ECAA2 was located in Prague, even though it used the numeral 2.

On February 20, 1928, a contact between EWAA and EC1RV was recorded, with the note that EC1RV was in Bohemia. On July 15, Slabihoudek overheard a contact between EC1AB and EWKX. A message was passed about some engine for fire pumps from the firm Smekal, signed Bondy, Prag 18. From that day on, people could read in all the card files and reports that EC1AB was Bondy, Prague 18. The soldiers already knew that EC2ET was in Olomouc, EC1RO in Prague, and from a contact between ECOK1 and EWBF they learned that "QSL via Radioklub Československý, Prague 2, Slovanský ostrov, no. 5." This trail was already dangerous. Yet no one realized that QSL, in amateur jargon, means not so much confirmation of reception as the sending of a station card. They knew the radio club's address (Kuník was on its committee), but it did not occur to them to investigate in that direction.

Major Dr. Kuník prepared for the intelligence and operations departments of the General Staff a paper in which he states:

"... Almost daily it is possible to intercept messages exchanged in agreed language between Hungarian and Czechoslovak stations. The intercepted Czechoslovak amateur stations are secret stations, which it would be necessary to identify very soon, especially on account of their lively contact with Hungarian radio stations..." On March 5, 1928, a meeting was held in the office of the head of Department XI at the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs at 36 Holečkova street, attended by the MPT (Dr. Kučera), the Ministry of the Interior (Dr. Novák), and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Plechatý), at which Dr. Kuník announced that the listening service had intercepted the signals of thirty secret Czechoslovak amateur transmitting stations, in particular:
EC1: BZ, FM, OB, RF, RO, RU, UZ, RV,
EC2: YD, ECAA2,
EC4: ABR, AJ, AV, CL, QH, QM, ID, and ECK4AF and ECK41N.
At this meeting the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs raised the question of licensing amateur transmitting stations. The representative of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, who knew the resolutions of the Washington international radiotelegraph conference and had an overview of the situation in other states, was in favor. Dr. Novák was against, arguing vigorously about the threat to internal and possibly external state security. Major Kuník sided with him. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs specified its position in a letter of May 25, 1928, ref. no. 72370/28/UI-G, addressed to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, in which it writes among other things: "... It is known that the establishment of transmitting stations is permitted in the United States, in the USSR, in Hungary, and a fairly liberal practice is established in a whole series of other states. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs is of the opinion that there is no reason for proceedings in Czechoslovakia to be stricter than in other states... By refusing licenses for transmitting stations, those who are driven by whatever interest to establish them cannot be deterred. Even severe punishments will not accomplish much here — quite the opposite. Besides, in most cases it is a matter either of scientific interest or of interest of a sporting character. It is not sufficiently logical for radio amateurs in the Czechoslovak Republic to be punished for what is permitted, indeed encouraged, in other states." A copy of this letter was sent to the Ministry of the Interior.

The Interior Ministry took Major Kuník's information into a decree of August 14, 1928, ref. no. 14001/28, which it sent to the provincial president in Prague and in Uzhhorod, to the presidium of the provincial offices in Brno and Bratislava, and for information to the MPT and MND. The decree was supplemented by a citation of the articles "Czechoslovak amateur stations heard on the ocean" (Čs. radiosvět 3/III, p. 84) and "Czechoslovak amateurs" (Čs. radiosvět 3/II, p. 99), and ended with the conclusion:

"Since in the Czechoslovak Republic — as is known here — no amateur transmitting stations have yet been licensed, all these stations exist without authorization and their owners commit an offense under §§ 18 and 19 of Act 60/1923 Coll. The Ministry of the Interior requests that this matter be closely watched and further action taken according to the results."

The wheels of the police search apparatus began turning at full speed. Uniformed and plainclothes members of the security forces and their informers comb the card files, prowl over rooftops, ask questions. Reports from gendarme stations, police commissariats and branch offices converge. They manage to track down over a hundred "black" listeners who do not pay the radio license fee, and criminal complaints are filed against them. There are fines and prison sentences from it. But they have no shortwave receivers and do not understand — apart from such exceptions as the police inspector Rousal, known from the house search at engineer Štěpánek's, who had served at the radio during his military service — the technical side of the matter. The search drags on for over a year.

Amateur radio meanwhile kept developing. The stations moved from the EC prefix to OK. In the 80, 40 and 20 m bands there appeared, besides OKAA2, OK1OK, OK2AJ, OK2UN, OK2YD, OK1RV, more: OK1AB Mézl, 1BZ Bozděch, 1FM Vološín, 1OB Roubík, 1RF Červený, 1RO Buchar, 1UZ Hron, 4AV Výborný, 1RB Erbs, 1YR Rokos, 1AZ Štětina, 3SK Plisch, 1MX Paulík, 2CM Škop, 2LO Chlup, 2PA Pátek, 2AL Švejna, the old familiar OK1MC and OK1KX, and 1PH, 3NZ, 1MK, 1UG, 2XO and IRU, whose identity we have not verified. The first Czechoslovak to earn the WAC diploma was Ladislav Vydra, EC2YD. His WAC was dated January 2, 1929; it was awarded for contacts made in 1927 and 1928. The second in Czechoslovakia and the first in Bohemia was Weirauch, EC1RV.


---


At gatherings of old-timers, the talk now and then turned to the days of the "blackout." "Did they know about us or not?" "They knew nothing. Because if they had known, they'd have picked us all off, just as they did whenever they managed to track someone down."

"That's not true," others objected. "They knew about us well enough, but they let us be so we could prepare ourselves and apply for licenses. They only picked on those they had some reasons against."

Engineer Srdínko, OK1SV, once recalled in Olomouc the examination he took on June 28, 1936 together with engineer Lhoták, OK1LH, and Ota Lowenbach, OK1YB.

Lhoták excelled. Morse, theory, regulations — everything went splendidly. Finally Dr. Burda said:

"Now you'll demonstrate a sample contact. Here, Mr. secretary Špinka will be station OK1AA, and you — let's say... D4LHO."

Lhoták went white as a wall, stared wide-eyed, and gaped at Dr. Burda. Then he wiped the sweat from his forehead.

"Mr. councilor, must it be?"

"Of course it must. And what doesn't seem right to you? You've been transmitting under that call sign for a year already, and now suddenly it should give you trouble?"

After returning from Olomouc I searched the station log from 1934. I really did find the call sign D4LHO in it. Then I tracked down engineer Lhoták and began asking. He remembered the examination and its course well — like every amateur of that era. It was such an experience that even the old gentlemen retain almost every detail in living memory. But engineer Lhoták could not recall the incident with Dr. Burda at all. In the protocols it is recorded that Bollard, OK1MC, demonstrated a contact between station OKX4 and IRD2. Brož, OK1GC, "made" G2AS.

But with Lhoták there is no record of any examination contact. So this episode perhaps belongs to the realm of legend.

The pioneering role of the Czechoslovak transmitting amateurs is an irrefutable historical fact. Motyčka was not only the first in Czechoslovakia to receive transoceanic stations on short waves, but also the first to transmit on short waves and to work with foreign countries — indeed with America and New Zealand. Other amateurs who followed Motyčka corresponded on short waves earlier than the post office and earlier than the army.

The military maintained their contacts on medium and long waves. It was similar in the neighboring states, and so the military listening service also devoted itself to medium and long waves. It began receiving on short waves only in 1928. In the military telegraph workshops at Kbely they knew a lot and tried many things. But they had their hands full with current requirements and tasks, and confined themselves to laboratory experiments.

The firm J. Wirfel Co, Prague, Na Florenci 13, offered the Ministry of National Defense a shortwave transmitter of foreign provenance on September 2, 1925. The offer was not accepted. Regular shortwave operation was introduced in our army only at the beginning of the thirties.

The results of the nationwide search for amateur transmitting stations converged at the Ministry of the Interior from the end of 1928 into the beginning of 1929.

"'Carefully conducted investigations and searches for the so-called secret radio stations in this administrative district have so far had no positive result. This investigation, of course, could be carried out by this office only by means of police and gendarmerie organs, without the use of suitable technical aids from the field of radiotelegraphy,' reports the presidium of the Provincial Office in Brno."

From Bohemia, from Slovakia and from Subcarpathian Ruthenia, negative results arrive as well.

"'Detection of the said stations is absolutely impossible without suitable receiving apparatus and personnel trained for that purpose, and to the tracking down of an unauthorized secret transmitting radio station only chance or excessive carelessness of the owner could lead,' writes Dr. Minarčík on behalf of the Provincial President in Bratislava."

"'Such investigations can be conducted only with an instrument called a radiogoniometer,' reports the presidium of the Provincial Office in Prague, 'on behalf of provincial president Šorm.'"

The presidium of the Provincial Office in Bratislava also writes about direction finders:

"'Goniometric service has so far been performed only by the military administration, and even that, they say, only with instruments that are not exactly perfect.'"

And how do matters really stand with those military direction finders?

A few were left over from the stocks of the Austro-Hungarian army, and we had some from the French. In 1921 the Vienna firm Mindagg delivered 21 goniometers to our military administration, and in 1925 two Seibt goniometers were purchased. In the mid-twenties, the standard equipment of the listening, so-called training, radio stations included the E 266 receiver, the Swedish 10 U FJI receiver, and the Telefunken E-276 direction finder. Not one of these instruments was adapted for shortwave reception. The first mention of a shortwave direction finder comes from the Czechoslovak military attaché in France, General Klecanda, to whom the Société d'entreprises électro-techniques, on April 14, 1926, offered the product of the firm Guy du Bourg de Bozes. It had ranges of 22–100 m, 90–900 m and 500–3100 m. The frame was switched by a two-pin plug. Short waves were received on two tubes, medium on three, and long on four. In 1928 the Czechoslovak seven-tube listening receiver (not a direction finder) PO 8 was being introduced, with A410 and B406 triodes, an intermediate frequency of 43 kHz, and a frame antenna covering 45 to 3200 m in six bands. It was a product of the military telegraph workshops at Kbely. The Kloudný RP 21 goniometer, pattern 35, with a shortwave range from 20 m down, did not come out of the military telegraph workshops until several years later.

The case of the Italia airship shows that direction finding was not easy. With shortwave goniometers, the highest attainable measurement accuracy was estimated at ±10°. Good results were obtained with strong medium- and long-wave transmitters. Amateur stations, which operated with a tiny power of a few watts, were audible on an outdoor antenna, but rarely on a frame antenna.

Soukup was tracked down because he also transmitted on medium waves. A bearing roughly determined the area where the transmitter was probably located. Decisive, then, was that Soukup was already known to the ministry as an applicant for a transmitting station license (refused May 24, 1926), and certain imprudent remarks of director Gesmacher had reached the ears of the MPT.

The most valuable source of information in the hunt for the "blacks" was the content of the contacts. Unlicensed stations did not give the operator's name, not even on QSL cards, and often did not state their location either. Nevertheless it was known about station OK1VP that it was in Mladá Boleslav, and OK1PH in Humpolec. When Colonel Skála, OK2VA, set out, the listening service correctly determined that he was in Olomouc, but for some reason now unknown it recorded the name — Colonel Loutník. The intelligence department of the General Staff knew that no Colonel Loutník existed in Olomouc. Colonel Koutník was put on the rack. He of course knew nothing about it, and station OK2VA kept transmitting calmly.

About station OK1OK they noted "Prague II. 531." The Prague was right, but Motyčka never lived at number 531. That was the number of the post office box to which the QSL cards were delivered.

The listening stations sent to Prague not only records of intercepted dispatches but also weekly surveys and evaluations of the material obtained. In all the paperwork, with absolute regularity, it is stated that nothing objectionable was found in the correspondence of amateur stations, whether Czechoslovak or foreign. In the safes of the intelligence department, data accumulates which — given the deployment of an agent network — could already yield positive results. But there are other tasks, more urgent for the defense and security of the state.

Nevertheless, amateur transmission is illegal, and something will have to be done about it.


---


AT THE CLOSE OF THE TWENTIES


---


Was the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs really so backward and obscurantist that it would take the suppression of amateur radio as its goal and refuse to permit it?

§ 9, paragraph f) of Government Regulation no. 82/1925 of the Collection of Laws established the conditions under which licenses for transmitting stations could be granted. The regulation on examinations was to be issued by the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs.

The first draft was prepared by Dr. Burda on May 18, 1926, and the second on July 8, 1926. The applicant must study:

1. the chapters on electricity and magnetism from Professor Novák's Physics textbook,
2. the entire domestic literature of the fields of radiotelegraphy and radiotelephony,
3. the ITU regulations.

He must be able to transmit and receive at a speed of 10 words per minute.

What idyllic times those must have been, when it was still assumed that someone could study the entire domestic literature of the field!! The multiplying magazines and books soon made point 2 doubtful, so in later versions of the draft examination regulations it no longer appeared, and together with points 1 and 3 it was replaced by a syllabus that was changed and supplemented several times.

On April 18, 1926, CSAA2 submitted his license application. Alone among all the applicants he read the government regulation thoroughly, and in its spirit emphasized (absolutely truthfully) the scientific side of amateur experimentation (in those days it was polite to write applications in the third person):

"Wishing to devote himself, as his lifelong profession, exclusively to radio as an electrical engineer, he is now studying with success at the Czech Technical University in Prague, in the field of electrical engineering.

By serious experiments in the field of radio he has been engaged for several years during his studies, as he can duly prove, among other things, by the proper detailed and systematic records of receptions of many foreign and overseas amateur transmitting stations.

Among other things he has worked chiefly in the theory of shortwave propagation. In that field he supplied, at their request — as he can prove — similar reports on their audibility, conditions, etc., to, for example, the well-known French station F8YNB on 45 m, the English G5MO (N. G. Dixon), and to Italian and Dutch stations. In doing so, the said parties expressly wished for similar experiments also on his part, so that they might establish direct contact with Czechoslovakia as with amateurs of other states; he was, of course, obliged to refer them to a later time.

The aim of the applicant is therefore to establish a proper and fully equipped station, which would be capable of similar experiments, eventually even with overseas countries, which would benefit — apart from the matter itself — assuredly also Czechoslovakia in that scientific contact with proper foreign amateur stations."

The MPT issued a refusal on account of the applicant's minority, and recommended that he do experiments at the transmitting station of the technical university. Schaferling attained his majority on November 7, 1926. Right after the New Year, on January 20, 1927, he submitted a new application. The ministry wanted to await the results of the international radiotelegraph conference in Washington, which was to convene in the autumn. Engineer Steinbach of Department XIX of the MPT noted on the file:

"Transmitting stations have not so far been licensed even to greater experts, for example engineer Liewehr, the patent office expert."

(That one, however, was cheerfully transmitting illegally as EC2PI.)

The Washington conference, from October 4 to November 26, 1927, was attended by Dr. Kučera and engineer Strnad. It was the first session since the London conference (1912). It established frequency bands for the individual services. With this conference the era of wavelengths ends and the era of frequencies begins, given in cycles, kilocycles or megacycles per second — later in Hz, kHz and MHz. The amateur service received international recognition and was incorporated into the official documents of the conference. By signing these documents, the Czechoslovak Republic obligated itself to respect the status and rights of transmitting amateurs.

"It is a question whether, in Schaferling's case as well, we should hold to the delaying and refusing position," opined Dr. Kučera, still before departing for Washington. "The application is recommended by Professor Šimek of the technical university, and abroad transmitting stations are being licensed."

"Here we are dealing with a station that is rather amateur than for scientific purposes," engineer Steinbach of Department XIX stood his ground. "If we permit this one, many similar applications will come, and we will no longer be able to refuse them."

OKAA2 would not give up.

"Most Illustrious Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs in Prague!

The undersigned, with respect, submitted on April 27, 1926 an application for a license for a shortwave transmitting station, which was refused him at that time by decision ref. no. 23571/XI-1926 of May 11, 1926," he begins his urgent appeal of January 20, 1927, announces that he is now of age, and states among other things:

"I permit myself to underline only that the school transmitter mentioned in the refusal does not at all serve those purposes, for it is set up only for longer waves, and besides, it is not in operation now either."

Dr. Kučera prepared a five-and-a-half-page analysis of this case from the standpoint of the resolutions of the Washington conference, which already contained all the important principles of the future licensing conditions, and arrived at the conclusion:

"Once the prerequisites still lacking are fulfilled — that is, the consent of the Ministry of the Interior and of National Defense, and a favorable result of the examination in radio engineering — there would be, from the standpoint of domestic regulation, no objections to the granting of the license. Nor do any international provisions prevent us from granting the applicant a license for an experimental transmitting radio station."

OKAA2 will not rest: "Since the Washington conference has turned out favorably, the undersigned again asks for the kind early permission of the said station..." he writes to the ministry on January 16, 1928.

On February 13, 1928, engineer Steinbach tries once more to torpedo the matter:

"It is evident from the wiring diagrams that this is a radiotelephone station. Such stations have so far in principle not been licensed to radio amateurs."

The next record is from February 22, 1928, by engineer Singer:

"Dept. XI has sent here new diagrams, which the applicant procured in a short time. These diagrams represent the schematic of a radiotelegraph station. We point out, however, that this wiring can easily be converted into a radiotelephone station. For that reason we recommend adding to the license certificate a rider that the applicant may use his station only for telegraphy, not for telephony."

And on April 26, 1928, the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs sent the Interior and National Defense ministries a weighty letter, ref. no. 25142/XI-1928, personally signed by Minister Dr. Fr. Jan Nep. Nosek, which says:

"The Ministry of Foreign Affairs' position — that at least on a limited scale and with the strictest selection of licensees, the granting of licenses for experimental (radio amateur) transmitting stations should begin now — is shared also by the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs, which has no departmental reasons whatsoever that would speak for the further refusal of these licenses and the rejection of applications for their granting.

The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs intends to lay this matter before the Council of Ministers, should the Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of National Defense persist in the refusing position expressed at the meeting of March 5, 1928."


---


Associate professor Stanislav Haderka, ex OK2HM, has a plane ticket in his pocket. Before he knows it, his plane will be landing in Cairo again. He lectures there at the university. Yet he still finds time to come on August 9, 1973 to the Svazarm in Brno at Na baštách, where Ota Halaš, OK2BRR, and engineer Ruda Burian, OK2PAT, had invited several old friends. Docent Haderka returns to his earliest youth:

"My first receiver was with a crystal detector. I got galena from engineer Jindra of the MPT. I went to meet him at the station and he gave me the galena — in the shape of a little cube. It was sensitive and worked well. The coil was massive, of large diameter, wound with enameled wire, plenty of turns. The cat's whisker, made, as they say, on the knee, was the troublemaker. I had nothing but a round brass rod, and that did no good. Only when I managed to get hold of a square rod in Olomouc did things work. The first thing I caught was the longwave station Nauen.

We lived in Mezice, post office Náklo near Olomouc. I went to school in Litovel, and I used to worry whether someone might have discovered in the meantime that I had an antenna stretched in the attic.

As a boy I saved up for a long time so I could buy a vacuum tube. In the end I bought it at Krejčí's in Olomouc. It had a metal base. I carried it like a holy relic the whole way. It was wrapped in that corrugated paper. Already home, in the village, I was crossing the little footbridge, lost my attention for a moment, the tube fell out of my hands, base-first, straight onto a stone — and that was the end of it. It was such a tragedy that to this day I remember exactly the spot where it happened to me."


---


Ing. Burian first heard about the radio as a seventh-grader from Professor
Štech at the secondary school in Telč. Štech had built himself a receiver
and demonstrated it to the students in physics class. Ruda then helped
the school janitor with the cleaning, so that he could go and look
at that radio with him in the science room.
      Then he set out for the mines near Třešť, where silver had once been
mined. With a hammer he broke up rocks and extracted galena (lead glance).
He made his contact spiral from a silver-plated string, and a rotating
piano wire also served him as an antenna.
      Mirek Kott, at not quite ten years of age, learned about the experimental
broadcasting at Kbely. Into the world of marbles and games with the boys
in Škroupova Street in Prague (now Havanská), of childish games and the first
life problems that elementary school brings, a new element penetrated:
radio technology. Among the fairy tales and stories for children there
appeared instructions for building a crystal set. Mirek got hold of some
enamel wire somewhere. He wound it on a little roller and scraped off
the insulation at the top. In his father's things he found a square rod
and a piece of sheet brass that suited a sliding contact. With that his
possibilities were exhausted. His father and an acquaintance stretched out
the antenna for him. The crystal and the earphones were bought by the whole
family. And in the end it played. But why, actually? That childish
"why," which knows so well how to drive parents into a corner, now sat
in Mirek's head.
      Such a sizeable coil of many turns had a large self-capacitance,
and its inductance together with this capacitance formed a tuned circuit.
Later versions of crystal receivers had variometers, and as the demands
for selectivity rose, inductively coupled tuned circuits with variable
capacitors were introduced.
      Not only students, but most of the population did not have so much money
that they could buy expensive apparatuses costing several thousand. Crystal
sets, a crystal set with an amplifier, and one- and two-tube sets represented
a technical revolution that made possible the expansion of broadcasting
in the late twenties and early thirties. Faulkner's little booklet "Radiotelephone
for 50 Kč," published in 1925 in the Library of New Ideas in Uhlířské
Janovice, reached five editions within a quarter of a year. The boom
in simple and cheap receivers was also good in that it indirectly exerted
pressure on the prices of the more complicated and still expensive sets.
      The number of broadcasting stations grows and their power increases.
The trend in receiver development heads toward greater sensitivity
and selectivity, and thus toward increasing the number of inductively
coupled circuits and especially high-frequency amplifier stages. These
oscillate easily under the influence of the anode-grid capacitance,
which therefore has to be neutralized. There arise neutrodynes (receivers
with straight amplification), and competing with them the superheterodynes,
which divide into two groups: tropadynes (oscillator and mixer in one
system of a single tube) and ultradynes (oscillator and mixer separate).
The mutual influence of the wiring and even of the components, or the
influence of the hand during tuning, begins to be cured by shielding.
      In Brno Motyčka no longer had time to serve as honorary secretary
of the radioclub, and it was no use even when the writing of letters
was entrusted to the typists of Dr. Linhart and to the Red Cross.
The Czechoslovak Radioclub was forced to hire a paid secretary. Staff
Captain Schneider became one. After some time he quarreled with
Dr. Baštýř and resigned the post. As his replacement Heindl brought
in a chemistry student, Karel Pešek, EC1KX.
      Motyčka did not want to transmit illegally. The radioclub had not even
been established yet when, on February 27, 1924, Motyčka set out for the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs to see Dr. Kučera and Eng. Strnad,
to ask them to permit amateur transmission. He was received kindly
and kindly shown the door.
      At the founding meeting of the Czechoslovak Radio Union on April 12
and 13, 1925, Motyčka in his speech vehemently demanded the liberation
of amateur transmission. His address was enthusiastically received by all
who knew what was at stake; indifferently by those whose interests were
limited to listening to broadcasting; and there were even some who received
Motyčka's arguments with hostility.
      The commentary of editor Richard in Národní listy came out like this:
      "Mr. Motyčka's rabble-rousing speech interested no one, and this whole
toy business with transmitting will shortly be banned abroad as well,
unless the amateurs themselves recognize the growing interference,
which will make broadcasting impossible too."
      The rejection of the Czechoslovak Radioclub's application for a transmitter
permit strengthened the negative attitude of the conservative officials.
      When the Board of Directors of Radiojournal was being put together,
Dr. Baštýř wanted to get onto it by hook or by crook. This plan failed
despite his great social connections, and Dr. Baštýř marched off to war
against broadcasting and against the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs.
In Čs. radiorevue he bombarded both institutions in his column "60 Million
Kč" with sharp and unscrupulous criticism, whose radical tone found
a positive as well as a negative echo in the radioclubs throughout
the state.
      Amateur transmitters originally built their organizational base around
the shortwave section of the Czechoslovak radioclub. Station cards arrived
at the radioclub through post office box 521. Pešek sent them out at
the radioclub's expense as commercial papers. He got into a conflict
with Baštýř, who accused him of financially damaging the radioclub.
(He is supposed to have said that "those transmitters are idiots and will
bring the club into trouble.") With that, Pešek's function ended.
      Eng. Pešek gathered around himself young men such as Eng. Bísek, Rokos,
and Červený (Soukup joined them too), who founded, in November 1928
at a meeting in a Smíchov brewery, the Association of Czechoslovak
Shortwave Experimenters (SKEČ), led in a spirit of opposition to the
old gentlemen of the radioclub and striving for good relations with the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs. Its chairman became Eng. J. Bísek.
      The radioclub issued a declaration that it was "an enterprise founded
for effect and for the glory of a vain employee who had been expelled
from the club." With the help of Motyčka, who remained loyal, the shortwave
section of the Radioclub of Czechoslovakia was founded. In the spring
of 1929 it was transformed into the independent society of Czechoslovak
Shortwave Amateurs (KVAČ), affiliated with the Radioclub. Its
establishment was approved by the Provincial Office in Prague on May 22,
1929. Doc. Dr. Šafránek was elected chairman.
      A radio contact between amateur stations ends with the exchange of
information, greetings, and the sign SK, which means the end of the
contact.
      The mutual relationship between amateurs, however, endures. An amateur
designs his station himself, builds it, operates it. He puts into it
time (usually all his free time) and money (his entire allowance),
his skill, his talent, his knowledge, his personality. He shares all
of that with his partners. Common interests and contacts, special in
form and content, different from the stereotype of everyday convention,
give rise to friendships, comradeship, and ethical values such as would
hardly be found elsewhere. Whenever I discovered that there existed
amateurs who hated each other and were incapable of crawling out of
the shell of human malice and spite, I felt every such (even if exceptional)
case as something perverted, repulsive, and incompatible with the basic
principles of amateur radio. It seems that a worldwide rarity of fratricidal
war between amateur transmitters was the affair of KVAČ versus SKEČ.
      In the first phase all shortwave amateurs originally organized in the
shortwave section belonged to SKEČ. Differentiation came only after
the rupture between the radioclub and Pešek. Vydra's letter of December
22, 1928 to Motyčka hints at it: "...Either Mr. Pešek and Rokos resign
their functions, or we give up all our functions in SKEČ and leave
SKEČ. We are convinced that all the Moravians and Silesians will
leave..."
      The founding general meeting of KVAČ took place on September 17, 1929.
Among the leading personalities of KVAČ belong Motyčka, Eng. Buchar,
Pištěli, Prof. Vopička, Weirauch, Vydra, Hron, Plšek, Schäferling senior
and junior, Dr. Šafránek, and Neumann.
      The clash between KVAČ and SKEČ occurred on September 14, 1929, when
it came to light that according to the Call Book of June 2, 1929 "the
sole representative of Czechoslovak amateurs is SKEČ." Already on
September 15 an emphatic declaration is sent to the editors of the
Call Book that the sole representative of Czechoslovak amateur
transmitters is KVAČ. Attached to the letter is a list of Czechoslovak
amateurs (as a precaution, without addresses).
      On November 27, 1928, the Radio Union sent to amateur organizations
in various states the announcement that it alone was the representative
of Czechoslovak shortwave amateurs, and on January 25, 1929 it sent
an application for admission to the IARU. From there came a letter
saying that SKEČ had already applied for admission to the IARU
and had sent a long list of amateur transmitters who were its members.
      KVAČ writes to Hartford that these amateurs are not members of SKEČ
and were placed on the list without their consent.
      "This accusation is very serious," replies K. B. Warner, Assistant
to the Secretary A. L. Budlong, on behalf of the IARU secretary on June
26, 1929. "If it should prove true, we shall take steps to reconsider
everything we have already undertaken toward the recognition of SKEČ."
      Now the battle breaks loose. Every amateur transmitter is sent
a correspondence card with a preprinted declaration to sign, stating
that he has left SKEČ and is not its member. Agitation proceeds from
person to person.
      Jan Biskup, later OK2BI, writes to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs:
"...Zdeněk Petr, OK2BR, drove by car from Brno to Borová to spirit away
the signed statement for SKEČ and win him over for KVAČ..."
      Moravia, led by Brno and Telč, stood behind KVAČ. A supporter of
SKEČ was, besides Biskup, OK2OX, O. Vlasák, an electrical engineer,
Austro-Hungarian military aviator, and owner of the first radio in
Zlín at that time.
      "SKEČ is preparing a general attack on Moravia," Motyčka informs
Petr on March 13, 1930. "It intends to send its attaché to you on
a motorcycle to talk around those Olomouc fellows." (By that "attaché"
Josef Štětina, OK1AZ, was meant.)
      Whole stacks of paper are written, endless hours are argued away,
letter after letter flies to West Hartford from both rivals, and there
they do not know what to do. It is reminiscent of the times of the
quarrelsome Přemyslids, who raced each other on the road to Nuremberg
to win the Emperor's recognition. The affair reaches even the pages
of QST. On April 30, 1930, Budlong writes to Motyčka:
      "We are thousands of miles from Czechoslovakia and it is still truly
impossible for us to form a clear picture of the situation between KVAČ
and SKEČ..."
      New members are dragged into the disputes, who quite by chance enter
one or the other society; there are even amphibians who secretly attend
both, because they find pleasant people and interesting surroundings
in each, and cannot understand what those two societies can have against
each other. And it is not clear even to people who — because of earlier
personal contacts and sympathies — get involved. When Motyčka arrives
in Brno for a meeting of the 3rd and 2nd districts of KVAČ, Petr,
Václavík, Škop, Pavlíček, Liewehr, and others descend on him, demanding
that he explain to them what the core of the dispute between Pešek
and the radioclub actually is,


---


FINALLY!!


---


On March 28, 1930, at a quarter past ten in the evening, the Prague express
passes through the last tunnel before Obřany. Far and wide little
lights leap out of the darkness. The signs on the large gasometers in
Židenice urgently call out: "Heat, cook, light with gas!" The passengers
rise, put on their coats, and prepare their luggage. There appear the
silhouettes of the slender towers of the cathedral on Petrov and the
station building with the sign Brno. The round clock shows 22.25 as
the express stops at the platform.
      "Beer—lemonade!" "Hot sausages!" "Fresh water!" The platform came
alive. Porters take suitcases from the passengers' hands. Everywhere
is full of people and bustle.
