      At the exit stands Zdeněk Petr, attentively watching everyone
who passes through. When he spots a tall man with a round face and
thick, combed-back hair, he walks toward him. The man carries his own
little suitcase. He had traveled on a hard third-class bench.
      "Welcome to Brno, Motyčka!"
      They stayed up long past midnight. Tomorrow, in the restaurant U
solnice, the meeting of Moravian and Silesian amateur transmitters
takes place. It was necessary to prepare the program of the negotiations
and think through all eventualities. There would be talk of strategy
and tactics against SKEČ, and above all, of how the applications for
concessions were proceeding. This question had already been on the
agenda. In the July 1929 issue of Čs. radiosvět an article by Dr. Burda
appeared on the legal aspect of licensing amateur transmitting stations.
During the fall several notices on this topic appeared in the daily
press, and in the December issue Radiosvět announced
that preliminary registration for the examinations was already possible. One
of the conditions was proof that the station was to be established
for scientific purposes.
      Motyčka was brief:
      "The matter is still being held up by some dispute between the Ministry
of National Defense and the Posts."
      What kind — that Motyčka did not explain, for he did not know either.
Those negotiations had a confidential character and nothing of them
reached the public. The Ministry of National Defense was willing to
agree to permitting amateur transmission on the condition
that a network of official stations be established to monitor this
transmission. Two must be in Bohemia, one in Moravia, one in Silesia,
and four in Slovakia.
      At the inter-ministerial consultation of May 5, 1930, the representatives
of the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs announced that a Radio Monitoring
Service had been established (so far only in Prague) and was already
functioning.
      "However, as far as goniometry for direction-finding shortwave
transmitters is concerned, the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs cannot,
at the present state of science and technology, take upon itself any
obligations in this matter."
      The Ministry of National Defense declared that as soon as it received
the minutes of the consultation, it would have no further objections,
and the Ministry of the Interior expressed itself in the same sense.
      The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs sent out invitations at the
beginning of May, and on May 19, 1930, before three in the
afternoon, several trembling men under thirty years of age gathered
in Jindřišská Street in front of the main post office. They all greeted
one another like old acquaintances. They climbed up to the second
floor, to the telegraph exchange.
      The chairman of the examining board was Dr. Kučera, and its members
Dr. Burda, Eng. Jaromír Svoboda (who directed the last and most important
stage of the construction at Poděbrady), and chief postal secretary
Alois Špinka, an experienced worker of the Prague central telegraph
station. He also had behind him a study stay at the French transmitting
center of St. Assise and at the receiving station
of Villescresnes near Paris.
      Špinka handed out telegraph forms. Each wrote his name in the
upper right corner above the column "Service data, direction of traffic."
The examination text had 250 letters, lasted 5 minutes, and fit onto
two forms. Weirauch already had several years of experience. With his
English girlfriend G6YL he used to have hour-long telegraph contacts
in her mother tongue; he had learned English well enough to read
technical journals, but he was so flustered that in the examination
text: "I say shall we go for another walk? It's fine again
now only rather windy and I am not at all tired..." etc., he made four
uncorrected errors. Then secretary Špinka unrolled before him a roll
of telegraph tape, and Weirauch had to read.
      In recent weeks he had not been transmitting. He lay among books
and studied. And to the questions: direct current, alternating current,
electric oscillations, transformers, what is the electric field and
how does it propagate, the tube as an amplifier, radiogonio, a closed
oscillatory circuit, protection of power sources, the wavemeter, the
resolutions of the Washington conference on call signs and on amateur
transmission — he answered correctly. His Morse errors were within
tolerance.
      The others were successful too, so that within a few days the news
appeared in bulletins and magazines and in the daily papers that the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs had granted the first six amateur
transmitting concessions:
OK1AA, Eng. Mirko Schäferling, Prague XII, Korunní 96,
OK1AB, Pravoslav Motyčka, Prague-Braník, Krčská 234,
OK2AC, MUC Zdeněk Neumann, Telč 39,
OK1AF, Josef Štětina, Prague-Nusle, Havlíčkova 169,
OK2AG, Ladislav Vydra, Telč 67/1,
OK1AH, Alois Weirauch, Městec Králové, square, no. 9.
      When, in a joyful mood, they found themselves again in Jindřišská
Street, they decided to celebrate their success together. They set
out to where Motyčka had begun with his experiments: to the Lucerna.
There they saw the first sound film "The Ship of
Comedians." They bought little gifts for their sweethearts and rode home.
      It was a great and significant day not only in the life of each of
them. It was a historic milestone.
      The period around 1930 is a dividing line in the history of Czechoslovak
radio technology in other respects as well. From April 4 to 13,
1929, Prague was host to the European Radio-electrical Conference,
at which the allocations of broadcasting frequencies were reorganized
and the division of frequencies of the fixed services was revised.
The significance of the conference was underscored by the participation
of the Soviet Union (which had not been invited to the Washington
conference and therefore did not recognize its resolutions). Fifty-two
thousand sheets of paper were written, 6,000 staples were consumed,
two water glasses and one ashtray got broken.
      In 1930 (December 1), by an exchange of telegrams between the heads
of state via the stations WKQ and OKI (13,255 kHz), in the evening
OKH (6,720 kHz), Czechoslovak radiotelegraph traffic across the ocean
was inaugurated. The number of telegrams soon exceeded the anticipated
volume.
      Dr. Baštýř (like many others of his generation in the radioclub)
was ceasing to keep pace with the development of radio technology.
He failed with his attacks against broadcasting and against the
Ministry of Posts (the number of broadcast listeners had exceeded
a quarter million and was approaching half a million), and in 1929
he left the post of editor of Čs. radiosvět. The strengthening of the
Prague broadcasting station from 5.5 kW to 120 kW was being prepared,
along with the further construction and improvement of transmitters.
      The end of the second decade of the twentieth century meant the
end of the first stage, in which the foundations were formed. With
the third decade the next stage began, in which postal, military,
and amateur radio technology developed on the laid foundations. The
first years were marked by the deep economic crisis. In the second
half of the decade the signs of the coming catastrophe were taking
shape.
      However significant the success of the first Czechoslovak amateur
transmitters was, one cannot say that their joy was not clouded.
They parted with certain misgivings. Štětina belonged among the most
active amateurs, used to be on the bands daily, but after the examination
it was as if he had been cut off. Contacts between
OK1AA and OK1AB cooled. They remained correct — an outsider would notice
nothing — but they were no longer as cordial as before.
      Weirauch asked that his call sign OK1RV be assigned to him.
He was not obliged. Nor was Štětina, nor Neumann, nor Vydra.
The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs would assign call signs in
alphabetical order according to chronological sequence. Štětina applied
for a change to OK1AZ. Without success. Into the next group belonged
Eng. Budík, OK1AU, Colonel Skála, OK2VA, and Prof. Vopička, OK1VP.
Štětina submitted an application for a change of call sign in writing
on October 10, 1930. The application was promptly rejected. Štětina
set out for the ministry in person, submitted a new application, and
explained that from earlier times he had a large stock of QSL cards
with the call sign OK1AZ. Dr. Kučera had understanding for it, the
ministry obliged Štětina, and permitted the change
to OK1AZ. This created a precedent, on the basis of which
Weirauch on January 17, 1931 applied for a change to OK1AW, arguing
that these letters key more rhythmically, that no confusion with S
or 5 would occur, and that they were the initials of his name. The
ministry obliged this application as well,
and thereby definitively abandoned the principle of chronological
sequence of call signs.
      "And how is it that Schäferling has OK1AA, when Motyčka started
earlier?"
      Motyčka did not like this topic. He always shrugged his shoulders,
and one could see that it was unpleasant to him.
      "As an official of the Ministry of Posts he knew how to arrange it
for himself," it was whispered on the side.
      In the state archive there is stored a letter to the Ministry of
Posts and Telegraphs, written on December 28, 1929, when Schäferling
was still a student:
      "To my application for permission for an amateur transmitting station
I allow myself to add the further request for assignment of the call
sign OK1AA, since my application, which I submitted in its first wording
already on April 27, 1926, and another on January 20, 1927, was surely
the first for the granting of an amateur concession..."
      When Motyčka was shown this document sometime in the early seventies,
he was moved.
      "It is true," he said. "I did not submit my application until 1929."
He gazed somewhere into the distance. Eng. Mirko Schäferling, OK1AA,
was already dead.
      How much bitterness would have fallen away, if only people had
known how to talk to each other!


---


—■ • • • ■■


---


Even though amateur transmission had been permitted since 1930, such
permission was not granted just for nothing, merely because someone
took a fancy to transmitting. A concession for an amateur transmitting
station could be granted only for scientific purposes, which had to be
documented by a recommendation from a university. Weirauch justifies
his application of November 29, 1929 as follows:
"...for experimental and research purposes, such as the investigation
of the periodicity of various natural influences governing radio
transmission, the search for means against fading, the testing of
various types of antennas, the setting of the sun and its influence
on the range of distant signals, experiments with 5 and 10 m waves,
the dependence of shortwave propagation on the time of day and on the
season of the year, etc. I acquired the necessary knowledge through
several years of private study," and he attaches the recommendation
of the Physics Institute of the Czech Technical University, signed by
Prof. Dr. František Nachtikal.
      Weirauch and his friends took these declarations of theirs seriously.
The twenties were not yet forgotten, when amateurs — earlier than the
professionals — had carried out long-distance contacts on short waves
and thereby discovered and proved their usefulness. When they began
with their experiments, the short waves below 200 m were almost empty,
and about what we know today of their propagation only the first timid
hypotheses were being voiced.
      OK1AW carefully recorded meteorological factors and various
phenomena which he observed in his experiments. At the beginning of
the thirties he was already a well-known and successful DX-man, and
he ventured into experiments on 28 MHz.
And on longer waves Weirauch had achieved WAC already in 1929, as the
second in Czechoslovakia and the first in Bohemia.
      On August 18, 1932, an interesting event took place: Professor
Piccard's second balloon ascent into the stratosphere (the first ascent
had taken place in 1931). The purpose was to observe changes in
electric potential, the ionization of the atmosphere, and the intensity
of cosmic radiation. At the last moment, but still before the balloon
with the professor and his assistant Cosyns rose from Dübendorf in
Switzerland, the post office sent Weirauch a slip with the laconic
message:
41 42.8 75 85 Piccard call sign B9
From the upright handwriting with its lower loops, energetic strokes,
and regular, precisely maintained spacing of letters and words, it was
evident at first glance that the sender was Motyčka.
      That day Weirauch hung his trade on a nail, sat down at the station,
and began to watch the indicated waves.
      Weirauch did not make contact with Piccard's balloon, but he heard
its station, was present, followed the whole course of this interesting
and gripping experiment, and thus became its indirect participant.
It was a lifelong experience. The assistant Cosyns sent Weirauch a
photograph of the B9 transmitter with a handwritten dedication. This
photograph is a precious memento, and Weirauch always treasured it.
      In 1930 the first Slovak stations began their activity as well: OK3S,
Eng. Samuel Šuba, held daily talks on the 30 m wave with OK3NK, Eng.
Kemický, between Pukanec and Bratislava. After a year Eng. Kemický
grew tired of it. Eng. Šuba applied for the examination and received
his original unlicensed-era call sign OK3SP. He had in fact wanted to
study mechanical engineering and devote himself to water turbines,
but he switched over to weak-current engineering. He also had the
opportunity to attend the lectures of Professor Barkhausen, he cultivated
astronomy as well, and he became the first Slovak engineer of radio
technology and a significant figure of the amateur radio movement.
      The thirties brought the end of amateur construction of broadcast
receivers. In the radioclubs people began to speak of stagnation and
of a crisis of amateurism. The clubs survived on inertia and sought
new content: sound film, television, and transmission. The bearers of
the hobbyist amateur radio activity became the amateur transmitters
together with the SWLs (shortwave listeners). Characteristic of this
period is the growth in the number of concessions for transmitting
stations.

            THE END OF BLACK


---


It is Friday, February 5, 1932, evening. Pravoslav Motyčka is returning
from a business trip. He enters the first floor.
      He reaches for the door handle. Suddenly he stops. Before the door
lies a sprinkle of cigarette ash. At the Motyčkas' no one smoked. He
enters and smells traces of tobacco smoke. He looks at his mother.
      "There were people here from the police."
      "Did they conduct a search?"
      "No, but you are to go there."
      She hands Pravoslav a slip of paper with a number noted on it.
First he tidies up what can be tidied, and only then sits down to eat.
The next day (Saturdays were still working days back then) he sets out
for the police headquarters.
      The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs had assumed that illegal
("black") transmission would end as soon as concessions began to be
granted. It even turned to the Ministry of Justice with a proposal for
an amnesty for unlicensed operators (932/Pp-XI-29 of December 27,
1929): "...one cannot see in it motives dangerous to public order and
punishable, so much as an expression of impatience and youthful
inconsiderate zeal..."
      The Ministry of National Defense had in fact been urging the Ministry
of the Interior to take action against them. One just did not know how
to bite into that sour apple without popularizing at the same time the
activity of the military listening stations. The chief of the intelligence
department, General Chalupa, writes on July 27, 1928 to the Minister
of the Interior (ref. no. 6228/conf. H.Q. 2nd dep. 1928):
      "...The basis for criminal proceedings could, in the view here, be
a criminal complaint against unknown perpetrators, which would contain
only the report that military radio stations had intercepted a number
of Czechoslovak amateur stations in communication, although these had
not yet been licensed."
      On February 22, 1930, between 16.20 and 16.48 hours, the military
station in Prešov intercepted a contact between OK2MAG and HAP4.
Conditions were not exactly good, there was interference on the band;
at OK2MAG things were not going particularly well, so the two stations
managed to exchange only the basic formalities of a so-called
"rubber-stamp" contact. At 16.44 hours HAP4 told station
OK2MAG its location: Debrecen, Kossuth utca 20.
      No other records of station OK2MAG exist. Whose call sign could
it have been?
      At that time Czechoslovak amateur stations used exclusively
two-letter call signs. The only exception was OK1EMA, staff sergeant
Jarda Pištěk of the Kutná Hora telegraph battalion. The most probable
explanation is that it was the badly-keyed call sign OK4QO (or rather
OK2QO), used by Alexander F. Janowitz, later HC1KE. HAP is the call
sign of the Hungarian police (HAP Budapest, HAP1 Szombathely, HAP2
Szeged, HAP3 Debrecen, HAP4 Székesfehérvár), which — like the Austrian
police — used to make contact with amateurs too. It is also not clear
why HAP4, when Debrecen was HAP3; the fact is that this contact stirred
our authorities' bile, given the tense Czechoslovak-Hungarian relations
of the time. The Ministry of National Defense communicated on March 2,
1930, ref. no. 292 secret-IV/2nd dep. 1930:
      "We have established 61 unauthorized transmitting stations. If
we subtract the 26 applicants for concessions, who had probably already
begun operating without waiting for the concession to be granted,
36 radio amateurs are engaged in transmitting secretly, and that
sometimes under suspicious circumstances. For example, on 22/2 of this
year station OK2MAG was in contact with the Hungarian police station
HAP4 (Szeged)!"
      The intelligence department deepened the confusion surrounding the
identity of station HAP4 and did not even communicate to the Ministry
of Posts and Telegraphs the content of the intercepted contact. There
remained only the dreadful fact of contacts between a secret Czechoslovak
amateur station and the Hungarian police. Dr. Kučera, red pencil in
hand, read through Československý radiosvět, especially its KVAČ
supplement. And on October 19, 1931 he sent the Ministry of the Interior
a confidential and urgent letter no. 66050/XI, phrased "by official
authority." "Subject: The magazine Radiosvět, the printing of articles
and reports about unauthorized radio transmitting stations."
      At the Interior Ministry they pondered this a little, and by decree
74364/1931 of January 15, 1932 they informed the Prague police
headquarters that Radiosvět of May 8, 1931 had written about stations
2CG, 2FN, 2GN, 3GW, 2KT, 2MU, 2NR, 3NZ, 2NY, 2CP, 3US, and 3WA, which
are unlicensed. "The police headquarters is directed, through a
protocol interrogation of the editor of the supplement, Pravoslav
Motyčka, to establish who established and operates the mentioned
stations."
      Police actuarial adjunct Václav Syrovátka put a sheet of paper into
the typewriter.
      "So you are Pravoslav Motyčka, born..."
      "January 20, 1899."
      "Where?"
      "In Nymburk."
      "Residing at..."
      "In Braník, Krčská 234."
      "Occupation?"
      "Technical official at Philips."
      "You are also the editor of the Československý radiosvět supplement
Hlídka KVAČ. Since when?"
      "Since 1929."
      "That is when the supplement was founded. But you had carried out
editorial activity earlier as well, hadn't you?"
      "I edited in Radiosvět the column 'Experimenting on short waves
and amateur transmission.'"
      "Correct. That means you are thoroughly familiar with this field
and with the people who engage in it."
      Motyčka had the feeling his throat had gone dry. He swallowed
with an empty throat.
      The interrogator laid Radiosvět on the table.
      In Motyčka's head whirled those countless articles about the
activity of Czechoslovak amateurs, about established contacts,
descriptions and even photographs of stations that had appeared in his
column over the last several years.
Already in 1927, no. 4, p. 108, the article "Czechoslovak experimental
transmitters," beginning with the sentence: "There is, pleasingly, a
sizable group of them, and more keep arriving."


---


The radiotelegraphist of the ship Chickasaw City, Robert Kreisinger, always
has plenty to do. Contact with coastal stations, telegrams, reception of
weather over the ocean, time signals, care for the proper running of the
ship's chronometer, and in poor visibility the direction-finding of
lighthouses and determination of the ship's position... Yet he still
finds time to listen to Czechoslovak amateurs as well, and to make
contacts with them. He works with a two-kilowatt spark transmitter.
Weirauch too has a QSL card from the ship station KUNZ.
      Motyčka thinks of the detailed article on this topic, and it is not
pleasant for him.
      The official of the intelligence department of the police
headquarters, Syrovátka, presents the article "From the activity of
the Moravian and Slovak members of KVAČ" and wants to know to whom
those call signs belong.
      "That is merely journalistic fantasy, to entertain the readers."
      "Who is the author of that article?"
      "The assistant editor Klein put it in without my knowledge, while
I was away on a business trip."
      "Do you know that Zdeněk Petr, of whom it speaks here?"
      Motyčka cannot say no. Radiosvět itself would convict him, and at
home he has a whole pile of correspondence with Petr. In the end he
admitted that the author of that article could be Petr. But he, Motyčka,
did not know those call signs.
They were, after all, Moravian and Slovak signs.
      That is enough for Syrovátka. After all, it is already Saturday
afternoon, and the interrogation must end. When Motyčka comes out of
the police headquarters, he breaks into a run. At the post office he
places a call to Brno, to Zdeněk Petr.


---


m   • • • m m
    Bohdan Klein is interrogated, and he confirms Motyčka's testimony.
He knows nothing more. Neither does the chairman of KVAČ, Doc. Dr.
Šafránek, on whom the summons came on February 13. He assures
Syrovátka, however, that "the endeavor of the KVAČ society will be
to weed out and expel members who have no concession." And he means
it in earnest.
      Zdeněk Petr was subjected to a thorough interrogation on May 7,
1932, at the Provincial Office in Brno (at that time he worked there
at Joštova St. 8/II, room 15), and Vladimír Novotný, OK2MU, at the
Brno police headquarters. The Ministry of the Interior presents copies
of the interrogation protocols to the Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs
and reports: "It was not possible to establish the names and addresses
of the owners of unauthorized amateur transmitters."
      The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs closes the case on March 25,
1933: "A positive result of our intervention is that the magazine
Radiosvět has stopped printing reports about the activity of suspicious
persons. KVAČ has already ceased to exist, and in the society ČAV
order has been established in this matter."
      The main military radio station on Petřín prepared an
eighteen-page report, in which it lists 625 amateur unlicensed
stations from the beginning of monitoring listening up to 1933. Of
these, 102 had already received a concession, so 523 were unauthorized.
      The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs considers the figure 523
exaggerated. There are listed call signs that no one has used for a
long time, that appeared once, and call signs intercepted in error.
A fundamental change takes place. The society Czechoslovak Amateur
Transmitters gave broad and emphatic publicity to the MPT directive
that no contacts should be made with the "black" stations. The amateurs
value their concessions, and they begin to understand the fight against
the "unlis" (unlicensed) as a matter of honor. They guard the purity
of the amateur bands and cooperate in uncovering "pirates." Zdeněk
Václavík, OK2SI, in a letter of August 4, 1933 to the Ministry of
Posts and Telegraphs, writes that he considers it his duty to report:
Kolesnikov, OK1KW, is transmitting without a concession from the
vicinity of Mukachevo in the 20 m band. Kříž, OK2MM, and Dančík,
OK3DC, draw attention to the unlicensed station of Skopal-Procházka,
OK2PT. The amateurs carefully read through the addresses of
concessionaires, especially the new ones, and are wary of every unknown
call sign. The committee of ČAV rejects on September 9, 1933 the
membership application of Alfred Winkler, who from June to July 1933
had transmitted illegally in Vítkovice under the call signs OK2BE,
OK2DF, SP3FFF, and under two German call signs.
      The army too marched out into battle against the black amateurs.
In decree MND no. 675 secret-IV/2 1933 of June 2, 1933, it is stated:
      "At the present state of technology, the detection of black
amateurs can be carried out successfully only with the help of reliable
licensed amateurs, who will gradually come into contact with the black
stations and contribute to their identification...
The first step is a free concession for members of the telegraph troops
with radiotelegraph training."
      The MPT then indeed grants soldiers who present the appropriate
recommendation concessions without fees and without examinations.
Professional soldiers appeared in ČAV. They came somewhat after the
funeral, for the "black" transmitting had practically already ended,
and was essentially limited to amateurs who had applied for a concession,
or intended to apply, but wanted first to familiarize themselves with
transmitting and thus prepare for the examination. The soldiers who
entered ČAV in this action were good telegraphists and experienced
technicians, and the other amateurs liked them.
      And what was the official view of amateur transmission in the
mid-thirties? We find it in a note on the reference sheet in file no.
1575/XI-35, which reached the MPT on January 8, 1935 ("The Czechoslovak
Community of Radio Amateurs" of Prague — an institution unknown in
itself — proposes to the ministry certain measures for the promotion
and spread of amateur transmission."):
      "The Ministry of Posts has no interest in the number of
transmitting radio amateurs expanding too greatly. It has an interest
only in this: that everyone who engages in amateur radio transmission
should have a proper concession and be in the official registry."


---


RADIO AND AVIATORS

On April 12, 1928, at 5.10 in the morning, the mechanics at the Irish
airfield Baldonnel spin the propeller and pull the chocks from the
wheels. The Bremen (registration mark D 1167) starts rolling, lifts
off at a speed of 110 km/h, and heads west, out over the Atlantic
Ocean. At the controls sit Köhl and Colonel of the Irish Army
Fitzmaurice. The third member of the crew is the director of the North
German Lloyd, von Hünefeld.
      After Lindbergh, the crossing of the Atlantic had also succeeded
for Chamberlain and Byrd, but only from America to Europe. From Europe
to America, Nungesser and Coli had set out in 1927 in the aircraft
L'Oiseau blanc. They were still sighted over the ocean, but then
vanished without a trace. The flight in the westward direction was
considered — if it was possible at all — to be the more dangerous.
The Bremen heads at a speed of 170 km/h toward the American shores.
It maintains an altitude of 500 to 600 m.
      At five o'clock in the afternoon the pilots begin to feel fatigue.
They have the setting sun in front of them, its reflection on the
water, pale white clouds on the horizon. Their eyes ache and burn.
They refresh themselves with tea from a thermos.
      The impact of a northwesterly wind. Behind a white stripe on the
horizon tower mountains of horrific shapes, leaden in color. The Bremen
climbs to 2,500 m, but cannot get out of the clouds. It descends to
70 m above the surface, where the wind is at least not so vicious.
They are in a low-pressure area. There is a violent downpour, the sea
torn up and raging, the aircraft trembles and lurches, sharp gusts of
wind throw them up to 30° off course.
      It is already dark. The cabin is dimly lit; a flashlight must be
shone on the compass. The temperature drops to 2 °C. Outside there is
thick fog; the airmen have no idea where they are. After midnight Köhl
calls to Hünefeld:
      "Coffee!"
      The last means of holding out.
      Toward morning Köhl dozes off. After 24 hours of flight the sky
has finally cleared. Fitzmaurice is flying at 2,000 m. But the compass
has gone mad! It shows utter nonsense and turns whichever way it
pleases. Below them, far and wide, a white plain. In the course of the
morning, snow-covered forests, mountains, and valleys appear in the
field of the telescope, but nowhere any building, any runway or road,
no chimney with smoke rising from it. And the gasoline is enough for
only a few more hours of flight.
      "Now that radio would come in handy!" all three lament in unison.
      At the military receiving station in Lučenec they have a
brand-new seven-tube receiver, the PO 8.
      Short waves they catch with the RP9 receiver. But they like best
to listen on the RP19, which they built themselves — and that
according to the instructions of Alois Weirauch in Radiosvět.
      On April 13, 1928, private Slabihoudek has the shortwave listening
duty. At four o'clock he intercepted on the 36 m wave this message:
QTC EUROPE DE NU1BBN = GERMAN FLIERS FORCED
DOWN LANDED SAFELY ON ISLAND OF CANADA AT 8R35
PM EST + QTC DE NU1BBN.
      On the listening group of the Provincial Military Command in
Bratislava someone appended to this record, in red pencil: "Concerns
the aircraft Bremen!"

m m • • • m m


---


In the twenties the pilots were still prejudiced against radio. They
considered it a needless burden. An antenna let down from an aircraft,
they said, got in the way of flying. They even claimed that
electromagnetic waves spoiled the oil and the gasoline.
      The military began radio operations on the aviation network
Cheb–Prague–Olomouc–Nitra on May 25, 1925. Aviation Regiment 1, Prague,
had the station KEL on the 720 m wave, Aviation Regiment 2 in Olomouc
LM, likewise on 720 m, and Aviation Regiment 3 in Nitra LA on the
540 m wave.
      At the end of the twenties the state was introducing radiotelegraphy
onto Czechoslovak civilian aircraft. Among the first to apply for a
post as radiotelegraphist was Arnošt Kredba of Prague VI/1, Na hrázi
276. He had served as a military radiotelegraphist in Königgrätz
(Hradec Králové), in Mödling, in Pula, and in Kotor. He wanted to fly.
On March 4, 1929, he took the examination at the telegraph central
station in Prague.
      On April 23, 1930, a consultation was held at the MPT with Eng.
Strnad, resolving to build an aviation direction-finding service in
Prague, Brno, and Mariánské Lázně, to construct a shortwave 300 W
transmitter for the aviation service
for 400,000 Kč, to buy shortwave receivers for 20,000 Kč, and to set
up teleprinters at the airfields.
      In 1933 a direction finder cost about 90,000 Kč, a little house
55,000. At the Brno airfield they built a Marconi goniometer in 1930.
The antennas were spread on the roof of the operations building, and
the direction was determined by rotating the induction coil. Next to
the radiotelegraphist's office dwelt the meteorologists, who had on
their desk a black crank telephone, connected
to the Prague–Brno–Bratislava cable. From it they received information,
which they entered with a fountain pen into a thick half-sheet-format
notebook. Daily they drew synoptic maps, prepared situation reports
and forecasts for the airfield, and leaflets for the pilots. Between
the radio and the meteorologists a window was built into the wall, so
that mutual contact worked splendidly.
      The goniometer worked less well. The proximity of the hangar, the
taxiing of aircraft, and indeed the whole surroundings interfered.
Therefore in 1934 it was decided to build in Brno as well a Telefunken
direction finder with a circular loop antenna, in a wooden house at
the other end of the airfield, away from the disturbing influences.
There worked the postal radiotelegraphists Petrů, Tlapák, and Vlastimil
Velík. I often used to go to see them, and at home in Svitávka I
listened to them. I always recognized at once who was at the key.
Mr. Velík had the habit of somewhat stretching the dash of the letter
B in the call sign OKB. The elements of Mr. Tlapák's call signs were
customarily slightly shorter and more clipped than Mr. Petrů's.
      The individual airfields exchanged among themselves on the
frequency of 248 kHz reports of takeoffs, landings, and numbers of
passengers. Contact with aircraft and direction-finding took place
on 333 kHz. An aircraft on the Prague–Vienna route regularly reported
itself over Batelov and Vranov. When an aircraft flew from Zlín (OKA)
to Prague (OKL), it reported itself over Prostějov. I told our folks
it was flying, and we went out to the yard. I switched over to the
loudspeaker. The report "Boskovice" could be heard over the whole
yard. A few moments later the aircraft was hovering over Svitávka.

MURDER IN ZÁHOŘÍ


---


On January 30, 1933, President Hindenburg appointed Adolf Hitler
Reich Chancellor.
      The burning of the Reichstag on February 27, 1933, became a
pre-prepared pretext for unleashing wild terror. The communist deputies
of the Reichstag and of all the provincial diets were arrested, all
significant party officials; the communist and social-democratic press
was stopped; emergency decrees were issued abolishing freedom of the
press and of assembly. Concentration camps are being founded.
      Reports of Nazi brutalities keep arriving. On March 16, 1933, it
was announced that Reichstag deputy Wilhelm Solmann had been attacked,
his lower jaw broken, and his body covered with wounds. The editor
Hugo Efferoth was transported from the Cologne Brown House to prison
with a stab wound in his back. Three SA men dragged the
social-democratic deputy Johann Stelling from his apartment in
Köpenick and gravely injured him. A few days later his corpse, with a
smashed face, was found dumped in a sack.
      The newspapers are full of information of this kind. The economic
situation is worsening. The price of butter rises from 2.20 marks to
3.40, of margarine from 40 pfennigs to 1.80 M, of sugar from 0.64 to
1 mark per kilogram.
      The deficit of the state budget is growing. The dictatorship of
the Nazi bigwigs and the party bureaucracy arouses resistance even in
a considerable part of the German bourgeoisie and former supporters
of the Nazi party. People begin to speak of a "second revolution."
      In the "Night of the Long Knives," June 30, 1934, special SS and
Gestapo squads ride out and murder on the spot 59 officials of the SA
storm detachments, 11 political figures, such as the former Reich
Chancellor General Schleicher with his wife, and several dozen other
persons.
      Gregor Strasser, co-founder of the NSDAP and until recently one
of Hitler's closest collaborators, is sitting on June 30, 1934 (the
text says 1930 — trans.) with his family at lunch. Five Gestapo men
in civilian clothes arrive. They arrest Strasser.
They hand him over to a unit of SS men. They bind him, drive him to
the Grunewald, deep into the forest. There they knock him to the
ground, trample him, and stamp him to death.
      Thus Strasser's brother, Dr. Otto Strasser, who had fled Germany
and settled in Prague, describes Strasser's end.

M B • • •   M


---


In the days when German amateurs still used the prefix K, one could
read on their station cards the note "QSL via KY4."
      KY4 was the call sign of the station of Eng. Rolf Formis, one of
the founders of the German amateur organization DASD and later the
technical chief of Stuttgart broadcasting.
      Formis fled Germany after Hitler's rise to power, and on April 25,
1934, crossed the Czechoslovak border near Železná Ruda. As a young
lad he had been in Siam. During the First World War he served in the
Turkish army, where he became a first lieutenant.
      There were many emigrants in Prague at that time. In the Fénix
palace was the office of a committee that supported them. Here Formis
too registered on May 4, 1934, and took lodgings with Mrs. Šmídová,
at Havlíčkovo Square no. 3.
He wrote articles, offered them to editorial offices, but saw that he
could not make a living that way. He set out for Turkey.
      On May 31 he arrived on foot in Uzhhorod, where he borrowed a map
of Romania from the municipal library. He knew German, Turkish,
English, and French, but not Romanian. That proved fatal for him. On
June 16 he was arrested in Romania. When he finally got back to Prague,
his path led to the five-story house no. 44 with a balcony, columns,
and allegorical statues, in Žitná Street. Here, in Otto Strasser's
headquarters, was the center of the Black Front, an anti-Hitler
organization that published the magazine Die deutsche Revolution.
Thanks to his knowledge and connections, Dr. Otto Strasser had
first-class information, which came in useful to Dr. Beneš as well,
and to the chief of the state security department of the Prague police
headquarters, Dr. Benda.
      On November 8, 1934, Formis moved to the Záhoří hotel, 8 km from
the settlement of Čím near Slapy (municipality of Přestavlky), where
he had rented room no. 6 on the first floor and room no. 17 in the
attic. He carefully locked this room and never allowed anyone to
enter it. The accommodation was provided to Formis by the tenant of
Záhoří, the well-known Prague hotelier Gráf. Strasser was living in
Žitná Street with his daughter, Mrs. Brodská, who was a widow. Dr.
Otto Strasser had in his pocket three passports: a Reich-German one
in his real name, another Reich-German one in the name Dr. Otto
Lobrochs, issued June 4, 1929 in Charlottenburg (under this name he
had an account at Živnobanka), and a Czechoslovak one in the name
Dr. Otto Baumann, issued January 1, 1935 by the police headquarters
in Prague. Among Strasser's close collaborators belonged also Heinrich
Grunow, who likewise lived at Žitná 44 and was the publisher of
Strasser's writings and of the Black Front's publications.
      From mid-November 1934, around noon (usually from 11 o'clock)
and around midnight, a German station made itself heard in the 49 m
broadcasting band on the frequency 6115 kHz with the announcement:
      "Achtung, Achtung, hier spricht die Schwarze Front, Landessender
Berlin." In Svitávka it could be heard excellently. It sarcastically
commented on the Nazi press — Völkischer Beobachter, Angriff, etc. —
broadcast speeches of Dr. Strasser recorded onto wax gramophone
records, quoted from the speeches of the dead Gregor Strasser, made
fun of Nazi parades, and called to battle against the rulers of the
Third Reich. The regime was displeased not only by its biting
criticism, to which Nazism was especially touchy, but also by its
well-informed items, often of a very confidential nature. Its signature
tune was the melody from Carmen, "Auf in den Kampf!" (in the Czech
version, "Toreador, watch out...").
      The RSN, the Radio Monitoring Service, located in the Postal
Technical and Testing Institute in Prague, had three working groups:
      1. Postal secretary Vladimír Fišer and postal assistant Miloslav
Janáček monitored the activity of amateur transmitting stations and
hunted for unlicensed ones (this they could do only, however, on the
basis of information obtained from intercepted contacts).
      2. Postal secretary Ferdinand Burle and postal assistant Antonín
Dvořák watched the German and Soviet broadcasting.
      3. Postal secretary Alexander Klempa and postal trainee Josef
Laclav took care of the Hungarian broadcasting.
      Ferdinand Burle came to work on April 4, 1934, at 12 o'clock. By
14.45 he had prepared and tested the recording apparatus (recording
was onto gelatin discs). At 14.55 he listened to music from Leipzig,
15.05 the water level on the Oder from Königs Wusterhausen, 15.10 a
literary lecture from Breslau, and so it went on until 21.00 hours,
when he recorded the Czech broadcast from Moscow. He entered his
observations in a correspondence protocol, which was a squared A4
notebook.
22.00: Moscow broadcast ended; 22.15: departure.
      On April 5 Burle reported at 8 o'clock, transcribed the text of
the Czech broadcast from Moscow, gave it the reference number
107/RSN-f-34, and left at 13.00.
      The Hungarian section intercepted an abundance of irredentist
broadcasting from Budapest, and Fišer with Janáček desperately tried
to discover whether some amateur was not transmitting something that
did not quite correspond to the concession conditions, or was
operating during broadcasting hours (which was forbidden), or whether
his sending was just plain bad — if only they too could show some
results.
      The broadcasting of the Black Front did not escape the attention
of the RSN, and on December 20 our post office began to take an
interest in where that transmitter actually was.
      The German police did not fail to notice Formis's disappearance
from Stuttgart. It had its agents among the German emigrants in
Prague as well. Since his return from Turkey after the First World
War, Formis had practically never left Stuttgart. Although, as an
educated man, he spoke standard German, the intonation of the voice,
the accent, and certain little things betrayed the announcer's origin.
The German police had at that time 40 listening stations in operation
with continuous duty. Each had a remotely controlled goniometer with
an error of at most 0.5°. The police radiotelegraphists took up to 360
characters per minute. The direction-finding vehicles were wooden,
with bodies screwed together with brass screws. Eng. J. Bříza of the
Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs saw all of this with his own eyes
when he made an official visit to Berlin's Adlershof.
      The Germans even dispatched, so it is said, an aircraft that
direction-finded during broadcasts. Be that as it may, on December 19,
1934, the German envoy Dr. Koch appeared at the Czechoslovak Ministry
of Foreign Affairs, protested against the activity of the Black Front's
transmitting station on Czechoslovak territory, and demanded its
liquidation.
      At Christmas Rolf Formis set up a little Christmas tree in his
living room no. 6 and reminisced. In Stuttgart, at Alexanderstr. no.
31, he had left his mother and his fiancée, Miss Schumacher. He was
40 on Christmas Day.
      Formis lived at the hotel on a daily allowance of 23 Kč, which
he received from Strasser. In the morning he slept late; in the
forenoon he read German newspapers and magazines. In the afternoon he
went for walks by the water and into the forest, then worked in his
room. The Záhoří hotel stood by the St. John's Rapids on the
red-marked tourist trail Slapy–Dobříš.
      On January 10, 1935, Envoy Koch appeared again at the Černín
Palace. Two days later again. The transmitter, it was said, was in
Záhoří, 80 km from Prague.
      On January 13 a dark-blue Daimler-Benz approaches Prague from
Lovosice. It bears the registration plate IP 48289, a powerful engine,
a canvas roof, and a conspicuously large trunk at the rear. It heads
for the main railway station. Hans Müller of Kiel and Edita
Kersbachová of Berlin step out, and put up at the Astoria-Wilson hotel
next to the station.
      On January 14 they set out for Štěchovice and put up at the
"U paroplavby" ("At the Steamboat") hotel. The terrain around
Štěchovice is good; they will ski there.
      On January 15 both appear in Záhoří and make the acquaintance
of Formis. They go for a walk together. They meet Hanička, the
granddaughter of the hotelier Gráf, and they all have their photograph
taken.
      January 16. Formis — as every Wednesday — departs for Prague.
Müller and Kersbachová drive to Štěchovice, and in the afternoon to
Prague. Müller buys a ticket to Berlin for the next day. In the
evening he returns with Edita to the hotel in Štěchovice.


---


-   • • •   m m
      At the wheel of car D-332 sits the driver Rozkošný. He is carrying
two passengers: Eng. Schäferling and Eng. Jirounek of the MPT. They
left Prague at 9 o'clock, and at noon they are in Písek. It is January
16, 1935. They lingered about an hour at Čížová.
They drove to the edge of the forest there; Eng. Schäferling put on
the earphones, slowly turned the receiver's tuning knob back and
forth, and carefully combed through the shortwave bands. At 12 o'clock
they stopped at the district gendarmerie headquarters, where Captain
Votruba was expecting them.
      A quarter of an hour later both engineers were back outside by
the car. It was only a few steps to the "U města Prahy" restaurant,
and it was lunchtime. During the stop at Čížová, however, they had
eaten their snack and did not want to lose time. Mr. Rozkošný started
the motor, and the D-332 set out toward the post office, to the right
toward Táborka and Ptákovna, and through the snowy landscape toward
Horní Záhoří. There it remained standing at the highest point of the
village, by the church, beside four stately chestnut trees with a
crucifix in the middle. Engineers Schäferling and Jirounek
successively visited several persons selected at the district
gendarmerie headquarters. They inspected their radios, and especially
the antennas and grounds. The same was repeated in Dolní Záhoří as
well; they even climbed into chimneys.
      The next leg of the journey led to Orlík and Zvíkov. The checking
receiver was in operation the whole time. At 17.45 they were back in
Písek at the district gendarmerie headquarters, where until half past
five they wrote up the protocol. Then they drove to the Black Eagle
for supper — really, for their lunch. At nine in the evening they were
back in Prague.
      The Polední list of February 21, 1935, reported as follows:
      "We were in a town in southern Bohemia. There we learned that
the gentlemen from the Ministry of Posts had been hunting for a
transmitter in southern Bohemia.
They arrived — let us say — in Něm. Záhoří near Tábor. They did not
trouble themselves much, but straightaway inquired where there was
dancing and jazz. When they learned that, they left the transmitter
be a transmitter and went dancing."
      The MPT filed a complaint against Polední list, and the
responsible editor Karel Kurt was sentenced to a fine of 100 Kč, or,
in case of non-payment, to 24 hours in prison — conditionally.

■■ • • •   M i


---


Eng. Formis visited Dr. Otto Strasser and reported on the German
guests who had come to ski in the surroundings of Štěchovice.
      "What nonsense!" Dr. Strasser grew angry. "To set out from Kiel
to ski at Štěchovice! Be careful!"
      "They look like lovers," Formis replied. "And they let themselves
be photographed quite readily..."
      On the morning of January 17 the engineer returned to Záhoří, and
at 11 o'clock transmitted his program.
      Müller drove in the morning from Štěchovice to Prague. He parked
the car in the garage of the Alcron hotel, and by the afternoon he was
already in Berlin, with Formis's photograph. Edith Kersbachová spent
the whole day at the U paroplavby hotel.
      On the afternoon of January 18 Müller flew back from Berlin to
Prague. From the Alcron garage he took the car, drove to Štěchovice,
paid the hotel, returned to Prague with Edita, and rented an apartment
in the Astoria-Wilson hotel.
      On Saturday the 19th and Sunday the 20th of January they waited
in vain for Formis, whom they had invited. Another German appeared:
Gert Schubert. At the hotel he greeted with a raised arm and the
salute "Heil Hitler!" and was not in the least concerned that this was
not permitted in Czechoslovakia.
      On January 21 all three drive around Prague, and in the evening
they are again in Štěchovice.
      On January 22 they drive around Štěchovice. They do not touch
the skis. Müller is looking for a rope.
      January 23. It is Wednesday, but the engineer breaks his settled
habit. He is not going to Prague. From 11 o'clock he transmits. Edita,
however, thinks he will go to Prague, and keeps watch at the buses.
Müller buys a strong rope. At 16.00 Edita comes with Müller on foot
from Štěchovice to the Záhoří hotel. At 17.00 they go out for a walk;
within an hour they are back. After supper Müller goes to lie down.
Formis remains alone with Edita in the lounge. They chat. Formis is
an abstainer and a non-smoker. He is always alone. Now he has the
company of a charming woman, a fellow-countrywoman. They have a drink,
and after 22.00 they go upstairs together. Shortly afterward the
waiter Flieger hears a heart-rending woman's scream and gunfire. He
runs upstairs. There stands Schubert with a pistol raised, and drives
him back down.
      The next day they find Formis dead. He had been shot once in the
forehead; the second wound was below the first bone of the xiphoid
process. A blow with a blunt object between the right temple and the
crown caused a fracture of the skull vault and was fatal. The gunshot
to the forehead shattered the right half of the cerebellum. According
to the cartridge cases found, four shots had been fired.
      Hitler expressly wished to get Formis alive. Hence that big
trunk. The murderers climbed down the rope and drove away. They
crossed the border at Sněžník, leaving the car standing at the barrier,
where on January 24 at 3.35 the customs superintendant Čáp found it.
At 5.30 there appeared at the customs house the brother of the
innkeeper from Rosenthal, Zeibig, presented Hans Müller's power of
attorney, and drove away the Daimler Benz, plate IP 48289. The German
authorities announced that "Müller, Schubert, and Kersbachová are
unknown persons, and their passports were forged." The Czechoslovak
intelligence service hunted for them and turned up various names:
Hans Müller is Kurt Büttner, or Walter Dahlberg; Gerd Schubert is
named Rudolf Schenk, and both are from the Dresden Gestapo;
Kersbachová's name is Käsbachová. She had been wounded, treated in
the hospital at Königstein, and died in a hospital in Dresden. In 1937
it was written that she had been seen in Switzerland. DL1CU claims
that Kersbachová was an acquaintance of Formis's from Stuttgart and
worked under pressure from the Gestapo. That, however, contradicts
what Formis said to Strasser (according to the testimony of Dr. Otto
Strasser to the police). Further, DL1CU writes that Käsbachová and
Müller were taken to a concentration camp and there — as inconvenient
witnesses — liquidated. The murderer was the third man, Alfred Naujocks.
Ivanov, in the book "The Night of Brown Shadows," states that Naujocks
appeared as Müller.
      In the state archive a large bundle of original documents on the
Formis case is stored. It is reading from which it is impossible to
tear oneself away. The murderers did not know that the transmitter was
in room no. 17. Therefore it remained preserved, survived the
occupation too, and is kept in the National Technical Museum
in Prague.
      A large training center of amateur radio sport in the GDR was
named after Rolf Formis. Formis's death was an important moment in the
issuing of decree MPT no. 904-Pp-I-1935, which establishes the
radio-electrical control service, known among amateurs as the Keser.


---


THE LAST SALUTE


---


July 22, 1936
      A single-engine Junkers 160 D approaches from Munich. At its
controls is the thirty-three-year-old Viktor Neubrandt, a Bavarian from
Munich. The radiotelegraphist, eleven years younger, Wilfried Haupt,
comes from Neukölln, near Berlin. The aircraft is new, built this year,
with a BMW Hornet 650 HP engine. The call sign is DULUR. At seven
minutes past seven he reports he is flying over Plzeň. At 7.21 he asks
Prague for information about air pressure and surface wind; at 7.22 he
receives it. It is an overcast, rainy day. He grumbles and announces
that in about 5 minutes he will be landing at the Kbely airfield.
      He does not stay long. At five minutes to eight he takes off, and
at 7.58 he is again in contact with the Prague gonio.
     OKL DE DULUR GT QAD 0753 QAB BX. (Good day, I took off
at 7.53. I am heading for Breslau.) — (The meaning of the abbreviation
QAB has since changed.)
     His watch runs behind Prague's.
     Breslau, DDU, transmits weather reports every twenty-fifth and
fifty-fifth minute of the hour on the 1260 m wave. Prague knows the
aircraft will need this information. It has it ready as a 35-word
dispatch, and at 8.02 relays it to the aircraft. The Junkers had passed
Nymburk and made itself heard again at 8.11 somewhere on the way
between Prague and the Czechoslovak border:
     L DE DUR QBF QAH 1700 QSO DDU +
      "I am flying in clouds at an altitude of 1,700 m and will now be
working with Breslau."
      Prague acknowledges. DULUR calls DDU. Breslau does not hear.
      Prague wants to help: DDU DE OKL QRZ DULUR. DDU, DULUR
is calling you! Perhaps in Breslau they have the loop turned just now
so that Prague is in a minimum. DULUR calls once more, but does not
finish.
     On the Prague–Brno–Bratislava route a Fokker IX, OKAFF, is
flying. A moment ago it took off from the Brno airfield at Černovice.
The radiotelegraphist has reported the takeoff and for now has a rest.
He does not take off the earphones, however, and watches the frequency
333 kHz. At 8.14 he intercepts a signal that excites him. Three dots,
three dashes, three dots. SOS. Then follows a dash, lasting about two
to three seconds, and it falls silent.
       OKB DE OFF = who was that?
 8.17: OKL DE OKB = who was that?
       10.10 hours: at the headquarters of the gendarmerie air patrol
in Hradec Králové the telephone rings. Prague is calling. Search for the
transport aircraft with registration DULUR on the Munich–Prague–Breslau
route. It did not reach Breslau, and at 8.14 hours it sent a distress
signal. At 10.20 hours the gendarmerie patrol is in the air, heading
for Trutnov. It flies through the space Trutnov–Žacléř–Janské Lázně–
Dvůr Králové–Úpice. It is raining heavily, the fog reaches down to
the ground in places, and visibility is poor. After forty-five minutes
of futile searching the gendarmes return to their airfield.
      Meanwhile the gendarmerie stations have obtained the first
information. Josef Propiš testified that he had seen an aircraft
falling, which twisted and turned over as it fell.
      "Mr. Propiš, can you tell us what the aircraft looked like?"
When Propiš had calmed down, it turned out that he had not in fact
seen an aircraft, but some flat object, which was falling, rotating,
and landed on a field by the Bukovina forest on the ridge that runs
north of Hořice and slopes down into the Bystřice valley. To the south
and southwest of the forest are fields that slope further into the
landscape.
      Jan Kráčmar reported that the aircraft had come from Miletín and
circled over the Bystřice basin toward Doubrava. A piece of a surface
hung on its tail. A direct witness was Stanislav Kareš of Polště no.
47. He was just working in a field when an aircraft flew in from the
direction of Miletín. At the edge of the forest it caught in the
trees, that light portion broke away, the aircraft rose, climbed, and
then flew into the forest.
      When the gendarmes arrived at point 463 near Chlumek, they found
a horrifying scene.
      The aircraft had flown into the forest against the slope, about
200 m from the southern edge. A ten-meter pine has its crown snapped
off. In the direction of entry the pines are broken off lower and
lower. About 12 m from that first pine is a pit 1 1/2 m deep and 2 1/2 m
in diameter, into which the engine plowed. The propeller broke in two.
The wreckage of the shattered aircraft flew onward by inertia almost
40 m, and the trees in that direction are snapped off at the ground,
others scraped and bent, and among them a tangle of dented and bent
sheet metal, interwoven remains of various lines and fittings. At the
front, the remains of the forward part of the fuselage, cabin, and
undercarriage. Parts of the lifting system and tail surfaces are
shattered and scattered through the impact area, the dynamo 5 m from
the engine. A piece of the left landing flap was found 200 m from the
point of impact.
      The aircraft carried no passengers. The bodies of both the pilot
and the radiotelegraphist were torn apart and disfigured beyond
recognition by sharp fragments of sheet metal. Only two pairs of
crushed watches remained. One showed 8.10, the other 8.14. A briefcase
with documents and a few small items were preserved.
      The records of radiotelegraphist Haupt agree — except for that
small time difference — with the station log of the OKL gonio. The
airmen left Prague in good condition and in good spirits. What then
happened aboard the aircraft, and what was the cause of their terrible
death, remains wrapped in mystery.
      The experts who investigated the crash of the aircraft admitted
the supposition that an engine defect had occurred. Kráčmar insists,
however, that the engine was working at full power. Kareš says he heard
the roar of the engine and a whistling. František Stehlík and his son
heard at home in Chlumek the roar of the engine, accompanied by a
hissing.
     Was a torn-off landing flap the cause of the disaster? Or did it
tear off as a consequence of the great air resistance during the
aircraft's fall?
     Did the controls fail?
     At the moment when Haupt wanted to make contact with DDU, at an
altitude of 1,700 m above the ground, something happened that rendered
the aircraft incapable of further flight.
Neubrandt could not see beneath himself. He wanted to avoid crashing
into the mountains in the Krkonoše region, turned around, and tried to
land in a place where he supposed there was flat ground. The fact that
he changed course by 180° and approached the crash site from Miletín,
that is, from the direction away from Breslau rather than toward
Breslau, and that the trees in the forest were stripped in a
southwesterly direction, suggests that the aircraft was capable of a
certain amount of maneuvering.
     Haupt sent the SOS signal and then just held the key pressed down.
There was no time for details. He evidently transmitted the dash also
so as to give the gonio stations the possibility of direction-finding
the crash site and dispatching medical aid there as quickly as
possible. Perhaps the aircraft's fall was so violent that keying was
impossible. From the cut-off trees one can judge that the aircraft
struck the ground at an angle of 70°–80°, that is, almost
perpendicular. Wilfried Haupt pressed the telegraph key to his last
breath. The transmitted dash is the last salute of the crew of aircraft
DULUR; the end of the dash is also the end of their life.
     When the Fokker OKAFF rose from the Bratislava airfield after
seven o'clock in the morning on September 11, 1937, a fire broke out
on board. The fire extinguishers did not help; the fire spread. The
pilot Selucký turned quickly around and landed.
The crew and the passengers were saved, but the aircraft burned up.


---


FROM SHORT WAVES TO VERY SHORT WAVES


---


On the fifth of May 1935 the day was May-like enough, but the weather
was rather early-spring.
For consolation, people invented for such days sayings like "in May
the shepherd's staff must not dry out," "a cold May — a paradise in
the barn," and the like.
    Professor Vopička listens to the monotonous impacts of wheels on
rail joints, observes the overcast landscape, watches the telegraph
wires rising and falling between the poles. He is carrying a bulky
rucksack and several heavy bags. He has a charged four-volt accumulator,
an anode battery of 32 flat cells, transmitting and receiving
apparatus, earphones, provisions, sweaters and outer garments, and an
unwired wooden monster — an antenna with a reflector for the 5-meter
wave. He is heading from Mladá Boleslav to the Krkonoše mountains. He
had not originally intended to go by train.
He inquired of acquaintances who owned automobiles (there were not
yet so many of them in Mladá Boleslav at that time), but they were all
"unfortunately already engaged." Well then.
He will go by train; what can one do. Someone should have gone with
him and helped him with the luggage.
      "138 km by train is an expensive pleasure, and the cable car to
Černá hora (28 Kč) is still more expensive. In short, I had no one,"
he recalls.
      Very short waves were, from the mid-twenties to the end of the
twenties, a laboratory affair. In issue no. 5 of Čs. radiosvět for 1931
an article by Ivan Šimon appeared, describing a simple transmitting
and receiving set for waves of about 3 m. With this set it was possible
to test the polarization and reflections of the waves and to carry
signals over several tens of meters. By 1932 the first amateur field
experiments had already taken place.
      In 1933, every Tuesday from 21.00 to 22.00 CET, station OK1AA
operated in the 56 MHz band with a transmitter of 10 W input. The
exchange of information about these experiments took place every
Thursday from 23.00 to 24.00 hours on the frequency 3,548.5 kHz.
      In the 56 MHz band Motyčka (OK1AB) and Bollard (OK1MC) worked
too. In Brno there began OK2DD (Laušmann), OK2FF (Kott), and OK2BR
(Petr); in Znojmo OK2SI (Václavík).
      About very short waves it was already known that they propagate
similarly to rays of light, and the greatest hope of a contact is
between points in direct line of sight. The amateurs sought out
high-lying places. On September 17, 1934, Eng. Schäferling, OK1AA,
climbed to the top of the antenna mast
in Liblice, whence he made contact with Prague, OK1AB and OK1MC,
and with OK1AW + OK1AQ on the hill Oškobrh. The effort to make contact
with Praděd, on which Fritz Wolletz, OK1RM, had settled, had no
success. On the night of the solstice of 1935 Schäferling, OK1AA,
transmitted from the Krkonoše. His signals were intercepted by Bollard,
OK1MC, on the commanding bridge of the stadium on Petřín, and by
Motyčka, OK1AB, at the lookout tower near Živohošť.
      Professor Vopička, OK1VP, would not gladly remain behind. He
transmits little.
All his free time is taken up by written agenda, the drafting of
mimeographed circulars, negotiations, consultations, and meetings. The
battle between KVAČ and SKEČ had ended, and at the founding general
assembly on March 23, 1932, in the Czechoslovak Radioclub at Konviktská
St. no. 5 in Prague, a new organization arose from the merger of the
two rival societies — the Czechoslovak Amateur Transmitters
(ČAV). Dr. Šafránek was elected chairman, Eng. Bísek,
OK1BK, vice-chairman, and Professor Vopička, OK1VP, first secretary.
On January 11, 1933, the liquidation general meeting of SKEČ took
place. The protocol of this general meeting, together with the minutes
of the liquidation general meeting of KVAČ, were sent for publication
in the Official Gazette, and thereby a period was put after the
fratricidal war of the two societies (at least outwardly). On December
7, 1932, at Postal Office No. 1 in Jindřišská Street in Prague, post
office box no. 69 was established, which it was possible to maintain
through the whole occupation and which served the amateurs in 1984
as well. On Professor Vopička's shoulders lay also a considerable part
of the burden of preparing the 1st national congress of the
Czechoslovak Amateur Transmitters
in Turnov, July 5–8, 1934.
     Hundreds of letters passed through his hands in the last few
years. The professor knows what the amateurs lack: a coherent
exposition of the basic aspects of amateur transmission, on the
technical side and on the operational side alike.
     So he sets to writing. There arose the engaging booklet "On the
Short Waves," which answered all the principal questions that arise in
the mind of a new aspirant to amateur transmission. The publishers
looked at this work rather skeptically. The professor had the cover
printed at a print shop and the whole text of the book reproduced
lithographically.
     The booklet was a success. The entire edition was sold out in
record time.
Orbis learned of it and proposed to Professor Vopička a new, expanded
edition. Vopička gazes out of the railway carriage window and ponders.
Very short waves must not be missing there, and instructions for
equipment for the 5 m band...
     He undertook the next journey to the Krkonoše on July 25, 1935.
From Luční hora he made contact with Kott, OK1FF, who was camping on
Ládví, and with Weirauch, OK1AW, on his beloved Oškobrh. Distances
respectable for that time: 70 and 55 km. During the OK1VP–OK1AW
contact, Weirauch was invited to come visit him. He needed no urging.
Already the following day they transmitted together from Pláň and
spoke over a distance of 130 km with Schäferling, OK1AA. On July 27
they set out across Výrovka to Studničná hora. From somewhere in the
middle they contacted Burda, OK1BM (near Mladá Boleslav), and again
Kott, OK1FF. The mountains were in clouds, it rained persistently.
Soon they were both soaked to the skin. When the water had penetrated
onto the tube sockets, into the capacitor, and onto the chokes, and
had soaked through the cords, it was the end. They trudged to the hut
on Výrovka and got into a kettle of raging passions, from which
thunders and lightnings flashed. The packed tavern
was waiting for the broadcast of the Sparta–Juventus match, and the
radio did not work.
     Dozens of pairs of eyes fixed on their transceivers, antennas, and
earphones.
     "Ahh... the gentlemen are from the 'foch' [the technical branch],
aren't they?" — and they were already making room for them and leading
them to the radio. Water dripped from the soaked clothes of both
Weirauch and Vopička, but no matter. Fortunately they had tools with
them, and some measuring instrument, and a few minutes before the
match they succeeded in getting the radio working. Only then could
they change clothes, dry off, and warm themselves with hot tea.
     All previous records were surpassed by Prof. Vopička, OK1VP, and
Eng. Schäferling, OK1AA, with the contact Klínovec–Studničná hora,
over a distance of about 200 km.
     In 1935 there appeared in the 56 MHz band the transmission "from
the hearth" [a regular on-the-air gathering]. At its birth the stations
were OK1AA, OK1AB, OK1MC, OK1SG, and OK1XW. The meeting was always
on Thursday at 18.00 CET. Great excitement was aroused at the time by
the news that the stations OK2BR (Zdeněk Petr) and OK2AK (Hans
Plisch) had succeeded in receiving, on Praděd, the sound of Berlin
television. (It was on June 28 and 29, 1935.)


---


A TRAGIC YEAR


---


Before World War II, about three and a half million Germans lived
in Czechoslovakia, especially in the border regions, and a large
portion of them formed the mass base of Henlein's Sudeten German
Party. Nazi policy strove to unite all Germans into a single state
(ein Volk - ein Reich) and from there toward world hegemony of the
superior Germanic race. Austria and Czechoslovakia were its first
gradual targets. How immediate the looming danger was, the workers
of the intelligence services realized best. They hunted for Reich
German agent networks, struggled with German and Hungarian espionage,
and tracked the intensive war preparations of the Wehrmacht. Border
fortifications were being built assiduously. Gendarmerie patrols were
stationed in the premises of broadcasting stations.
      In February 1937 the Hodža government agreed with democratic
German politicians on adjusting the representation of Germans in the
public administration, on language practice and other matters. The
Henleinists sternly rejected this agreement and provoked disturbances
and a brawl in the chamber of deputies. In the autumn of the same
year our intelligence service learns of the transmitters of Nazi
espionage in Prague, in Plzeň, in Litoměřice, in České Budějovice,
in Brno and in Košice. People go to work and to amusement alike,
living through their everyday worries and small joys. Amateurs argue
at the general meeting of the ČAV (Czechoslovak Radio Club) in
Prague from 28 March, 15:45, until half past three in the morning
of 29 March. A special edition of the communist daily Moravian
Equality (February 1938) characterizes the situation with the words:
"Our people resemble a carelessly sleeping family whose roof is
catching fire above their heads."
17 March 1938, 56 MHz band.
      1003 PD DE AB = Douglas flew over toward Ruzyň+
      1013 KQ DE RY = R5
      1023 OM DE EKX = R5
      1156 AB DE PD = 23 lighter aircraft flew over Braník,
                            6 bombers separated toward the SE +
      1256 to all from the central station = end of alarm+
 OK1AB takes part in the exercise of the Civil Anti-Aircraft Defense.
The desire of radio amateurs to serve in the defense of the state
manifested itself ever more intensely in the tense atmosphere of the
second half of the thirties. In 1938 OK1BZ recommended in Short
Waves not only the use of amateur equipment and operating networks,
but above all the training of conscripts, both practical and
theoretical. It must be stated, however, that the authorities of the
time took an attitude of rejection or indifference toward such
efforts of the amateurs.
     On 23 July 1937 an exercise of the Civil Anti-Aircraft Defense
with the participation of amateur stations took place in Hradec
Králové. The KSR (State Communications Administration) intercepted
the correspondence and made a notification to the MPT (Ministry
of Posts and Telegraphs) for violation of the concession conditions,
with a request for instructions on how to proceed against the
accused. The matter was settled by the directorate of posts and
telegraphs in Pardubice with an emphatic declaration that the
participation of amateurs in the exercise of the Civil Anti-Aircraft
Defense is in the public interest. The one who had the most to fear
was OK1BP, Bedřich Pomezný, who — as a gendarmerie staff sergeant —
could least of all afford any entanglement.
     Amateur transmitters had their participation in actions for the
defense of the state directly forced upon them, and that already at
the stroke of twelve.
     At the end of summer 1938 Motyčka worked exclusively on 56 MHz.
On 22 September several brief but telling entries are recorded in
his diary: "Tower." "The Prague line." "Krocínka." "The chateau."
An air raid on Prague is anticipated. The amateurs stand watch.
Nerves are stretched to breaking point.
     On 2 September the Gestapo and SS are put on alert in Germany
and the requisitioning of motor vehicles begins. In Berlin a
conference of senior commanders of the army, navy and air force
convenes.
     On the evening of 12 September, after Hitler's speech in
Nuremberg, violence breaks out in the border regions, in which
21 people are killed and 75 wounded. The government declares
martial law in the border districts.
     The war was to break out on Wednesday, 28 September at 14:00.
That morning the British Prime Minister Neville Chamberlain reviewed
Hitler's speech of the previous day, delivered at the Sports Palace
in Berlin. He found in it several friendly sentences addressed to
Mussolini. He asked the officials of the Foreign Office to contact
the British ambassador in Rome, Lord Perth. Could Mussolini prevail
upon Hitler to wait another 24 hours? Lord Perth went to the Foreign
Minister Count Ciano, who immediately had himself announced to
Mussolini. The latter telephoned Hitler, who in the meantime had
received Chamberlain's telegram. The Munich conference came about,
at which Germany, Italy, Great Britain and France agreed to the
cession of a third of Czechoslovak territory to Germany, including
the fortifications and important industry. "Under the cover of
phrases about saving world peace, a deed was committed whose
shamelessness surpasses everything that has happened since the
(first) world war," comments the Soviet Pravda on 4 October 1938.
     For the amateurs these events meant the revocation of their
concessions and the confiscation of their transmitters. The
occupation of the Czech lands and the Second World War put the
final full stop to hopes of their return.
     And one more interesting thing from the year 1938: we bid
farewell to the term "electron tube." We shall say "elektronka."


---


■ ••• H

After the occupation of Bohemia and Moravia the Reich German chief
of civil defense issued a decree that by Saturday, 18 March 1939,
15:00, all firearms including ammunition had to be surrendered, even
air rifles (with the exception of historical weapons). Another decree
contained a ban on possessing transmitting equipment.
     Weirauch packed the transmitter (ECO with 802) into a large box,
tied it with twine and laid a drum revolver on top of the box.
House searches began at the amateurs' homes.
     They came to the Weirauchs on 20 March. There were eight of
them, in uniforms, one passenger car and one truck. Alois happened to
be in Prague on that very day. His mother gave them the box with the
revolver as well. They wanted to see where it had been installed, so
she led them into the adjoining room, where the rest of the equipment
still stood. They inspected everything, took the telegraph key, the
microphone and the unassembled receiver. They left the power supply
for the transmitter and the receiver in place. They left the revolver
there too. That was not their business — they were only after the
transmitters. All the station diaries, photographs and papers,
exemplarily kept with Weirauchian care since 1927, Alois had hidden
in good time, and thus saved relics of great value, important for
the study of the history of amateur radio.

OCCUPATION


---


The Vinohrady street climbs a steep hill. Past the allotment colony
"At the Beam" we reach the highest point, a wooden triangulation
tower. Across the gardens stretches the little Kejbaly lane. Among
the group of low flat-roofed houses there stands out a one-story
sprawling building no. 80 with wide windows and a red pantile
hipped roof.
Far below us are the Brno town hall, Petrov, the Maloměřice cement
works. In the distance all around, dark wooded ridges and hills.
     The oldest inhabitants have lived here since 1945. None of them
knows that in house no. 80 (formerly Vinohrady no. 54) there used
to be the office of the Brno KSR (State Communications
Administration). An ideal spot, high up, quiet, far from the tram
and from any sources of interference.
     The task of the Radio-electrical Control Service was to monitor
licensed transmitting stations, hunt for unlicensed receiving
stations and, when necessary, inspect the licensed ones, trace and
eliminate sources of interference, inspect both the manufactories
and the shops of radiotelegraph and radiotelephone equipment, and
watch persons who by their education, knowledge, abilities and
inclinations might become clandestine radio amateurs. It had at its
disposal monitoring stations and search squads attached to the
telegraph construction offices. It was founded in 1935; it was headed
by departmental councillor ing. Jaromír Svoboda with his deputies,
technical commissioners ing. Jaroslav Jirounek and ing. Josef Beňa.
     In the autumn of 1935 the recruitment of the first workers and
the purchase of automobiles took place. The choice lay between the
Walter-Bijou, Praga Lady, Tatra 75 and Škoda Rapid. The main
requirements were reliability at high speeds, an inconspicuous
appearance, powerful headlamps, loud horns, unbreakable glass, a
reinforced accumulator and a freewheel clutch. (The Škoda-Superb cars
won the greatest favor in the KSR.) Suitable places were sought for
"listening posts": detached buildings with the possibility of
installing the apparatus on upper floors. No high-voltage or similar
power lines in the vicinity, the inhabitants of the house must be
reliable, access, lodging and board without difficulties, and
eventually the possibility of stretching an antenna. HRO and
One-ten receivers, avometers, tools and other necessary props were
purchased.
     The Prague KSR settled in Krč, in villa no. 250 in K Habrovce
street. Ing. Mirko Schäferling became its chief. Some people at
the ministry found it unfitting that it should be precisely a man
who had broadcast illegally ("načerno") for so many years. Yet none
of the possible candidates could equal him in knowledge, practical
experience and especially in mastery of the Morse code and operating
procedure. In the end they agreed that a former poacher makes a good
gamekeeper.
     The Brno KSR was entrusted to ing. Lhotský, OK2LS. (Its activity
began in his flat at Sladová street no. 16.) His deputy became ing.
Svatopluk Krčma, OK2XY. When he applied for a concession on
8 August 1935 (he had been "infected" by Pavlíček, OK2CC), he asked
the MPT to excuse him from the examination, since he was an
electrical engineer and a second lieutenant of the telegraph corps.
The ministry complied, but did not excuse him from the practical
part. He had to appear on 30 October 1935 at the post office in
Uherské Hradiště, where the postal secretary František Dvořák
played him the telegram: IVAN KASKA BEOGRAD = URGENT = QUOD DEUS
CONJUNXIT HOMO NON SEPARAT +; then one Italian, one Ukrainian and
finally one Polish. Ing. Krčma had to take everything down by ear
and write it straight onto telegraph forms.
      Ota Červený, Alois Horký, OK2HY, Alfred Šuster, Leo Tvarůžek,
Augustin Stejskal, Emil Horníček, OK2RG, the former military
radiotelegraphist Gabrhelík worked at the KSR, and in 1937 Gustav
Košulič, OK2GU, joined there for 700 crowns a month. He was a
trained electrical technician and had served at the military radio
station on Špilberk. In spring 1938, at a cimbalom-band dance in the
Slovácká búda on Jakubské square, he met Marie Uhrová. They took
whole-day walks in the woods around Brno, went canoeing together,
and on 14 October 1940 they married.
      By then the KSR no longer existed and Košulič worked for the
switching-off service.
      On 17 March 1939 they summoned engineers Lhotský and Krčma to
the post directorate in Kounicova street. Gestapo men were waiting
for them there and began questioning them. At the KSR they had in
time burned all the papers, but everything including the cipher keys
was preserved at the directorate with government councillor Buček,
who had always wished to be informed in detail about every step of
the KSR and required a copy of every record, every report and every
letter. The next day a house search took place both at the Krčmas'
in Žabovřesky and at the Lhotskýs'. Both engineers were arrested,
taken to the police headquarters, from there to the prison at Cejl,
and a few days later to Špilberk, where, together with a number of
others, they were locked up in the chapel.
      "I at least had the luck to have a stairway under my head at
night," recalls ing. Krčma.
      At the end of April they returned home. We met for a little
chat: ing. Lhotský, OK2LS, ing. Krčma, OK2XY, Emil Horníček, OK2RG,
Josef Běloch, OK2UA and myself. Ing. Lhotský had a large gaping
wound on his forehead.
      "The millstone," he said curtly.
      "That was their usual amusement," explained ing. Krčma. "The
prisoner had to lie on his stomach, bend double, grasp his feet from
behind, and roll on his belly as the SS man kicked him. And it was
all one to him where he kicked."
