      "I met the one who did it to me yesterday on Česká street,"
said Lhotský. "He came rushing up to me like an old acquaintance and
asked how I was doing. The idiot!"
      On the way to the railway station I met Mr. Veliký.
      "How is OKB doing?"
      "Don't even ask! In foreign service!! We are not alone there
anymore. And we are not OKB, but DYB; Zlín is DYA, Budějovice DYC."
    Shortly after I was walking down Minoritská street with a
recommendation letter to the post office. A familiar face smiled at
me at the little window. It was Mr. Petrů.


---


■1 • • •   M


---


The pilot Radoslav Selucký had his hands full. Not at the airfield.
In the mountains. With the manager of the Daněk hotel in the
Beskydy, he organized the crossings of Czechoslovak airmen into
Poland. War between Germany and Poland was imminent. They
escorted several dozen of our airmen across. Then Selucký himself
set out on the journey.
      After the collapse of Poland he reached the Red Army via Malé
Bronovice, Tarnopol and Kamenets-Podolsky to Jarmolintsy. There he
was invited to cooperate with Soviet intelligence. Together with him
Vladislav Bobák, Miloslav Hůla, Jaroslav Lonek and Jan Vycpálek
completed the intelligence course. By February 1940 they were all
back in the Protectorate.
      They gathered information about airfields, aircraft production,
the deployment of military units, the quantity and type of armament
and equipment, the arms industry, troop movements and
militarily important matériel, new combat means. Drawings, plans,
samples of anti-tank shells and other materials they sent through
couriers to the Soviet consulate general in the villa Tereza in
Prague. Reports are delivered by radio. It is faster, the risk of
personal contacts that can be traced falls away; but danger lurked
here in the form of interception and direction-finding.
      One of Selucký's important informants was also the
radiotelegraphist Miloslav Janáček, who in 1934 moved from the RSN
to Czechoslovak Airlines, from there to Air France, and during the
war he was assigned to the Letov works.
      Two of the group underwent special radiotelegraph training in
the course: Selucký and Hůla. For the latter this was especially
easy, because he worked as a radiotelegraphist at ČSA.
      A participant in the foreign resistance, the airman Miroslav
Plecitý (later, in 1942, shot down by German anti-aircraft defense
over Bremen), left behind in Prague a sweetheart, Marta Delamová.
They had met in the aeroclub. Marta was involved in gliding. Hůla
and Bobák established contact with her (they had messages for her
from Plecitý). Marta gave Hůla lodging with her at first and
procured him a room for transmitting in Rumunská street no. 14 at
Mrs. Adamčíková's; she found a flat for Bobák in Přemyslovská
street no. 11, and Selucký settled at Střížkov at Arnošt Kredba's;
the latter no longer flew and worked as a technical clerk. Vycpálek
lived at Blanická 26. With a subsidy from the Soviet intelligence
service he bought a stationer's shop in Kamenická 44. He was a
trained electromechanic, had worked at Avia, at the Siemens works
and at the airfield. In the Lucerna arcade, at the firm Rádio Brehm,
he bought a nine-tube Detrola radio, which was the first source of
parts for the transmitters.
      Vycpálek's stationer's shop was an excellent "counter" where
the agents could meet inconspicuously, hand over and receive
materials, exchange a few quick words. Vycpálek's flat was the third
station of Hůla's transmitter, which broadcast on the 34 m wave
under the call sign HBT. The Soviet counter-station identified
itself as VXW and replied on 32 m. Selucký also worked with
station VXW, namely as HCL on 64.3 m; he listened to station VXW
on 43.5 m. HCL's first attempts to establish contact did not go
well. Selucký was at a loss. He was a pilot and did not have enough
experience with radio technology. He went to see Jan Šanda, who had
flown ČSA routes since 1928 and was now assigned to the tram depot
in Košíře. He lived in Ruzyně in a one-story villa with a balcony,
in the middle of a garden. In the attic he had a workshop with
tools and measuring instruments. He put Selucký's transmitter in
order and promised to build another.
      The group's radius of action widened. Chrudim, Pardubice,
Choceň, Zlín, Adamov, Moravská Ostrava, Kladno, České Budějovice,
Napajedla, Mělník, Plzeň, Česká Třebová — these are the main places
where they collected their knowledge. They even dispatched their own
agent to Austria. He brought back an important piece of information
from Vienna. The group had a reliable and capable courier: Marta
Delamová.
      "I had a handbag with double leather and double lining, a
zipper on top. In it I carried the documents. It was made specially
for this purpose. Bobák brought it to me," she recalls years later.
      "I had two identity cards. One in my real name, the other in
the name Jiřina Procházková. Both flawless."
      At the end of summer 1940 Bobák was sent to Brno. Marta went
with him. They lodged in Slavíkova street no. 17 (now Došlíkova 12),
in a two-story residential villa, not yet quite finished. The
staircase was being completed and the tiles at the entrance had
not yet been laid.
      From the corridor one entered a small vestibule. The door on
the left led into the kitchen, on the right into the room. Through
the kitchen window they looked into the garden, from the room into
Slavíkova street.
      Much work awaited Bobák: the Zbrojovka arms works, the
airfield and a host of other things. As soon as they were settled
in, he set about the task of the first order: communications. He
established contact with Gustav Košulič, OK2GU.
      How? At a distance of thirty or forty years, the tangle of
intricate events is hard to unravel.
      Gustav's mother, Eva Košuličová, maintains: "Gusta was brought
there by Majka's relatives. Her father was a stoker at the ČSD
railways, a convinced communist. He supplied Bobák with reports
from the railway, about movements of troops and ammunition."
      Marie Košuličová: "Gusta was introduced to Bobák by Horký. And
where he got to know Bobák, he will tell you himself."
     Alois Horký, ex-OK2HY: "It was Košulič who pulled me in with
Bobák."
     Marta Delamová: "I don't know. Radio was not my business."


---


mm ••• mm


---


Sometime at the end of August or the beginning of September 1940,
Jan Habrda, OK2AH, and Josef Sedláček, OK1SE, met in front of
telegraph construction office 7 in Michle. They set out into the
fields toward Bohdalec. They had worked together at the Prague KSR
until its dissolution.
      "I called you, Josef, so you could advise me," Habrda began.
"A man came to see me, I had never seen him in my life. He says he
is an agent, a resistance worker, come from abroad, and Košulič
sent him."
      "Did Gusta send some letter or note?"
      "No. But that man knows a lot about us. He knows you,
Bollard and Kott by name. In the end he pulled a pistol out of his
pocket."
      "As an argument?"
      "Well, probably so I would believe him."
      "What if he is a provocateur?"
      "I got married recently. I wouldn't like to get into trouble.
But I don't want to refuse either."
      "You should verify it with Košulič. Send him a telegram or
a letter."
      Habrda wrote a registered letter to Košulič and promptly
received a positive answer.
      "Bobák came to us shortly after our wedding," says Habrda's
wife. "My husband was not at home, so he came once more, they went
out together, and when he returned he told us what they wanted of
him and that they would use the back room of our flat (U smyčky 9,
at that time the terminus of tram no. 14). His father, mother and
brother were with us. The father was blind. He was terribly against
it and warned that it would end badly."
      The Košulič couple arrived in Prague. Habrda showed them
everywhere, gave Gusta a microphone as a keepsake. Habrda would not
broadcast. His circumstances did not permit him to go somewhere to
transmit. But he would build the transmitter. The transmitting he
would leave to Košulič.
      He set about building an electron-coupled push-push with two
nine-watt tubes of type 59. These were American tubes popular among
amateurs (half the price of the equivalent European ones), which
were used as the output stage in the popular Pento SW3AC receiver.
But precisely these were what he lacked. Sedláček helped out.
Habrda built the transmitter together with the receiver into a
suitcase, which Marta Delamová took to Brno. Selucký drilled
Košulič in the operating rules, and on the 64.3 m wave the station
HBK sounded, in contact with the Soviet KYR (52.8 m).
      Besides tubes Habrda needed other components, and for these
he approached Vladimír Kott, OK1FF.
      "I would introduce you to the leader of the group; you should
advise him how to organize radio communications," and he arranged
a meeting at the Fénix café. Kott was without question the most
experienced of the whole group. By the end of the twenties he was
already devoting himself to listening to telegraphy on short waves.
And when in 1930, at Zdeněk Petr's in Brno, he saw a transmitter
for the first time in his life, he built one too. In 1932 he took
the examination and went straight into long-distance operation, DX,
in the 20 m band. In DX, technique, experience and knowledge of
operating procedure came into their own. Kott truly excelled in
that field and reached a leading position. As one of the
longest-serving employees of the KSR he also knew the technique of
searching for and direction-finding stations.
      Kott gave Bobák a whole series of good pieces of advice,
especially concerning transmitting sites, frequencies and handling.
      Košulič was an enthusiast for broadcasting. In the army, on
the KSR network, and now again. Once he said at work to Horký:
"Today I passed 600 groups."
      "Be careful," admonished ing. Krčma. He knew how the
authorities hunted for clandestine transmitters. It even went as
far as spark "stations" with induction coils and coherers in school
cabinets. On 20 May 1940 he had, with a colleague from the
switching-off service, ing. Kuchař, to seal an "apparatus for the
demonstration of electric oscillation" at the girls' secondary
school on Babák square. Next in line came the girls' secondary
school in Augustinská street and the II secondary school in
Vranovská street.
      "Aah, they are dim!" Košulič brushed him off. "A circus of
tin cans!"
      He had no inkling that the German interception service at the
Institute of Noblewomen in Prague on Hradčany had already caught
HBT and HCL and other stations, and that its telegraphists — and
not only at the Institute of Noblewomen in Prague — were on
receive day and night. Since the wavelengths were not changed, not
one session escaped it.
     The Germans had a secret weapon against clandestine
transmitters: the Messempfänger ESD with a frequency range of 90 to
450 MHz in four bands and a precision measuring instrument at the
output. The CO and ECO transmitters were solo-oscillators and had a
whole fan of harmonics. (It was customary for amateur stations to
call "CQ TEN" in the 10 m band, to distinguish themselves from the
harmonics of higher bands...). The ESD receiver (also called RS)
heard harmonics up into the VHF range, and thereby made it possible
to locate the station. With frame antennas it was possible, even on
short waves, to determine with considerable approximation where
the transmission was coming from. The German experts immediately
recognized the Soviet method of encipherment. The frequency and
volume of dispatches and the result of direction-finding gave them a
picture of the organization that had appeared in their preserve.
The Prague Gestapo informed Berlin and the offices in the
Protectorate. The combing of suspicious places began.
     Hůla moved to Ostrava. There he had a new wave: 67.4 m.
Counter-station 54.2 m. The call signs remained. He was also assigned
a new basis for encipherment: Temptation in Budapest by Kôrmendi.
     At the radio station in Prague Vladimír Novák took up duty.
Call sign FRS, wave 36.2 m (the Soviets M2T on 32.3). He enciphers
under J. V. Rosúlek: The Woman in the Fortress.
     With Bobák there was another radioman: Alois Horký, OK2HY, an
experienced radio mechanic. He helped Košulič solve some technical
problems, and at Hubert Svoboda's farm in Křetín he tested the new
transmitter.
     In the last days of September 1940, before departing for
Plzeň, Selucký met on Jungmannova street in Prague a classmate from
the aviation school in Cheb, Jaroslav Bednář. They went for a
drink. Selucký saw in him a colleague of the kind he had associated
with since his arrival from the Soviet Union. He did not suspect
that Bednář worked for the Nazis and was one of their most capable
informers. When, with the routine of an experienced type-caster, he
began telling how he was a merchant, how he supplied various
military units, and laid his order book on the table, Selucký fell
for it.
     At the next meeting Gestapo agents waited at a distance. From
that moment they never let Selucký out of their sight and attached
themselves to the heels of everyone Selucký met.


---


On Saturday, 14 March 1940, in the afternoon, Gusta Košulič bathed,
put on his Sunday clothes, and by eight in the evening was getting
ready to go out. Marie knew he was going to transmit.
     "When will you come home, Gusta?"
     "Very late at night or not until morning. Definitely don't wait
up for me."
     Majka Košuličová was expecting a child. With tenderness in her
eyes she looked her husband over. He wore an elegant gray suit, a
plush coat, new shoes and a new hat. It suited him.
     Gusta read her thoughts:
     "Look at your boy! Aren't I a dashing fellow."
     When he had said goodbye to his wife, he pondered for a moment.
First he should ride to Freedom Square (he fundamentally refused to
recognize the new name — Hitler Square) and see whether anyone was
strolling about near the clock. (That clock stood where Masaryk
street — Göring street under the Protectorate — ran into Freedom
Square. The people of Brno used to arrange meetings there. At any
hour of the day a few people stood about there, and it was not
conspicuous when someone from Bobák's group was among them. It was
the sign that all was well in Slavíkova street and that it was
possible to transmit.)
     "Nonsense," Gusta waved his hand, and he did not ride to
Freedom Square. In Slavíkova street he passed through the gate,
unlocked the house door and entered the corridor. He locked it
behind him and climbs the stairs upward. He unlocked the door of
Bobák's flat.
     It is empty here. Marta has evidently not yet returned from
Ostrava and Bobák is who knows where. Košulič lays aside his coat
and hat and unpacks the station. From the curtain rod by the
kitchen window he takes out the materials for transmission. Long
before the session he switches on the receiver. He listens to the
lively static, finds familiar stations and signals that appear only
once, like someone passing through a living promenade from who knows
where to who knows where. KYR appeared for a second exactly on
time, and reacted immediately. Košulič lives through the same
excitement as once during sessions on the military network at
Špilberk, during amateur contacts and during radio links between the
individual KSR offices.
     "A ferocious telegraphist," ing. Krčma characterized him. "That
one would transmit from morning to evening."
     He feels euphoria as he realizes that every touch of the key
carries far away, somewhere to Kiev... He is aware that he is now a
soldier. He is fighting. Against an enemy whom he hates as an
occupier, and also because it drove him from his beloved, favorite
work. Quickly and precisely he taps out the groups of digits.
     Suddenly he hears a clatter and a crash. He tears off the
earphones, jumps up, but the handcuffs are already on his wrists,
and two men are searching him to see whether he has a weapon. The
cabinet doors stand open.
     He did not come to breakfast. Maňka cooked lunch, set the
table for two, but again nothing. In the morning she was still calm;
by afternoon she is already angry, but fear grips her more and more.
At half past four the bell finally rings.
     "That's about time! But now I'll leave you out there a while,
so you see what that costs you. And I'll give you a proper talking-to."
     The bell was impatient. When she opened it, there was some
strange man behind the door. He thrust his foot into the door and
wedged his way in. Another one behind him. They carried out a
thorough house search.

mm        mm


---


On 12 March 1941 Marta is getting ready to go to Ostrava. Hůla has
prepared samples of armor plate and production drawings, which must
be delivered to the Soviet resident in Prague.
     At five in the morning someone rings. Marta goes to open the
door. The Gestapo! Both Bobák and Ma are armed. But there is no
time. They throw themselves at Bobák. Marta manages to grab her
little bag, some papers, and locks herself in the toilet.
     "I tore up all the documents one by one and flushed them
away... Then the Browning and the identity card. If they had found
those on me, it would have been the end. I looked around
desperately for what to do with the ID, and finally I managed to
stuff it under the rear, screwed-on part of the toilet-seat lid."
      On the door of Bobák's former flat there hangs a metal
elliptical plate bearing the name Müller.
      "When we moved in here," says Mrs. Müllerová, "the flat was
empty. Bare walls. But I remember that after the war Marta Delamová
came, saying she had an identity card under the toilet lid here,
and indeed it was there."
      "And I still have it to this day. I threw the Browning into
the water tank up above and began flushing the cartridges down. But
mistake! They would not flush. The water just ran over them. So I
fished them out again. And by then the Gestapo men were already
breaking into the toilet. I wound the cartridges into the
handkerchief, which I kept holding in my hand. We went out. Two in
front of us, two behind us, and one between me and Bobák."
      She managed to inconspicuously drop some of the cartridges
into the snow in front of the house, but she got rid of the rest
only in the prison at Cejl, where the toilet had a hole in the
ground. They searched her thoroughly — she was not even allowed to
keep the little chain with the locket on her neck — but it occurred
to no one to unroll her crumpled, damp handkerchief.
      In March 1941 the Mlčoch family, the "officially" oldest
tenants, were already living in that villa.
      "Did you know Bobák, Mr. Mlčoch?"
      "I didn't associate with him in any way. Once I went down to
the cellar and Bobák was there. Something as if he was burying in
the coal. I took them for the husband and his wife. How it really
was, I only learned after the war, when Marta came for the identity
card."
     "When they were arrested, that must have been a great stir,
wasn't it?"
     "Not at all. Bobák and Marta lived withdrawn lives; no one knew
about them, and no one even noticed that they had vanished. Only a
neighbor noticed somehow and said there were Gestapo men in that
flat. They kept watch there for about three weeks."
     "What antenna was on that house?"
     "I know nothing about any antenna being here."
     "Wasn't there one in the attic under the roof?"
     "Certainly not. We hung laundry there. I would have noticed."
     On 4 March 1941 Selucký was arrested, on 6 March Hůla and
Vycpálek, and in March Lonek.
     The Gestapo's first concern was the cipher keys. It deployed
every means of psychological and physical pressure, such that not
one of the arrested could endure them. By deciphering the
intercepted dispatches the Gestapo obtained a detailed picture of
the activity of the whole network and could begin investigating the
individual cases.


---


m
      Gusta Košulič sits in a cell of the prison at the police
headquarters. What he had considered absolutely impossible has
happened. If only he had... — but that no longer has any meaning.
Maňka was waiting for him, was looking forward... After all it is
only a few steps from here to the "šalina" streetcar, the number one
or the number six, and in a little while he would be home in
Královo Pole, in Purkyňova street. In Heršpice his parents are
waiting for him, at work his colleagues... And his brother
Arnošt... The thoughts swarm. Each one pierces his heart like a
sharp knife. He feels the pain up into his throat. If he were not a
man, he would break into tears.
      He has behind him the first preliminary interrogation. Are
the Gestapo men brutal? Nonsense.
      "Mr. Košulič, you were caught in the act. Denial would have no
meaning. Tell us everything and you will go home. A confession is a
mitigating circumstance and can even be grounds for immunity from
punishment. You want to go home, don't you?"
      "Yes, certainly..."
      "Did you broadcast to Russia?"
      "Yes, I broadcast." (He knows it anyway, one way or the other.)
      "What was in those telegrams?"
      "I don't know. I received them already prepared."
      "Who else went to Bobák's?"
      "I don't know. I never saw anyone there."
      The interrogator does not press.
      "Think over all the details once more, and then we will let
you go. We have no interest in keeping you here."
      On 17 March he writes a postcard to Maňka. He needs to
exchange those fine clothes for old, worn ones. "I hope I shall
return soon."


---


The Gestapo knows that Marta Delamová lived with Bobák and heard a
great deal. She must therefore be initiated into many things. She
will be an important witness and will help uncover further links
and contacts.
      Right after the opening sentences of the interrogation the
Gestapo men fly into a rage. Yes, Marta lives with Bobák. She is
his fiancée and his mistress. She keeps house for him, but knows
nothing. She does not know Košulič, and at the confrontation she
tells him so to his face. Perhaps he was there sometime, but a lot
of people come to see Bobák, and she takes no interest in them.
The Gestapo already knows that a courier named Jiřina exists. They
pounce on Marta:
      "You are Jiřina!"
      "No. I am Marta. I know no Jiřina."
      The Gestapo knows a great deal, but has no evidence. The
first blows fall; Marta does not give up. If they had her false
identity card in their hands, it would be bad. But nothing is lost
yet. And Bobák promised her he would not betray her.
      The interrogators begin to understand that they will get
nothing new out of her. But she should at least admit that she knew
of Bobák's activity. Otherwise they have nothing to present against
her in court. They press burning cigarettes against her bare legs.
The pain is unbearable and does not stop even after Marta is taken
back to her cell. Marta cannot sit or stand, cannot kneel or lie
down.
      Meanwhile the Gestapo works up the findings it has obtained
from the investigation of the group. It also profits from Bobák's
notebook, in which they found a series of names. For a long time,
for example, they could not figure out who Kott was. Of Marta
Delamová there is no mention anywhere, and all confrontations
failed. The Gestapo men make a last attempt. They order that Marta
must stand in front of her cell until she confesses. By day, by
night, without food, without drink, without sleep.
      It is boring; Marta examines on the cell door details she had
never noticed before. Behind her back she hears stirrings,
footsteps, muffled voices and talk. She knows prison life well and
knows what these sounds mean: breakfast, distribution of work,
escort to interrogation, arrival of new prisoners — Zuwachs. At
night it is quiet. Marta is hungry, tired, her eyes close. A female
warder rouses her, beating Marta whenever she passes by and thinks
Marta is not standing properly at attention.
      Interrogation.
      Standing before the cell again.
       Marta no longer even perceives; her memory is leaving her,
she is unable even to think — nothing but terrible weariness, a
dull pain penetrating her whole being. Only not to speak... She
faints; the canings bring her back to consciousness; with her last
strength she stands up, collapses, is beaten again, pulls herself
together once more, but falls, and it is over.
      She comes to herself in the prison infirmary on Karlovo
square, receives artificial nourishment, and under a doctor's care
returns to life.
      The investigation of the Soviet intelligence service in the
Protectorate was reserved for Prague. Gustav's next message, a
letter of 24 May 1941, is written in the Pečkárna prison:
      "My dear little Mánička!
       I think of you day and night, what you may be doing and how
you are bearing your present hard life, full of cares and
unpleasantness, which I have brought upon you by my
thoughtlessness....... I suffer terribly whenever I remember you.
For the first several days I thought I would go mad from it
all..."
       In the autumn of 1941 the police investigation was already
finished, and the whole group was transferred to the court
investigative custody in Dresden. Meanwhile a little daughter,
Jana, had been born to the Košuličs. Gusta asks for an English
textbook and would like the Sunday Czech newspapers. Gusta would
gladly have České slovo, but that is forbidden. Before his arrest he
weighed 76 kg; in Dresden only 68 kg. Otherwise he is fit:
       "I am once again a whole man and do not in the least want to
despair."
       Letters from home revolve mainly around little Jana and
family matters. But occasionally something else as well:
       "Everyone from your hillside greets you and keeps their
fingers crossed, except ing. Lhotský, who has been away from Brno
for 3 months now," his wife writes him on 30 November 1941. She
could not write openly that he had been arrested.
       In a letter of 9 January 1942 his mother tells Gusta that in
Židenice they confiscated all the people's radios. In Královo Pole,
they say, it is to happen too, but so far nothing yet.
       On 14 January 1942 Košulič, along with the others, was taken
to Ichtershausen near Erfurt and lodged in block K. He is placed in
cell no. 66. He writes from Ichtershausen on 1 February and gives
his brother Arnošt detailed instructions on how to repair the
radio: "shield the grid and anode leads of the AF3 tube, the leads
to the loudspeaker and to the tuning capacitor. Use little beads
and over them slip the metal braiding. Wind the ends of the
shielding with binding wire and solder them, so they do not fray.
Wind the grid block with impregnated cloth, over it a strip of
copper sheet, solder it and ground it."
       Gusta is losing weight. He weighs 64 kg. His wife writes him
on 8 February 1942:
       "We are all getting thin here too. There is hardly anything,
not even vegetables; you must stand in line for them and then you
still get nothing. That has never happened here before!... We also
had frosts down to 30°. Coal is not to be had at all; we have last
year's, but every other family is freezing."
       In a similar tone his mother writes on 18 February: "The
shops are empty; there is not even paper."
       On 29 January the longed-for English textbook arrived. "In
the cell I am alone," Košulič reports on 1 March 1942. "I work all
day, even on Sunday, so that very little time remains for
English... The work is dirty; at present I am lacquering belts."
       The letter of 5 April 1942 should have been written a week
earlier, but the prisoners received their writing materials late,
and besides the pen was in such a desolate state that it was
impossible to write with it. Košulič comments: "Perhaps I shall
write this letter with that spade... No changes have taken place
here as yet, apart from that food. There is still plenty of
work and..." — the rest is blacked out by the censor. Against the
light, however, it can be read: "...and less and less food." Gusta
may write only once a month. His letter of 10 May 1942 is
optimistic: "Nothing lasts forever; I too shall return again and
all will be well for us....... Nothing has changed here except the
ban on all newspapers (since 1 May I have not even received the
Sunday papers and further..." (The rest is scrawled to
unreadability by the censor.) It probably concerned food again,
because hunger was becoming an ever greater problem in
Ichtershausen. He advises his brother Arnošt to buy measuring
instruments through Svítil.
      Gusta received two letters a month, one from his wife and one
from his mother. In the middle of May 1942 a decree was issued that
a prisoner might receive only one letter a month, and that only in
German. Both women therefore began writing joint letters.
      Gustav Košulič received a parcel on 12 May 1942. It contained
a washcloth, a toothbrush, toothpaste, a slice of bread and
caramels. Food was not allowed to be sent into the prison. In a
letter of 14 June Košulič explains why it was probably possible
this time: "That is because the bread was completely dried out like
a stone and the caramels melted with the heat (not suitable for the
NSV). It didn't bother me, though; I fell upon our home-made bread
with such appetite that I quite scraped my gums raw. I sucked the
caramels together with the paper, ha!" (Here hunger spoke
involuntarily. NSV = Nazionalsozialistische Volkswohlfahrt was the
Nazi welfare organization, to which foodstuffs sent to prisoners in
parcels were handed over.) The letter continues: "I live like a
hermit, I know nothing of the world; by day I work diligently and
in the evening I study, or, if I am too tired, I converse with you
(of course only with the photographs). I have some of them fastened
to the inside of the little door of the wall cupboard, in which I
keep my food bowl, cutlery, soap and so on. At night I always open
the cupboard, so that I can see you nicely from my bunk, and you
can see me."
      Malnourishment and hunger — a tight, importunate hunger that
does not let up for a moment, not after breakfast, not after lunch,
not after supper, today, tomorrow, the day after, day after day —
does not remain without effect. Gustav Košulič, who in spring
weighed 64 kg, weighs only 59 kg by the end of summer. In the
autumn of 1942 their affair gathers rapid pace.
      On 29 September the indictment is served on him, and on
20 October the summons to court. On the night of 4 to 5 November he
is transported to Berlin, to the court prison in Moabit.
      On the transport he sees the others. Although talking is
forbidden, they nevertheless exchange a few words; one tells
another how it has been with him, and those who survive will tell
it at home.
      Košulič's cell no. 200 in corridor B.I was spacious, clean,
with central heating and a flush toilet. And for the first time in
a long series of months Gustav ate his fill.
      How will it turn out in court?
      "I am calm and hope for the best. Keep your fingers crossed
for me! I expect that by the end of November at the latest I shall
put on the wooden clogs and the black uniform in some penitentiary,"
he writes on 5 November.
      On Wednesday, 11 November at 8 o'clock they brought him from
Moabit to Bellevue Str. no. 15, before the 1st Senate of the People's
Court. The session was opened at 9 o'clock on the II floor, in
courtroom 3. In the front bench sat Ladislav Bobák, Jan Volek, Josef
Koblížek, Josef Žák, Antonín Beránek, Milan Kubišta, Marta
Delamová, František Váňa, Ladislav and František Havelka, Vlastimil
Erben and Jindřich Pintěra, Ladislav Volejník and Oldřich Kriegel.
In the second, Karel Mach, Gustav Košulič, Jan Habrda, Vladimír
Kott, Alois Horký, Jan Fichtel, Antonín Kubišta, Leopold
Úředníček, Karel Haas, Tymek Dienstibier, Emanuel Krejčí, Jaroslav
Svoboda, dr. Emil Mazal, Jindřich Symerský, ing. Osvald Duba and
Josef Váca.
      The 1st Senate of the People's Court sat in the composition
dr. Kohler, General Tille, dr. Schreimüller, SS Brigadeführer
Goetze, SS Gruppenführer lieutenant-general of SS weapons Petři,
and NSDAP Brigadeführer Heinsins.
      At 9 o'clock the presiding judge opened the session. At 9:30
the public prosecutor Nebel began reading the indictment. Koblížek
had his own lawyer; the others were represented ex officio by dr.
Walter Schwarz. At 10:30 the interrogation of the accused began.
After the midday recess the session moved to courtroom no. 2 on
the I floor and ended with the interrogation of Kott from 18:00 to
18:50. The interpreter was dr. Ježek.
      The following day the session was opened at 9 o'clock; the
verdict was read at 16:00, and at a quarter to six in the evening
it was over. The warders let them stay together for just under a
quarter of an hour, so that they could still converse and say
farewell. Those who were sentenced to death never returned to
Moabit. Bobák, Košulič, Habrda, Pintěra and others were taken to
Plötzensee, to the prison at Konigsdamm no. 3.
      Delamová, Horký and Mach, according to the public
prosecutor's motion, were also to be sentenced to death, but they
received prison terms. Marta's courage paid off.
      Selucký, Vycpálek, Hůla and Lonek were tried with their
groups in separate trials. The total number of those arrested is
estimated at 200; 135 were tried, and more than 50 executed.


---


IN THE DEATH CELL


---


5 January 1943
     "I am glad that you received that terrible news so bravely; I
had fears... What awaits me cannot pass me by; I firmly believe in
God, however, that He will not permit me to end in such a
manner...
     I am alone in a cell with a wooden floor and central heating, a
large window. I work, I paste bags for coffee... Three times a
week I have a half-hour walk; the food as in Ichtershausen..."
     The death sentence was carried out a hundred days after the
verdict. The prisoner was placed in a cell with a large spy-hole,
in the so-called death cell. Every evening a warder came, bound the
prisoner's hands and dimmed the light. Day after day, every
morning he removed the shackles from the prisoner's hand and
announced:
     "99 days yet..."
      "98 days yet..."
      "97 days yet..."
 16 February 1943
      "I work, I read and I sleep. Only the last of these I found
hard to get used to. For here we go to bed on weekdays at 18:00, on
Saturday, Sunday and holidays already at 17:00. That means that at
that time everything must be out of the cell except shirts,
underpants, handkerchief and books. And so nothing remains for me
but to lie down, for I am in my underwear, and it is a little cold
for that. But to sleeping with shackles on and under the dimmed
all-night light I first had to accustom myself. But even that I
finally overcame."
      He even comments on the night air raids on Berlin: "That noise
does not upset me; I go on sleeping calmly — I have nothing to
lose!"
March 1943
      After the reading of the verdict the presiding judge asked
each of them whether he accepted the sentence. Stunned and crushed,
Košulič does not accept.
      The front is still far away; time is faster. Gustav's mother
strives to save her son. She too files a petition for clemency. She
corresponds with the judicial authorities and achieves at least
that the vitamins, sugar and cod-liver oil from the parcel are
issued to Gustav. Gusta weighs only 56 kg. The mother writes to the
lawyer dr. Walter Schwarz, Berlin, Friedrichstrasse 100 03, that
she has filed a petition for clemency for her son and asks him to
take up the matter, since he defended him in court. But no answer
comes.
24 May 1943
      All the letters of Mrs. Eva Košuličová had been written in
neat script, in straight, tidy lines. This letter is written in a
scrawling, scattered hand; the lines run downhill. The handwriting
is testimony that the mother is mad with grief, pain, terror and
despair.
     "My poor, unhappy child!
      I have undertaken everything that is possible for me as a
human being..."
      The mother evidently received word that the petition for
clemency was rejected, the verdict had become final. Every morning
the warder counts off to Košulič the days of his life.
      "One hope still remains," writes the mother.
13 June 1943
      "I was at your workplace to get a certificate for you and
sent it at once to the senior public prosecutor. All your
colleagues stood around me; each would gladly have helped; they
were fond of you, they pity you and greet you cordially..."
19 June 1943
     "My dear ones!
      I received your (3) letters and everything else... The
parcels are opened in front of me and I unpack them personally. I
urgently need Hellada soap, toothpaste, a toothbrush, good skin
cream and Sana. (The requested things he will receive on 1 July.)
      I am healthy, apart from my feet, but terribly weak..."
      He asks Arnošt for photographs of little Jana and of Maňka.
 10 July 1943
      "Two more days!"
 12 July 1943
      "The sentence will be carried out today at 19:00."
      Košulič was imprisoned in building 3, dept. 6/248. On the day
of the execution they took him to a small cell on the ground floor
near the place of execution. Through the shaded windows not much
light penetrates. The cell makes a gloomy and oppressive
impression.
      A knock at the door. Košulič turns. Someone has inserted a key
in the lock. It was not the custom to knock when entering a cell. A
man in a black cassock entered, the priest dr. Blanek.
      They brought paper and writing materials. Farewell letters.
One for Maňka, the second for his mother.
      "Dear and beloved little Mánička!
      It must be; I am departing whence there is no return. God did
not heed all our joint prayers; at 19:00 the sentence will be
carried out. Be strong; bring up our dear little Jana well; you
must live only for her. And when the pain that my departure causes
you has passed, find yourself a worthy and decent man with whom you
could join your further life and so bear your heavy cross... I
thank you once more for that, though short, for me unforgettable
great happiness that I lived through by your side. I also greatly
regret that I could not see our golden little Jana at least once
and clasp you both one last time in my arms. And when Jana is a
grown and sensible girl, remember sometimes the unhappy G. K."
      "Dear little mother"
      ...Do not be angry with me, I beg you, for my deed and for
the pain I am causing you. Be strong; do not forget my wife and
little Jana... With all my heart I greet, embrace and kiss you all
for the last time."
      Last meal: three open sandwiches and a bottle of beer, the
"Henkersmahlzeit" — the executioner's meal. Is it meant as a sign
of humanity, an effort to sweeten the condemned man's last moments?
Or is it part of a calculated laceration of the nerves and further
mental torment?
      Gustav packed up all the letters and photographs and wrote
his mother's address.
      At half past six an elderly, unfriendly man appeared and
firmly bound his hands behind his back. Endless, torturous thirty
minutes between life and death.
      At 19:00 the executioner comes with his assistants. In the
anteroom of the execution chamber the public prosecutor and
officials of the prison administration are waiting.
       "What is your name?"
       "Gustav Košulič."
       "Born?"
       "20 May 1911."
       "You were sentenced by the People's Court to death for
preparation of high treason and for treason. Master executioner,
carry out your duty!"
       A similar fate met Jan Habrda, OK2AH. It was either the
evening of 14 or 15 March 1941 (Mrs. Habrdová does not remember
exactly), when he brought up some things from the cellar and was
assembling them.
       At midnight the bell rang. Five men forced their way into
the flat, one holding a revolver in his hand. Two of them spoke
Czech.
       "How do you come to this?" asks Mrs. Habrdová.
       They looked at one another. "Are we in the right place?" one
asked the other.
       "Ganz sicher."
       They took all the radio material and almost all the books,
even Babička (The Grandmother), and packed them into suitcases.
They searched the beds and threw everything out of the cabinets.
       "And all the time they kept saying they were looking for
plans. They would not leave until they found the plans," recalls
Mrs. Habrdová. (They were evidently looking for the schematics and
assembly drawings of the transmitters.) "By three o'clock they had
had enough. They took my husband and drove off."
       Two days later she learned he was at Pankrác. Then letters
came from Dresden, from Ichtershausen, from Berlin, until one day
a farewell letter arrived.
       Marta Delamová was transported to the penitentiary at Jauer,
Horký to Straubing, Kott to Nuremberg.
       Mrs. Eva Košuličová began to search for her son's body and
for the circumstances of his death. The priest dr. Otto Blanek,
Berlin - Rheinikendorf Ost, Residenzstrasse 90, wrote to her
(7 August 1943):
       "Your son was calm and composed. He greets his parents and
brother, and the parents and siblings of his wife, and asks that
you do not abandon her and help her..."
       The governor of the Plötzensee prison, senior government
councillor Vacano, informed her on 16 August 1943 that her son had
been officially buried and the grave is not known.
       Mrs. Eva Košuličová would not rest content with that. She
writes to the senior Reich public prosecutor at the People's Court
in Berlin W9, Elbiger Str. 14, who answers her on 23 August 1943:
       "I inform you that I do not see myself in a position to
issue to you the body of your son or to tell you where he was
buried.
       By order of:"
                                       (Illegible signature).
       Further details Mrs. Košuličová learns only from the
cemetery administration of the administrative district of Wedding:
     "Gustav Košulič was cremated in the Wedding crematorium,
under number 172235. The urn was placed in a communal grave. Its
later exhumation and relocation is not possible."
     Dr. Zochlar of Hranice also remembers Gustav Košulič, OK2GU;
he belonged to Lonek's group and got to know Košulič at Pankrác.
He was in the last cell and they conversed through the wall; in
person they then met at Ichtershausen on various occasions
(swimming, the sick-bay, etc.).
     After the war, on 10 October 1945, he wrote to Mrs.
Košuličová:
     "Your son was a brave man, a good man and a good comrade. He
sacrificed his life for an idea to which he wished to serve
according to his best knowledge and conscience."


---


OK1CB

He was born on 12 March 1895 in Prague at Královské Vinohrady.
He spent his childhood in Přemyslova street and in the romantic
corners of the Vyšehrad ramparts, on Císařská louka and along the
Vltava. From the elementary school he went to the burgher school and
from there to the gymnasium.
     "Without a school-leaving exam you'll get nowhere..." his
father, an employee of the Prague gasworks, used to say. He had a
great deal of work and had no time left for his son, who had
already lost his mother at the age of four. Otakar's world was
above all his gang. He had great natural intelligence; he could
certainly have studied well, but it did not amuse him. He came into
conflict with his natural-history professor and solved the
intolerable situation in his own idiosyncratic way: he ran away from
home. He ran far. To South America.
     When I set out to the school at Na Smetance to look for some
records, they greeted me with:
     "Batlička? Is that the writer?"
     On the catalogue card of the Prague university library, under
Batlička's book The Camp of the Lost, we read about the author:
"He practiced a hundred trades in half a hundred countries; in the
interwar period he taught boxing, flew as a sportsman, raced
motorcycles, was the first private owner of a radio transmitter;
during the occupation he devoted himself to illegal intelligence
work and died in a concentration camp."
     In the preface which Bohumil Jírek wrote for his book Dance on
the Mast after Batlička's death, we read:
     "His station was more perfect than the state radio. In 1925
the Shah of Persia visited him in Sámova street, wishing to see
with his own eyes the apparatus by which contact with the Near East
had been achieved. He spoke with Piccard's balloon, the Nobility
expedition and the Papanin team. He caught the distress call of a
sinking Japanese ship, and so long broadcast her position into the
ether that he summoned help. He received the thanks of the Japanese
embassy for it. In 1930 he opened free courses in radiotelegraphy
for the young at the Nusle branch of the radio club."
      It was also written of him that on his travels around the
world he worked as a ship's telegraphist and fitted out his
transmitting cabin in Sámova street like a ship's cabin.
      And how did Batlička die? On the memorial plaque on the
house where he lived it says that he was executed. The Voice of
Revolution writes that he was beaten to death during an attempted
escape from the camp. According to one version outside the camp,
according to another inside the camp.


---


M • • • Mi
     Before the First World War the borders were not yet so strict.
Batlička reached Hamburg, and a few weeks later his father
received a postcard from Argentina (ing. Jirát, ex OK1KI, saw it).
He took part in an expedition to the Mato Grosso, stayed on the
Amazon, among the Indians, and in Mexico. The First World War
caught him in Buenos Aires.
     Of the extent of his travels and of the milieu in which he
moved, we can form an idea from his short stories. The plots unfold
in South, Central and North America, in India, in Africa, at sea
between America and Europe and on the seas of the southern
hemisphere, in Australia, in primeval forests, in deserts, in great
cities, on ships and in aircraft.
     After his return he works for some English firm, at the YMCA as
an interpreter, and at the Hamburg-America Lines. Once he looks out
of the window and at the recruiting office of the Red Cross in
Neklanova street sees a sympathetic girl's face. He cannot tear
himself away.
     "That's the one I'll marry!!"
     He seizes a small-bore rifle, takes aim, and fires.
     Her name was Marta Špačková and she came from Plzeň. The
recollections of people to whom she told the story of meeting
Otakar Batlička differ slightly. According to some he shot through
her heel stiffener, according to others her hat. But that is not
the point. The important thing is that shortly afterward, on 29
April 1920, they were married. Batlička's wanderings were over.
     Later they moved to Sámova street 624 (under the Protectorate
Hemina 624, now Čiklova 7). It is a street that slopes down; on the
right is a short row of houses, on the left a triangular park. The
house number has been preserved.
     Batlička spoke several foreign languages; in his pocket he
carried a certificate of a successfully passed helmsman's
examination and other papers for ship service.
     But if you read anywhere that Batlička served as a ship's
radiotelegraphist, don't believe it. For a simple reason: at that
time he did not yet know how. It is also not true that he was the
first amateur transmitter.
       All kinds of visitors came to the Batličkas'. But that the
Shah of Persia was there in 1925 to see the first transmitter that
had established contact with the Near East — that is a bit too
much.
       The bacillus of amateur radio infected Batlička through his
schoolmate, police inspector B. Ertl, OK1WZ. He invited him to the
SKEČ, where Batlička made friends with Štětina, OK1AZ, Mayer,
OK1NA, ing. Budík, ex OK1AU, and with Archman, OK1PK. On 15
November 1931 he filed his application.
       The Ministry of Posts and Telegraphs summoned Batlička for
3 March 1932 at 10:00 before the examining board composed of dr.
Kučera, dr. Burda, ing. Singer and the secretary Špinka. Špinka
played a French text. Batlička made (at a speed of 50 marks per
minute) 3 errors, which he corrected during the examination, and 4
uncorrected ones. That was enough. He also knew something from the
theory, and on 3 March 1932 he was granted the concession with file
number 13 620 and assigned the call sign OK1CB.
      His fate was sealed.
       Ota quickly overcame beginner's difficulties and became an
excellent operator. His domain was the 40 m band, which in that
period offered him good DX possibilities. He achieved the WAC, and
his language skills, acquired during his stays in foreign
countries, enabled him to converse in Spanish, English and German.
It is generally asserted that he knew more than he stated in the
personal questionnaire when taking up employment — among other
things, that he was one of the few Europeans who commanded the
language of the American Indians, and he simply considered it
pointless to talk about it in the personnel office of the Prague
Electric Company. He became an amateur in body and soul, and as
such he knew how to recognize keenly the crucial problem:
publicity and the winning of further enthusiasts, especially the
young.
      He was a splendid speaker and a masterful storyteller. He had
something to talk about and he knew how. He was fond of young
people, and they followed him. In 1933 he began his lecture
courses. One of his most prominent pupils, Karel Kamínek, OK1CX,
characterized his teacher Batlička in Short Waves 1946 as follows:
      "Enterprising, courageous, attentive to all progress, he
became self-taught in the field of short-wave transmission and
among the first in our country obtained an amateur concession. He
applied his excellent organizational abilities in the then
short-wave section of the Nusle Radio Club, where by his enthusiasm
he won over for amateur transmission a series of young people, and
in the courses which he organized himself he trained about 15
amateur transmitters and many SWLs."
      It was not enough for him to be a good driver and a
conscientious conductor. Already then he came up with the idea of
installing microphones and loudspeakers in trams. The tragedies of
miners in collapsed mines moved him, and he wished to give them a
tool to ease rescue work — radio communication. He was the first
among us to attempt anything of the sort.
     Batlička worked until two o'clock in the afternoon. He napped
a little, and at half past ten in the evening switched on the
transmitter. Sometimes also at five in the afternoon. His favorite
band was 40 m, but he also went out on 80 and 20 m. He transmitted
at a calm pace of 50 to 60 marks per minute, which was then usual
among DX men. His keying was rhythmical, regular; only at SK he
slightly prolonged the last dash. His domain was night DX
operation. He was interested in skip in relation to terrain. With
that, evidently, was connected also his investigation of the
propagation of electromagnetic waves beneath the earth's surface
in mines.
     And what about the rescue of the Japanese ship? No documents
could be found anywhere. No one remembers anything, not even the
year in which the event should have taken place. It cannot be
assumed that amateur transmitters would not have talked about it.
     By a careful reading of those passages of Batlička's stories in
which radio communication figures, we find that although he was an
outstanding amateur transmitter, he did not listen to and did not
know the operation of mobile ship and aircraft stations.
     Professor Piccard's balloon ascent into the stratosphere was
the sensation of the year 1932. He reached an altitude of 16,700 m.
Many amateurs strove for contact with him. The operation was
monitored by the receiving station of the ČTK news agency and for
the most part also by the radio station at the Kbely airfield.
Batlička kept watch on Piccard, but had no contact with him.
     The claim that Batlička established contact with the airship
Italia is nonsense. At that time Batlička did not yet transmit.
The Italia established no contacts with amateurs.
     Prague amateur transmitters met during the occupation At the
Bears' (U medvídků). Batlička did not go among them. All the more
diligently he frequented the editorial office of the magazine Young
Herald, with the editors Foglar and dr. Bureš, who week after week
placed his stories in the magazine.

*■1 ••• mm
     Prague, the Fragner firm, the year 1940. The telephone rang on
engineer Jirát's desk. It was Batlička. "Come to me this evening!"
     In Sámova street engineer Jirát met Marta.
     "Ota is waiting for you. He told me to go out for a little
walk."
     OK1CB was at home alone.
     "Now, Emile, I am going to tell you something. Either you say
yes, or you say no. If no, then imagine that what I tell you was
never uttered."
     "You know I won't say no."
       Ing. Jirát was 16 years younger than Batlička, but he
belonged to the same generation of amateur transmitters. He passed
his examination on 9 October 1931 and was assigned the call sign
OK1KI. He worked with a COFDPAPAPPA transmitter, but after the war
he never returned to short waves. He had been a member of the
SKEČ.
       Ota spoke in a hushed voice. Then Marta returned and ing.
Jirát said goodbye.
       Shortly afterward ing. Jirát walks down Dvořákova street in
Smíchov and stops in front of house no. 13. Dvořákova street was
renamed Pecháčkova, and house no. 13 is now house no. 2 on 14
October square. A dark evening, not a light anywhere. There is a
war; the blackout is strictly observed. Ing. Jirát mounts the
staircase to the first floor, to the second, ever higher, to where
the stairs end. To the right and left are sheet-metal-clad doors,
and one door of wood, without cladding. Here, in the studio of the
painter Emanuel Pryl, a resistance group meets, led by major ing.
Vladimír Ellner.
      The events of the years 1938 and 1939 shook the whole nation.
Their dramatic course, the mounting tension, mobilization,
catastrophe, the occupation of the Czech lands, the arrogance of the
occupiers, terror — all this created an atmosphere of resistance.
Although in the first months of the occupation the economic
situation did not yet deteriorate markedly, the Nazi regime, by its
unblushing press propaganda for the Reich and its Führer and by the
importunity with which it paraded itself constantly, took care —
involuntarily but thoroughly — that no one could ever grow
reconciled to it or merge with it. Resistance organizations were
born spontaneously. The content of their activity ranged from
political propaganda through sabotage and intelligence work to the
collecting of explosives, arms and ammunition and the preparation
of armed uprising. The group of major Ellner has the task of
establishing radio contact with the Soviet Union. In Pryl's studio
there is room enough to stretch an antenna and a counterpoise; from
there the first, trial contact will be conducted.
      Many years have passed since that evening, but ing. Jirát
has memories that do not fade. He was Batlička's friend. We tracked
him down in Prešov. He opened the little book On the 57-Meter
Wave. On the title page, written in the hand of Batlička's wife, is:
      "Prague, 13 July 1965. To the best and most noble friend,
whom Ota sincerely loved very much, Marta Batličková dedicates in
remembrance."
      "That equipment had to be put into operation," ing. Jirát
relates. "We met for that in some street in Podolí. The transmitter
was in a suitcase, and the receiver likewise. The power supply was
separate. The transmitter was crystal-controlled."
      "What make was it?"
      "It was American work. The instruments were labeled with
Russian inscriptions. There was an instruction manual with it, which
I was to translate. It was written in Russian, but it was
impossible to find one's way in it, because it was translated from
the English original into Russian: that translator evidently knew
English well, but knew nothing about radio. We tried it, and in the
end it worked well."
     "And what impression did you have of those instruments?"
     "The transmitter did not excite me so much, but the receiver —
that was a fairy tale."
     "What antennas did you use?"
     "An antenna and a counterpoise came with it. The antenna could
be stretched either in the attic or outside, and after the end of
the session it was always packed up."
     The station was operated by Batlička. Ing. Jirát monitored the
operation in his flat in Strašnice.
     "On what receiver?"
     "On a Big Ben."
     "But that had no beat-frequency oscillator?"
     "It had none, but it could be heard."
     It is evidently as ing. Jirát says. I myself have heard plenty
of telegraphy on a broadcast receiver, and one far less good than
the Telefunken Big Ben.
     "Do you remember the call sign?"
     "The call sign was FKX, and that X changed according to the
cover names of the operators. The counter-station had the permanent
sign FKX. We always worked in the evening."
     "On what wave?"
     "It was near the 40 m band, as far as I can remember, that is,
about 47 m. The counter-station was a little bit alongside."
      "From whom did you receive the texts to transmit?"
      "From Ellner."
      "What did such a telegram look like?"
      "When we received word that they were listening, that is,
when FKXK sounded, we transmitted our report, which consisted of
groups of digits."
      "Did you have a schedule, or was the counter-station
permanently on receive?"
      "The sessions were fixed in advance. The date and the hour.
Major Ellner had it written on paper that had come from the
Soviets. And one more thing: Ellner gave me the instruction for
control monitoring because the first group and the fourth from the
end form the basis for the cipher. I would not swear to that four,
but since it sprang up from my subconscious, it is probably
correct."
