■ ••• M


---


In October 1941 it was relatively warm. A mild autumn with cool,
foggy mornings and sunny, warming noons. On Sunday, 12 October, the
pussy-willows blossomed, and near Příbram someone found a boletus
of 1¼ kg, perfectly healthy.
     On Monday the hatter Fetter came to the Batličkas'; he acted as
courier and arranged whatever was needed. Sometimes they met at his
place. They looked over some papers, which Fetter then took away.
On Tuesday, 14 October, Ota set out, as usual, early to work. He
had already been appointed a clerk, and that pleased his father.
Marta was shelling peas.
     Batlička stepped onto the not-very-wide staircase, walked
down and pondered. Two men in coats were walking toward him. He
looked back. Two others were descending from the third floor.
Before he knew it, they were upon him. In the left trouser pocket
a metal, elliptical badge on a little chain; in the coat pockets,
pistols and service cards.
     He is taken aback. His Walter PPK pistol lies inactive in its
hiding place in the kitchen.
     Two returned with Ota to the flat and made a search; two kept
watch outside. They did not find the pistol. A dustpan and broom
had been hung on it. The spare cartridges were stored among
papers, and Fetter in his absent-mindedness carried them off
together with the papers.
     On the same day, at almost the same hour, major Ellner, Pryl,
Vojtíšek and a series of others were arrested.
     Even before Batlička's arrest took place, the Gestapo
investigator, Oskar Fleischer, already had a good overview of the
activity of the FKX circle. He knew that the station operated by
František Chyba had had, from July to September, at least nine
sessions with the Soviet side; on even days it used the sign HA +
one optional letter, on odd days YU + an optional letter, and the
wavelengths 48, 61 or 65 m. FKX replied on the 46 or 53 m wave.
Jindřich Frode had crystals of 5347 kHz and 4618 kHz at his
disposal. His call sign was on even days IR + an optional letter,
on odd days 10 + an optional letter. The wavelengths of the Soviet
station FKX remained the same. The receivers were small for that
time, 10 x 20 x 12 cm, and covered 2–18 MHz in three bands. The
transmitter was fitted with one 6H6 tube and three 6L6s. The set
included a 15 m antenna and an 8 m counterpoise. Worse was that
the Gestapo also knew the cipher key. It knew that its basis was
the book by Vojtěch Martínek: "Before the Roots Take Hold." It
knew which groups of the radiogram contained the instructions for
deciphering; it could therefore decipher the intercepted dispatches
and profit from them.
     Under such circumstances Batlička had little hope. When the
interrogations in the Pečkárna were over, he was transferred to
the prison in the Terezín fortress.
     Jirát's luck was that Batlička had engaged him without the
knowledge of the higher levels of the resistance organization, and
did not inform them even afterward.
     On the evening of 5 February 1942 they told Batlička that he
would go on a transport. Rumors and conjectures of that kind had
been circulating for some days. A transport means change, tearing
one out of the prison stereotype. To ride out, to see the
countryside, houses, people and — if only from afar — the life
that had ceased to exist for the prisoner. A transport into the
unknown. Someone caught from the warders' talk the word
Himmelsfahrt — the journey to heaven.
     The transport (about 400 people) assembled in the third
courtyard, and between three and four in the morning set out for
Bohušovice. They marched without breakfast, through frost and
snow. At the station a pleasantly heated passenger train waited.
On the carriage windows were pasted the inscriptions Arbeiterzug
(workers' train). Everyone had a seat, but no one might leave it
without permission. SS guards strictly watched the carriages and
did not permit a word to be spoken.
      In Prague the train took on still more people and set out
toward České Budějovice. Late in the afternoon it was moving along
the bank of the Danube. Toward evening it stopped at a small
station at the confluence of the Danube and the Enns.
     "Aussteigen! Los!"
     On the station building the inscription: MAUTHAUSEN. They
passed through a wide gate in the wire fence. In front of the
station a steep, snow-covered slope, ending below in a wall. All
around, plenty of SS men. By that wall they formed them into
fives. About 1 km beyond the town is a crossroads. From there a
road leads in a wide arc to the concentration camp. A cart track
crosses it, climbing through a hollow up the steep hill.
     Here it began.
     "Los! Los!"
     Shouting, blows, kicks. The SS men strike out wildly. The rear
ranks push against the front, panic arises. The whole transport
breaks into a run. On this road the first casualties usually fall,
who are then finished off on the spot. Batlička's transport is
made up above all of young men. They are undernourished from
several months of imprisonment, they have behind them the Gestapo
interrogations, they have had nothing in their mouths since
yesterday evening, but they are young and they race up the hill.
They run past wooden barracks, past the SS kitchens and post.
Ahead of them looms a high, gloomy building of granite blocks.
Above, an enclosure of five strands of barbed wire on insulators.
     Mauthausen was a category III camp, intended for incorrigible
cases that were to be liquidated by heavy labor under unbearable
conditions.
     In the personal files of Batlička at the Prague Transport
Company there is a record that he died on 13 February 1942. Ing.
Gerndt remembers that an official notification of it arrived from
Mauthausen, and the cause of death is given as illness, probably
typhus.
     "Mrs. Batličková received a little bag of ashes and the
announcement that her husband died of pneumonia," maintains ing.
Jirát and adds: "Pryl was of the opinion that Batlička was
executed." He presents Pryl's article, Otakar Batlička — comrade,
fighter, hero, where this is stated explicitly. "Eman thought he
was shot in the bunker, but during his whole stay in the camp he
learned nothing. Still under the Protectorate some returnee came
to Marta and told that Ota had been shot while escaping.
     "I heard that too," joins in ing. Jan Budík, ex OK1AU, "and
indeed in spring 1942 in Buchenwald."
     "From whom?"
      "I did not know that man, or I could not conjure him up. Of
course I remembered Batlička very well. I was arrested already in
March 1939 and taken to Dachau, so I did not know what had become
of Batlička. In spring 1942 I was moved to Buchenwald, and there
someone approached me with the news that Ota, during a transport
from one camp to another, had attempted an escape and had been
shot in the course of it. I did not want to ask too many
questions, so as not to end up at the political department for
interrogation. I never met the man who brought me this information
again."
      Even if it had been a matter of mere internment, it would
have had to be tried by the summary court in the time of the
civil state of emergency. Was Batlička sentenced?
      "Batlička was not tried. No one of the whole group ever came
before a court," maintains ing. Jirát.
      We cannot ask Emanuel Pryl. He died on 28 February 1980.
      "I know nothing of my husband having been before a court,"
declares Mrs. Prylová categorically. "I never heard from him that
he had been tried."
      Some time had passed after the end of the war when American
military vehicles appeared at the quarry not far from Štěchovice.
Part of the men stood guard, the other part dug out several crates.
Before the Czechoslovak security organs could arrive, the Americans
drove off. With the crates. Crate 414/IV concealed the key to the
secret of Batlička's death: a list of 1,823 persons sentenced by
the Prague summary court. Batlička, Frôde, Chyba, Toufar, Ellner,
lieutenant-colonel Mašín and others are listed there. The verdict
against these people is dated 19 January 1942, the last day of
validity of the civil state of emergency in the territory of the
Prague Oberlandrat. Emanuel Pryl was also sentenced to death, but
the verdict against him evidently was not confirmed (the verdicts
of the summary courts became enforceable only after K. H. Frank's
signature).
      When ing. Jirát learned of this, he was astonished:
      "I was in contact with Eman from his return to Prague until
1962. Then I visited him once more, I was in his studio, and Eman
then saw me to the train for Slovakia. You can be sure that at all
our meetings we returned to the fate of the group. He never
mentioned a trial — and yet the experience of being condemned to
death is not something one simply gets over!"
      Did Pryl conceal from Jirát that he had been tried? If so,
then why? And why was the verdict against Batlička and the others
not published, when such publication was customary and precisely
that was supposed to serve the purpose of intimidation?
      The answer to this question is provided by a letter of the
president of the Prague summary court, Joachim Illmer, of 6
November 1941 to K. H. Frank. Illmer reports that the summary
courts in Prague and Brno still have 140 persons to sentence. The
carrying out of the verdicts in Prague and Brno is, he says, not
expedient, and publication is not necessary — better said, it is
entirely undesirable. He proposes that these persons be sentenced,
but that the verdicts be carried out in a concentration camp.
      Prof. Ant. Nykl was on 6 January 1942 sentenced by the
summary court to transfer to the Secret State Police. He survived
Mauthausen.
      "How was it at the summary court?"
      "At a court? I know nothing of any such thing."
      "So you were not tried?"
     "No. I was never before any court. I am hearing it now for
the first time from you. Pryl did not know, any more than anyone
in our transport. Truly not. We thought we had been sent there by
some order for liquidation."
     Zdeněk Deyl, who was sentenced by the summary court on the
same day as comrade Nykl, also knew and heard nothing. Even the
elementary regulations of proceedings before the summary court
were not observed. The delirium of a totalitarian regime, drunk
with the feeling of its own power and the notion that it would
rule forever, that it could do with people whatever it pleased
and need not heed even its own laws and decrees.
      Pryl lectured in September 1945 in Světec. He described how
after their arrival at the camp twelve names were called out, among
them Batlička's. All of them were at once led to the washhouse,
from there to the bunker, and there, he said, they were executed.
Such a bunker we can see in Ravensbrück (north of Berlin, GDR) or
at Rabštejn near Česká Kamenice. If it was to fulfill its
repressive and intimidating mission, it had to be substantially
worse than the whole camp. None of the Mauthausen prisoners I met
had firsthand knowledge of it. However, recollections of
prisoners from other camps have been preserved, and since the
Germans had a sense for standardization, we may assume it was no
different in Mauthausen. Unless an exception was prescribed, only
a half ration of food was given in the bunker. There was no
heating. The prisoners were left only their underwear; their
socks and wooden clogs were taken away. The prisoner had to stand
barefoot and motionless on the cold concrete floor from morning
till night. Every movement, observed by the guard through the
spy-hole, was a pretext for inhuman torment. Inaction was worse
than work in the quarry. Every day was endless. Every night was
endless too. Cramps from hunger and cold did not let the prisoners
sleep. Such were Batlička's last days.
      O. Batlička was executed by shooting on 13 February 1942.
This is proven by the entry in the register of the dead, book I.,
item 103. That day there was a hard frost. The execution proceeded
— like everything in the camp — in stereotyped, well-worn ruts.
Batlička died at the camp place of execution (executions in the
bunker took place only from 1943). A vertical wall, 2.80 m wide,
shored with wooden beams, had been dug out in the slope. The space
in front of it was paved with bricks and strewn with sand. On the
right stood a trough with wooden shavings and a barrel of
chlorinated lime; on the left a portable gallows with a tipping
trapdoor. The prisoners were brought as they were in their cells:
in underwear and barefoot. The whole staff was present: the camp
commandant Standartenführer Franz Ziereis, his adjutants Viktor
Zoller and Adolf Zutter, the head of the camp protective custody
Georg Bachmayer, the chief of the camp administration Xaver
Strauss, and the head of the political department Karl Schultz
(all in the rank of Hauptsturmführer). Schultz read out the
execution verdict.
      The prisoners stood facing the beams, and six paces behind
them the firing squad.
      The crematorium commando loaded the dead bodies onto
carts, raked over the sand and sprinkled it with chlorinated lime.
Schultz handed Ziereis the report on the completed execution, and
the firing squad, at Bachmayer's command, marched off in parade
step to their quarters.


---


RADIO IN THE SLOVAK NATIONAL UPRISING


---


On 30 August 1944 at 11:00 the Slovak radio broadcast a march,
after which a lecture on the theme "Woman and the Home Economy"
was to follow, relayed from Prešov. The march had not yet died away
when, on the wave of Banská Bystrica, Chalúpka's verses "Mor ho!"
sounded, and the announcement: "We call on Slovaks and Slovak
women to listen to the program broadcast by Banská Bystrica and
not the program of Bratislava. All who hear this appeal, let them
urgently alert all their acquaintances to the extraordinary reports
of the Free Slovak Transmitter." There followed a proclamation to
officers, NCOs and soldiers and further reports, read by the
announcer of the Free Slovak Transmitter, a professor at the
electrotechnical secondary school in Banská Bystrica, Ladislav
Sára. Ing. S. Šuba, OK3SP, captured these historic moments on
gelatin discs.
       According to the original plans, the call to uprising was to
go out from Bratislava. They counted on the help of a military unit
that would keep the transmitter in operation at least three hours
and then — according to the situation — carry out its destruction.
The Bratislava garrison hesitated, and before it could rouse itself
to action, the Germans occupied the transmitter. Western Slovakia
was lost in the very first days of the uprising. Even after that,
however, the center of the uprising maintained contact with
Bratislava, by means of 100 W transmitters in the 80 m band,
manufactured in Banská Bystrica for CPO purposes.
       The Banská Bystrica transmitter worked at a frequency of 392
kHz with a power of 30 kW. The wavelength of 765 m was somewhat
unusual — between the network of Reich railways and the police
network (DQH) and the aircraft direction-finding stations, that
is, between the end of the medium-wave and the beginning of the
long-wave broadcast band. It was chosen with regard to propagation
in mountainous terrain, and in its time there was plenty of
polemical discussion about it. Banská Bystrica had no studio of its
own. It took over the programs of Bratislava, Košice, Moravská
Ostrava, Brno and Prague. After the occupation of Košice by
Hungary, it relayed programs from Prešov.
      The technical head of the Prešov station, ing. Samo Šuba,
OK3SP, placed the technical equipment at the disposal of the
studio's establishment. Literary material also arrived from Prešov,
and gramophone records with forbidden music. Among them was the
Czechoslovak state anthem. From this studio, set up in Horní
street 21, Banská Bystrica called to the fight against fascism,
for freedom and for the renewal of the Czechoslovak Republic.
      On the very first day three German aircraft appeared above
Laskomerský hill above the transmitter building and dropped
twelve bombs. There were huge explosions, but the broadcasting
went on. On the second of September six of them came. Thirty-five
bombs fell on Laskomerský hill. One hit the distilled-water cooler
(for cooling the anode of the output stage). The transmitter fell
silent in mid-speech. OK3AL arranged the repair of the cooler at
Podbrezová. He was a second lieutenant. As a Czechoslovak soldier
he fulfilled his duty in the uprising and reported for duty. The
air raids on Banská Bystrica intensified. It was decided not to
renew the activity of the transmitter on Laskomerský hill.
      OK3ID discovered at the Tri Duby airfield a transmitter
that had been used for goniometric direction-finding. It was
necessary to build a modulator for it and adapt it for
radiotelephone operation. This work was taken on by OK3AL, OK3ID,
OK3IT, OK3IX and the radio technicians of the Banská Bystrica
station. The transmitter was mounted on a truck and began
operation the very next day. The transmitter worked from various
locations. The station chief, Imrich Ikrényi, OK3IP, author of the
book Amateur Short-Wave Antennas, prepared antenna systems at the
anticipated sites. The questions of connecting the modulation,
electricity supply and telephone linkage had already been solved
in advance. The 765 m wavelength was used only at night. By day
they retuned to 560 m.
     The Germans tried to silence the Free Slovak Transmitter.
From Prievidza a twin-boom Focke-Wulf with a direction finder kept
coming, but it vanished as soon as Slovak fighters took off.
Broadcasting went on from Banská Bystrica, from Slovenská Ľupča,
from Dubová, from Zvolen, from Brezno, from Staré Hory. When the
Nazis failed to destroy the transmitter, they deployed jammers
against it.
     The Protectorate media were not allowed to report on the
Slovak National Uprising. At the end of August the press carried
only a brief report that President Tiso had asked the German
command for help in securing calm and order. Further sporadic
information appeared in tucked-away corners of the dailies only
in mid-September. Only after the fall of Banská Bystrica does
Slovakia reach the front pages of the Protectorate dailies, and
the matter is called by its right name: they write of the Slovak
uprising.
      The Free Slovak Transmitter fought to its last breath. On
26 October it moved to Donovaly, to the slope behind the church.
They stretched the antenna from the church to the Šport hotel, and
planted 60 little trees around the station for camouflage.
27 October 1944: Banská Bystrica fell. The command and the
political leadership withdrew to Donovaly. Gustáv Husák writes in
the book Testimony of the Slovak National Uprising: "From a room
not far from us one could hear the radio worker, who made up for
the weak power of the little transmitter with a mighty voice and
encouraged the insurgent fighters in that tragic midnight hour. A
gallows joke was born: let him open the window, and his voice
will be heard by the whole remainder of the free territory..."
     That day they broadcast for the last time. They set out for
Jergaly, but came under enemy aircraft fire, in which the truck
was damaged. A tank pulled them back into the forest near
Donovaly. There they dismantled the transmitter, hid some of the
parts, and destroyed the rest with hand grenades.


---


BOMBS ON PRAGUE


---


TO OK1AW! Dear Lojzík!
     I survived the air raid well in the factory canteen. Of
course, it was all just luck, whether one's house caught a hit or
not. Fortunately the bombs fell on all sides about 100 m away from
where I was. Into the baker's house, where I had been about six
minutes before the raid, a high-explosive bomb fell, and it took
the building out from the street to the second floor. The masonry
or the bomb killed the brother of the director Inemann right
there.
     My daughter was with me in the shelter; she too had been
having lunch there. My son Robert came to me from the technical
school. The alarm caught him at the end of Jiráskův bridge; he had
barely reached us when it began to fall. By Jiráskův bridge a big
corner building has three floors less. Another corner building at
the corner of Podskalská and Resslova, opposite the house where
Jirásek lived, was brought down by a bomb, and the surroundings
and the ruins burned. Today they are demolishing it, together with
a piece of the neighboring house of the Central Bohemian power
stations, in front of which another bomb burst in the street and
scattered a tram so that only the underframe with motors and
wheels remained.
     If my personal experience interests you, it went like this:
My daughter and I had picked up the side dish in the kitchen, and
they had just announced there that the serving of food was
stopping, because the air-raid siren was sounding. We had reached
the table and were dividing the sausages we had bought to go with
the factory porridge, when Robert arrived. At that moment several
unusually hard crashes sounded, after which glass began to rain.
The electric lighting went out. Choking dust poured from the
ventilation shafts of the masonry, giving a hint of what it must
look like outside. After five minutes, when the crashes did not
repeat, I went up the stairs from underground with several
colleagues to the street entrance. People from the house were
coming toward us. They were mostly women with children, some cut
by glass.
      From the entrance we saw a dreadful picture. One tower of
Emaus swept away by the explosion, the other burning in the roof.
At the corner of our street a house dug out by the explosion to
the second floor, and rubble halfway across the street. All around
us glass, and dust with smoke filling the air. The view of Emaus
was suddenly darkened by an explosion. We flinched back into the
corridor. A delayed-action bomb burst opposite the baker's and
buried the street in clay. We climbed out only after a good while.
By then I had already told the children that I was going to the
Philipska for my things, so that they should wait for me and go
nowhere. I went out into the street onto shards of glass. Some two
people, a woman and a man, were carrying covered stretchers, from
which blood was dripping. From the corner, five people were
pushing a Rapid car; someone was lying inside. They called to us
asking whether we could drive a car. My companion sat into the
car. Later we learned that the car was not roadworthy. The man
inside was a doctor, whose leg was severed by the door, slammed
by the bomb explosion in Podskalská street. He was dead.
     I reached Václavská street. The rear tract of our Philipska
was burning, and in front of it a crater in the roadway, from a
bomb. Everywhere glass, plaster, stones, sheet metal from
incendiary bombs; in the walls of the houses, holes from bomb
splinters. Our main building received splinters of bombs that
burst in front of the Ressl monument, from the direction of
Karlák. The hospital had a hit in the front face, and two floors
swept away as if by a huge paw. The craters in the lawns of
Charles Square were swarming with technical companies digging out
the unfortunate people hit in the air-raid shelters under the
lawns. Faust's house at the corner of Slupi was burning, likewise
the General Hospital and the anatomical museum. From Vinohrady I
saw clouds of smoke from the rooftops in the direction of the
theater. Bomb splinters from the military hospital flew clear
across the whole of Karlák, and still had force enough to break
through two plywood doors, or smash a window frame and bury
themselves in the wall. Of our clerks, one was hit in Václavská
street. One leg broken, and on the other his fingers were torn
off. Another was cut in the back by glass. A clerk from the
accounts office lost his wife and daughter in the collapsed house
at Pankrác. He lost everything; he dug there for two days with
our people before he found his dead. All that remained to him was
what he had on and in his briefcase. Everything else was
scattered beyond use. In Vinohrady and at Pankrác, houses were
destroyed by high-explosive bombs and fires.
     When I had collected my things in the Philipska — the
binoculars and the English dictionary — I made my way home with
the children. It was not easy, for everything was closed off from
Palacký bridge to Vršovice. A technical squad was heading for
Palacký bridge, however, and so I tried to slip through with the
children, which I managed, though at first the children were
terrified by the sight of the corpses strewn on the Prague side of
the bridge. The bridge took three hits. The railing above the
arches was swept away together with the people. Five corpses
remained on the ground there, three without heads, one with the
brain knocked out, one dead woman. The boy then asked whether I
had seen the hand with the rings on the ground. I had not.
     Emaus was a ghastly sight. The Ministry of Social Welfare
had one corner gone from the Morána side; a bomb flew through
into a two-story cellar. The house opposite the ministry burned
still the next day and burned through to the cellars, into which
in the end the pianos, fireplaces and all the iron that could not
burn collapsed.
     The house of the agricultural education society burned down;
some houses in Slezská, formerly Fochova and Mánesova streets, are
smashed to rags. Grébovka burned to its foundations, together
with the vivarium.
     I end my transmission and greet you once more cordially
                                    Your
                                           P. Motyčka
Prague, 13 March 1945


---


■ ••• H
     Along the main thoroughfare of the Baťa works in Zlín a large
passenger car drives at a leisurely pace. It is heading for the
gate. It will be driving out, into the town. On the front fender
flutters a flag with the swastika.
     The uniformed Werkschutz on gate duty should check the car
and its passengers and write down the number. He does none of it.
He opens quickly, and as the car draws near, he stands at
attention, heels together, toes apart, stretches himself as tall as
he can, raises his arm in salute, and remains in that pose until
the limousine with the swastika vanishes in the streets of Zlín.
The guard knows that car. In it rides the Abwehrbeauftragter ing.
Duchek, chief of the Nazi counter-espionage and intelligence
service of the Baťa works, one of the principal collaborators of
the Zlín Gestapo.
     The car wanders here and there about the town. Finally it
turns into Cigánov and stops at number 324.

• • • ■■

Radio amateurs in prewar Zlín were always active.
     Right after the liberation they met. Sgt. Froňka, OK2TD,
ing. Jos. Gajda, OK2DS, F. Karas, OK2KD, Boh. Kovárník, OK2ZE, K.
Mojžíš, OK2QC and K. Oulehla, OK2OO set about building an ECO
transmitter with two 802 tubes. They used tubes and components
that Mojžíš and Josef Krahulík had saved from destruction and, at
the risk of their own lives, smuggled by car from the factory to
Mojžíš's flat at the Hovorkas' in Cigánov, past the Abwehr
commissioner Duchek. At that time they were repairing his radio
and "borrowed" his car. The station was installed in the
gymnasium, and under the call sign OK2DS it worked from the end of
May to the beginning of August 1945.

SPRING 1945


---


"We crossed the bridge by the National Theater, turned into
Karmelitská and headed past the Malá Strana secondary school
toward Velkopřevorské square. There we did not get through. There
was a barricade in the street, and shooting was going on. Public
transport was not running. We took refuge in a narrow lane between
the printing works and the ministry of education.
     Then the plaster began to fall. We jumped into the car and
drove off. By then it was dark.
     We reached Kinského garden. In the barracks were our men; in
Kinského garden, the SS. As soon as anything moved, firing broke
out. That happened to us too. Bullets smacked against the paving.
The driver and my companion jumped out and lay down on the lawn. I
crouched down to the floor in that car and instinctively held my
breast pocket to my forehead. The firing lasted only a few
minutes, but those minutes were endless. We continued toward
Arbesovo square. Every little while some patrol stopped us.
     "Who are you? From where? Where are you going?"
     My companion had an identity card of the Czech National
Council, so it was all right. In Švédská street, somewhere on a
balcony or on the roof, there was some German with a machine gun.
Those were terrible crashes. We drove on. Of course, without
lights. After a few dozen meters, a patrol with weapons in hand.
     "Who are you? Where are you going? And why without lights?"
     So we switched them on. At Klamovka we were stopped again.
     "Why are your lights on?"
     We put them out, and finally we rolled up below the steps.
When I climbed up and turned toward our house, some idiot from our
street began firing at me. I kept ducking to the wall, and I was
glad when I finally reached home. It was midnight."
     Thus traveled, during the Prague uprising in May 1945, ing.
Karel Pešek, OK1KX, from the seat of the Czech National Council in
Bartolomějská street to Smíchov, Pod Lipkami 40. He was bringing
a transmitter for repair. He set to work at once and found a torn
lead from the coil. Perhaps from the impact of a parachute drop.
When he began to transmit, he discovered that his own transmitter
was more powerful.
     He was one of those who in May 1945 recognized that the hour
had struck when amateur transmitters could prove that their
activity could be immediately useful to the state and to human
society. He joined those who helped to replace with their stations
the postal links broken by the events of the war.
     For an experienced amateur it was no problem to build a
simple and effective apparatus. And since he was not after routine
amateur operation, he chose the call sign OKX.
     In the Prague uprising the station OKX was at the disposal of
the Czech National Council and broadcast, according to its
instructions, above all information that was being spread by every
possible means in the hope that the Allied listening service would
catch it. A further task was to establish contact with the eastern
parts of the state territory, cut off from the
telecommunications network. Here ing. Pešek found a partner in the
operator of the Ostrava station OK2MA, the secondary-school
teacher Antonín Macháň. (He was four years older than OK1KX, but
began with radio later. Emil Zavadil, ex OK2HX, introduced him to
the secrets of short waves. They were preparing for the
examination together, but Macháň got ahead of him, so as to be the
first in Ostrava, and obtained his concession in April 1931.)
     OK2MA maintained regular sessions with OK1KX from 20 May to
7 June 1945. Between Prague and Ostrava they carried several dozen
telegrams, a large part of which has been preserved in the form of
documentary records. The contacts were difficult. Macháň could
sit down at the key only at 23:00. The sessions took place in the
3.5 MHz band with an input power of less than 10 W, amid strong
atmospheric disturbances and interference from powerful American
and Soviet military stations, which worked without interruption.


---


mm   ••• M
      In the cemetery at Svitávka, on 12 May 1945, two
Czechoslovak soldiers were buried — Jaroslav Jurčík and Josef
Jirout, taken prisoner in the area of Holešov. Their smashed,
disfigured faces and wounds all over their bodies were testimony
of their death. The perpetrators of the murder were members of an
armored unit which on 1 May murdered in Přerov and then moved on
to Svitávka. In the last hours of their power they murdered the
technical clerk Pěta from Sebranice, whom they had arrested at the
Vaculka; all three they buried in the park on the little island.


---


Work was going on to restore transport and supplies; newspapers
and letters began to arrive.
    "It's worse with the telegraph and the telephone," said Bohuš
Bednář. "We can't get through either to Brno or to Prague. Only to
Boskovice and a few villages around."
    "There was an appeal on the radio asking amateur transmitters
to help the post offices. Shouldn't we try it?"
      I had a Pento SW3AC. František Matuška (now OK2PAF) also
listened.
      "Come to the post office. We'll arrange it."
      In all the assorted junk left behind by the Wehrmacht there
was also a Torn Fu.1b transmitter-receiver. Franta Matuška began
putting it into operation.
      There was no rail link with Brno. The trains ended at Adamov.
One day the news spread through Svitávka that a truck would go to
Brno. But it would take only those who had a permit from the
National Committee. There were enough volunteers to fill several
railway wagons. I, however, was lucky enough to obtain that rare
paper. And in Brno I managed to find ing. Krčma, OK2XY, of the
KSR.
      "Listen at 18:00 in the 80 m band. We broadcast under the
sign OK2Y."
      The postmaster Mazal and his deputy, the later postmaster
Kronek, accepted our offer with joy.
      The post office was located in the town-hall building at the
corner of the square by the road to Míchov, its front tract facing
the square, the rear the river. Bohuš climbed up the little tower
and fastened one end of the wire there. The other we tied to a
tall wooden rake.
      On 25 May 1945 at 18:00 we caught the station OK2Y. The Torn
Fu.1b had a separate transmitting section and a separate receiving
section. Each had its own dial, calibrated in kHz. We tuned the
transmitter to the same division as the receiver was tuned to, and
began calling. The whole staff of the post office gathered eagerly
around us. OK2Y did not answer us. It called the general call a
few times and fell silent.
      We had not expected such a failure, and could not explain
it.
      "We probably have a bad antenna. It's bent, low, and doesn't
have enough clear space around it."
      "A 2.5 m rod antenna takes 120 mA of antenna current, and
ours only 20 at most."
      On 26 May the whole post office built a new antenna. Right
across the river. We tied the wire to a wooden peg and one after
another tried to throw it across the river. It was raining and an
ugly wind was blowing. In the end it succeeded. But when we
tightened the antenna, the wire broke and crashed into the river.
      Someone advised us: "Go to the Jarošes."
      Eduard Jaroš, owner of the mill and the power plant, gave us
a coil of good, strong wire; the antenna "pulled" better: 40 to 50
mA. How long it was, we of course did not know.
     At 18:00 OK2Y sounded again. And again we could not raise it.
Step by step we tuned to the frequencies of the stations OLB, OLS,
OLR; we called persistently and long, but in vain. These stations
were not in the amateur band.
      The station OLB transmitted on 4100 kHz. Its transmitter was
placed beside the transmitter of OK2Y on the top floor of the post
and telegraph directorate in the former Kounicova street; the
receiver of station OLB likewise at the directorate, and the
receiving station of OK2Y in Rezkova street. Station OLB worked
mostly with Prague.
      There the activity was concentrated in the Revolutionary
Radio Station Letná. Originally these were jammers, which, as in
Moravia, were operated by employees of the Protectorate post
office by order of the Reich German authorities.
      Already in January ing. Škop and comrade Janoušek had
discussed how to make use of this station when it "burst." On 5 May
our people occupied the receiving station at Bílá Hora and the
jamming station in the Technical Museum at Letná. At midnight,
precisely on 6 May at 00:30, the modulation line to the radio —
which was broadcasting from Strašnice on the 415 m wave — was
ready, and permanent contact with the National Council was
established. At the same time, broadcasting began on the 49 m
wave, and an hour later also in the 40 m band. These transmitters
worked above all in sessions intended for abroad.
       After the liberation, Letná began radiotelegraph operation
on 10 May with OLC (České Budějovice) and OLB (Brno), on 11 May
with OLS (Bratislava). The Prague radiotelegraphists immediately
recognized that at the key of OLS sat Ďurica, OK3FD. They knew his
hand. Ladislav Janoušek with the mechanic Knébel drove around the
former German installations and stuck paper labels with the round
stamp of the ministry of posts and telegraphs on instruments that
might be useful to the telegraph exchange.
       On 17 May, the day the children in Svitávka went to school
for the first time since 20 November 1944, there appeared at the
Revolutionary Radio Station Letná the air-force lieutenant Milan
Český (then under the name Bajer). He loaded a transmitter, a
receiver and some small items and flew to Ostrava. After landing
he went to the SNB station beside the town hall.
      "Help me unload and install this."
      "I am a military radiotelegraphist," volunteered Olda Král
(now OK2OQ). "For six years I've had no earphones on, but I'll get
back into it."
       They set up the transmitter at the former exhibition
ground, and installed the receiving station at the town hall on
the 3rd floor. Olda repeats the Q-code and the Z-code to himself
and diligently trains by listening to OLP and OLB. Suddenly he
hears a dispatch from Prague:
       "OLB DE OLP — at 18:00 listen for Ostrava and help us
establish contact. We cannot raise Ostrava."
       He telephones the transmitter.
       "Tune, until I get you on the assigned frequency." With his
receiver he guides the transmitter. "Now fine-tune, so that I hear
you as well as possible."
       Král gradually lowers the volume on the receiver.
      "Enough, leave it like that, and at 18:00 let it be ready."
       18:00
       OLR OLR OLR DE OLP OLP OLP ZHC?
       OLP OLP OLP DE OLR OLR OLR ZOK GA
       OLR DE OLP — greetings, comrades, brothers, friends. We are
glad to have contact with Ostrava — QTC.
       Oldřich Král, OK2OQ, takes the first telegram. He remembers
it to this day. It was for Anežka Kučerová of Karviná, from her
son, a soldier, that he was alive and well.
       Gradually the Poděbrady transmitters OLM, OLH, OLF and, in
Moravská Ostrava, OLO are put into operation.
      The station OK2S calls them all one after another. We
carefully tune the dial of transmitter S to the same division as
on the dial of receiver F. I ride home on my bicycle; Franta
calls. I listen briefly. A tremendous signal.
      27 May 1945. OK2Y OK2Y OK2Y DE OK2S OK2S... Nothing.
      The mood at the Svitávka post office sinks below freezing.
Our station is in the little room behind the office. Parcels are
usually stored there. The station is laid out on a table on which
there are still some papers and an ashtray full of stubs and
cigarette ash. In the alcove stands a cabinet with various forms,
an Austro-Hungarian list of all the communities of the monarchy
and other useless things. Through the barred window one can see
across the river to the Štáchas' house. Now Franta has gone to
listen. I am at the key. In twenty minutes he is back.
      "The signal is like thunder."
      We try to clarify the situation.
      "If it weren't transmitting, no current would flow into the
antenna."
      "I'll take the receiver in my rucksack, get on my bicycle,
and ride toward Brno. Every few kilometers I'll try. We'll see how
far it can be heard."
      Franta shrugged.
      "Before you get anywhere, the post office will have its
lines in order."
      After lunch I brought a half-dismantled Hartley, an
absorption loop and a wavemeter. We played with the Hartley and
started it up. It had originally been intended for other purposes,
but it was never so used. Fortunately. It had a wretched tone,
key clicks, and a whole fan of subharmonics in the broadcast band
on medium waves. The absorption loop glowed strongly. The antenna
milliammeter, pulled out of the Torn Fu.1b, however, showed barely
half deflection.
      The postal workers had nothing left for us but a pitying
smile.
      Franta tries both transmitters. Earphones on. The bulb in
the absorption wavemeter glows. Suddenly he cries out:
     "Jožko! I've got it!"
     I jumped to him. He points with his hand at the wavemeter.
     "The dial on the 'zender' (transmitter) doesn't agree. It's
terribly out of calibration. We're transmitting outside the band."
      He sits on his bicycle and rides home for tools.
      At 18:00 we did not call OK2Y. The transmitter, taken out of
its cabinet, lay on the table. It was not easy. We had no
schematic. We were both SWLs. I was 462, Franta 708. We had no
experience with transmitters.
      When we were in the thick of the work, the door opened. In
came Franta's XYL Ela and Míla Jedličková. We had completely
forgotten about supper.
      The girls stayed with us at the post office. And it was
well. The failures and lack of success, the intense activity and
tension of the last days began to take effect. Several times we
fell into depression; we wanted to drop everything and walk away
from it. But thanks to the understanding and interest of Ela and
Míla, thanks to their encouragement, their patience and sympathy,
we persevered.
      At half past one in the morning František Matuška was
finished with the repair. At two the apparatus was mounted in its
cabinet, tested and ready for operation. At half past two we
parted at the secondary school. It was already 28 May 1945. In
those few hours that remained till dawn, there could be little
talk of sleep. It was a restless half-sleep and a feverish
weariness.
      In the morning we were at the post office again. In the
transmitting room a bluish cloud of smoke still hung in the air.
      That day we established the first contact, and that with
station OK2GR. Mr. Kronek fastened on the town-hall gate a
conspicuous notice in ornamental lettering:
      Telegrams are accepted
      for Třebíč, Náměšť and
      for Velké Meziříčí.
      The spell of bad luck was broken. Bohuš Bednář
congratulated: "Boys, I believed in you."
      Our joy knew no bounds. OK2GR had been a very active amateur
before the war. He wrote interesting articles. He took me in hand
at the worst time, when the Nazis closed the Czech universities
and I was looking for work. He took me to the firm ELECTRUM,
Rádio REL in Brno, where ing. Kolesnikov, OK2KW, was also
employed.
      The next station we contacted was OK1KV, the Turnov Radio
Club. It took from us telegrams for Prague. And OK2DS, Zlín.
      On 29 May 1945 at 18:00 we finally raised station OK2Y.
On the gate appeared the notice:
      "Telegrams are accepted without restriction."
      On 30 May we were to have a session at 6:00 with OK2Y, but
it did not appear. That same day we wrote to ing. Krčma:
     "Dear engineer.
     Yesterday evening we worked with station OK2Y and arranged a
session for 6:00. Our district still has neither telegraph nor
telephone connection with Brno; therefore we placed ourselves at
the disposal of the post office, so that as amateurs we might be
useful and so fulfill our duty toward the state. That assumes,
however, that your station will meet us halfway. As soon as the
post offices of our district begin taking telegrams for Brno, it
will not be possible to handle them in a single session at 18:00,
when there is still QRM and QRN. We therefore ask you to arrange
for us a connection with station OLB, which we hear well, or that
station OK2Y call us in regular sessions, for example at 11:00,
15:00 and 18:00. At the same time we could take everything for the
Boskovice district and for Zlín, with which we maintain a transit
connection via OK2DS.
     We strive to serve you well and to uphold the honor of
Czechoslovak short-wave amateurs. We look forward to your enabling
us this service, and we shall listen at 11:00 and 15:00 on the
frequencies of OK2Y and OLB. Pse QSP 73 to OK2RZ."
     (I myself no longer recalled this letter. Ing. Krčma
remembered it and kindly provided me with a photocopy of it.)
     Ing. Svatopluk Krčma, who was building up the Brno
radio-technical office and organizing and directing the work of
stations OLB and OK2Y, granted our request in full and created the
conditions for our successful activity. We had no direct connection
with station OLB. It did, however, take over some of our dispatches
via OK2Y for further transport. At OLB worked the radiotelegraphists
Kalia, Zedníček and Halenka. They performed their duty devotedly
and under difficult circumstances. Since the trams did not run,
they walked to work. Kalia from Černá Pole, Zedníček from the then
Masaryk quarter. S. Kalia remembers station OK2S well.
     On the morning of 30 May we dispatched the first two batches
of telegrams to Brno and to Turnov, and we received dispatches for
Boskovice and other places of the district, which our post office
at once relayed by telephone. By half past ten we already had a
clear table and were getting ready for lunch. All the while,
however, we monitored the 80 m band. At 11:09 we hear CQ DE
OK2GR. We call him.
     OK2S DE OK2GR — greetings — urgently need Prague as soon as
possible. OK1KV will not be QRV until five. Do you have a sked
with him? What about OK2DS? HW?
     We answer that we have Brno at 14:30 and Turnov at 17:00.
     OK2S DE OK2GR — a man from Dachau is in Prague and cannot
hold out any longer. By chance a car from Třebíč is in Prague, and
we need urgently to notify MSG NW — Radio Prague — the National
Committee of Třebíč asks Radio Prague for the immediate
announcement of this news: Lieutenant Jedlička of Třebíč, now in
Prague, should call with the car for the gravely ill Miroslav
Kružík, returning from Dachau. He is at Prague 14, Na Nivách 278.
We ask that you alert Lieutenant Jedlička to this appeal — HW?
     OK2GR DE OK2S QSL AS
     "That will be hard. So far the farthest we have raised is
Svitavy," say the postal workers. And they call Svitavy.
     "Just today the linemen are working on the line there, and a
moment ago we were talking with Česká Třebová."
     "Try it once more; it concerns a man returning from a
concentration camp."
     Several endless minutes.
     Česká Třebová reports. After another few minutes we hear
Czechoslovak Radio, Prague. But a hitch. It does not hear
Svitávka. The operator at Česká Třebová helps. She follows the
connection, repeating after Svitávka word for word, until Radio
has the whole message flawlessly.
     We inform OK2GR.
OK2S DE OK2GR — r — I'll turn on the radio to check — if there were
5–10 hams like you, that would be fb tfc.
     We go to lunch. We turn on the radio too. At 13:30 we hear in
the repatriation reports an announcement from Třebíč. But it is not
our doing. Just a few lucky chances. It will be another month
before there is a connection with Česká Třebová and Prague. And
whether Lieutenant Jedlička hears the radio announcement at all...
that really no longer concerns us.
17.25 contact with OK2Y. Telegrams no. 10 and 12 from Boskovice,
no. 7 from Svitávka, no. 3 from Letovice. QRX 8.00 — 73 SK.
     Sessions with OK2Y: 8.00, 8.12, 9.20, 10.30. Telegrams from
Boskovice, from Letovice, from Svitávka.
11.00 OK2S DE OK2Y QRU SK.
14.30 OK2S DE OK2Y QRU SK.
17.20 OK2S DE OK2MV — ere msg — to Radio Journal Prague. We ask
for news of the son Rudolf Pospíšil of Hodonín, who was interned
in the camp Oranienburg. Last news September 1944 — Josef
Pospíšil, Hodonín, Sušilova 84.
     1 June 1945
9.15 OK2GR DE OK2S QTC 1 Velké Meziříčí, OTC 1 Křižanov.
     OK2S DE OK2GR — this morning the local farmers came to me,
saying they have several wagons of good eating potatoes and no use
for them. That I should give the news to the world, otherwise the
potatoes will spoil — so QSP in Brno and other places.
10.00 OK2GR DE OK2Y — About those potatoes we shall inform the
national committee.
10.05 OK2S DE OK2Y QTC Sebranice.
11.00 We hand station OK2Y telegrams from Letovice and from
Svitávka.
12.00 We hand station OK2Y two telegrams from Boskovice. QRX
14.30.
14.30 We take from OK2Y a telegram to Šebetov.
15.00 OK2Y transmits by voice:
     "To all amateur transmitters in Moravia.
     On the basis of the radio appeal of the ministry of posts and
telegraphs we call on all of you to cooperate.
     The transmitting station OK2Y will work for you all day and
expects your call every hour on the hour, beginning at 8.00 until
20.00.
     Register with your post offices and national committees, so
that you can carry dispatches from your vicinity. Telegrams for
you will be taken over by the nearest post office, and the
telegrams received you will likewise hand in at the same office.
Contact with abroad is forbidden. So far we are working with
these stations: OK2GR at Velké Meziříčí, OK2S at Svitávka, OK2MV
Hodonín. Try to establish contact with newly appearing OK
stations, alert them to us, so that our circle may be larger and so
that we can proceed against unauthorized ones.
     We need contact with the districts east of the Morava river,
such as Olomouc, Zlín, Kroměříž, Hranice, Mor. Ostrava.
     If you decide to cooperate, fix the exact hours when we can
surely call you, and report them to us. Station OK2Y broadcasts
news and information for amateurs in the 3.5 MHz band by voice
daily at 15:00. We take over telegrams only by telegraph, for it
cannot be assumed that in a short time an amateur could build a
sufficiently powerful voice station to be well heard under all
circumstances. OK2Y listens all day, and therefore it is possible
in case of need to call at other times too, up to 19:00."
16.00 We have for OK2Y two telegrams from Svitávka and one from
Vítějevs.
2 June 1945
8.20 OK2S DE OK2GR transit from Velké Meziříčí to Brno.
"8.45 OK2S DE OK2DS — honor to labor — hr is a telegram for
Prague — Mr. Klement Gottwald, deputy premier of the ČSR, Prague
— comrade, the united working people of the Zlín region await you
on Wednesday 9 June at a public rally in Zlín. Say whether you
will come. Honor to labor. — KSČ Zlín."
"The Central Committee of the SČM Prague — we ask for the sending
of directives for the leadership of the SČM — district leadership
of the SČM Zlín."
     At the next session we pass these telegrams on to Brno for
delivery to Prague. We append the telegrams from Svitávka, from
Boskovice and Letovice which in the meantime had reached our post
office by telephone.
     So it went on and on. One little intermezzo: soldiers
arrived who had been listening to us and knew about us. And they
said they urgently needed our transmitter. Franta gave it to them.
The next day he already had a new one built: a TPTG with an AL4,
which was in operation almost to mid-August. In the time of its
activity station OK2S carried 2,793 telegrams.


---


The town hall in Svitávka was torn down after some years and a
cinema was built on its site. When the post office was moved, all
the useless papers were destroyed, including those concerning
station OK2S. Some documents, fragmentary notes and above all
memories have been preserved. We remember station OK2Y, its
operators Běloch (OK2PCU), Saxl (OK2SZ), Šustr, and indeed
everyone who helped.

            IN PLACE OF A CONCLUSION


---


May 1984. A receiver tuned to the 2 m band at 145.700 MHz is
alive. It talks from the early morning hours until the next day
almost till dawn, and then again.
      Into the conversation, at intervals, sound in Morse the sign
of the repeater OK0C and two-tone beacon signals for tuning.
Stations from Trutnov, from Náchod, from Poděbrady, Olomouc,
Liberec, Litovel, Opava talk together. Direct contact is more
intimate and the circle of its audibility limited (via the
repeater OK0C it can be heard from Aš to Opava). And for direct
contacts, station cards are collected. Here powerful instruments
and ingenious antenna systems come into their honor, to which
must be added good conditions and a bit of luck. Then it is
possible on two meters to correspond even between Czechoslovakia
and Spain, as OK2BFH managed on 28 June 1979.
     It is obvious that we ought to have some mirror that would
reflect those very short waves into the distance. Such mirrors
exist: ionized trails of meteors, the aurora, and the moon, which
reflects electromagnetic waves. It requires great power, perfect
directional antennas with high gain, able to aim skillfully at
that moon, and especially sensitive receivers with negligible
noise. Oldřich Kalandra, OK2TU, in Svitavy has built beside his
house a twenty-meter lattice mast with an enormous antenna, which
turns round and round and tilts 70° as well. The designer of the
OK0C repeater, Standa Blažka, OK2MBS, established on 15 May 1981
in Nová Paka a contact in the 145 MHz band with Australia, and by
1984 already has on two meters the WAC (Worked All Continents) and
over 50 countries.
     Ing. Karel Jordán achieved the first contact by meteor-trail
reflection on 13 August 1981 with the Bulgarian station LZ1LW. He
was interested in moonbounce operation and measured the noise of
the sun at rising and at setting. Astronomy had been his hobby
since his earliest student years. Before the Second World War he
inherited from an uncle old magazines, earphones and a crystal
set. He put it into operation and became a radio amateur.
     Jordán had the opportunity to develop his hobby in the
Svazarm radio clubs during his studies in Brno and in Prague. The
club stations OK2OBA and OK1KUR, field days at Zlaté návrší and
at Louná, races and contests, monitoring of the experiments of
German stratospheric balloons — these were the milestones that
marked out for Karel Jordán the road all the way to the memorable
16 October 1972, when at 8:30 he established the first
Czechoslovak contact via satellite, and that with the station
SK6AB, with the university in Gothenburg. (The official
Czechoslovak ground station for space communications began its
activity in April 1974.)
      The second to achieve a contact via satellite is Standa
Blažka, OK1MBS. The third is Ondrej Oravec, originally OK3CDI, now
OK3AU, the first Slovak station on space communications.
      Ondrej's amateur career began in the fifties in Košice at the
Svazarm club station OK3KSI. He listened to the first Sputnik.
There followed satellite contacts with India, Africa and the
eastern regions of the USSR, and on the threshold of the eighties
he already has over 2,000 QSL cards for contacts from Košice to
every corner of the world. Nearly half bear the note that it is
the sender's first satellite contact.
      "Interesting was the African station 5Z4ZJ somewhere in the
bush in Kenya in 1976. It was a doctor who made the chassis of his
transmitter from an aluminum injection-box. A crystal suited him,
which he used as a flying doctor for communication and whose
harmonic happened to fall on the up-link frequency. He made the
reflector for the antenna from fencing wire," Ondrej recalls.
>>But satellites are not just for chatting. They are used for the
study of the propagation of electromagnetic waves, for the
forecasting of the sporadic E layer and of aurora, and for
verifying the results of vertical sounding by the ionospheric
radar."
      Ondrej Oravec, OK3AU, is an excellent telegraphist. He has
experience from Ruzyně airport and from amateur operation. The
possibility of communication during the passes of the first
satellites lasted only a few tens of seconds.
      "One must have good clocks and must know how to schedule
one's time, especially at home. When I calculate the passes well,
the satellite appears for me to the second exactly. The equipment
can be simple, but decisive is perseverance in the work," Oravec
concludes.
      The long waves, which in the twenties and thirties were
packed with stations, present in 1984 quite a different picture.
It would even seem that they gape emptiness. There are the
worldwide navigation networks, time signals and a few stations
with weather reports.
      The Italian postal administration asked in 1935, very
urgently, the Czechoslovak ministry of posts and telegraphs to
switch over at last, in the Prague–Rome circuit, from long waves
to short, where operation is more economical. Yugoslavia submitted
an equally pressing request. Prague was then the only station
that still worked with Belgrade on the long wave. Barely half a
century later, radio communication is moving to satellites, to
very short and ultra-short waves.
      The division of waves at the international conference of
1927 ended at five meters. When amateurs began to broadcast after
the Second World War, OK1AA called proudly: "General call on the
wave of a mere five meters." In 1982 the engineers Jan Franc,
OK1VAM, and Josef Smítka, OK1WFE, set between Klínovec and Praděd
a European record in communication in the 5.7 GHz band.
      In 1984 Ondrej Oravec could even do without precise watches
and would not have to orient his antennas so carefully as he is
accustomed. The satellite AO 10 is above us several hours a day
and permits even long contacts with the most distant corners of
the globe. The antenna is not so delicate a matter either. When
ing. Jordán, OK1BMW, found that AO 10 was already flying, he
leaned the antenna against a ladder on the balcony and made the
contact on the first try.
      Amateur and professional stations controlled by computers
are already commonplace today. The process of automation of
stations will continue. A worldwide amateur network in the 1296
MHz band, computer-controlled, is being prepared.
      "Communication via third-generation satellites will really
be contact with people connected to the same telephone exchange,"
Oravec opines.
      How deeply mistaken were all those who wrote after the
Second World War that the development of radio technology was
finished and that at most only an occasional minor improvement
could be expected! That the historical role of amateurs had been
played out with the first transatlantic contact on short waves!
But — what next? Futurologists imagine the amateur transmitting
station as a robot. One will speak Japanese into the microphone
and the conversation will go out of the transmitter in English.
The receiving path will work in reverse. But that is only the
ripening of the stage we are already living through. There were
sparks, tubes, transistors. We have integrated circuits, modules,
microprocessors, computers, space communications. The future is
veiled in a mystery that awaits the young generation of the
XXI century.
